->www.e-rara.ch<-

# A Forerunner to Cosmographic Dissertations, containing the Secret of the Universe <note>original: "Mysterium Cosmographicum"</note>, regarding the wonderful proportion of the celestial spheres, and the genuine causes for the number, magnitude, and periodic motions of the heavens...

## Johannes Kepler
## Nicolaus Copernicus <note>The astronomer who proposed the Sun-centered model of the universe</note>
## Michael Maestlin <note>Kepler’s professor at the University of Tübingen, who helped edit the work and added his own appendix</note>
## Johannes Schöner <note>A noted 16th-century mathematician and polymath</note>
## Gruppenbach <note>Georg Gruppenbach, the printer in Tübingen</note>

## Tübingen, 1596

## ETH Library, Zurich

->Shelf Mark: RAR 1367: 1<-

->Persistent Link: http://dx.doi.org/10.3931/e-rara-445<-

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page serves as the title page for Johannes Kepler's first major work. It contains handwritten library marks and a circular stamp from the Zurich Observatory (Sternwarte Zürich).</meta>

<gloss>The Forerunner</gloss> <gloss>The Forerunner <note>original: "le Precurseur"</note></gloss>

# The Forerunner

### OF COSMOGRAPHIC DISSERTATIONS, CONTAINING THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY,

# ON THE ADMIRABLE
## PROPORTION OF THE CELESTIAL
### ORBS, AND ON THE CAUSES
->of the number, size, and periodic motions of the heavens, both genuine and proper,<-

### DEMONSTRATED, THROUGH THE FIVE
->regular Geometric solids,<- <note>These are the five Platonic solids: the tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron.</note>

->BY<-

## MASTER JOHANNES KEPLER, OF WÜRTTEM-
->*berg, Mathematician to the Illustrious provincial estates of Styria.*<-

<insert>ZÜRICH Observatory</insert>

> I die daily, I confess: but while my labor holds me
> along the constant paths of the heavens:
> I do not touch the earth with my feet: but before the Thunderer <note>A poetic reference to God or Jupiter.</note>
> I feast on nectar and divine ambrosia.
<note>This is a famous epigram attributed to the ancient astronomer Claudius Ptolemy, expressing the spiritual ecstasy found in studying the stars.</note>

->*Added is the learned* ACCOUNT BY MASTER GEORG JOACHIM<-
->RHETICUS, *on the Books of Revolutions, and the admirable hypotheses on the number,*<-
->*order, and distances of the Spheres of the World, by the most excellent Ma-*<-
->thematician, and Restorer of all Astronomy, LORD NICOLAUS<-
->COPERNICUS.<- <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus was the sole pupil of Copernicus; his "Narratio Prima" was the first published summary of the Copernican sun-centered system.</note>

<image-desc>A small decorative printer's ornament featuring a stylized floral design with symmetrical scrolls and leaves.</image-desc>

### TÜBINGEN
## Printed by Georg Gruppenbach,
### IN THE YEAR 1596.

<summary>This is the title page of Johannes Kepler's landmark 1596 work, which attempted to explain the structure of the universe using the five Platonic solids. It also includes an endorsement of the Copernican system by Georg Joachim Rheticus.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page. The catchword "ILLV-" from the previous page's footer matches the start of the author's title ("Illustrious") on the title page, confirming this is the subsequent leaf containing the introductory poem.</meta>

## FRIENDLY READER
### GREETINGS.

What the world is, what cause moved God, and the reason for creating,
From where God derived the numbers, and what rule governs so great a mass,
What causes the six circuits <note>original: "sex circuitus"; a reference to the six known planets of the time: Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>, and in which sphere each of the
Intervals fall; why Jupiter and Mars—
Though not in the first orbits—are separated by so great a gap:
Here, Pythagoras teaches you all these things through the five figures <note>original: "quinque figuris"; the five Platonic solids which Kepler used to model the distances between planetary spheres.</note>.
Indeed, he taught by example that we can be reborn,
After two thousand years of wandering, until he becomes Copernicus,
This better observer of the World <note>original: "Mundi speculator"</note>, by that name. But you:
Do not prefer the acorns of the past to the newly discovered grain <note>This is a classical proverb. Acorns were considered the primitive food of early man; "grain" represents the superior, modern discovery of the Copernican system.</note>.

->I. K.<- <note>The initials of the author, Johannes Kepler.</note>

<vocab>Pythagoras <gloss>Ancient Greek philosopher who believed the universe was built on mathematical proportions.</gloss>, Copernicus <gloss>The astronomer who proposed the sun-centered model of the universe.</gloss>, Jupiter <gloss>The fifth planet from the sun.</gloss>, Mars <gloss>The fourth planet from the sun.</gloss>, Observer of the World <gloss>A title Kepler uses here to honor Copernicus as a visionary.</gloss>, Five figures <gloss>The Platonic solids: cube, tetrahedron, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron.</gloss>, Friendly Reader <gloss>A standard Latin salutation to the audience.</gloss></vocab>

<summary>This page contains a short, programmatic poem in Latin by Johannes Kepler, greeting the reader and outlining the astronomical mysteries he intends to solve using the five Platonic solids.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Nicolaus Copernicus, planetary orbits, Mars, Jupiter, Platonic solids, cosmology, mathematical harmony</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the work. It follows the introductory poem where Kepler (signing as I.K.) invited the reader to abandon "acorns" for the "new grain" of Copernican astronomy. The catchword "culo" on this page suggests the next page begins with "speculo" (mirror), continuing the metaphor of seeing God reflected in the "Book of Nature."</meta>

# TO THE ILLUSTRIOUS,
## NOBLE, MOST DISTINGUISHED,
### AND VIGOROUS LORD, SIGISMUND
Frederick, Free Baron <term>Libero Baroni; a title of nobility indicating a baron who held his land directly from the Emperor.</term> of Herberstein, Neuperg, and Guettenhaag, Lord in Lankowitz, hereditary Chamberlain and Cup-bearer of Carinthia, Councilor to His Imperial Majesty and to the most serene Archduke of Austria, Ferdinand; Governor of the Province of Styria:

-> AND, <-

### *TO THE LORDS [WHO SERVE AS] THE ILLUSTRIOUS*
### *ORDINARY FIVE COMMISSIONERS OF THE ESTATES OF STYRIA,*
->*most eminent Men, my gracious Lords*<-
->*and benefactors, greetings and*<-
->*my services.*<-

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring intricate floral and foliate scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>What I promised seven months ago—a work which, by the testimony of the learned, is beautiful and pleasing, and far to be preferred to annual prognostications <note>original: "annuis prognosticis"; Kepler refers to the popular astrological calendars and weather predictions common at the time, contrasting them with his serious astronomical work.</note>—I finally at last present to your Assembly <note>original: "Coronae vestrae"; literally "your crown," a respectful address to the gathered body of the regional government.</note>, Most Eminent Men. It is a work, I say, small in size and produced with modest labor, but with subject matter wonderful in every respect. For if one looks at its antiquity, it was attempted two thousand years ago by Pythagoras; if one looks at its novelty, it is now for the first time being published among men by me. Does its magnitude please? Nothing in this entire world is greater or more expansive. Is dignity desired? Nothing is more precious, nothing more beautiful than this most radiant temple of God. Does it please you to know something of a secret? Nothing is or ever has been more hidden in the nature of things. Only in this one respect does it not satisfy everyone: that its utility is obscure to the unthinking. Yet this is that very book of Nature, so greatly celebrated in the sacred scriptures; which Paul <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul, specifically Romans 1:20, regarding God's attributes being visible in creation.</note> holds out to the nations, in which God—as if the Sun reflected in water or a mirror—

<summary>In this formal dedication, Kepler presents his work to the noble governors of Styria, positioning his planetary model as a recovery of ancient Pythagorean wisdom and a study of the "Book of Nature." He contrasts his mathematical astronomy with common astrological almanacs, emphasizing the spiritual dignity of studying the cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>Sigismund von Herberstein, Styria, Pythagoras, Book of Nature, Apostle Paul, planetary theory, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle from page 1. The previous page ended with the phrase "so that, as if in a mir-," which is completed here by "ror, they may contemplate [God]." This refers to the theological concept of the "Book of Nature" where God is seen through His handiwork.</meta>

# DEDICATORY LETTER

*...[mirror], they may contemplate [God]. For why should we Christians take less delight in this contemplation, we whose duty it is to celebrate, worship, and admire God with true devotion? This is done with a spirit that is all the more devoted the more correctly we understand what and how great the things are that our God has created. Indeed, David, a true worshiper of God, sang many hymns to the Creator, the true God, in which he draws his arguments from his admiration of the heavens.*

> *The heavens declare, he says, the glory of GOD. I will see your heavens, the work of your fingers, the Moon and the stars, which you have founded: Great is our Lord, and great is his power; he who counts the multitude of stars, and calls them all by name.* <note>These are excerpts from the Psalms (19:1, 8:3, and 147:4-5), which Kepler uses to provide a biblical mandate for the study of astronomy.</note>

*Elsewhere, full of the spirit and sacred joy, he cries out and calls to the world itself:*

> *Praise the Lord, you heavens; Praise him, Sun and Moon, etc.*

*What voice do the heavens have? What voice do the stars have, by which they might praise God in the manner of a human being? Only that, insofar as they provide the evidence for humans to praise God, they themselves are said to praise Him. While we give voice to the heavens and the nature of things in these pages and make that voice clearer, let no one accuse us of vanity or of labor spent in vain.*

*I will say nothing of the fact that in this material there is a great argument for Creation, which the [pagan] Philosophers denied <note>Kepler refers to the Aristotelian view that the universe is eternal, contrasting it with the Christian doctrine that the world was created at a specific point in time.</note>: we see how God, like one of our own Architects, approached the construction of the world with order and rule, and measured each part so carefully that it is as if art did not imitate nature, but God Himself had looked toward the building methods of future humanity.*

*Yet why is it necessary to value the utility of divine things with a coin, as if they were a side-dish <term>obsonij; a relish or piece of food eaten with bread, implying something bought merely for consumption</term>? For what, I ask, does the knowledge of natural things—or indeed all the rest of Astronomy—profit a hungry belly? Yet wise men do not listen to those barbarians who shout that these studies should therefore be abandoned. We tolerate painters who delight the eyes, and musicians who delight the ears, even though they bring no material profit to our affairs. And the pleasure derived from the works of both is considered not only human but even honorable. Therefore, what...*

<meta>catchword: tur (completing the word "igitur" or "therefore")</meta>

<summary>Kepler argues that studying astronomy is a religious duty, using the Psalms to show that the heavens "speak" of God's glory. He defends the value of "useless" scientific knowledge by comparing it to the honorable pleasure provided by art and music.</summary>
<keywords>theology and science, Book of Nature, King David, Psalms, Creation, Architect of the Universe, utility of astronomy, aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory letter from the previous page, where the author was discussing how studying the heavens is a form of worship. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a thought about why it is natural for the human mind to seek joy in understanding the universe.</meta>

# D E D I C A T O R Y

*...what inhumanity, what folly it would be to begrudge the mind its own honorable joy, while not begrudging the eyes and ears theirs? It is contrary to the nature of things for anyone to fight against these recreations. For He, the most excellent Creator <note>original: "Creator optimus"</note>, who introduced nothing into nature without providing abundantly for both necessity and for beauty and pleasure: would He have blessed the human mind—the mistress of all nature and His very own image—with no pleasure of its own?*

*Indeed, just as we do not ask what hope of gain makes a little bird sing—since we know that pleasure is inherent in song because the bird was created for the purpose of singing—so too we should not ask why the human mind takes such great pains to seek out these secrets of the heavens <note>original: "caelorum arcanis"</note>. For this reason, our Maker <note>original: "Opifice nostro"</note> has joined the mind to the senses; not merely so that man might sustain himself (which many types of living creatures can do far more skillfully even with a brute mind), but also so that from those things which we perceive with our eyes, we might strive toward the causes of why they exist and come to be—even if we were to derive no other utility from it.*

*And just as other animals and the human body are sustained by food and drink, so the human soul itself—which is something distinct from the man—is nourished, increased, and in a sense matures on this "food" of knowledge <note>original: "cognitionis pabulo"</note>. A man is more like a dead thing than a living one if he is untouched by a desire for these things. Therefore, just as by the providence of Nature food never fails living creatures, so we can rightly say that there is such a great variety in things, and such hidden treasures in the fabric of the heavens, so that fresh food would never be lacking for the human mind, nor would it grow tired of the old, nor would it rest, but would have in this world a perpetual workshop for exercising its office.*

*Nor truly is the nobility of these feasts—which I draw out from the richest storehouse of the Creator in this little book, as if onto a dining table—any less because they will either not be tasted or will be rejected by the majority of the common people. More people praise the goose than the pheasant, because the former is common and the latter rare. Yet no palate of an Apicius <note>Marcus Gavius Apicius, a famous Roman gourmet and lover of luxury in the 1st century AD.</note> would rank the former below the latter. Thus, the dignity of this subject matter will be all the greater the fewer praisers it finds, provided only that they are intelligent. Not the same [common]...*

A 3
<meta>catchword: common-</meta>

<summary>In this page of the dedication, the author argues that the human mind was divinely designed to find joy in scientific discovery. He compares the intellectual pursuit of astronomy to a necessary "food" for the soul and a "noble feast" intended for those who appreciate the complexity of the heavens.</summary>

<keywords>Creator, human mind, nature, secrets of the heavens, food of knowledge, Apicius, astronomy as worship, divine purpose</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory letter from page 3. The author is concluding a philosophical argument about why intellectual pursuits, specifically astronomy, provide a unique form of fulfillment that worldly power cannot offer.</meta>

# L E T T E R

...they are also suitable for princes. Nor are these celestial matters the common property of everyone; rather, they are the nourishment of a noble mind <note>original: "generosi... animi pabulum"</note>. This is not by my wish or effort, nor by the nature of the studies themselves, nor by any "envy" on God's part <note>The author suggests God does not hide knowledge out of spite</note>, but rather because of the stupidity or laziness of the majority of men. Princes are accustomed to having certain items of great price served among their second courses <note>A reference to "dessert" or luxury items served after a meal to stimulate a sated appetite</note>, which they use only when they are already full, for the sake of relieving boredom. In the same way, these and similar studies will finally taste sweet to the most noble and wise person only when he has ascended through villages, towns, provinces, and kingdoms to the rule of the world, and has looked closely at nearly everything—and has not found anywhere anything (as is the way with human affairs) that is truly blessed, lasting, or of such a kind that his appetite could be satisfied and ended by it. For then he will begin to seek better things; then he will ascend from the earth to this heaven; then he will transfer his mind, weary from empty cares, to this peace; then he will say:

->Happy the souls, whose first care it was to know these things<-
->And to climb into the dwellings above,<-

wherefore he will begin to despise those things which he once judged most excellent; he will prize these works of God alone, and finally take pure and sincere pleasure from these contemplations. Therefore, let whoever wishes, and as much as they wish, despise these and similar meditations <note>original: "meletemata," a Greek term for exercises or reflections</note>, and let them seek for themselves comforts, riches, and treasures from every side. Let this glory be enough for astronomers: that they write for philosophers, not for loud-mouthed brawlers <note>original: "rabulis," referring to aggressive, uneducated pleaders or shysters</note>; for kings, not for shepherds. I fearlessly predict, however, that there will be some who will find here a solace for their old age; namely those who, as long as they held public office <note>original: "Magistratus"</note>, conducted themselves so that they remained free in their morals and conscience, and thus fit to enjoy these delights.

There will arise again some Charles <note>A reference to Holy Roman Emperor Charles V (1500–1558), who abdicated his many thrones to retire to a monastery</note>, who, although all of Europe could not contain him while he ruled, might be contained by the tiny cell of Saint Just <note>The Monastery of Yuste in Spain, where Charles V retired</note> when weary of empires. To such a man—among so many spectacles, titles, triumphs, so many riches, cities, and kingdoms—a single planetary sphere <note>A mechanical model of the solar system</note>, whether of the Turrian type <note>original: "Turrianica," referring to Juanelo Turriano, the famous clockmaker and engineer who built planetary models for Charles V</note> or now of the Copernican-Pythagorean <note>original: "Copernicopythagoræa," referring to the sun-centered model advocated by Nicolaus Copernicus</note> type, might be so pleasing that he would exchange the whole world for it, preferring to rule circles with his finger rather than nations with his power.

I do not say these things, most distinguished gentlemen, so that I might bring a new paradox—old men becoming students—onto the stage or into the schools; but so that it may appear
<meta>catchword: what kind of</meta>

<summary>The author argues that astronomy is a "luxury" for the noble mind, providing a peace and satisfaction that worldly power cannot achieve. He cites the example of Emperor Charles V, who preferred mechanical planetary models to ruling Europe, to illustrate the superior joy of celestial study.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, nobility, Charles V, Juanelo Turriano, Copernicus, planetary sphere, retirement, contemplation</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory letter to the governors of the Styrian Estates in Graz. Johannes Kepler is finishing his argument on why mathematics and astronomy are the most appropriate subjects for the education of the nobility, and expresses his gratitude for their financial support.</meta>

# DEDICATION

...what the genuine time is for gathering the harvest from these studies. For why should I feel differently about the sowing of the seeds than the most prudent men of your Circle <note>original: "vestra Corona," referring to the governing council of the local estates</note>, who judged these studies to be among the foremost which should be offered to the young minds of the Nobility in your school. For they believe that there is no class of men more suited to cultivate <term>Mathematics</term> <note>In the 16th century, "mathematics" was a broad category including astronomy, geometry, music, and physics</note> than the Nobility—since other arts for earning a living are not so necessary to them—nor are any studies more suitable for the Nobility than <term>Mathematics</term>. This is because these studies possess a certain hidden and wonderful power above all others for training fierce spirits toward humanity and toward a sober contempt for earthly things. Even if this fruit is obscured for the young by the difficulty and unfamiliarity of the material, it reveals itself in its own time to the old, as was just mentioned.

And I have said these things thus far, both concerning these present pages and concerning all <term>Astronomy</term>, to you, lovers of Astronomy and of all Literature, most distinguished men. I do so to remind you of what you have long held to be true: that this little work which I humbly offer and dedicate will not be of no use to you, who are truly generous and truly noble. If this discovery deserves any praise, it belongs in large part to you, who through your liberality and your stipend have provided me with the opportunities and the leisure <note>original: "ocium," meaning the specialized time for study and contemplation away from public business</note> to comment upon these things in this way. Receive, therefore, most distinguished men, this token of a grateful heart, and take me, a humble client, into your favor; and finally, grow accustomed to being numbered among the <term>Atlases</term>, <term>Perseuses</term>, <term>Orions</term>, <term>Caesars</term>, <term>Alfonsos</term>, <term>Rudolfs</term> <note>Kepler links his patrons to a lineage of great figures: mythical heroes like Atlas who held up the sky, and kings like Alfonso X of Castile and Emperor Rudolf II, who were famous for sponsoring astronomical tables</note>, and the other promoters of <term>Astronomy</term>. Farewell. On the Ides of May <note>May 15th</note>: which day, a year ago, was the beginning of this labor.

->Humble Mathematician in your school at **Graz**,<-

->**Master Johannes Kepler**<-
->**of Wurttemberg.**<-

<summary>In this concluding dedication, Kepler argues that mathematical study refines the noble character and expresses his gratitude to his patrons in Graz for the funding that allowed him to complete this work.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Graz, mathematics, astronomy, nobility, patronage, Alfonso X, Rudolf II, education</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to the Styrian Estates to a personal preface addressed to the general reader. Kepler begins to describe the specific scientific and theological motivations behind his work.</meta>

# PREFACE TO THE
## READER.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring a floral scrollwork design with architectural motifs.</image-desc>
IT IS MY PURPOSE, Reader, to demonstrate in this little book that the most Good and Great Creator, in the creation of this moving World and the arrangement of the Heavens, looked to those five <term>regular solids</term> <note>These are the "Platonic Solids": the tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron. Kepler believed these geometric shapes dictated the spacing and number of the planets.</note> which have been most celebrated from the time of Pythagoras and Plato down to our own, and that He coordinated the number of the heavens, their proportions, and the system of their motions to the nature of those solids. But before I allow you to come to the matter itself, I shall discuss with you a few things regarding the occasion of this book and the reasoning behind my project, which I have judged to concern both your understanding and my own reputation.

Six years ago, while I was a student at <note>Tübingen</note> under that most distinguished man, Michael Maestlin, I was so moved by the many inconveniences of the common opinion concerning the world <note>the Earth-centered or Ptolemaic system</note> that I became greatly delighted with <term>Copernicus</term> <note>Nicolaus Copernicus, who proposed that the Earth revolves around the Sun</note>, of whom my teacher made very frequent mention in his lectures. Not only did I frequently defend his views in the physics disputes of the candidates, but I also wrote a careful dissertation on the "primary motion," <note>original: "motu primo," usually referring to the daily rotation of the heavens, which Kepler argues is actually the Earth's rotation</note> asserting that it happens because of the revolution of the Earth. And I was already at the point where I would ascribe the Solar motion to the Earth itself—just as Copernicus did for Mathematical reasons—but I would do so for Physical, or if you prefer, Metaphysical reasons. For this purpose, I gradually collected the advantages that Copernicus has in Mathematics over Ptolemy, partly from the lips of Maestlin and partly by my own effort. Joachim Rheticus <note>A student of Copernicus who published the first summary of his theories</note> could have easily freed me from this labor, as he followed each point briefly and clearly in his *First Narrative*. Meanwhile, while I was "rolling that stone" <note>An allusion to the labor of Sisyphus; original Greek: παρέργως (*parergōs*), meaning as a side-project or incidentally</note> alongside my theological studies, it happened conveniently that I came to Graz to succeed Georg Stadius of pious memory. There, the nature of my office bound me more closely to these studies. In the explanation of the principles of <term>Astronomy</term>, all those things which I had previously heard from Maestlin or had attempted myself were of great use to me. And as in Virgil, "Fame thrives on movement and gains strength as it goes," so for me, the diligent contemplation of these matters became the cause of further contemplation. Until finally, in the year [15]95, when I desired to spend my leisure from lectures well and according to the requirements of my office, I threw myself into this subject with the full force of my mind.

And there were three things in particular for which I stubbornly sought the causes as to why they were thus and not otherwise: the Number, the Size, and the Motion of the <term>spheres</term> <note>the planetary orbits</note>. What emboldened me to attempt this was that beautiful harmony of things at rest—the Sun, the fixed stars, and the space in between—with God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. I shall pursue this analogy further in my <term>Cosmography</term>. Since the stationary things were thus arranged, I did not doubt that the moving things would also reveal themselves. At first, I approached the matter through numbers, and I considered whether one orbit was the double, triple, or quadruple of another, or whatever it might be, and how much each orbit differed from the others in the system of Copernicus. I wasted a great deal of time in that labor, as if at play, since no equality appeared either in the proportions themselves or in their increases. I gained nothing from it, except that I carved those very distances, as produced by Copernicus, most deeply into my memory. Because these various...

<meta>catchword: attempts (natuum)</meta>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's preface, transitioning from his initial interest in the Copernican system to a description of his early, failed attempts to find a mathematical law governing the distances between the planets.</meta>

->TO THE READER. 7<-

The recounting of these movements may, like sea waves, toss your mind anxiously here and there, Reader; and once you are exhausted by them, you may then all the more gladly betake yourself to the causes explained in this little book, as if to a safe harbor. Yet, from time to time, certain reasons—which will follow below—consoled me and raised my spirits toward a better hope. This was because the motion always seemed to follow the distance closely; where there was a great gap between the spheres, there was also a great gap between the motions. If (I thought) God adapted the motions to the prescribed distances of the spheres, then surely He also accommodated the distances themselves to some specific rule.

Since, therefore, this path did not succeed, I tried an approach by another way, one surprisingly bold. Between Jupiter and Mars, I interposed a new Planet, and likewise another between Venus and Mercury—two which perhaps we do not see because of their tiny size—and I assigned to them their own <term>periodic times</term> <note>original: "$\pi\epsilon\rho\iota o\delta\iota\kappa\dot{\alpha}$" (periodika). These are the times it takes for a planet to complete one full orbit.</note>. For in this way, I thought I would achieve some equality of proportions, which would be decreased in order among the pairs toward the Sun and increased toward the fixed stars. For example, the Earth is closer to Venus in the size of the terrestrial sphere than Mars is to the Earth in the size of the Martian sphere. However, in this scheme, even the interposition of one planet was not sufficient for the immense gap between Jupiter and Mars <note>In the symbols: ♃ and ♂</note>. For there remained a larger proportion from Jupiter to that new planet than there is from Saturn to Jupiter. Moreover, even if I obtained some sort of proportion by this method, there would be no rational end and no certain number of moving bodies; it would continue toward the fixed stars until they were met, and toward the Sun forever, because the division of the space remaining after Mercury would proceed toward infinity by this proportion. Nor could I guess from the "nobility" of any number why, out of an infinite possibility, so few moving bodies existed. Nor does <term>Rheticus</term> <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus, the first disciple of Copernicus and the man who published the first account of the heliocentric system.</note> speak plausibly in his *Narrative* when he argues from the "holiness of the number six" to the number of the six moving Heavens. For he who disputes concerning the construction of the world itself ought not to draw reasons from those numbers which acquired some dignity only from things created *after* the world.

Again, I explored another way: whether in the same quadrant the distance of any Planet might be the remainder of a <term>sine</term>, and its motion the remainder of the sine of its <term>complement</term>. Let there be imagined a Square AB, described from the Semidiameter of the entire Universe AC. ...passes through $CED$. Then, on the true radius of the world $AC$, let the Sun, the Fixed Stars, and the Moving Bodies <note>The planets</note> be marked according to the proportion of their distances: from these points, straight lines are to be raised, extending as far as the Quadrant facing the Sun. Therefore, whatever the proportion of the parallels was, I imagined the same for the <term>moving force</term> <note>original: "virtutis mouentis." Kepler's early concept of a physical force emanating from the Sun that pushes the planets.</note> assigned to each planet. In the line of the Sun, this force remains infinite, because $AD$ is touched but not cut by the Quadrant. Thus, there is an infinite power of motion in the Sun—namely, nothing but motion in its very act. At Mercury, the infinite line is cut off at $K$. For this reason, its motion is now comparable to the others. In the fixed stars, the line is entirely lost and compressed into a mere point $C$. Therefore, there is no power for motion there. This was the theorem that had to be examined by calculation. But if anyone weighs this properly, he will see that I lacked two things: first, that I was ignorant of the <term>total sine</term> <note>The "sinus totum" refers to the radius of the circle used in trigonometric calculations, which serves as the base unit.</note>—or rather, the magnitude of that proposed quadrant; and second, that the strengths of the motions were not expressed except in the proportion of one to another. He who, I say, weighs these things properly will not without merit doubt whether or not I could have reached the goal at all by this difficult path. And yet, through continuous labor and the infinite repetition of sines and arcs, I achieved enough to understand that this hypothesis could not hold.

Almost the entire summer was lost to this <term>cross</term> <note>original: "cruce." A metaphor for a torturous or agonizingly difficult problem.</note>. Finally, on a slight occasion, I happened upon the matter more closely. I judged it to have happened to me by divine providence, that I should obtain by chance what I could never reach by any labor. I believed this all the more because I had always prayed to God that if Copernicus had spoken the truth, these things might succeed. Thus, on the 9th or 19th day of July in the year 1595, when I was about to demonstrate to my students the "jumps" of the <term>great conjunctions</term> <note>The meeting of Jupiter and Saturn in the sky, which occurs roughly every 20 years and creates a pattern of triangles in the zodiac.</note> through eight signs, and how they gradually pass from one <term>trigon</term> <note>A group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same astrological element (fire, earth, air, or water).</note> to another, I inscribed many triangles, or quasi-triangles, in the same circle, so that the <margin>*as you see in the following diagram.*</margin> end of one was the beginning of the next. Therefore, at the points where the sides of the triangles intersected each other, a smaller circle was sketched. For the radius of a circle inscribed in a triangle is half the radius of the circumscribed circle. The proportion between the two circles appeared to the eye almost similar to that which exists between Saturn and Jupiter; and the triangle was the first of the figures, just as Saturn and Jupiter are the first <note>outermost</note> planets. I immediately tried a square for the second distance between Mars and Jupiter, a pentagon for the third, and a hexagon for the fourth. And since even the eyes cried out against the second distance—which is between Jupiter and Mars—I joined a square to the triangle and the pentagon. It would be endless to pursue each attempt.

And the end of this failed effort was the same as the beginning of the final and successful one. Namely, I thought that by this path—if indeed I wished to preserve the order among the figures—I would never reach the Sun, nor would I have a reason why there should be six moving spheres rather than twenty or a hundred. And yet the figures pleased me, being quantities, and things prior to the heavens. For quantity was created in the beginning with the body, while the heavens were created on the second day. But if (I thought), instead of the Quantity and proportion of the six Heavens established by Copernicus, only **Five figures** could be found among the infinite others which had certain peculiar properties beyond the rest, the matter would be according to my wish. And yet I pressed on again: what are plane figures doing among solid spheres? Solid bodies should rather be consulted. Behold, Reader, this discovery and the matter of this entire little work! For if someone even lightly skilled in Geometry were told this in so many words, the **Five regular bodies** <note>Also known as the Platonic Solids: the cube, tetrahedron, dodecahedron, icosahedron, and octahedron.</note> with the proportion of their circumscribed to their inscribed spheres would immediately come to his mind; that Euclidean <term>scholion</term> <note>An explanatory note or comment in a mathematical text.</note> to the 18th proposition of the 13th book would immediately stand before his eyes. Where...

<header>PREFACE TO THE READER.</header>
<meta>catchword: CHAPTER</meta>

bodies, so that I could change nothing in them afterward, even when I worked with the most exquisite reasoning. To preserve the memory of the event, I record for you the insight just as it occurred, and the words as they were conceived at that very moment. *The Earth is the sphere <note>original: "Circulus." While Kepler literally says "circle," in his model these represent the three-dimensional orbital spheres of the planets.</note> that is the measure of all: Circumscribe a dodecahedron <note>A 12-sided solid made of pentagons.</note> around it; the sphere encompassing this will be Mars. Around Mars, circumscribe a tetrahedron <note>A 4-sided pyramid made of triangles.</note>; the sphere encompassing this will be Jupiter. Around Jupiter, circumscribe a cube; the sphere encompassing this will be Saturn. Now, inscribe an icosahedron <note>A 20-sided solid made of triangles.</note> inside the Earth; the sphere inscribed within it will be Venus. Inscribe an octahedron <note>An 8-sided solid made of triangles.</note> inside Venus; the sphere inscribed within it will be Mercury.* You now have the reason for the number of the planets.

This was the occasion and the success of this labor. See now also my purpose in this book. Indeed, I could never express in words the pleasure I perceived from this discovery. I no longer regretted the time lost, nor was I weary of the labor; I fled from no difficulty of calculation, and I spent days and nights in computing, until I could see whether the insight I had put into words agreed with the orbits of Copernicus, or whether the winds would carry my joys away. If I found the matter to be as I supposed, I made a vow to God, the Best and Greatest, that at the first opportunity I would publish this admirable specimen of His wisdom for all people to see; so that although these things are not entirely perfect in every part, and perhaps some things remain which flow from these principles (the discovery of which I could reserve for myself), others who are strong in talent might nevertheless bring forth as much as possible for the illustration of the Divine Name at the earliest time alongside me, and might sing praise to the most wise Creator with one voice. When, therefore, a few days later the matter succeeded, and I perceived how aptly one body sat after another between its planets, and I reduced the whole business into the form of the present booklet, and it was approved by the famous mathematician Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631) was Kepler’s university professor and one of the first to teach the Copernican system.</note>: you understand, friend Reader, that I am bound by my vow, and cannot follow the custom of the Satirist <note>A reference to the Roman poet Horace, who in his *Ars Poetica* advised authors to keep a manuscript hidden for nine years before publishing.</note>, who commands that books be held back until the ninth year.

This is the one cause for my haste; and so that I may remove every scrap of sinister suspicion from you, I gladly add another, and recite to you that saying of Archytas <note>A Greek Pythagorean philosopher and friend of Plato.</note> from Cicero: *If I had ascended into heaven itself and had thoroughly perceived the nature of the world and the beauty of the stars, that wonder would be unpleasant to me unless I had you—a fair, attentive, and eager Reader—to whom I could tell it.* Once you have learned these things, if you are fair, you will abstain from the criticisms which I foresee not without cause. But if you leave those things in their proper place, yet you fear whether they are certain, and whether I have sung my triumph before the victory: then at last approach these pages themselves, and learn the matter we have been discussing. You will not find new and unknown planets interposed (as was done a little while ago—I do not approve of such audacity), but those ancient ones, only slightly displaced, yet so protected by the intervention of straight-lined bodies <term>regular solids</term>—however absurd this may seem—that hereafter,

<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

### *By what reasons the hypotheses of Copernicus are made consistent. And an explanation of the Copernican hypotheses.*

<sig>B 3</sig>

<image-desc>The page begins with an ornamental decorative initial "E" featuring swirling foliate patterns.</image-desc>

ALTHOUGH it is a pious act, right from the beginning of this discussion about Nature, to see whether anything contrary to Holy Scripture <note>original: "Sacris Literis"</note> is being said, I nevertheless think it untimely to raise that controversy here before being prompted to do so. I promise this in general: I shall say nothing that is harmful to Holy Scripture, and if Copernicus is proven guilty of such in my eyes, I shall consider it of no account. And this has always been my mind, from the time I first began to know the books of Copernicus’s *Revolutions* <note>Referring to *De revolutionibus orbium coelestium* (On the Revolutions of the Heavenly Spheres), published in 1543.</note>.

Since, therefore, I was hindered by no religious scruple in this regard from listening to Copernicus if he spoke things that were consistent, the first thing that won my confidence was that most beautiful agreement of everything that appears in the heavens with the teachings of Copernicus. He not only demonstrated past motions repeated from the furthest antiquity, but also predicted future ones beforehand—not indeed with absolute certainty, but yet far more certainly than <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose Earth-centered model was the standard for over a millennium.</note>, <term>Alphonso</term> <note>King Alfonso X of Castile, sponsor of the "Alfonsine Tables" used by medieval astronomers.</note>, and the others. But this is far greater: that of those things which we learn to marvel at from others, Copernicus alone most beautifully renders the reason, and removes the cause of wonder—which is the ignorance of causes. I could never teach this more easily to the reader than if I should become the advocate and persuader for him to read Rheticus's *Narratio* <note>original: "Narrationem Rhetici." This refers to the *Narratio Prima* (First Account), the first published summary of Copernican theory by his student Georg Joachim Rheticus.</note>. For not everyone has the leisure to read Copernicus's books of the *Revolutions* themselves.

And at this point, I could never agree with those who rely on the example of an "accidental demonstration," which, from false premises, infers something true by <term>Syllogistic</term> <note>A form of logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed premises.</note> necessity. Who, I say, relying on this example, contended that it could be that the hypotheses which please Copernicus are false, and yet from them the true <term>phenomena</term> <note>original: "φαινόμενα" (phainomena) - the observed positions and movements of the planets in the sky.</note> follow as if from genuine principles.

For the example does not fit. For that sequence from false premises is accidental, and such is the nature of falsehood that as soon as it is applied to another related matter, it betrays itself—unless you willingly grant to that arguer that he may assume infinite other false propositions, and neither

<meta>catchword: ever (vnquam)</meta>

<summary>Kepler opens his first chapter by defending Copernicus's heliocentric model, arguing it is not only mathematically superior to the Ptolemaic system but also reveals the physical causes of celestial events. He specifically counters the philosophical claim that a model can produce true predictions even if its physical premises (the Sun at the center) are false.</summary>

<keywords>Copernicus, Holy Scripture, Ptolemy, Alphonso, Rheticus, Narratio Prima, Syllogistic logic, phenomena, astronomical hypotheses</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument from Chapter 1. He is contrasting the consistency of the Copernican system with the inconsistencies he sees in the older Ptolemaic (Earth-centered) model. He specifically addresses why the old model could sometimes predict the right positions of planets even if its underlying theory of the world's structure was wrong.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY</header>

ever in forward or backward motion remains consistent with himself. The situation is different for the one who places the Sun in the center. For command him to demonstrate anything you please of those things that truly appear in the heavens, based on a hypothesis once established—to go backward, forward, to gather one thing from another, and to do whatever the truth of things allows: he will not hesitate in anything, if the theory be genuine, and even from the most intricate windings of demonstrations, he will return most consistently to the same point. 

But if you object that the same could partly still be said, and once could be said, of the ancient tables and hypotheses—namely, that they satisfy the phenomena <note>original: "φαινομένοις" (phainomenois) — things as they appear to the senses.</note>—and yet those are rejected by Copernicus as false; therefore, it could be answered in the same way to Copernicus: namely, that although he gives an excellent account of those things which appear, he nevertheless errs in his hypothesis. 

I respond, first, that the ancient hypotheses give absolutely no account of several primary points. Such as the fact that they are ignorant of the causes of the number, quantity, and time of the retrograde motions <note>The "backward" loops planets appear to make in the sky, which the Copernican system explains as an effect of the Earth overtaking other planets.</note>; and why these agree so precisely with the position and mean motion of the Sun. In all these matters, since a most beautiful order appears in <term>Copernicus</term> <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), the astronomer who proposed the Sun-centered model.</note>, it is necessary that a cause also be present. 

Second, regarding even those hypotheses which provide a consistent cause for appearances and agree with what is seen, Copernicus denies nothing; rather, he assumes and explains them all. For though he seems to have changed many things in the usual hypotheses, in reality it is not so. For it can happen that the same result occurs from two presuppositions that differ in "species," because those two fall under the same "genus," and it is for the sake of that genus that the matter in question primarily occurs. 

Thus <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>The 2nd-century astronomer whose model placed Earth at the center.</note> demonstrated the rising and setting of the stars, not by this immediate and equated "middle term"—because the Earth is immobile in the center. Nor does Copernicus demonstrate this same thing by this "middle term"—because the Earth, distant from the center, is revolved. For it was sufficient for both to say (as both indeed said) that these things happen because some separation of motions intervenes between the heaven and the Earth, and because no distance of the Earth from the center is perceived among the fixed stars. 

Therefore, Ptolemy did not demonstrate by a false and accidental means those appearances <note>original: "φαινόμενα" (phainomena)</note> he demonstrated. He only sinned against the law of "essentiality" <note>original: "κατ᾿ αὐτὸ" (kat’ auto). A logical term meaning "in itself" or "by its own nature." Kepler argues Ptolemy was right about the geometry but wrong about the physical cause.</note> in that he thought these things occurred because of the specific arrangement he imagined, when they actually occur because of a general principle of relative motion. From which it appears that Ptolemy, from a false arrangement of the world, nevertheless [demonstrated things that were] true and consistent with the Heaven and our eyes...

<meta>catchword: monſtra- (demonstrate)</meta>

<summary>Kepler defends the Copernican system by arguing it is more logically consistent than the Ptolemaic system. He explains that while Ptolemy's Earth-centered model could predict planetary positions, it did so by mistake, confusing specific cases for general laws of motion.</summary>
<keywords>Copernicus, Ptolemy, retrograde motion, phenomena, scientific method, celestial mechanics, logic, planetary motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument from page 12, where he was discussing the superior explanatory power of the Copernican system. He is now transitioning to his second reason for supporting Copernicus: the "physical" or "cosmographical" harmony of a Sun-centered universe.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>C H A P T E R  I.</header>

showed, and that because of it there is no reason to suspect anything similar regarding the Copernican hypotheses. Rather, what was stated at the beginning remains: that Copernicus’s principles cannot be false, since they provide such a consistent account of many **phenomena** <note>original: "φαινομένων" (phainomenōn) — things as they appear to the senses, specifically the observed motions of the stars and planets.</note> which were unknown to the ancients, insofar as the account is rendered from them. <margin>In Tycho Brahe <unclear>which [spheres] to the orb...</unclear></margin> That most successful Tycho Brahe saw this—an astronomer greater than any praise—who, although he completely disagreed with Copernicus regarding the location of the Earth, nevertheless retained from him the very thing that gives us the causes of things previously unknown: namely, that the Sun is the Center of the five planets. For even this narrower premise is enough to demonstrate the **retrogressions** <note>The apparent "backward" loops planets make in the sky, which are naturally explained if the Earth is moving.</note>: "The Sun, immobile in the center." Indeed, that general point is sufficient: "The Sun is in the center of the five Planets." Why Copernicus took the specific instance for the general type, and moreover placed the Sun in the center of the world while making the Earth mobile around it, was for other reasons.

For to move from Astronomy to Physics, or Cosmography, these Copernican hypotheses not only do not offend against the Nature of things, but they assist her much more. She loves simplicity; she loves unity. Nothing idle or superfluous has ever existed within her; instead, she often destines one thing for many effects. Yet, within the common hypotheses for **inventing spheres** <note>A reference to the "epicycles" and "deferents" used in the older Ptolemaic system—imaginary circles within circles—to explain planetary motion.</note>, there is no end; within Copernicus, a great many motions follow from very few spheres. I will remain silent for now about the **overlapping of the spheres** <note>In the older models, the physical spheres of Venus and Mercury were thought to intersect or occupy the same space, which was a physical impossibility in the science of the time.</note> of Venus and Mercury, and other problems with which ancient Astronomy still struggles, given its excessive freedom in inventing spheres.

And so, this Man [Copernicus] not only freed Nature from that burdensome and useless equipment of so many immense spheres, but he also opened to us an inexhaustible treasure of most divine reasonings concerning the most beautiful fitness of the whole World and of all bodies. Nor do I hesitate to affirm that whatever Copernicus gathered **after the fact** <note>original: "à posteriori" — reasoning from observed data back to the underlying cause.</note> and demonstrated to the eye by means of geometric axioms, all of it—as Aristotle himself could testify if he were alive (which Rheticus frequently wishes)—could be demonstrated **from the start** <note>original: "à priori" — reasoning from first principles or the nature of things to the observed effects.</note> without any confusion. But Rheticus’s *Narratio* <note>George Joachim Rheticus was Copernicus's only pupil; his "Narratio Prima" (First Account), published in 1540, was the first public description of the Copernican system.</note> and Copernicus himself have already dealt with all these things more extensively and with the dignity they deserve; if anything can be explained more fully, it will be for another time and place. For now, it is enough to have touched on them, so that this mention makes clear to the reader the second reason that drew me to the Copernican side.

<meta>catchword: Neither</meta>

<summary>Kepler argues that the Copernican system is physically superior because it explains the motions of the planets with far fewer "spheres" than the ancient models. He also credits Tycho Brahe for adopting the Sun as the center of the five planets, even while Tycho maintained that the Earth remains stationary.</summary>
<keywords>Copernicus, Tycho Brahe, simplicity of nature, planetary spheres, retrogressions, Rheticus, a priori reasoning, cosmography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument from page 13. Having established the general physical and cosmographical advantages of the Copernican system, he now introduces the specific influence of his teacher, Michael Maestlin, and begins a technical explanation of the Earth's motions as the basis for the rest of his work.</meta>

# THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

Nor did I embrace this school of thought <note>original: "sectam" — here referring to the Copernican "party" or astronomical system.</note> rashly, or without the very weighty authority of my teacher, Michael Maestlin, the most renowned Mathematician. For although he was my first leader and guide, both to other studies and especially to these philosophical teachings, and therefore by right ought to have been mentioned in the first place, he nevertheless provided me with a third reason for believing this way for a certain specific reason: namely, that he discovered that the Comet of [15]77 <note>The Great Comet of 1577 was a major celestial event that Maestlin and Tycho Brahe used to prove that comets were not atmospheric phenomena but traveled through the planetary spheres.</note> moved most consistently according to the motion of Venus as put forward by Copernicus; and having deduced its position from its super-lunar <note>Above the moon; meaning the comet was in the heavens, not the Earth's atmosphere.</note> height, he found it completed its course within the Copernican sphere of Venus itself. Now, if anyone considers how easily a falsehood disagrees with itself, and on the contrary, how constantly truth harmonizes with truth: they will not wrongly take the greatest argument for the Copernican arrangement of the spheres even from this fact alone.

*However, so that you may find all these things that I have said about both hypotheses to be most true: receive this brief explanation of the Copernican hypotheses, and the two tables pertaining to this.*

For understanding the order of the Spheres of the World <term>Sphæra Mundi: The concentric "shells" or paths in which planets were thought to move.</term> according to the opinion of Copernicus, look at the first Table at the end of this chapter, and the things written upon it. Four motions are attributed to the Earth by Copernicus in different respects (Copernicus, intent on brevity, says three, which in reality are four <note>Copernicus listed rotation, revolution, and the "third motion" of the axis. Kepler likely counts the motion of the aphelion or the change in eccentricity as a fourth distinct physical motion.</note>), all of which provide some apparent variety to the motions of the remaining Planets.

<margin><unclear>Concerning</unclear> the motion of the Earth</margin>
The first motion is of the Sphere or Orbit itself, which carries the Earth around the Sun annually like a star. And that orbit, since it is eccentric <term>eccentric: an orbit where the center is not exactly on the Sun.</term>, with an additional changing eccentricity, must be considered by us in three ways. First, removing the eccentricity; this Orbit, and the motion of the Earth, provides these advantages: that we do not need the three eccentrics used in the common hypotheses <note>The "common hypotheses" refer to the Ptolemaic/Geocentric system.</note>, namely, those of the Sun, Venus, and Mercury. For because the Earth is carried around these three planets, we Earth-dwellers believe those three are carried around us while we remain immobile. Thus, from one motion, they make three. And if there were more stars inside the orbit of the earth, they would ascribe this motion to even more of them. By establishing this orbit, the three large epicycles <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one; used in old astronomy to explain planetary loops.</term> of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, along with their motions, also fall away. How that happens, in the joined paral-

### CHAPTER I.

...can be seen in the parallel diagrams. For again, because the Earth travels in its own orbit within the sight of Saturn (which is as if stationary, because it is slower), moving away from and toward Saturn: the inhabitants <note>the observers on Earth</note> think that Saturn moves in its own epicycle <term>Epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one; used in old astronomy to explain why planets seem to speed up or slow down.</term>, approaching and receding, while they believe themselves to remain at rest in the center of their own orbit. Therefore, they think the circle **A B** is the epicycles **g, i, l**.

Likewise, because of this same approach and recession of the Earth toward the Planets in its own orbit, the latitudes <note>The vertical distance of a planet above or below the ecliptic plane.</note> of the five planets themselves seem to us to take on some variety; to save this oscillation <note>original: "librationem"</note>, it was necessary for Ptolemy to establish five other motions: all of which fall away once the single motion of the Earth is assumed.

And although all these motions—eleven in number—are banished from the world by substituting this single motion of the Earth: nevertheless, the causes of many other things are still explained, which Ptolemy could not explain from so many motions.

For first, it could be asked of Ptolemy: how does it happen that the three eccentrics <term>Eccentrics: orbital paths where the center is shifted away from the Earth, used to explain variations in planetary speed.</term> of the Sun, Venus, and Mercury have equal revolutions? The answer is that they do not truly revolve themselves, but the single Earth revolves instead of them.

2. Why do the five Planets become retrograde <term>Retrograde: the apparent "backward" motion of a planet in the night sky relative to the stars.</term>, but the Luminaries <note>The Sun and the Moon.</note> do not?

The answer regarding the Sun is that it remains at rest: hence it happens that the motion of the Earth, which is always forward, seems to belong to the Sun itself, purely and undisturbed, only through the opposite part of the heavens.

Regarding the Moon, however, it is because the annual motion of the Earth is truly shared by its sphere along with the Earth. Now, two things that have the same motion in every respect seem to be at rest relative to each other. Hence the Earth's motion is not perceived in the Moon, as it is in the other planets.

Regarding the "superior" planets—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars—the answer is: Because they are slower than the Earth, and because that circle and motion of the Earth is thought to belong to them. Therefore, just as for those who might look out from the globe of Saturn <note>Kepler frequently uses "observers on other planets" to help readers visualize relative motion.</note>, the Earth would sometimes seem to move forward while it went through the half-circle **P B N** above the Sun, and sometimes to move backward while it went through **N A P**, and to stand still at **N** and **P**: so it is necessary that to us looking out from the Earth, Saturn seems to be rolled in opposite directions. For instance, while the Earth is in **B N A**, Saturn is seen in *b n a* of the other table.

The "inferior" planets, Venus and Mercury, seem to move backward for this reason: they are faster than the Earth. Hence, just as if the Earth stood motionless, Venus, running in the more distant part of its circle, describes a path plainly contrary to the one it completes in the part of its circle near the Earth.

# THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

3. Thus, one might ask (though Ptolemy offers no answer) why there are such tiny epicycles in the large orbits, and such massive ones in the small orbits: that is, why the **correction angle** <note>original: $\pi\rho o\sigma\theta\alpha\phi\alpha\iota\rho\epsilon\sigma\iota\varsigma$ (prosthaphaeresis), the angular difference between a planet's mean and true position</note> of Mars is larger than that of Jupiter, and Jupiter's larger than that of Saturn? And why does Mercury not have a larger one than Venus, since it is lower <note>closer to the Sun</note> than Venus; especially since among the other four, the lower planet always has a larger one? Here the answer is easy. For the ancients thought the true orbits of Mercury and Venus were epicycles. Yet Mercury, being the swiftest, also has the smallest orbit. Among the superior planets <term>Superior planets: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, which orbit further from the Sun than Earth does.</term>, however, as each one is closer to the orbit of the Earth, it holds a greater proportion to it, and so appears larger. Therefore Mars, being the closest, has the greatest **equation** <note>A mathematical correction used to account for the difference between a planet's apparent and mean motion.</note>, and Saturn, being the highest, has the smallest. For if the eye were placed at **G**, the orbit **P N** would appear to it under the angle **T A V**. But if the eye were at **L**, that same orbit would be seen under the angle **R L S**.

4. Likewise, the ancients wondered—not without reason—why the three superior planets are always at their lowest point in their epicycle when in opposition with the Sun, and at their highest point when in conjunction. For example, if the Earth, the Sun, and **G** were in the same line, why could Mars not be in any other place in its epicycle except at **$\gamma$**? In the system of Copernicus, the cause is easily rendered. For it is not Mars in an epicycle, but the Earth in its own orbit that causes this variation. Hence, if the Earth moves from **A** to **B**, the Sun will be between **G** (Mars) and **B** (the Earth). And then Mars will seem to have ascended in its epicycle from **d** to **$\gamma$**. But when the Earth is at **A**, which is the point closest to **G**, then **G** (Mars) and the Sun will appear opposite to each other from the perspective of **A**. And these are the things which can be demonstrated to the eye from the diagram.

Now, let us next consider the **eccentricity** <term>Eccentricity: a measure of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</term> of this orbit. Copernicus makes the **Apogee** <term>Apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from the center of motion.</term> of the Sun (or the Earth), as well as those of the others, move—not through the carrying-spheres, but through a small epicycle returning to its start slightly more slowly than its own orbit. This motion of the Apogee also introduces something into the motions of the other planets. For Ptolemy calculates the eccentricities of the others from the center of the Earth; but if the center of the Earth’s eccentric and the Apogee have moved into another part of the Zodiac according to the sequence of the signs, leaving behind the slower apogees of the others, some change in the eccentricities of the other planets will occur. Ptolemaic astronomy will again wonder greatly at this, and will flee to the fabrication of new orbs by which it might demonstrate that these things are so

He argues that the Copernican model removes the need for the "extra" mathematical constructions required by Ptolemy.</summary>

### CHAPTER I.

...could happen, even though they follow from the single motion of the Earth. And this indeed will only happen after many centuries, but thirdly, the change in the Earth's eccentricity—by which the center of the eccentric orbit approaches the Sun and recedes from it—has introduced something significant in *Mars* and *Venus* from the time of *Ptolemy* <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer who championed the Earth-centered model.</note> down to our own. Since their eccentricities <note>The measure of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</note> seem to have changed, what do you think *Ptolemy* would say? Would he, if he were alive, again adopt new circles into that infinite crowd of others? None of these are needed at all in the system of *Copernicus*. *Copernicus* achieved these so many and great things through the position and motion of a single Circle **AB**; hence, although it was small, he rightly gave it the name **GREAT**. <note>The *Orbis Magnus*, or the Earth's orbit around the Sun.</note> This first motion of the heavens was common to the *Moon* and the *Earth*.

<margin>2nd motion of the Earth</margin>
Now let us see further what the remaining motions of the Earth achieve, which happen within that little sphere of the *Moon* at **A**.

The second motion, therefore, is not of the whole orbit, but of a celestial little sphere, closely enclosing the globe of the Earth like a kernel. It tends in the opposite direction from east to west, just like the epicycles <note>Small circles whose centers move around the circumference of larger circles.</note> of the superior planets, by which their eccentricity is preserved by *Copernicus*. By its annual arrangement, the equinoctial <note>The celestial equator.</note> always tilts toward the same part of the universe. For the poles of the Equinoctial (or of the body itself) are distant from the poles of this orbit by 23 and a half degrees. Since this motion is a little faster than the annual motion of the Great Orbit, it causes the intersections of the circles—that is, the locations of the equinoxes—to move gradually forward. <note>This explains the "precession of the equinoxes," where the positions of the stars seem to shift slowly over centuries.</note> Therefore, through this tiny sphere, that monstrous, huge, <term>starless <note>original Greek: ἄναστρος (anastros)</note></term> new Sphere of the *Alphonsines* <note>The Alfonsine Tables were medieval astronomical tables; they added extra spheres to the sky to account for complex motions.</note> falls away, as its function has been transferred to that little sphere where it was previously necessary. There also falls away the motion of the deferents of the Apogee <note>The point in an orbit furthest from the center.</note> of *Venus*, which does not move otherwise unless the fixed stars are stated to move.

The third motion is of the poles of the terrestrial globe, consisting of two oscillations <note>original: librationibus (librations), a slow "wobbling" or rocking motion.</note>, one of which is twice as fast as the other and at right angles to it. This is managed through four circles, such that two circles make each oscillation, and the oscillations themselves, mixed together, present the appearance of a twisted wreath, in this manner:

<image-desc>A line drawing of two vertical, overlapping ellipses intersected horizontally by a straight line, representing the "corollæ intortæ" (twisted wreath) pattern described in the text for planetary libration.</image-desc>

One oscillation occurs in the Colure <note>A great circle on the celestial sphere passing through the poles.</note> of the solstices and accounts for the variation in the declination of the Zodiac, noticed long after *Ptolemy's* time: *Ptolemy* would have had to devise something similar, and some moderns, having already fashioned an eleventh sphere of the world, [attempt] to provide...

...have attempted. Another libration <note>A slow, oscillating or "rocking" motion.</note>, which occurs in the colure of the Equinoxes <note>original: "coluro Aequinoctiorum"; the celestial circle passing through the poles and the equinoctial points.</note>, accounts for the unequal precession of the Equinoxes. It also eliminates the "trepidation" <note>An older astronomical theory that the stars moved back and forth like a pendulum.</note> of the eighth sphere of fixed stars—which is the outermost sphere in the system of **Copernicus**—and restores it to its rightful state of rest. And lest this motion fail to pay some interest to the other movements: it removes the irregularity of motion that the movements of all seven Planets, as well as the movements of their apogees <note>The point in an orbit furthest from the center.</note>, would otherwise have had (though not without the aid of several new circles), because it has been discovered that all motions advance uniformly through the fixed stars.

<margin>4th motion</margin>
Finally, the fourth motion is proper to the terrestrial globe itself and the air surrounding it. Its period is 24 hours, moving in the same direction as the others, namely from west to east. Because of this, the entire rest of the universe is thought to be carried from east to west—a great miracle of undisturbed secondary motions. Thus, that incredibly high and swift tenth sphere, the **starless** <note>original: "ἄναστρος" (anastros)</note> sphere, falls away. In the system of **Ptolemy** <note>The 2nd-century astronomer who placed Earth at the center.</note>, the speed of this sphere and the whole universe would be so great that they would cross several thousand miles in the blink of an eye. And I ask you, look at the diagram and consider that this Earth of ours, whose motion is being debated, barely equals a seventieth part of the diameter of the small lunar circle at <term>A</term>. From this little circle, then, turn your eyes to the vastness of **Saturn**, and from there to the immeasurable height of the fixed stars; and finally, conclude whether it is easier to do and to believe: that that tiny point within circle <term>A</term>—and thus the Earth—rotates in one direction, or that the whole universe moves in the other direction with ten distinct motions (since there are ten separate spheres) with unspeakable speed, and that it respects nothing except that tiny point, that little image of the Earth, keeping it alone immobile because there is nothing outside it.

This is followed by the orbit of <term>MERCURY</term>, which is seen
among the smallest circles around the Sun; its revolution is completed
in approximately 88 days.
This is followed by the orbit of <term>VENUS</term> (marked CD), *whose* revolution around the same Sun
is 224 and two-thirds days.
That which follows this (marked AB) is the orbit of the <term>EARTH</term> <note>original: "TELLVRIS"</note>, whose revolution is 365
and a quarter days. It is called the <term>GREAT ORBIT</term> <note>original: "ORBIS MAGNVS"</note>, because of its multiple
uses in calculation.
Around the <term>Earth</term> is a small circle like an <term>epicycle</term> <note>a smaller orbit whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</note>, the <term>LUNAR SPHERE</term>, at point A. It returns to the same fixed star by its motion through the space of a year along with the Earth around the Sun. But its own specific revolution relative to the Sun takes 29 and a half days.
After this is the Orbit of <term>MARS</term> (marked GH), which has one fixed use relative to the stars,
or rather to the Sun; it completes its circuit in 687 days.

After a great interval, the <term>Sphere of JUPITER</term> (marked IK) follows this, having
a circuit of 4332 and one-fifth days.
The *first* and *greatest* orbit, L M V, belongs to <term>SATURN</term>; its periodic time
is 10759 and one-fifth days.
The <term>FIXED STARS</term>, however, are removed by such an inestimable interval
that the distance between the Sun and the Earth is not perceptible in comparison to them.
And these are at the furthest edge, just as the Sun is in the center, entirely immobile.

the Great Orbit of the Earth has in relation to the Sphere of Mars.

or the arc R L S or R S, is the parallax in relation to the Sphere of Saturn.

Z A, is the parallax of the Sphere of Mercury, in relation to the Great Orbit.

## CHAPTER II. 19

The body was that which God created in the beginning; if we possess the definition of "body," I believe it will be reasonably clear why God created no other thing in the beginning but the body. I say that Quantity <note>In this context, quantity refers to geometric extension—the physical dimensions of space.</note> was the pattern set before God: to achieve this, all things which pertain to the essence of a body were required. Thus, the quantity of a body, insofar as it is a body, becomes a certain form and the very origin of its definition. Furthermore, God willed that quantity should exist before all other things so that there might be a comparison between the Curved and the Straight. In this one matter, the divine Cusanus <note>Nicholas of Cusa (1401–1464), a philosopher and mathematician who argued that the human mind’s relationship to truth is like a polygon’s relationship to a circle—always approaching but never quite reaching it.</note> and others seem to me to have shown great insight: they valued the relationship of the Straight and the Curved to one another so highly that they dared to compare the Curved to God and the Straight to created things. Indeed, those who have tried to compare the Creator to his creatures, or God to man, or divine judgments to human ones, have performed a task no more useful than those who attempt to equate the curved to the straight or a circle to a square.

And although the fitness of quantities and the nobility of the curved resided sufficiently in God alone, yet another much greater reason was added: the image of the Triune God in the spherical surface. This consists of the Father in the center, the Son in the surface, and the Spirit in the equality of the relation <note>original: σχέσεως (*scheseos*); a Greek term for the "disposition" or "connection" between things.</note> between the central point and the surrounding boundary. For those qualities which Cusanus attributed to the circle, and others perhaps to the globe, I claim for the spherical surface alone. Nor can I be persuaded that anything among curved things is nobler or more perfect than the spherical surface itself. For a globe is more than a spherical surface, being mixed with "straightness" because of the volume that fills it within. A circle, however, cannot exist unless it lies upon a flat, straight plane—that is, unless a spherical surface or a globe is cut by a straight plane, there will be no circle. From this it can be seen that many properties flow down secondarily from the cube to the globe, or here from the square to the circle, because of the straightness of the diameter.

But why, finally, were the distinctions between the Curved and the Straight, and the nobility of the Curved, set before God in the adorning of the world? Why indeed? Unless because it was entirely necessary for the most perfect Creator to establish a most beautiful work. *For it is not right, nor was it ever right (as Cicero speaks from Plato's Timaeus <note>The Timaeus is Plato's famous dialogue on the creation of the physical world by a "Demiurge" or Craftsman.</note> in the book On the Universe) for him who was best to make anything except the most beautiful.* Since, therefore, the Creator pre-conceived the Idea of the world in his mind (we speak in a human manner, so that we men may understand), and since an Idea is of a prior thing—specifically of the best possible thing—so that the form of the future work might also be the best: it is clear that by these laws which God himself prescribes to himself out of his own goodness,
