    *   Maintain paragraphing.<meta>This page continues Kepler’s theological justification for the structure of the universe. Having previously established that God created "body" or physical matter first, he now argues that the fundamental geometric laws—specifically the distinction between the straight and the curved—are physical manifestations of the Divine Essence and the Trinity.</meta>

### THE MYSTERY OF COSMOGRAPHY

...He could not have taken up the Idea of any thing for establishing the world other than the Idea of His own essence. This essence can be considered in two ways, according to how excellent and divine it is: first in itself, insofar as it is one in essence and triple in persons; and second, through a comparison made with created things.

He willed to imprint this image, this Idea, upon the world, so that the world might become the best and most beautiful. And so that the world might be able to receive this image, the most wise Founder <note>original: "Conditor"</note> devised Greatness <note>In this context, Kepler refers to "Quantum," the philosophical concept of physical extension or quantity.</note> and quantities. The whole essence of these quantities, so to speak, would fall into two categories: the **Straight** and the **Curved**. Of these, the Curved would represent God to us in those two ways just mentioned <note>Referring to the center, surface, and intervening space of a sphere as a symbol for the Trinity.</note>. For it must not be thought that such suitable distinctions for prefiguring God existed by chance, as if God did not think of them and instead created the physical body for other reasons and by some other plan, and that only afterwards the comparison of the Straight and the Curved, and their similarity with God, arose of its own accord, as if by accident.

Rather, it is more likely that from the beginning of all things, the **Curved** and the **Straight** were chosen by God by a certain design to foreshadow the divinity of the Creator in the world. To make these exist, there had to be quantities; and so that quantity might be had, the Body was created first of all.

Let us see now how the best Creator employed these quantities in the structure of the world, and what our reasoning suggests was done by the Founder. We shall then look for this design both in the Ancient and in the new hypotheses <note>Kepler is referring to the competing astronomical models of his day: the "Ancient" geocentric model of Ptolemy and the "new" heliocentric model of Copernicus.</note>, and we shall grant the prize to the one in whose work this design is found.

Aristotle has already argued abundantly enough that the whole world is enclosed in a spherical shape, drawing his arguments, among other things, from the nobility of the spherical surface. Even the outermost sphere of the fixed stars in the system of Copernicus <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), who proposed that the Earth revolves around the Sun.</note>, although it lacks motion, maintains this same shape and receives the Sun as the center into its innermost bosom. Furthermore, the circular motion of the stars proves that the other orbs are round. Therefore, that the **Curved** was used for the adornment of the world needs no further proof. However, since we see three kinds of quantities in the world—the shape, the number, and the magnitude <note>original: "amplitudinem"</note> of bodies—we have so far found the Curved only in the shape. For there is no explanation for magnitude derived from the fact that a thing is inscribed in something similar (a sphere within a sphere, a circle...

<summary>Kepler argues that the distinction between "the Straight" and "the Curved" was a deliberate divine choice to mirror the Trinity in the physical world. He asserts that the spherical nature of the universe is a necessary consequence of this divine geometry.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, geometry, the Straight and the Curved, Aristotle, Copernicus, spheres, cosmological design, quantity</keywords>

### CHAPTER II.

circle) from the same center, it either touches everywhere or nowhere; and the spherical itself, since it is the only and unique thing in its class of quantity, can be the subject of no other number than three <note>original: *ternarius*. Kepler relates the three parts of a sphere—center, surface, and the intervening space—to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</note>. Therefore, if God had looked only to the Curved in the creation, then besides the Sun in the center (representing the Father), the sphere of fixed stars or the "Mosaic waters" <note>original: *aquas Mosaicas*. A reference to the "waters above the firmament" mentioned in Genesis 1:7, which Kepler identifies here with the outer boundary of the universe.</note> at the boundary (representing the Son), and the celestial breeze filling everything—that is, the extension and firmament which would be the image of the Spirit—besides these, I say, nothing else would exist in this worldly edifice. 

But now, since there are also innumerable fixed stars, and a very certain catalog of moving bodies <note>The planets</note>, and the sizes of the heavens are unequal to one another, it is necessary that we seek the causes of all these things from "straightness" <note>Kepler moves from the sphere (the "curved") to polyhedra (the "straight") to explain the specific number and spacing of the planets.</note>. Unless, perhaps, we think that God did anything in the world at random while the best reasons were available; no one will persuade me of this, even regarding the fixed stars, whose position seems to us most confused of all, like a fortuitous scattering of seeds.

Let us come, therefore, to the **Straight quantities**. Just as the spherical surface was previously assumed because it was the most perfect quantity, so let us now pass in one leap to the *bodies* <note>three-dimensional solids</note>, as those which are perfect quantities made from straight lines and consist of three dimensions; for it is fitting that the <term>Idea of the world|The Platonic concept of a divine blueprint or archetype used by God during creation.</term> be perfect. But let us cast out straight lines and surfaces from the finite, most orderly, and most beautiful world, as they are infinite and therefore least capable of order. 

Again, from the bodies—of which there are infinitely infinite kinds—let us select some by taking a census through certain marks; for instance, those which have sides, angles, or planes that are equal to one another, either individually, alternately, or mixed in any constant way, so that by good reason we may arrive at something finite. If a certain class of bodies described by fixed conditions consists of species finite in number, yet is multiplied into a huge abundance of numbers, let us use the angles and centers of the planes of those bodies to demonstrate the multitude, size, and position of the fixed stars, if we can. But if this labor is not for man, then let us defer seeking the reason for their number and position for as long as it takes for someone to describe to us every single one, how many and how great they are. 

Therefore, setting aside the fixed stars and leaving them to Him "who alone counts the multitude of the stars and calls each by its name" (Psalm 147), let us turn our eyes toward the few nearby and moving stars <note>The planets</note> of the most wise <term>Artificer|God as the divine craftsman or architect of the universe.</term>.

<summary>Kepler argues that while the "curved" sphere represents the Trinity, the "straight" geometry of polyhedra explains the diversity of the planets. He concludes that since the fixed stars are too numerous for human understanding, he will focus on the few moving planets to find the universe's mathematical blueprint.</summary>

# THE MYSTERY OF THE COSMOS
<header>OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY</header>

<margin>5 regular bodies</margin>
Finally, if we make a selection of bodies and cast out the whole messy crowd of mixed shapes, and instead keep only those whose faces are all flat, equilateral, and equiangular, we are left with these **five Regular Bodies** <note>These are now commonly known as the Platonic Solids.</note>, to which the Greeks gave these names: the **Cube** or Hexahedron, the **Pyramid** or Tetrahedron, the **Dodecahedron**, the **Icosahedron**, and the **Octahedron**. Regarding the fact that there cannot be more than these five, see Euclid, Book 13, in the scholion after Proposition 18.
<margin>*</margin>

Therefore, just as the number of these bodies is fixed and very small, while the varieties of other shapes are either innumerable or infinite, so it was fitting that there be two kinds of stars in the world, distinguished from each other by an evident difference (of the sort found between motion and rest). One kind is like the infinite, such as the **fixed stars**; the other is limited in number, like the **Planets**. This is not the place to argue the causes of why the latter move and the former do not. But, granted that the Planets required motion, it follows that to achieve this, they must have received round orbits.

We have the orbit because of motion, and the [solid] bodies because of number and magnitude. What remains then, but to say with Plato that **God always geometrizes** <note>original: θεὸν ἀεὶ γεωμετρεῖν (*theon aei geometrein*). This famous phrase is attributed to Plato, though it does not appear in his written dialogues.</note>, and that in this fabric of moving things, He inscribed the bodies within the orbits, and the orbits within the bodies, until no body remained that was not clothed inside and out with moving orbits. For one can see from Propositions 13, 14, 15, 16, and 17 of the 13th book of Euclid how suited these bodies are by their nature to this inscription and circumscription <note>In geometry, to "inscribe" is to draw a figure inside another so they touch; "circumscribe" is to draw it around the outside.</note>. Thus, if five bodies are inserted into one another, with orbits serving as the intermediaries and enclosures, we arrive at the number of **six orbits**.

<margin>Proposition.</margin>
If any age of the world has reasoned about the arrangement of the universe in this way—setting six moving orbits around a motionless Sun—it has surely passed down the true Astronomy. *Now, Copernicus has exactly such six orbits, and each pair is in such proportion to the other that these five bodies can all be most perfectly placed between them; this shall be the sum of everything that follows.* Therefore, Copernicus ought to be listened to, until someone either produces hypotheses more suited to these philosophical principles, or shows that it could happen by chance that something deduced with the best reason from the very principles of nature could creep into numbers and into the mind of man. For what could be said or imagined that is more wonderful, or more suited to persuade?

<summary>Kepler identifies the five Platonic solids as the archetypal "straight" forms that explain why there are exactly six planets (the orbits between the five solids) and argues that the Copernican system's dimensions perfectly match this geometric model.</summary>
<keywords>Regular Bodies, Platonic Solids, Hexahedron, Tetrahedron, Dodecahedron, Icosahedron, Octahedron, Euclid, Plato, Copernicus, six orbits, geometry of the universe</keywords>

# CHAPTER II.

...[can be] than that those things which Copernicus established from the phenomena <note>original: *φαινόμενοις* (*phainomenois*); the observable appearances of the stars and planets.</note>, from effects, and from consequences—like a blind man steadying his step with a staff (as Rheticus <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus (1514–1574), the mathematician who first published an account of Copernicus's heliocentric theory.</note> himself used to say)—by a lucky rather than a confident conjecture, and believed to be so; those things, I say, are found to be most correctly established by reasons *a priori* <note>Reasoning from theoretical first principles rather than from observation.</note>, from causes, and derived from the idea of Creation.

<margin>The teachings of Pythagoras.</margin> For if anyone wishes to receive and mock these philosophical reasons without reasons of their own and with mere laughter—simply because I, a new man at the end of the ages, bring forth these philosophical matters while those ancient lights of philosophy remain silent—I shall bring forth **Pythagoras** from the most ancient age as a leader, author, and guide. He is much mentioned in the schools because, seeing the excellence of the **five Solids**, he judged it not unworthy of the Creator's care to look toward them two thousand years ago for the same reason I do now. Having assessed mathematical things physically and according to their individual accidental properties, he accommodated non-mathematical things to them. For he equated the **Earth** to the **Cube** because both are stable (though this is not strictly said of the cube). He gave the **Icosahedron** to the **Heaven** because both are mobile; the **Pyramid** to **Fire**, because this is the form of a flying spark; he distributed the remaining two bodies [the **Dodecahedron** and **Octahedron**] between air and water because of a similar kinship on both sides with their neighbors. But indeed, Copernicus was missing for that man [Pythagoras], who could first say what existed in the world; without him [Copernicus], there is no doubt he [Pythagoras] would have found *why* it was so. This proportion of the heavens would now be as well known as the five solids themselves; it would be as well accepted as that opinion concerning the **motion of the Sun** and the **rest of the Earth** <note>Kepler refers here to the traditional Geocentric (Ptolemaic) view which was the standard "opinion" for centuries.</note> has become strong in the course of time.

But come, let us finally test whether those proportions of the solids exist between the spheres of Copernicus. And at the beginning, let us judge the matter roughly. The greatest difference of distances in Copernicus is between **Jupiter** and **Mars**: as you see in the explanation of the hypotheses in Table 1, and below in chapters 14 and 15. For the distance of Mars from the Sun does not equal a third part of Jupiter's. Let us therefore seek the solid that makes the greatest difference between the circumscribed and inscribed sphere—allow us this catachresis <note>original: *κατάχρησις* (*katachrēsis*); a figure of speech in which a word is used in an unusual way, here referring to treating a hollow sphere as a solid volume.</note> of judging the hollow as a solid—which is the **Tetrahedron** or **Pyramid**. Therefore, there is a Pyramid between Jupiter and Mars. After these, Jupiter and

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s geometric proof of the Copernican system. Having introduced Pythagoras on the previous page, Kepler now applies his theory of the five Platonic solids to the specific distances between the known planets, calculating which solid fits into the gap between each planetary orbit.</meta>

**THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY**

**Saturn**. For it equals a little more than half of that one <note>Kepler is comparing the ratio of the orbits of Saturn and Jupiter to the ratio of the inner and outer spheres of a cube.</note>. A similar difference appears in the innermost and outermost sphere of the **cube**. Therefore, **Saturn** surrounds the cube, and the cube surrounds **Jupiter**.

An almost equal proportion exists between **Venus** and **Mercury**, not unlike the spheres of the **Octahedron** <note>A regular solid with eight triangular faces.</note>. Therefore, **Venus** surrounds this body, and **Mercury** is clothed by it.

The remaining two proportions—between **Venus** and the **Earth**, and between the **Earth** and **Mars**—are the smallest and nearly equal, namely the inner is three-quarters <note>original: *dodrans*</note> or two-thirds <note>original: *bes*</note> of the outer. In the **Icosahedron** <note>A regular solid with twenty triangular faces.</note> and the **Dodecahedron** <note>A regular solid with twelve pentagonal faces.</note>, the distances between the two spheres are also equal; and they employ the smallest proportion among the remaining regular solids. Therefore, it is likely that **Mars** surrounds the **Earth** with one of these bodies between them, and the **Earth** is separated from **Venus** by the remaining one. Thus, if anyone asks me why there are only six moving spheres <note>In Kepler’s time, only Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn were known; his theory of the five Platonic solids "explained" why a seventh planet could not exist.</note>, I will answer that there ought not to be more than five proportions, specifically as many as there are regular solids in mathematics <note>original: *mathesi*</note>. Six terms <note>The six planets.</note> complete this number of proportions.

### Annotation on Chapter Two.

<margin>Preceding page.</margin>
(And because of these five) The nobility of these solids comes from their simplicity and from the equality of the distance of their planes from the center of the figure. For just as God is the norm and rule for creatures, so the **Sphere** is for solids. Indeed, the sphere has these stated properties: 1. It is the simplest, because it is enclosed by a single boundary—namely, itself. 2. All its points are most equally distant from the center. Therefore, among solids, the **regular solids** <note>The five Platonic solids: Cube, Tetrahedron, Octahedron, Dodecahedron, and Icosahedron.</note> approach most closely to the perfection of the Sphere. Their definition is this: that they have 1. all sides, 2. all planes, and 3. all angles equal to each other in both kind and magnitude, which is a mark of simplicity. From this established definition, it follows naturally that: 4. the centers of all planes are equally distant from the middle; 5. when inscribed in a globe, they touch the surface with all their angles; 6. they remain fixed within it; 7. they touch an inscribed globe with the centers of all their planes; 8. therefore, the inscribed globe remains unmoved; 9. and it shares the same center with the figure. By these features, a second likeness to the Sphere is achieved, which arises from the equality of the distance of the planes.

<meta>catchword: Scho-</meta>

<summary>Kepler assigns specific Platonic solids to the gaps between the six known planets and argues that the existence of only five such solids explains why there are exactly six planets. He then begins a geometric defense of the "perfection" and "nobility" of these five regular solids compared to the sphere.</summary>

Show here, how well they agree with what the worthy Copernicus
Handed down; skillful Learning shows you these riches,
Which has given no small gifts with praise
To the Author, the Lord of Teck.

## CHAPTER II.

<margin>* above in the same place.</margin>
The commentary <note>original: "Scholion"</note> reads as follows: I say that besides the five mentioned figures, no other solid figure can be established which is contained by planes that are equal, equilateral, and equiangular to one another. For a solid angle <note>The vertex where the corners of the faces meet.</note> cannot be formed from two triangles, nor from any other two plane figures.

But from three triangles, the angle of a **Pyramid** <note>Also known as a tetrahedron.</note> is formed.
From four, the angle of an **Octahedron**.
From five, the angle of an **Icosahedron**.

For from six equilateral and equiangular triangles meeting at the same point, a solid angle will not be formed. Since the angle of an equilateral triangle contains two-thirds <note>original: "bessem"</note> of one right angle <note>i.e., 60 degrees.</note>, six such angles will be equal to four right angles <note>360 degrees, which creates a flat surface rather than a corner.</note>. This cannot happen. For every solid angle is contained by angles that sum to less than four right angles, according to Euclid, Book 11, Proposition 21.

For these same reasons, a solid angle cannot be formed from more than six such plane angles.

But from three squares, the angle of a **Cube** is contained.
From four, none can be formed; for again, they would equal four right angles.
From three equilateral and equiangular pentagons, the angle of a **Dodecahedron** is contained. But from four, none can be formed. Since the angle of an equilateral pentagon is one right angle and a fifth part of a right angle <note>108 degrees.</note>, four such angles would be greater than four right angles. This cannot occur.
Indeed, a solid angle will not be contained by any other polygonal figures, because an absurdity follows from this as well. Therefore, it is clear that besides the five mentioned figures, no other solid figure can be established which is contained under equilateral and equiangular planes.

| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

# CHAPTER III.

## *That these five bodies are distinguished into two orders; and that the Earth is correctly located.*

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring intricate floral patterns, acanthus leaves, and scrollwork in a square frame.</image-desc>

FURTHERMORE, it could seem accidental, and arising from no cause, that the six orbits of Copernicus <note>Kepler refers to the six known planets of the time: Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> contain these five bodies within the distances between one another—unless there were that very order among them, the order in which I have placed each one. For if Saturn were as close to Jupiter as Venus is to the Earth, and if conversely these two were separated from each other by as great an interval as Jupiter and Mars are in Copernicus’s system, a different order would have had to be used in inserting the bodies. For in the first place, between the first two orbits, there would be the <term>Dodecahedron</term> <note>A 12-sided solid with pentagonal faces.</note> or the <term>Icosahedron</term> <note>A 20-sided solid with triangular faces.</note>, and in the fourth place, the <term>Tetrahedron</term> <note>A 4-sided pyramid with triangular faces.</note>. Since this order cannot be allowed by mathematical reasoning, it would be easy to expose the futility of the theorem I have conceived. 

But now let us see by what reasons it is proved that the bodies ought to have been arranged in this very order between the orbits. To begin with, these bodies are distinguished into three primary ones—the <term>Cube</term>, the <term>Tetrahedron</term>, and the <term>Dodecahedron</term>—and two secondary ones—the <term>Octahedron</term> <note>An 8-sided solid with triangular faces.</note> and the <term>Icosahedron</term>. To show that this distinction is most true, note the properties of each class:

1. The primary bodies differ from one another by their faces; the secondary bodies both use the same triangular face. 
2. Each of the primary bodies has its own unique face: the Cube has the square, the Pyramid <note>Kepler uses "Pyramid" interchangeably with Tetrahedron.</note> has the triangle, and the Dodecahedron has the pentagon. The secondary bodies "borrow" their triangular face from the Pyramid. 
3. All the primary bodies use a simple angle <note>Meaning a vertex or corner.</note>, namely one contained by three faces; the secondary bodies take four or five faces into a single solid corner. 
4. The primary bodies owe their origin and properties to no one; most of the secondary bodies have obtained theirs from the primary ones, through a process of exchange, and are, as it were, born from them. 
5. The primary bodies cannot be moved elegantly <note>Referring to rotation or placement within spheres.</note> unless an axis is driven through the centers of one face or its opposite; whereas the secondary bodies are moved by an axis driven through opposite angles <note>Vertices.</note>. 
6. It is characteristic of the primary bodies to stand; it is characteristic of the secondary ones to hang. For whether you roll these onto a base or set those up on a corner, the eye will shrink from the deformity of the sight in both cases. 
7. Finally, add that the primary bodies are three in number—a perfect number. The se-

the two secondary [bodies] are imperfect in two ways: namely, that those [primary bodies] possess all types of angles—the **Cube** a right angle, the **Pyramid** <note>Kepler uses "Pyramid" to refer to the four-sided Tetrahedron.</note> an acute angle, the **Dodecahedron** an obtuse one—while these [secondary bodies] both fall only within the category of the obtuse. Indeed, the angle of the **Octahedron** wanders through all three types: it is obtuse at the joining of the edges; it is a right angle between two edges coming together from opposite sides; and the solid angle itself is acute. Since, therefore, the distinction between the bodies was manifest, nothing could have been done more appropriately than that our **Earth**—the sum and compendium of the whole world, and indeed the most worthy of the moving stars—should be distinguished by its sphere between the said orders, and should obtain that place which we attributed to it above.

### CHAPTER IV.

->*Why three bodies should surround the Earth, and the remaining two be contained within it?*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop-cap letter 'P' with an ornamental background featuring vine-like scrolls and a bird, possibly a swan, at the base.</image-desc>

Permit me now, kind Reader, to play for a little while in a serious matter, and to indulge somewhat in allegories. For I believe that the causes of many things in the world can be derived from the love of God for man. Certainly, no one will deny that in decorating the dwelling-place of the world, God repeatedly looked toward the future inhabitant. For the end of both the world and all Creation is Man. Therefore, I believe that the **Earth**, which was to give and nourish the genuine image of the Creator, was judged worthy by God to travel among the middle planets so that it would have just as many within the embrace of its orbit as it would have outside. In order for God to achieve this, He counted the **Sun** among the remaining five Stars <note>Kepler refers here to the planets known at the time—Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—which were often called "wandering stars."</note>, even though it differed entirely in kind. This seems all the more harmonious because since the Sun was previously established as the image of God the Father, it is credible that by this association with the other Stars, it ought to have provided arguments to the future settler of <term>love for humanity</term> <note>original: "φιλανθρωπίας" (philanthrōpias)</note> and <term>human-like emotion</term> <note>original: "ἀνθρωποπαθείας" (anthrōpopatheias); this term refers to the theological concept of attributing human feelings or suffering to God.</note> which God was going to employ toward men, humbling Himself even to domestic familiarity. For in the Old Testament, He frequently entered into the number of men, and wished to be called the friend of Abraham—just as we see the Sun enter into the number of the moving bodies. However, since the Sun was surrounded by the Earth: once the things I have said were established, that order of bodies necessarily had to be included within the Earth which embraces at least two—

...ich is why two mobile planets, along with the immobile Sun, might produce the same number of three as is found in the bodies excluded from the Earth’s orbit. Thus, especially with the Moon orbiting the Earth, the most excellent Creator placed our dwelling in the middle of seven planets. For if the order of the remaining three solids had approached the Sun, there would have been four stars inside Earth's orbit with the Sun, but only two outside. Since this numerical *disorder* <note>original Greek: *ἀταξία* (ataxia)</note> lacks reason, it was omitted by the Creator. Furthermore, since containing belongs to perfection (as an action) and being contained belongs to the passion of the more imperfect, and because the primary solids are more perfect than the others, it is fitting that the order of the three primary solids should contain the Earth, while the rest are contained within the boundary of the Earth's orbit. And so we have, in passing, the reason why three planets move outside Earth, and two inside. If this is less convincing to the Reader, let him consider that this is an honorary point, not the principal one. For even if we did not know the cause why three stars went above the Earth (or Ptolemy's Sun), nevertheless the following points would stand with the preceding ones, because the REALITY of the matter is certain to us. Nor has anyone ever doubted that **Saturn** ♄, **Jupiter** ♃, and **Mars** ♂ are superior <note>meaning "outer" or further from the center</note> planets. Let us only hold to this: since in the system of Copernicus three planets are above the Earth, it is necessary for us to place the order of the three primary bodies—the **Cube**, the **Pyramid** <note>Kepler’s term for the four-sided Tetrahedron</note>, and the **Dodecahedron**—outside the orbit of the earth, and the **Octahedron** and **Icosahedron** within it, if we wish to win the prize in this endeavor.

### CHAPTER V.

#### *That the Cube is the first of the bodies, and placed among the highest planets.*

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'V' with ornate floral and geometric motifs in a square border.</image-desc>
LET US now come to the three primary bodies, and let us assign to each their own spaces. And indeed the **Cube** ought to approach the fixed stars <note>the outermost sphere of the universe in the Copernican model</note> and constitute the first proportion, which is between **Saturn** and **Jupiter**. This is because the fixed stars are the most worthy part of the world outside the Earth—like the circumference of a circle (after the center)—and the **Cube** is the first body in its order. 1. For it alone is generated from its own base, while the other four are not generated by their own faces, but are either cut from the **Cube**—like the **Pyramid**, by removing four right-angled pyramids—or increased, like the **Dodecahedron**, by adding six pentahedrons <note>literally "five-faced figures," referring to the roof-like shapes added to each square face of a cube to form a dodecahedron</note>. 2. It alone can be divided into homogeneous cubes without any remaining prismat...

...can be resolved. 3. It alone is equal in every direction and extends into three straight dimensions. For the faces of the other solids are inclined; and in some cases, while they present themselves through two straight sections, they fail to form a section in the third. 4. From this it follows that the cube alone has as many faces as the triple dimension has boundaries—namely six—and double that number of edges <note>original: *laterum*</note>, namely twelve. 5. It alone has an equal angle everywhere, specifically the right angle. But in a regular Pyramid <note>The tetrahedron, which Kepler refers to here as a "Pyramid"</note>, when it sits on its base, its alignment differs when compared to the middle planes if you twist it toward an angle; nor do its solid angles square with that rule which measures the long lateral angle placed between them. 6. Hence also to the cube alone belongs what Simplicius—in his commentary on the first book of *Aristotle’s On the Heavens*, chapter 1—cites from the *Single Book* <note>original Greek: *μονοβίβλῳ* (monobiblo)</note> of Ptolemy regarding the cause of perfection in the number three: namely, that no more than three straight lines perpendicular to each other can meet at a solid point to divide it into right solid angles. 7. It is the simplest body of all rectilinear solids. Though this status is sometimes disputed in favor of the Pyramid, it is easily proved by this: that the measure of the pyramid is the Cube, and it is proper for the measure to be prior to the thing measured. Indeed, the cube is a measure not only by human institution—whereby people, in measuring any solid, conceive of its quantity in their minds as small cubes—but much more so by Nature. For a right angle is equal to any other right angle when it is extended onto a plane. It is therefore perpetually equal to itself, and thus unique, while of the other angles <note>acute and obtuse</note> there are infinite variations on both sides. It is fitting for a measure to be one and the same, and also finite. 8. From this comes such a fruitful inscription of the right angle into a circle, without which as an intermediary, neither the triangle, nor the pentagon, nor the shapes derived from them, can be inscribed. 9. But nor should we overlook that skillful nature has most perfectly attributed those same six dimensions <note>original Greek: *διαςάσεις* (diastaseis)</note> to the most perfect animal <note>meaning Man</note>: no obscure argument for how much this body is held in

OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

# CHAPTER VI.

## That the Pyramid lies between Jupiter and Mars.

1. NOW, as to why the Pyramid follows the Cube, no one will wonder much, since the Pyramid almost dares to contend with the cube for the position of primary importance. 2. Furthermore, either the pyramid itself or its irregular <term>homologs</term> <note>original Greek: *Ὁμόλογα*; these are corresponding or proportional parts</note> contribute to the construction of the other solids. For the <term>Icosahedron</term> <note>A 20-sided solid made of triangles</note> is composed of 20 Pyramids, slightly shorter than those of a <term>Tetrahedron</term>; the <term>Octahedron</term> <note>An 8-sided solid made of triangles</note> is composed of eight that are even shorter. Although the <term>Dodecahedron</term> <note>A 12-sided solid made of pentagons</note> consists of a "hidden square" <note>Kepler refers here to the geometric relationships between pentagons and the golden ratio/rectangles</note>, it nonetheless must be resolved into pyramids. 3. Nor should we overlook this: that the <term>Tetrahedron</term> can be resolved into four perfect pyramids and one <term>Octahedron</term> whose sides are half as long. 4. Just as in plane geometry all many-sided figures are resolved into triangles, so the remaining solids are resolved into Pyramids for the sake of measurement; we then measure these pyramids by cubes, just as we measure triangles by squares. The pyramid is therefore the measure of the others, and it is most easily measured by the cube. 5. Hence, most of its lines, like those of the cube, take their quantity so easily from the ratio of the diagonal, yet not otherwise than through square numbers. 6. Furthermore, the regularity of the pyramid depends solely on its sides, whereas the cube’s regularity also depends on its angles. Thus, among equilateral solids, there is only one type of Pyramid; but in a <term>hexahedron</term> <note>original Greek: *ἑξαέδρῳ*; a six-sided solid or cube</note>, even with equal sides, there is an infinite variety of possible angles. For this reason—if there were no other arguments—I leave it in doubt whether the pyramid should be preferred to the cube or placed after it.

7. Humans, imitating this skill of nature, first erect their building materials perpendicularly and join them at right angles, and then they strengthen and stabilize them with triangles.

8. Furthermore, since the pyramid has an acute angle, it takes precedence over those with obtuse angles. For in any order, that which possesses its proper measure is always first; it seems that the "less than proper" <note>the acute angle</note> follows this, because it seems to be further from infinity than the "more than proper" <note>the obtuse angle</note>, and it is also simpler. For an obtuse angle appears, in a certain way, to be a multiple composed of a right angle and an acute angle. Therefore, it is less surprising that the small number of angles in its base, and the small number of the bases themselves, does not make the pyramid inferior to the cube. For the number of angles and bases necessarily follows from the specific type of angle chosen. Thus, if the right angle is prior to the acute, the <term>hexahedron</term> <note>original Greek: *ἑξάεδρον*; the cube</note> is also prior to the <term>Tetrahedron</term>.

<summary>Kepler argues that the Tetrahedron (Pyramid) is the second most fundamental solid after the Cube, justifying its placement between the orbits of Jupiter and Mars based on its role as a geometric "building block" and its simple, acute angles.</summary>
<keywords>Tetrahedron, Pyramid, Cube, Hexahedron, Jupiter, Mars, geometry, Platonic solids, measurement, angles</keywords>

He then begins Chapter VII, moving from the "Primary" solids to the "Secondary" solids (the Octahedron and Icosahedron), explaining their geometric relationships and their positions closer to the Sun (between Earth, Venus, and Mercury).</meta>

...hedron, a <term>four-sided-faced solid</term> <note>original: *Tetragonoedrum*; the Cube</note> rather than a <term>three-sided-faced solid</term> <note>original: *Trigonoedrum*; the Pyramid or Tetrahedron</note>. 9. And this can also be gathered from the fact that the perfect is everywhere first, then that which is deficient, and finally that which exceeds. Since the <term>number six</term> <note>original: *Senarius*; in classical mathematics, 6 is the first "perfect number" because its divisors 1, 2, and 3 add up to 6</note> of faces is perfect, it follows that the pyramid—which is "deficient" <note>having only four faces, fewer than the perfect six</note>—should not indeed precede the cube, but follow it immediately.

We have now established why the pyramid is in the second place between Jupiter and Mars. Previously, it was left in suspense which body should be in the third place between Mars and the Earth. That, indeed, is easily decided here. Since the <term>Dodecahedron</term> <note>a 12-sided solid</note> is the remaining one of the <term>primary solids</term> <note>Kepler distinguishes the Cube, Tetrahedron, and Dodecahedron as "primary" because they stand upright on their faces and have distinct geometric priorities</note>, it will be the third in order, between Mars and the Earth; what should be thought of its properties will easily become clear once a comparison with the former ones is made.

# CHAPTER VII.

->*On the order and properties of the secondary solids.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring a scrolling vine and floral motif.</image-desc>
REGARDING the SECONDARY solids, since the Octahedron is prior to the Icosahedron, it might seem strange to some why that which is later in the order of Nature should precede in the arrangement of the world? For because Mars, via the Dodecahedron, is more strongly joined with the Earth, it follows from what we have said that the <term>Icosahedron</term> <note>a 20-sided solid</note> lies between the Earth and Venus. And many things prove that the <term>Octahedron</term> <note>an 8-sided solid</note> is prior to the Icosahedron. First, the Octahedron is born (not truly, but as if it were born) from the Cube and the Pyramid, which are the first in their order; from the former it borrows its number of sides, and from the latter its triangular base. The Icosahedron, however, is born from the Pyramid and the Dodecahedron, which are the last in their order. For again, it borrows its base from the former, and its number of sides from the latter. 2. If you look at the Octahedron and Icosahedron from their angles, the former shows the square base of the cube, the latter the pentagonal base of the Dodecahedron. 3. The Octahedron is of equal height to the cube, as we shall see, and the Icosahedron to the Dodecahedron. 4. The Octahedron with the cube, and the Icosahedron with the dodecahedron, exchange the number of bases and angles <note>Kepler is describing the concept of "duality": a cube has 6 faces and 8 vertices, while its dual, the octahedron, has 8 faces and 6 vertices</note>. For the bases of the Cube and the angles of the Octahedron are six; the former's angles and the latter's bases are eight. Thus the bases of the Dodecahedron and the angles of the Icosahedron are twelve on both sides; conversely, the former's angles and the latter's bases are twenty. 5. The Octahedron imitates the right angle of the Cube, the Icosahedron imitates the Dodecahedron's...

<summary>Kepler concludes his ranking of the "primary" solids by placing the Dodecahedron between Mars and Earth, then begins justifying the order of the "secondary" solids (Octahedron and Icosahedron) based on their geometric "birth" and duality.</summary>

...obtuse. From these facts, it is clear that the <term>Octahedron</term> <note>an eight-faced solid</note> is the head of its own order, just as the cube is the leader of the primary ones.

## CHAPTER VIII.

### *That the Octahedron is between Venus and Mercury.*

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial Q containing stylized foliage and floral motifs, framed within a square border.</image-desc>

However, it does not immediately follow that the Octahedron should succeed the <term>Dodecahedron</term> <note>a twelve-faced solid</note> in the arrangement of the world. 

1. For because there are truly two different orders of solids, they can look toward different regions of the world with their "heads" <note>Kepler personifies the solids, suggesting they have an orientation or priority</note>. 

2. And indeed, since the Cube approaches the most noble region of the world outside the Earth—namely the circumference or the sphere of the fixed stars—it was fitting that the head of the other order should approach a more noble place in the world inside the orbit of the Earth. Nothing, however, is more noble than the center and the Sun. 

3. But if we also consider the position of both orders as a single sequence, what could be done more elegantly than for that sequence to be enclosed on both sides by similar and primary bodies? 

4. It is also more beautiful for the <term>many-faced bodies</term> <note>original: *multifacia*; polyhedra with many sides, like the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron</note> to follow one another in the middle, and for the sequence to depart gradually from a high number of bases on both sides toward a lower number (if nothing else forbids it). This is better than if a body with few bases were to follow one with many, only to be followed again by another with far more than either of the two. 

5. And since the Dodecahedron was the last in its own order <note>the "Primary" solids: Cube, Tetrahedron, Dodecahedron</note>, it was fitting that it should be succeeded by a solid from the other order that was similar to it. 

6. This also pertains to the dignity of the Earth: that it should be surrounded on both sides in a similar manner, as much as possible. Since it had turned out that the Earth was closely surrounded on the outside by a many-faced solid, it was fitting that it should also be closely enclosed on the inside by a many-faced solid. 

Therefore, these two orders of these five bodies have been reduced into one by the most wise <term>Creator</term> <note>original: *Conditore*; Kepler views God as the Supreme Geometer</note> in such a way that they are turned toward each other with their "heels" at the Earth—which is their <term>partition wall</term> <note>original: *maceries*; a boundary or dividing wall</note>—while they depart with their "heads" toward different regions of the world.

<summary>Kepler explains why the Octahedron is placed between Venus and Mercury, arguing that the Earth's orbit acts as a central dividing line (a "wall") between two distinct classes of geometric solids.</summary>
<keywords>Octahedron, Dodecahedron, Earth, Sun, planetary spheres, geometry, Creator, cosmic order</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the geometric ordering of the Octahedron in Chapter VIII to a broader physical and astrological application of all five solids in Chapter IX. Kepler is moving from pure geometry to "Physics," or natural philosophy, to justify why certain planets have "beneficent" or "maleficent" qualities based on the shapes assigned to them.</meta>

# CHAPTER IX.

->*The solids distributed among the Planets, their properties adapted, and the mutual kinship of the planets demonstrated from the solids.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'N' featuring a stylized floral pattern within a square frame.</image-desc>
I cannot pass over this without touching upon a certain part of <term>Physics</term> <note>original: *Physices*; here referring to natural philosophy or the study of the physical causes of celestial motions</note> regarding the qualities of the Planets. This will show that even their natural powers follow this order and maintain a mutual proportion to one another. For if you reckon those planets that orbit the Earth alongside the solids inscribed within them, and assign to the inner planets (those within Earth's orbit) the solids by which each is circumscribed—which I believe can be done with excellent reason—then Saturn will have the <term>Cube</term>, Jupiter the <term>Pyramid</term> <note>the tetrahedron</note>, Mars the <term>Dodecahedron</term>, Venus the <term>Icosahedron</term>, and Mercury the <term>Octahedron</term>. As for the Earth, since it is nothing but a boundary <note>original: *limes*</note>, it is not counted with either group. Moreover, <term>Astrologers</term> distinguish the Sun and the Moon by a great distance from the other five, so there is no need to mention them here; thus the number of solids matches beautifully with the five Planets.

Jupiter, therefore—being <term>beneficent</term> <note>original: *beneficus*; in astrology, a planet that exerts a positive or helpful influence</note> while placed in the middle of <term>maleficent</term> <note>original: *maleficorum*; "evildoers," or planets like Saturn and Mars that were traditionally viewed as having a harsh or destructive influence</note> ones—has moved many to wonder, and even spurred <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose work defined medieval and Renaissance cosmology</note> to investigate the causes. We see something similar in the pyramid: placed between two bodies that are partly related and partly repellent, it differs so much from both that it almost loses its place in the previous reasoning. Each of the three superior planets <note>the planets outside Earth's orbit: Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars</note> harbors a hostile hatred for the others. Furthermore, nothing in their appearance seems to agree among the bodies of those three. Yet, Mars conspires with Saturn only in "malice." I compare this to the inconsistency of the angles, which is unique to them and common to both. Therefore, the proof of goodness will be the opposite: namely, the stability of the angles on the faces alone. This is the argument for why Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury are beneficent. The Cube, which is Saturn’s solid, measures all the rest by its own <term>rectitude</term> <note>original: *rectitudine*; a pun on the mathematical "right angle" and moral "uprightness" or "correctness"</note>. The planet itself makes men "measurers," and in character it is rigid, a guardian of the right, not yielding a hair's breadth—inexorable and inflexible. Such is the nature of a right angle.

<summary>Kepler assigns each of the five Platonic solids to a specific planet and argues that the geometric properties of these shapes (such as the "uprightness" of the cube's angles) explain the astrological characteristics and "temperaments" of the planets.</summary>
<keywords>Platonic solids, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury, astrological qualities, geometry, Ptolemy, beneficent planets, maleficent planets</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter IX, where Kepler provides a "physical" and astrological justification for the arrangement of the planets. He is linking the geometric properties of the five Platonic solids—which he believes determine the orbits of the planets—to the traditional astrological characteristics of those planets (such as "beneficent" Jupiter or "solitary" Saturn).</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY</header>

The kinship is most evident in the bases <note>the "bases" refers to the faces of the geometric solids</note>, for since Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury (I mean the planet in terms of its solid body) all use the same base <note>the equilateral triangle</note>, we have the cause of their friendship, as mentioned above. For stability resides first in the triangle.

The second degree of kinship is the apparent plane with an angle acting as a center <note>original: *umbilico*, literally "navel"</note>. Let us no longer wonder, then, what delights might lie hidden within harsh and fiery Mars, for the sake of which delicate Venus, frustrated with her husband’s bed, conspired with Mars <note>a reference to the mythological adultery of Venus and Mars, used here to explain the geometric link between the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron</note>. For the pentagon of Mars is found within Venus. Similarly, the quadrangle <note>the square</note> of Saturn is found in Mercury, which unites the same character in both. The third degree is when the same feature of one solid exists, or appears to exist, in two others; in those cases, they agree on matters regarding a common friend. Therefore, in the affairs of Jupiter, Venus agrees with Mercury, because they both use the common base of Jupiter <note>the triangle</note>. In the affairs of Saturn, Mercury agrees with Mars for a short while, because in the former is Saturn's square, and in the latter, a cube <note>Saturn's solid</note> is hidden. It also appears from here why Venus has no kinship with Saturn, and what is most important, why the versatile nature of Mercury adapts itself to all four, though least of all to Mars.

Furthermore, Saturn is solitary and a lover of solitude, clearly because the rectitude <note>original: *rectitudo*; referring to the 90-degree right angles of the cube</note> of its angle cannot bear any inequality, even the smallest, for the sake of which it might become manifold. On the contrary, Jupiter, having obtained one angle from the class of infinite acute angles, has therefore become social <note>original: *popularis*</note>, though moderately and temperately. For he is the author of more honorable friendships. Likewise, Mars and Venus are social themselves, but too much so. For their obtuse and lavish angle denotes intemperance. Mercury is of the nature of both Saturn and Jupiter by reason of its angle. Indeed, the learned love solitude, yet they are not inhuman. They love those who take pleasure in the same studies; and they set a limit on social interactions, more so than Jupiter, whose every action is in assemblies of men and among those clothed in purple <note>referring to royalty, high-ranking officials, or the clergy</note>.

Jupiter and Venus are fertile. This is surely because Jupiter contributes to the composition of most things, while Venus is, as it were, the offspring of Jupiter, since one Venus <note>the icosahedron</note> contains within itself twenty small Jupiters <note>the icosahedron can be divided into twenty tetrahedrons</note>. Jupiter, however, is more inclined toward males, and Venus toward females: hence he is called male, and she female. For the <term>Pyramid</term> <note>the tetrahedron, assigned to Jupiter</note> is the active cause, while the <term>Icosahedron</term> <note>assigned to Venus</note> is the effect and the offspring. From these same principles, a somewhat more explained reason is given as to why Mercury is of "promiscuous sex" <note>hermaphroditic or neutral</note> and why he is mediocre in fertility.

<page-num>35</page-num>
<sig>E 3</sig>

# CHAPTER IX.

First of Jupiter, then of Saturn, and finally of Mercury, their tranquility and stability of character comes from the fewness of their faces <note>Kepler refers to the "planes" or faces of the geometric solids associated with these planets. Fewer faces suggest a more stable, less "rolling" shape</note>; the turbulence and fickleness of Venus and Mars comes from their multitude. **Woman is always a varied and changeable thing.** <note>original: "Varium & mutabile semper foemina," a famous line from Virgil’s *Aeneid* 4.569</note> And the figure of Venus <note>the Icosahedron, with 20 faces</note> is the most varied and rolling of them all. And these are the degrees of character.

From this, the middle position of Mercury and the "middle-ground" reliability of Mercury reflects the versatile and swift wit of the **Octahedron’s** mobility. For if you roll it upon two opposite corners, four continuous sides pass through the middle of the figure in a straight path. In the other figures, however you roll them, you will see the sides proceed through the middle in a transverse and obstructed way.

Mars, with many edges, produces fewer faces; Venus, with just as many edges, produces more faces <note>Mars is the Dodecahedron (30 edges, 12 faces); Venus is the Icosahedron (30 edges, 20 faces)</note>. Furthermore, many of Mars's efforts are in vain; Venus is equal to him in effort, yet enjoys more prosperous fortune. Nor should this be a surprise. For dances are more easily organized than wars, and it was fitting for loves to reach their end sooner than rages: because the latter destroy men, while the former give birth to them. In the same way, Mercury is more fortunate than Saturn.

# CHAPTER X.

->*On the origin of the noble numbers.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring floral and leafy scrollwork within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>
It would be an endless task to pursue every single detail; nor should the Astrologer think further on these matters without benefit. Let us now look at the **Arithmetic of Astronomers** and their sacred numbers: 6, 12, 60. Therefore, with the exception of the quarter and the sixth part (namely 15 and 10), all the multiple parts of sixty are found within these five solids. Conversely, with the exception of the plane angles of the Octahedron and the Cube (both of which have 24), all other things that are counted are a multiple part of sixty. Thus, I am led to think that there is hardly any number—not even by <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>the ancient Greek philosopher who believed that numbers were the essence of all things</note>—to which any natural thing could be assigned that is more proper to it than this number 60 is to the five solids mentioned.

There is **one** Cube, **one** pyramid <note>the tetrahedron</note>, **one** Dodecahedron, **one** Icosahedron, **one** Octahedron—each solitary and without a peer.

There are **two** secondary solids <note>the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron, which share certain symmetries</note>; **two** orders of solids; there are **two** that are always similar to each other; and **two** such similarities.

<summary>Kepler concludes his astrological comparison of the planets by linking their "temperaments" to the mobility of their geometric solids, then transitions to explaining why the number 60 (the base of astronomical measurement) is derived from the properties of the five Platonic solids.</summary>

<header>THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY</header>

Three angles of the bases in the pyramid, Icosahedron, and Octahedron, because their bases <note>In this context, "bases" refers to the faces of the geometric solids.</note> are three-sided. There are three primary bodies. There are three differences of angles.
Four angles and sides of the base in the Cube. Four solid angles <note>vertices</note> of the pyramid. Four bases of the same.
Five bodies. Five angles and sides in the dodecahedral base <note>a pentagon</note>.
Six angles of the Octahedron. Six edges <note>original: "latera"</note> of the pyramid. Six faces of the cube. A beautiful number.
Eight faces of the octahedron. Eight angles of the cube.
Twelve faces of the dodecahedron. Twelve edges of the Octahedron. Likewise of the cube as well. Twelve angles of the Icosahedron. Twelve plane angles of the pyramid.
Behold, this number [12] is found in all five [solids].
Twenty faces of the Icosahedron. Twenty angles of the dodecahedron.
Twenty-four plane angles of the Octahedron and the cube. This is an outsider number <note>meaning it is not a primary count of faces or vertices</note>, but it is neither of a primary thing, nor is it truly an outsider: for it is twice 12, three times 8, and four times 6, all of which are contained in 60.
Thirty edges of the Icosahedron and Dodecahedron.
Sixty plane angles of the dodecahedron and Icosahedron.
Beyond this, nothing else is counted, unless we wish to enter into the sums of all edges and angles, which is out of place. Then there would arise 18 angles of the denominating bases. 50 faces, just as many angles, 90 edges. 180 plane angles. These are all related numbers.

## CHAPTER XI.

->*On the position of the bodies, and the origin of the Zodiac.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring floral scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>
I shall have the physicists <note>Kepler refers to "natural philosophers" who studied the physical world through Aristotelian causes rather than mathematical ones.</note> as my enemies in these chapters, because I have deduced the natural properties of the planets from immaterial things and mathematical figures. Furthermore, I even dare to investigate the origins of the circles <note>the planetary orbits</note> from the bare imagination of certain sections. To these few, I wish to respond: that God the Creator, since He is Mind and does what He wills, is not prohibited from being consistent <note>original catchword "stantes" suggests "remaining consistent" or "standing firm" in the next page's opening</note> in fitting powers and designating circles to things that exist either without matter or in the imagination...

<header>CHAPTER II.</header>

->...considering those things that stand before us.<- And since He wills nothing except with the highest reason, and nothing exists outside of His will: let the **Adversaries** then say what other reasons God had for fitting these forces, etc., when there was nothing besides quantities? But if, finding nothing, they flee to the unsearchable powers of the <term>Foundational Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Conditricis Sapientiæ"</note>: let them indeed keep this restraint in inquiry for themselves, and let them enjoy it with a reputation for piety. Let them, however, allow us to provide probable causes from quantities, provided we say nothing unworthy of so great an **Artificer** <note>Kepler frequently refers to God as the "Opifex," the master craftsman or architect of the universe.</note>. Therefore, bound by no religious scruple, I proceed to the investigation of the **Zodiac**.

At the beginning, I consider that a more probable placement of the bodies cannot be devised than when the **Cube**, the largest of the figures, is inserted into the sphere in any way whatever—for in a circle there is no beginning. However, it is necessary to establish principles without a specific reason, lest there be an infinite retreat, and so that we may eventually have a transition from infinite potential to a finished act. Now, therefore, let one of the faces be considered the base. The **Pyramid** <note>The Tetrahedron.</note>, which is to be inserted into the cube by means of the Jovian sphere <note>The orbital sphere of Jupiter.</note>, must hold its base <term>parallel</term> <note>original Greek: "παράλληλον"</note> to the base of the cube; and the **Dodecahedron** must be parallel to the base of the pyramid. The properties of the secondary bodies behave otherwise, as we have seen. The **Icosahedron** must then be raised within the Dodecahedron, so that its diagonal becomes perpendicular to the centers of both opposite bases of the dodecahedron. In the same way, the **Octahedron**, the smallest of the figures, must be suspended within the Icosahedron, so that a straight line passes:
1. Through the center of the base in the cube, 
2. Through the center of the tetrahedral base, 
3. Through the center of the pentagonal face <note>original: "quinque anguli"</note> of the Dodecahedron, 
4. Through a vertex <note>original: "angulum"</note> of the Icosahedron, 
5. Through a vertex of the octahedron, 
6. Through the center of the world and the solar body, 

and further through similar intervals through the opposites: 
7. The Octahedron vertices, 
8. The Icosahedron vertices, 
9. The center of the Dodecahedral plane, 
10. The vertex of the Tetrahedron, 
11. The center of the cubic plane. 

For the sake of greater clarity, I refer you to the illustration for chapter two where all the bodies are expressed in this manner. These being thus arranged, not only will the square appearing in the Octahedron (which is equally distant from the two said vertices) divide all the figures—and indeed the whole world—into two equal halves if it is extended all around; but also the mutual sections of all the sides (which one can consider to be in the middle between the said vertices and centers), if they are placed regularly, which to one looking out from the center...

What remains, then, but for us to say that the **Planets** were commanded by the **Creator** to travel that path, marked by so many clear points, especially since it lies midway between the centers and angles assumed and bound together above, acting like poles.

### CHAPTER XII.

#### Division of the Zodiac, and the aspects.

**MANY** have regarded the division of the **Zodiac** into twelve precise signs as a human invention, namely, one in which nothing of a natural reality underlies it. For they do not think these parts <note>original: "μόρια" (moria); here referring to the 30-degree segments of the Zodiac.</note> differ in their natural powers or influences: but [having] assumed

<meta>This page continues Chapter XII of the Mystery of the Cosmography, where Kepler transitions from the physical nesting of the Platonic solids to the mathematical and harmonic divisions of the Zodiac (the ecliptic) and musical strings.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

# CHAPTER XII.

->adopted because of the suitability of the number for ratios. Although I do not entirely oppose these views, nevertheless, so that nothing is rashly rejected, I shall propose the cause of this division from those same principles, to which it is not unlikely that the Creator accommodated properties (if they possess any distinct ones).<-

We have seen above what the subject of numbers might be. And certainly, besides quantity, or that which is similar to quantity, endowed with any power whatsoever, there is nothing in the entire universe that can be numbered, except for God, who is the very venerable Trinity itself. Now, therefore, we have dissected all the bodies <note>the five Platonic solids</note> through the **Zodiac** <term>Zodiac: the belt of the heavens within which the planets move, here treated as the ecliptic plane</term>. Let us see whether the Zodiac itself has gained or suffered anything from this sectioning. Of the solids cut in the said manner, the face of the **Cube** resulting from the section will be a square, as will that of the **Octahedron**; the face of the **Pyramid** <note>the tetrahedron</note> will be a triangle; and the remaining two <note>the dodecahedron and icosahedron</note> will be decagons <term>Decagon: a ten-sided polygon</term>. Four times three times ten make the sum of one hundred and twenty. Therefore, when the square, triangle, and decagon are inscribed in a circle at the same point, they distinguish various arcs on the circumference, all of which are measured by 

<image-desc>A geometric diagram consisting of a circle within which an equilateral triangle and a square are inscribed. Both figures share a single vertex at the top point of the circle's circumference.</image-desc>

a portion no larger than a one-hundred-and-twentieth part of the whole circle. Therefore, the natural division of the Zodiac is into 120 parts, based on the regular placement of the bodies between the spheres. Since triple this amount is 360, we see that this division is not supported by no reason at all. Now, if we again describe the square and the triangle separately from the same point, the smallest portion of the circle will be a twelfth part of the circuit, namely a **Sign** <term>Sign: one of the twelve 30-degree divisions of the Zodiac</term>. Thus it is a marvel that the monthly motion of the Sun and Moon, and the great conjunctions of the Superior Planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, square so aptly with the portions which are distinguished from these same bodies through the triangle and the square.

And indeed, take an external example of how much this division into twelve is prized by nature: so that although the cause may not be entirely known, an occasion may nonetheless lie open for thinking more excellently of these five figures hereafter.

<image-desc>A small illustration of a musical staff and a diagram of harmonic string division. On the left is a five-line staff starting with a Gamma (Γ) symbol. To the right, horizontal lines represent a musical string (fides) with points of division, terminating in a small square symbol.</image-desc>

Let some musical string <note>original: "fides"</note> be proposed, and its sound be **Gamma ut** <note>The lowest note in the traditional musical scale of Kepler's time, corresponding to a low G</note>. However many notes occur from **Gamma ut** up to the octave that are consonant with **Gamma ut**, that is how many times, and no more, you can rationally divide the string, so that the parts of the divided string are consonant both with each other and with the whole. Furthermore, the ears indicate how many notes of that kind occur; I shall state it with a diagram and numbers.

<meta>This page continues Chapter XII of the Mystery of the Cosmography. Kepler is now drawing a direct mathematical parallel between the geometry of the Platonic solids (which he used to divide the 360 degrees of the Zodiac on the previous page) and the harmonic divisions of a musical string. He is attempting to show that the same "divine" proportions govern both the heavens and music.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

# THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

<image-desc>Seven segments of musical staves numbered 1 through 7 at the top. Each segment displays notes on a five-line staff with numerical labels underneath indicating string proportions. These illustrate the "proportions of the strings" (fidium proportiones). For instance, segment 1 shows a base note with a '1' below it, representing the full length of the string.</image-desc>

Look now at the harmonies themselves, and the proportions of the strings in numbers: where the lowest note signifies the sound of the entire string; the highest, the sound of the shorter part; the middle, the sound of the longer part. The lowest number indicates into how many parts the string must be divided; the others indicate the lengths of the parts.

<image-desc>A second set of six musical staff segments numbered 1 through 6. These segments include notes and numerical ratios (such as 1:5:6 or 3:5:8), illustrating more complex harmonic relationships and musical "concords" or consonances.</image-desc>

And these sounds alone seem natural to me, because they possess an indubitable number. Other sounds cannot be expressed by a fixed proportion to those already established. For you will draw one sound of F <note>original: "F fa ut", a pitch in the medieval solmization system</note> from C <note>original: "C sol fa ut"</note> above, and another from B-flat <note>original: "B mi molli"</note> below, however much these two may appear to be perfect fifths <term>Fifth: a musical interval encompassing five notes of a scale, considered highly stable or "perfect"</term>. But to the point. The first and second concords are, in a way, companions; so too the fifth and sixth. Since all of them are imperfect <note>In Kepler's time, "imperfect" intervals included thirds and sixths, which were considered pleasing but less stable than the "perfect" octave or fifth</note>, they always harmonize in pairs—one "hard" <note>major</note> and the other "soft" <note>minor</note>—so that they are, in a sense, equivalent to the individual perfect concords. Nor do they have very different divisions. For 1/6 and 1/5 relate to each other as 5/30 and 6/30, which differ only by one-thirtieth. Likewise, 3/8 and 2/5 relate to each other as 15/40 and 16/40. They differ, therefore, only by one-fortieth part.

And so, strictly speaking, we have only five concords in Music, corresponding to the number of the **five bodies** <note>the Platonic solids</note>. But if you seek the least common multiple of the seven divisions—6, 5, 4, 3, 8, 5, and 2—you will again find 120, just as above when we were discussing the division of the **Zodiac**; whereas the least common multiple of the perfect concords is again 12. It is quite as if the perfect concords arise from the square and triangle of the **Cube**, **Tetrahedron**, and **Octahedron**, while the imperfect ones arise from the decagon <term>Decagon: a ten-sided polygon</term> of the remaining—

<meta>The catchword "rum" completes the word "reliquorum" (the remaining [solids]), referring to the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron which were associated with the decagon on the previous page.</meta>

<summary>Kepler demonstrates that musical harmonies (concords) are derived from the same geometric ratios as the Platonic solids, linking the mathematical laws of music to the 120-part division of the Zodiac.</summary>
<keywords>harmony, string proportions, musical concords, Platonic solids, least common multiple, major and minor, mathematical music theory</keywords>

<page-num>41</page-num>

<header>CHAPTER XII.</header>

<sig>F 2</sig>

...would arise from these two bodies. And this is the second kinship of the [Platonic] bodies with musical concords. But because we are ignorant of the causes of this kinship, it is difficult to assign individual harmonies to individual bodies.

Indeed, we see two orders of harmonies: three simple perfect ones, and two double imperfect ones—just as there are three primary bodies <note>The "primary" solids are the Cube, Tetrahedron (Pyramid), and Octahedron, which Kepler associated with the outer planets.</note> and two secondary ones <note>The "secondary" solids are the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron, associated with the inner planets.</note>. However, since the rest do not match up, this reconciliation must be set aside and another attempted. Truly, just as the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron, by means of their decagon <note>A ten-sided figure; the faces of these solids relate to the pentagon and decagon.</note>, increased the number twelve up to 120, so here the imperfect harmonies do the same.

Therefore, the perfect harmonies must be assigned to the Cube, the Pyramid, and the Octahedron, and the imperfect ones to the Dodecahedron and the Icosahedron. To this is added another point—and by Hercules, it points a finger at the most hidden cause of these things, which we shall discuss in the next chapter—namely, that there are two treasures of geometry: one, the ratio of the hypotenuse <note>original: "subtensæ in rectangulo," referring to the side opposite the right angle</note> to the sides; the other, a line divided by extreme and mean ratio <term>Golden Ratio: A mathematical proportion (roughly 1:1.618) where the ratio of the whole to the larger part is the same as the larger part to the smaller. Kepler considered this "divine."</term>. From the former flows the construction of the Cube, the Pyramid, and the Octahedron; from the latter, the construction of the Dodecahedron and the Icosahedron. This is why it is so easy and regular to inscribe a Pyramid in a cube, or an Octahedron in both, just as a Dodecahedron is inscribed in an Icosahedron. 

However, it is not so obvious how individual harmonies should be assigned to individual bodies. Only this much is clear: the harmony called the "fifth" (the fourth in our order) is owed to the Pyramid, because in it, the smaller portion is 
<margin>1/3</margin>
part of the whole, just as the side of a triangle (which the Pyramid uses) subtends 1/3 of a circle. Many things below will confirm this when we deal with 
<margin>a</margin>
astrological aspects. To understand them here, let us think 
<margin>as if</margin>
of the musical string not as a straight line, but as a circle. Thus, the division of the said harmony will produce a triangle, in which an angle is opposite a side, just as in the pyramid an angle is opposite a face. 

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a circle with an inscribed equilateral triangle. The triangle's vertices touch the circumference, effectively dividing the circle into three equal arcs.</image-desc>

There remain, therefore, for the Cube and the Octahedron, the so-called octave and fourth—the third and seventh in our order. But which of them shall hold which harmony? Shall we say the secondary bodies receive those that describe lines, and the primary ones those that describe figures? Then the so-called fourth would be owed to the Cube. For if you make a circle from the string, and draw a straight line from one quarter to another for as long 

<meta>catchword: until (original: "donec")</meta>

<summary>Kepler attempts to pair the five Platonic solids with specific musical harmonies, arguing that the "primary" solids (Cube, Pyramid, Octahedron) relate to perfect harmonies through the Pythagorean theorem, while the "secondary" solids (Dodecahedron, Icosahedron) relate to imperfect harmonies via the Golden Ratio.</summary>

He is specifically looking for geometric justifications—such as inscribed polygons—to explain why certain intervals and planetary alignments are harmonious or influential.</meta>

...until you return to the first point, a quadrangle is formed, such as the plane of the <term>Cube</term> <note>A square.</note> also possesses. On the other hand, the octave, which is half the string, will be due to the <term>Octahedron</term>. For a line drawn to the midpoint in a circle and back to the same point creates nothing but a line. Thus, the first imperfect double harmony <note>original: "prior imperfecta duplex," likely referring to the minor third or sixth in Kepler's harmonic system.</note> will be due to the <term>Dodecahedron</term>. For lines drawn through the fifths and sixths of a circle make a pentagon and hexagon. The remaining second imperfect double harmony will go to the <term>Icosahedron</term>, because lines drawn through two-fifths, repeated until the same point, only make lines, marked here with a dagger (†). So too for lines drawn through three-eighths, marked here with an asterisk (*). 

Or should we prefer to give the fourth to the <term>Octahedron</term>, because it subtends a fourth of the circle twelve times? Which no side of the cube does? Thus the most perfect harmony, the octave, will be left to the <term>Cube</term>, as it is the most perfect body. Perhaps it is more fitting to leave the first imperfect to the <term>Icosahedron</term> because of the hexagon, which is more related to the triangular base than the pentagonal: but to give the octonary division to the <term>Dodecahedron</term> because of the cubic number 8, since the cube can be inscribed in a dodecahedron. Let these matters remain undecided until someone finds the true causes.

Let us come now to the <term>aspects</term> <note>In astrology, an "aspect" is the angular distance between planets that creates a specific influence.</note>. And since we have just made a circle from the string: it is easy to see how the three perfect harmonies can be beautifully compared with the three perfect aspects, namely with ☍ <note>Opposition (180°)</note>, △ <note>Trine (120°)</note>, and □ <note>Square (90°)</note>. The first imperfect, the <term>B-flat</term> <note>original: "B mollis," a term for the "soft B" or the minor key/flatted note.</note>, is exactly like the sextile, whose symbol is this, *, and which they say is the weakest aspect.

We have the cause (which <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer who codified the major astrological aspects.</note> did not give) why planets distant by one or five signs are not considered to be in "aspect." For as we have seen, Nature recognizes no such concord in musical voices. For since there is the same logic for planetary influence and musical harmonies in other things, it is believable that it is so here too. The cause on both sides is without doubt the same, and stems from the five bodies: which I leave for others to seek. 

Since therefore all four harmonies agree with their aspects, and yet three harmonies still remain in Music: I have sometimes suspected that it should not be neglected in the judgments of birth charts if planets are distant by 72, 144, or 135 degrees, especially since I see that one of the imperfect ones has its own aspect. Although it will easily be clear to any keen-eyed observer of <term>meteors</term> <note>In Kepler's time, "meteors" referred to all atmospheric phenomena, including weather, which was thought to be affected by planetary aspects.</note> whether any power resides in these three rays, since they [change] the other aspects of the air...
