<meta>This page continues Kepler's investigation into the geometric foundations of harmony. On the previous page, he attempted to link the musical intervals (like the octave and fifth) to the sides of regular polygons and the Platonic solids. He now addresses why certain geometric ratios that create beautiful music (like 3/8 or 1/5) do not necessarily result in effective astrological "aspects" or planetary influences.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

### CHAPTER XII. ###

...confirm the changes by the most constant experience. Indeed, the reasons why one might probably explain why [the ratios] 3/8, 1/5, [and] 2/5 sound on a string, but do not function in the <term>Zodiac</term> <note>The belt of the heavens through which the planets move.</note>, could be these:

1. The <term>opposition</term> <note>An astrological aspect where planets are 180° apart.</note> alone, the two <term>squares</term> <note>90° apart.</note>, and the <term>trine</term> <note>120° apart.</note> with the <term>sextile</term> <note>60° apart.</note>, each complete a semicircle; but these three rays have no partner for this task that Music does not utterly reject.

2. The remaining rays have an easy ratio derived from the diameter; however, the side of a pentagon, and the line subtending two sides of a pentagon, or three of an octagon, are at a more remote degree and are irrational.

3. The reason is that the trine with the sextile, and a square with a square, form a right angle. The remaining rays [do so] in no way with any accepted line. 

4. The imperfect <term>B-flat</term> <note>original: "B mollis." In early music theory, "soft B" referred to the minor intervals or the hexachord containing B-flat, which Kepler here links to the sextile aspect.</note> is in a certain way perfect, because it uses the same division as the perfect [intervals], and is a half-fifth. Thus it is no wonder that it alone among the imperfect [intervals] corresponds to an astrological <term>aspect</term>, namely, the sextile, which is likewise half of a trine. For the others are neither suited to the duodenary <note>A system based on the number twelve, like the twelve signs of the Zodiac.</note> system, nor are they part of any perfect [interval].

5. Finally, six triangular angles, four square angles, and three hexagonal angles—encompassed in two semicircles—fill every space on a plane <note>Kepler is referring to "tessellation," where only certain shapes can tile a flat surface without leaving gaps.</note>. But three angles in a pentagon are less than four right angles [360°], and four are greater. From this it is also clear why neither the octangular nor the dodecangular ray, nor any of the others, functions. 

And here I almost separate the causes of the aspects from the causes of the <term>concords</term> <note>Musical harmonies.</note>. For certainly, that reasoning which arises from angles is genuine to rays; since the effect exists because of the angle formed at a point on the Earth's surface where they mingle, and not because of the figure described in the circle of the Zodiac, which exists more in the imagination than in the reality of the thing. However, the division of a string occurs neither in a circle nor uses angles, but is completed on a plane along a straight line. Nevertheless, both concords and aspects can have something in common, caused by the same thing in both, as was said above. I leave that to be investigated by the industry of others. <term>Ptolemy's</term> *Harmonics*, which <term>Regiomontanus</term> was going to publish with the commentary of <term>Porphyry</term> (though <term>Cardano</term> asserts they have not yet been printed), undoubtedly deal with this matter. See also what from <term>Euclid's</term> *Harmonics* might be related to this.

Having previously discussed the "signs" and "probabilities" of why the five Platonic solids might correspond to the planetary intervals, he now begins the rigorous mathematical calculation to prove that the distances between the planets match the ratios of spheres nested within these geometric solids.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY</header>

## CHAPTER XIII.

### *On calculating the spheres that are inscribed within and circumscribed around the solids.*

---

Up to this point, nothing has been said except for certain agreeable signs and likely arguments <note>original: "εἰκότα" (eikota), meaning plausible or probable reasoning as opposed to certain proof.</note> regarding the theorem we have undertaken. Let us now move to the distances <note>original: "ἀποστήματα" (apostēmata), a Greek term used in astronomy for the intervals between celestial bodies.</note> of the astronomical spheres and the geometric demonstrations; for unless these agree, we will undoubtedly have wasted all our previous effort. First of all, let us see in what proportion the spheres inscribed within each of these five <term>regular solids</term> <note>The Platonic solids: tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron.</note> stand to the spheres circumscribed around them.

Indeed, the radii or semidiameters of the circumscribed spheres are equal to the semi-diagonals of the solids. For unless all the angles of the figure touch the same surface, it will not be a regular solid. Moreover, two mutually opposite angles and the center of the figure are always located on the same line or axis of the sphere. The one exception is the <term>Tetrahedron</term> <note>A four-sided pyramid. Unlike the other solids, its corners are not directly opposite other corners, but rather opposite the centers of its triangular faces.</note>, which has individual angles opposite to the individual centers of its faces.

Now, the straight line connecting the center of the figure and its base is the radius or semidiameter of the inscribed sphere, according to the last proposition of the 15th book of <term>Campanus’s Euclid</term> <note>Johannes Campanus was a 13th-century mathematician whose edition of Euclid's Elements was the standard for centuries.</note>. For the inscribed sphere must touch all the centers of the faces of the figure; and the inscribed figures share the same center with the circumscribed ones.

Since this is the case, it is easy to see that the <term>square</term> <note>original: "potentia." In Latin geometry, this refers to the square of a line's length ($a^2$).</note> of the radius by which a circle is circumscribed about the base must be subtracted from the square of the radius of the circumscribed sphere, so that the remainder is the square of the sought-after line—that is, the radius of the inscribed sphere. In the attached diagram, $HOM$ is the axis of the circumscribed sphere, whose center (at $o$) is shared with the inscribed figure. $HGL$ is one plane of the figure, which here serves as the base; $I$ is the center of that base; and $HI$ is the radius of the circle circumscribed about that base.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating a sphere with center o. A vertical axis runs through o, labeled M at the top and H at the bottom. A horizontal plane, representing the face of a solid, is marked with points H, G, and L, with I as its center. A right-angled triangle is formed by the center of the sphere (o), the center of the face (I), and a point on the circumference of that face (H). The line oI represents the radius of the inner (inscribed) sphere, while oH represents the radius of the outer (circumscribed) sphere.</image-desc>

The straight line dropped from the center of the sphere $o$ to $I$ (the center of the smaller circle) will be perpendicular to that circle and to the line $HI$. Therefore, in the triangle $HIO$, the angle at $I$ is a right angle. Consequently, the square of $HO$ equals the squares of $HI$ and $IO$ combined. Thus, the square of $HI$ subtracted from the square of $HO$ leaves the square of $IO$, which is what we seek, according to the 47th proposition of the first book <note>The Pythagorean Theorem ($a^2 + b^2 = c^2$).</note>.

<summary>Kepler begins the mathematical portion of his work, demonstrating how to use the Pythagorean theorem to calculate the ratio between a sphere that fits inside a Platonic solid and one that encloses it.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, Platonic solids, inscribed spheres, circumscribed spheres, Pythagorean theorem, Campanus, Euclid, radii, astronomical distances</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 44, Kepler is moving from a general description of the Platonic solids to the specific geometric proofs required to calculate the ratios between the spheres that nest within and around them. This page focuses on calculating the lengths of the edges (sides) of these solids relative to the diameter (axis) of the circumscribed sphere.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>C A P V T XIII. 45</header>

From this it is evident that to obtain the value in all figures, one must first seek the radius of the base **H I**. Moreover, the radius **H I** is obtained once the side of the figure to which the circle is circumscribed is known. Hence, in turn, to obtain the radius of the base, one must first seek the side of any given figure.

Assuming therefore the radius of the circumscribed sphere of any solid to be a <term>total sine</term> <note>original: "sinus totius." In the 16th century, sines were treated as lengths of lines in a circle of a fixed large radius, often 1,000 or 100,000, rather than ratios.</note> of 1,000 parts (this magnitude of radius suffices for our purpose), the <term>power</term> <note>original: "potentia." In geometry, this refers to the square of a line's length ($x^2$).</note> of the cubic side, by Proposition 15 of Book 13 of *Euclid's Elements*, is a third part of the power of the axis <note>The "axis" here refers to the diameter of the circumscribed sphere.</note>; so that if the axis has 2000, the side of the cube has 1155. The power of the side of the octahedron, by Proposition 14 of the same book, is half the power of the axis. The power of the side of the tetrahedron is, by Proposition 13 of the same, one and a half parts of the power of the axis <note>This means the diameter squared is 1.5 times the side squared, or the side squared is 2/3 of the diameter squared.</note>. And up to this point, that golden theorem of *Pythagoras* regarding the powers of the sides in a right-angled triangle (Proposition 47, Book 1) has been of use. In the remaining two bodies, there is need for that other *treasure* of Geometry, concerning a line cut according to the <term>extreme and mean ratio</term> <note>Now commonly known as the "Golden Ratio."</note>, which is Proposition 30 of Book 6. For the side of the *Dodecahedron* is the larger part of a cubic side cut according to the extreme and mean ratio, by the corollary to Proposition 17 of the thirteenth book. Thus, for finding the *Icosahedral* side, one first seeks the radius of that circle which touches five angles of the Icosahedron, which is **A C** in circle **A B**. Its power is a fifth part of the power of the axis, by the corollary to Proposition 16 of the thirteenth. Therefore, by Propositions 5 and 9 of the same book, if that radius **A C** is cut according to the extreme and mean ratio, the larger segment **A D** is the side of the decagon <note>A ten-sided polygon.</note> that can be inscribed in that same circle **A B**. The combined powers, therefore, of the total radius **A C** and the larger segment **A D** make the power of the pentagonal side **E F** in that circle, by Proposition 10 of the thirteenth. Since this lies between two angles of the Icosahedron, it will indeed be the side of the Icosahedron, by Propositions 11 and 16 of the same.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a circle with an inscribed regular pentagon. The vertices are labeled A, D, E, F, and B. The center of the circle is labeled C. Line segments connect the center C to vertices A and B. Another segment connects A and D. A chord EF is drawn horizontally near the bottom.</image-desc>

We [now] have the sides of all figures in proportion to the axis of the circumscribed sphere. It follows that we investigate the radii of the circles which are circumscribed around the bases from the sides already known: which anyone will achieve most easily with the help of sines, considering that here there is no need for highly precise numbers. If, however, anyone prefers to work more skillfully, I will set down for them the foundations of the matter from *Euclid*. Since, therefore, there are at least three forms of bases—triangular, quadrangular, and pentagonal—in the triangular ones indeed, the side **G H** [squared] is triple [the radius squared]...

<image-desc>A small geometric diagram showing a circle with an inscribed equilateral triangle. The vertices are labeled G, H, and I.</image-desc>

He explains the use of the Pythagorean theorem for the simpler solids and the Golden Ratio for the dodecahedron and icosahedron.</summary>

<margin><image-desc>Geometric diagram of a circle with an inscribed square. A radius is drawn from the center point I to a vertex H. The side of the square is labeled G-H.</image-desc></margin>

...of the sought radius **HI**, by Proposition 12 [of Book 13], which is often cited. In a square, the side **GH** squared <note>original: "potest." In geometry, "to be able" (posse) refers to the "power" or the square of a line's length.</note> is double the sought radius squared; finally, in a pentagon, the joined <term>powers</term> <note>The "power" (potentia) of a line is its length squared ($x^2$).</note> of the side **GH** and the subtending chord **KH** (from the given lines) are five times the power of the sought radius **HI**, according to Proposition 4 of the fourteenth book by <term>Campanus</term> <note>Johannes Campanus of Novara was a 13th-century mathematician whose version of Euclid’s *Elements* was standard in Kepler’s time.</note>. We now have the radii of the circles at the bases in the same proportion as the sides.

Therefore, by subtracting the powers of the [base] radii from the power of the <term>total sine</term> <note>original: "sinus totius." This refers to the radius of the outer circumscribed sphere, treated here as a standard value of 1,000.</note>—which is the length of the semi-diameter or radius in the circumscribed sphere—there will remain, as proved above, the powers of the radii of the inscribed spheres <note>The inner spheres that fit perfectly inside the Platonic solids.</note> which we are seeking. However, as I said, you will use sines more conveniently and easily.

But at this point, we must not overlook certain other shortcuts, lest we labor too fruitlessly. First, the spheres inscribed in the **Dodecahedron** and the **Icosahedron** are of the same size, provided the figures are inscribed within the same [outer] sphere. For the bases of both figures have the same radius, according to Proposition 2 of the fourteenth book. The same judgment holds for the **cube** and the **octahedron**. For the <term>axis</term> <note>The diameter of the circumscribed sphere.</note> squared is triple the cubic side squared, and this is double the radius squared in the base; therefore, the axis squared is six times the radius squared in the base. Conversely, in the octahedron, the axis squared is double the side squared, and this is triple the radius squared in the base. Therefore, here too, the axis squared is six times the radius squared. Since, by hypothesis, the radius of the circumscribed spheres (or **HM** in the first diagram of this chapter) is the same, and the radius of the bases **HI** is also the same, and the angle **IOH** is always a right angle: therefore, the radius of the inscribed spheres—namely the third side **OI**—will also be the same, by the converse of Proposition 26 of the first book. Thus, once the inscribed spheres of the cube and the icosahedron are obtained, there is no need to inquire about the octahedron and the dodecahedron.

Furthermore, in the **cube**, since the side itself is the height of the figure, half the side will be half the height—namely, the line connecting the centers of the [entire] figure and the base. Thus, there is no need for an inquiry into the radius in the base.

Thirdly, the **octahedron** and the **pyramid** <note>Kepler uses "pyramid" to refer to the Tetrahedron (four-faced solid).</note> of equal sides have the same height. Therefore, the larger the side of the pyramid, the higher the figure itself. Likewise, an octahedron and a pyramid with sides twice as large have the same inscribed sphere. For if a pyramid is cut at the midpoints of its sides, it falls away into four [smaller] pyramids and one octahedron with sides half as large. And since a pyramid has four faces, the smaller cut-off pyramid does not take away the center from any of them, as that center is far below the cut; it remains, therefore, in the octahedron from its...

<meta>The catchword "& to or-" (et to or-) at the bottom suggests the next page continues with a description of the octahedron's relationship to the internal spheres or "orbs."</meta>

<summary>Kepler provides geometric proofs and mathematical shortcuts for calculating the radii of spheres nested within Platonic solids. He demonstrates that certain solids, like the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron, share the same internal dimensions when placed in the same sphere, simplifying the calculations for his cosmic model.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

...inscribed in the sphere, touching the four original centers and, by the definition of a regular body, also touching the four new centers resulting from the section at the same time. Whether we first have the inscribed sphere of the pyramid <note>In this context, Kepler uses "pyramid" interchangeably with the tetrahedron.</note>, or of the octahedron or cube, the quantity of the other inscribed sphere will be very easily obtained through the proportion of the sides.

To these, add what Candalla <note>François de Foix, Count of Candale (1512–1594), a mathematician known for his work on Euclid's *Elements*.</note> and others have already demonstrated concerning the bodies: such as that the <term>power</term> <note>original: "potentia." In geometry, the "power" of a line refers to the area of the square formed by that line ($x^2$).</note> of the diameter **NM** in the sphere circumscribed around the tetrahedron is $4 1/2$ times the power of the radius **HI** in the base of the tetrahedron, according to Corollary 1 of Proposition 13 in the thirteenth book [of Euclid’s *Elements*]. Furthermore, there, the height or perpendicular **NI** of the body is <term>two-thirds</term> <note>original: "bes." A Roman term for the fraction 2/3.</note> of the diameter **NM**, and the power of that **NI** is two-thirds of the power of the side **GH**. Also, the radius **OI** of the sphere inscribed in the pyramid is a fourth part of the perpendicular **NI** itself, a third of the circumscribed radius **NO**, or a sixth of the diameter **NM**, according to Corollary 3 of Proposition 13 of the thirteenth book as interpreted by Candalla.

Lines connect vertex N to the base points G and H, and point I to G and H, illustrating geometric proportions discussed in the text.</image-desc>

# CHAPTER XIV.

-> *The primary purpose of this small book: astronomical proof that these five bodies are placed between the spheres.* <-

THEREFORE, to come to our main proposal: it is well known that the paths of the planets are <term>eccentric</term> <note>Paths that are not perfectly centered on the Sun; this "off-center" nature explains why planets appear to speed up and slow down.</note>; and thus the opinion accepted by physicists is that the spheres possess a thickness as great as is required to demonstrate the varieties of their motions. And so far, Copernicus agrees with our philosophers. But now, a significant difference of opinion is seen. For the physicists believe that from the lowest part of heaven...

<sig>G</sig>
<meta>catchword: of the moon</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the geometric derivation of the proportions of the five Platonic solids and provides a reference table of their dimensions relative to a sphere. Kepler then begins Chapter 14, transitioning to his main argument: using these geometric shapes to explain the spacing and "thickness" of the planetary orbits.</summary>

<keywords>Platonic solids, geometry, tetrahedron, octahedron, dodecahedron, icosahedron, cube, inscribed sphere, circumscribed sphere, Candalla, Euclid, Copernicus, eccentricity, planetary spheres</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 46, Kepler is moving from the abstract geometric proofs of the Platonic solids to their physical application in the cosmos. He is now addressing a major discrepancy: if the planets are arranged according to these solids, why is there so much empty space between their orbits compared to the traditional model?</meta>

<header>THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY</header>

...from the lunar surface up to the tenth sphere <note>In traditional geocentric cosmology, the "tenth sphere" was the *Primum Mobile*, the outermost moving sphere of the universe.</note>, there is nothing empty within the celestial orbs; rather, orb is always touched by orb, and the lowest surface of a higher orb is completely joined with the highest surface of the one below it. For instance, to someone asking what the physical "place" of the heaven of Mars is, they respond: it is the interior surface of Jupiter’s orb. In the works of Ptolemy and the common descriptions of Astronomy, this reasoning can perhaps be maintained because there was no occasion and no evidence there for investigating the actual proportions of the orbs. 

Just as no one easily contradicts those who have written about the New Indies <note>A reference to the Americas, which were being explored and described by travelers during Kepler's era.</note> if they have not personally surveyed those places, so the Astronomer cannot reject the petty reasonings of the "physicists" <note>Here, Kepler refers to traditional natural philosophers who relied on Aristotle rather than mathematical observation.</note> regarding the contact of the orbs, provided that the experience of observations and the requirements of his hypotheses have not carried him up into heaven itself and among the orbs. 

Now, however, from the hypotheses of Copernicus and from that motion of the earth, it follows that there is no gap between neighboring orbs that does not exceed the <term>eccentricity</term> <note>A measure of how much a planet's orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</note> of both orbs by many parts. Take an example of this from the orbs of the Earth and Venus—those, namely, which are least distant from one another. 

<margin>
Copernicus, Book 5, chapters 21, 22. And below in the Table.
</margin>

Where the average distance of the Earth from the center of the world is 60 parts, the average distance of Venus from the same center is 43 1/3. The difference is 16 2/3 <term>scruples</term> <note>original: "scrupula." Small units of measurement used in early modern astronomy, typically 1/60th of a larger unit.</note>. Now, the Earth at its <term>perigee</term> <note>The point where the planet is closest to the center of its orbit.</note> approaches Venus by 2 1/2 scruples. Venus likewise moves toward it at its <term>apogee</term> <note>The point where the planet is furthest from the center of its orbit.</note> by 2 1/2 scruples; the sum is 5 scruples. Therefore, these two bodies remain twelve scruples apart even when they are at their closest to one another.

If anyone asserts that this intermediate space is filled by their "deferents" <note>The moving circles in the Ptolemaic system that carried the planets.</note> for the nodes and the circles of latitudes, let him consider this: those functions can be performed by orbs far thinner than those required to fill such a great <term>hiatus</term> <note>original: "hiatum." A wide gap or opening.</note>. Nature should not be burdened with the monstrous mass of such great orbs. Indeed, by Hercules! <note>A common Latin scholarly exclamation used for emphasis.</note> all of Copernicus’s hypotheses are so well-proportioned, so fitting, and so mutually supportive that we hardly seem to need any orb that wanders beyond the path of a planet to account for its motions. 

But let us suppose that in the nearby spaces, these gaps are filled by such orbs; let us see what that looks like. Since the distance from the perigee of Jupiter to the apogee of Mars is calculated to be twice as long as the space from Mars itself to the center of the World (for Jupiter’s distance is triple that of Mars), is it then for the sake of a tiny Planet...

<meta>catchword: to ser-</meta>

<summary>Kepler challenges the traditional Aristotelian view that planetary spheres must physically touch, using Copernican data to prove that vast empty gaps exist between planets like Earth, Venus, and Mars. He argues that nature is "thrifty" and does not fill these spaces with unnecessary, massive physical orbs just to avoid a vacuum.</summary>
<keywords>Copernicus, orbital gaps, eccentricity, Earth, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, celestial mechanics, vacuum, planetary distances</keywords>

# TABLE IV. SHOWING THE TRUE MAGNITUDE OF THE CELESTIAL ORBS, AND of their intervals, according to the numbers and opinion of Copernicus.

<image-desc>A large circular diagram illustrating the Copernican model of the solar system. At the center is the Sun, labeled 'C'. Concentric circles represent the orbits of planets. The innermost orbits are for Mercury (☿), Venus (♀), Earth (🜨) with its Moon, and Mars (♂). Further out are the orbits of Jupiter (♃) and Saturn (♄). The outermost circle is marked with degrees (0, 10, 20, 30, etc.) and astrological symbols for the zodiac. Various lines, both solid and dotted, indicate epicycles and eccentricities. Key points and regions are labeled with letters: S, R, I, H, K, L, A, B, C, D, E, F, G, T, V, X, Y, Z, and also O, P, Q, M.</image-desc>

The outermost circle represents the Zodiac in the starry Sphere <note>The "starry sphere" refers to the fixed stars, which Copernicus believed were stationary and very distant.</note>, described from the center of the World or the Great Orb <note>original: "Orbis magni"; this refers to the Earth's annual orbit around the sun.</note>, or even from the terrestrial globe, because the entire Great Orb stands in relation to it.

A. The system of Saturn, concentric from G, the center of the great orb.

B. The system of Jupiter.

D. The circle or path of the center of the terrestrial globe, concentric from center G, with the Lunar small sphere <note>original: "sphærula Lunari"</note> traveling around it. Two dashed circular lines denote the thickness of the Earth's path with the Moon inserted.

E. Two small circles outlining the thickness of the system of Venus, within which all the variety of its movements is perceived.

F. The space between two small circles, in which all the variety of the movement of the star Mercury is perceived.

G. The Center of all, and near it the Solar body.

The circle passing through O and P (of which only two arcs appear here) is the center-epicycle <term>centrepicyclus: a secondary circle used in planetary models to account for variations in speed and distance</term> of Saturn.

The curved line through Q and R, passing through the perigee <term>perigee: the point in an orbit closest to the center</term> of the epicycle placed at the apogee <term>apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from the center</term> of the eccentric, and through its apogee at the perigee of the eccentric, is the eccentric path of the planet. It is not a true circle, yet it does not sensibly differ from a circular line.

H. I. The thickness enclosed by two concentric circles, which the eccentric path of Saturn claims for itself.

The curved line, or quasi-circle through M and P, passing through the apogee of the epicycle at O and through its perigee at V, is the eccentric, which Ptolemy calls the Equant <note>original: "Æquantiem"; a mathematical concept used by ancient astronomers to explain the observed speed of planets, which Copernicus sought to replace with purely circular motions.</note>.

K. L. The thickness intercepted by two dashed concentric circles, which the entire epicycle and the equant require.

The planet truly never ascends beyond V, nor descends below V.

Let the rest of the spheres be understood as described by the specific details of the orbs; however, lest the multitude of lines cause the work to be obscured rather than clarified, they are omitted here. Therefore, in Jupiter and Mars, their eccentric path, and likewise the containing concentric circles, are shown; in the others, only the concentric ones are produced from above.

**Intermediate Spaces.** <note>These refer to the gaps between planetary orbits, which the author famously associated with the five Platonic solids.</note>

T. The Dodecahedron.
V. The Icosahedron.
X. The Octahedron.
Y. Z. is the space between Saturn and the fixed stars, similar to the infinite.

It notably links the gaps between planets to Platonic solids and describes the vast space leading to the fixed stars.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER XIV.</header>

...to vary the little motions for the senses <note>The author is critiquing complex traditional models that added "little motions" or epicycles to explain planetary behavior.</note>, in length and in breadth—is this whole space, twice as thick as all of Mars, filled with such portentous orbs? What is this luxury of Nature? How inept? How useless? How very little customary for her? And from this, it is possible to see that in the Copernican system, no orb is touched by another, but huge intervals of the systems are left—certainly filled with celestial air <note>original: "coelesti aurâ"; a reference to the subtle fluid or ether thought to fill space.</note>—but belonging to neither of the neighboring systems. 

<margin>Table FIVE belongs here.</margin>

In this table, I place before your eyes the magnitudes of the orbs and the intervals according to their true proportions, just as those numbers were expressed by Copernicus. Since I initially declared that I would provide the reasons for these spaces from the 5 <term>regular bodies</term> <note>These are the five Platonic solids: the tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron. The author (Kepler) famously hypothesized that these shapes fit between the planetary spheres.</note>—specifically, why such large individual spaces were left between two planets by the Best and Greatest Creator, namely that each individual figure produces an individual interval—let us now see how successfully that has been attempted. Let us debate this cause before Astronomy as the Judge, and Copernicus as the interpreter.

I allow as much thickness to the orbs themselves as the ascent and descent of the planet requires <note>This refers to the difference between a planet's closest and furthest distance from the Sun.</note>; however, you will see whether this suffices below in Chapter 22. But if the figures are placed between them, as I said: the <term>lowest surface</term> <note>The inner boundary of an orbital shell.</note> of the superior orb must be equal to the <term>circumscribed</term> <note>A sphere that perfectly encloses a geometric solid, touching its vertices.</note> sphere of the figure, and the highest of the inferior orb to the <term>inscribed</term> <note>A sphere perfectly contained inside a solid, touching the centers of its faces.</note> sphere. Furthermore, the figures are to be judged in that order which I confirmed by reasoning above.

->Quare<-
->[Therefore]<-

But if the lunar system is added to the thickness of the terrestrial orb; therefore, if the lowest surface of the terrestrial orb—also encompassing the moon—is 1000, the highest surface of Venus in Copernicus is 847. And the highest margin of the terrestrial orb with the Moon is 801, if Mars has its lowest point at 1000. Here I would like you to look back again and again at the small table in the second chapter, namely at some kind of image of this interposition <note>The author is asking the reader to visualize how the Platonic solids "fit" into these gaps.</note>.

Behold, numbers that are closely parallel to each other, and indeed those for Mars and Venus are the same. Those of the Earth and Mercury are not very different, with only Jupiter's differing excessively; but what in...

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY</header>

Let no one wonder at such a great distance. Indeed, in Mars and Venus, the neighbors of Earth's sphere, you see how much diversity is caused by the little orb of the Moon being added to the thickness of the terrestrial orb. This little orb barely equals 3 minutes, where the Earth's orb has 60 <note>Kepler is comparing the "thickness" of the shell containing the Earth and Moon to the radius of the Earth's orbit. He uses "scrupula" (scruples) to mean minutes of a degree.</note>.

From this you can gather how easily it would have been noticed, and how great an inequality of numbers would have appeared, if these things were attempted against the nature of the heavens; that is, if God himself had not looked toward these proportions in the Creation. For certainly this cannot be accidental, that the proportions of the bodies are so close to these intervals—both for other reasons and, most importantly, because the order of the intervals is the same as that which I assigned above to the bodies using the best reasoning (see Chapter 3). For even if 635 differs from 577, it is nevertheless closer to nothing else than to this very number.

# CHAPTER XV.
->The correction of distances and the diversity of the prosthaphaereses.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'N' containing a landscape scene with buildings and foliage.</image-desc>
Indeed, lest I give you, dear reader, any occasion for rejecting this whole business because of a slight disagreement, you must be advised here of what I would wish you to remember well: Copernicus's intent was not centered on <term>Cosmography</term> <note>The study of the physical structure and "architecture" of the universe.</note>, but on <term>Astronomy</term> <note>Here meaning the mathematical calculation and prediction of planetary positions.</note>. That is, whether he errs slightly in the true proportion of the orbs is of little concern to him, provided he establishes from observations those numbers which are most suitable for demonstrating motions and computing the locations of the planets as much as possible. But if someone should strive to give more suitable ones, and correct these numbers of Copernicus so that he disturbs the <term>prosthaphaeresis</term> <note>original: "prosthaphæresi"; a technical term for the correction added to or subtracted from the mean motion of a planet to find its true position.</note> not at all or only a little, he will easily be allowed that by Copernicus.

Therefore, so that I may finally put the finishing touches on this business, and so it may appear what and how much is changed for each individual planet in the parallaxes of the terrestrial orb: I shall build a new world. Since the proportion of any <term>eccentricity</term> <note>original: "ἐκκεντρότητος"; the distance by which a planet's orbit deviates from a perfect circle centered on the sun.</note> to the radius of the orb has been previously investigated by skilled practitioners, if anything is changed in the farthest or nearest distance of the orb from the center of the world through the positioning of the bodies, it will have to be observed proportionally in the <term>eccentricity</term> <note>original: "ἐκκεντρότητι".</note>.

<summary>Kepler defends the slight numerical discrepancies in his model by arguing that Copernicus focused on calculating planetary positions rather than the physical structure of the universe. He proposes "correcting" these distances to align with his Platonic solid theory while maintaining astronomical accuracy.</summary>

->At the time of Ptolemy, around the Year of Christ 140.<-
->At the time of Copernicus, around the Year of Christ 1525.<-

<image-desc>
The page features two large, complex astronomical diagrams side-by-side. These diagrams illustrate the "positions of the centers of the eccentric spheres of the world" according to the Copernican system. The left diagram corresponds to the time of Ptolemy (c. 140 AD) and the right to the time of Copernicus (c. 1525 AD). Both diagrams share a common center point 'A' representing the Sun. Various circles (epicycles and eccentric orbits) and intersecting lines (labeled with letters like H, T, I, S, Q, R) show the geometric relationships and shifts in planetary orbits (Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury) and the Earth-Moon system (centered at B). Astronomical symbols for the planets and the Sun are included to mark key positions and centers.
</image-desc>

At A, the Sun is the center of the World.
The small circle at B is the circle of eccentricity of the Great Orb of the Earth <note>original: "Orbis Magni Telluris." In Copernican astronomy, the 'Great Orb' refers to the Earth's annual path around the Sun.</note>. At the summit of this circle, or the point farthest from the Sun, the center of the Great Orb's eccentricity was located in the time of Ptolemy; however, in the time of Copernicus, it was in a closer position. That is to say, the eccentricity of the Great Orb was nearly at its maximum in the former time, and nearly at its minimum in the latter. This can be seen in the former diagram on the left, and in the latter diagram on the right.

Line A B in the first diagram is 4170 parts, where the radius of the Great Orb is 100,000. Hence, the Earth's maximum distance from the Sun is 104,170, and the minimum is 95,830. But in the other diagram, that eccentricity is nearly at its minimum, at 32,195.

A C is the small circle of eccentricity for Venus (♀). Its radius (where the Great Orb's radius is 100,000) is 1040; and B C (in the right figure) is the eccentricity of the center of the small circle from the center of the Great Orb B, which is 3120. But A C, the eccentricity of the same from the Sun A, is 1262. Hence the maximum distance of Venus from the Sun (☉) is 74,232, and the minimum is 69,628.

D is the center of the small circle of eccentricity for Mercury (☿). Its radius is 2114 1/2 of the same parts as above; and its eccentricity from the center of the Great Orb B is 7345 1/2. But A D, its eccentricity from the Sun, is 10,270. From this, the maximum distance of Mercury from the Sun (☉) is found to be 48,114 1/2, and the minimum 23,345 1/2.

E is the center of the small circle of eccentricity for Mars (♂). Its radius is 7602 1/2; and B E, the eccentricity from the center of the Great Orb, is 22,807 1/2. But A E, the eccentricity from the Sun, is 20,342. From this, the maximum distance of Mars (♂) from the Sun (☉) is 164,780, and the minimum 139,300.

# CHAPTER XV.

The beginning will be from the maximum distance of the earth upwards, and the minimum downwards, toward the center.

Before all else, however, the numbers of Copernicus must be unraveled and particularly adapted to the present undertaking. For although he undoubtedly placed the center of the entire universe in the solar body, nevertheless, to assist calculation with a shortcut—and so as not to disturb the diligent reader by receding too far from Ptolemy—he computed the maximum and minimum distances of all the Planets, as well as their locations in the Zodiac (which retained the names of Apogees and Perigees <note>Apogee: the point furthest from the center; Perigee: the point closest to the center.</note>), not from the center of the Sun, but from the center of the "Great Orb" <note>The *Orbis Magnus* refers to the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun.</note>, as if that were the center of the Universe. This was done even though that center always stands apart from the Sun by as great an interval as is the maximum eccentricity <note>original: "ἐκκεντρότης" (ekkentrotēs), the distance by which an orbit's center is displaced from the Sun.</note> of the Earth (or Sun) at any given time. If I were to retain these numbers in the present business, this inconvenience would follow: either an error would be committed in the inscription, while the orb of the earth was considered as a solid body when it was actually at least a surface—as can be seen in the preceding Table IV—or I would leave no thickness for the terrestrial orb, as I have for the others. Thus, the centers of the dodecahedral planes and the angles of the icosahedron would be on the same spherical surface; and so the whole world would settle more tightly and become far narrower than the experience of motions and observations allow. And when I revealed this difficulty to Michael Maestlin, my most distinguished teacher, in order to find out whether he would approve of this newly proposed Theorem: with an unexpected zeal for helping me, he took this labor upon himself. Not only did he recompute the distances of the Planets themselves from the Prutenic Tables <note>Astronomical tables published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551, based on the Copernican model.</note>, but he also <margin>Table FIVE belongs here.</margin> constructed the present Table for me; and thus he relieved me—occupied as I was with not a few other matters—from a great, difficult, and troublesome labor. With the author’s permission, I share this table with you, Reader; and I commend it to you as something that will not only be useful to you in the present business, but will also solve a most intricate knot before your eyes, and even lead you, as if by the hand, into the very inner sanctum of the Prutenic Tables and of Copernicus. For it is pleasant to learn from it how the different apogees <term>Auges: from the Arabic *auj*, referring to the highest point or apogee of a planet's orbit.</term> of the Planets fall into different places in the Zodiac: which, in the case of Venus, produces a difference of more than a full third. For its apogee is in Taurus and Gemini, and its aphelion <note>original: "ἀφήλιον" (aphēlion), the point in an orbit furthest from the Sun.</note> is in Capricorn and Aquarius. It is also to be seen that the lines of distances from the Sun are far different than

# OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

than from the center of the terrestrial orb. This difference is greatest in <term>Jupiter</term>: because the entire <term>eccentricity</term> <note>original Greek: "ἐκκεντρότης" (ekkentrotēs), the distance by which the center of an orbit is offset from the Sun.</note> of the Earth is added to its distance. In Jupiter, however, the value changes little, because it is not like Saturn, which is at its highest point when viewed from the direction of the Sun; rather, Jupiter's highest point is in <term>Libra</term> <note>The zodiac sign of the Scales.</note>, where it is almost equally distant from both the center of the Sun and the center of the Great Orb.

And from this, there is also a clear ocular demonstration of what <term>Copernicus</term> <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), the astronomer who proposed the heliocentric system.</note> suggests in the briefest words in Book 5 of *On the Revolutions*, chapters 4, 16, and 22, near the end, regarding the changeable eccentricity of Mars and Venus in relation to the Earth’s movement. <term>Rheticus</term> <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus, Copernicus's student, who published the first account of the Copernican system.</note> pursues this more fully in his *First Narrative*. There is also another matter of which this table reminds us, but since it can be more conveniently discussed elsewhere, I will postpone it for now. Now to the matter at hand.

I shall set forth a fourfold order of numbers:
1. In the first will be the distances of the planets from the center of the Great Orb <note>The Earth's annual orbit.</note>, just as those distances and numbers are elicited from Copernicus and the <term>Prutenic Tables</term> <note>The astronomical tables based on Copernican theory published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551.</note> simply and without change.
2. In the second will be the distances of the orbs from the center of the Sun, which result from Copernicus's work after that resolution of numbers for which you have just seen the table.
3. In the third and fourth will come again the distances of the planets from the <term>Sun</term>, as those have been changed by the interposition of the [Platonic] bodies.
4. The third order will be from that structure of the world which takes as its foundation the simple thickness of the terrestrial orb, not including the Lunar system. Finally, the fourth will produce a thickness of the terrestrial orb great enough to cover the radius of the Moon's orbit both above and below.

### CHAPTER XV.

Now, I will further add a table of the arcs that correspond to those sines <term>sines</term> <note>In Kepler’s time, a "sine" was a line segment length within a circle of a specific radius, rather than a modern trigonometric ratio.</note> produced by the highest departures of Venus and Mercury, assuming the mean distance of the Earth is the <term>whole sine</term> <note>original: "sinus totus." This refers to the radius of the fundamental circle used in calculations, usually assigned a value like 10,000 or 100,000.</note>. For the superior planets, the mean distance of the Earth is used if their longest departures are the whole sine. These arcs will be very close to the maximum <term>elongations</term> <note>The angular distance between a planet and the Sun as seen from Earth.</note> of Venus and Mercury from the Sun, and to the <term>apogeal prosthaphaereses</term> <note>original Greek: "ἀπογείοις" (apogeiois). The differences will be placed between them on both sides.

# CHAPTER XVI.

## *A specific warning regarding the Moon, and regarding the matter of the bodies and the orbs.*

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'N' showing a figure sitting in a landscape with trees and architectural elements in the distance.</image-desc>

Therefore, the orbit of the Moon, however small it may be, raises no small concern <note>original: "scrupulum," literally a small sharp stone that causes discomfort or doubt.</note>. Thus, it is now time for me to say something further regarding the Moon. And I begin indeed without beating around the bush to sincerely explain my mind to you, Reader: namely, that I will follow in this cause wherever the proximity of the numbers leads. 

If the inclusion of the Moon makes the numbers and arcs of <term>Copernicus</term> <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), whose heliocentric model Kepler is refining.</note> more accurate, I will say that this system must be reckoned as part of the thickness of the <term>Great Orb</term> <note>The "Great Orb" (Orbis Magnus) is the sphere carrying the Earth in its annual path around the Sun.</note>. But if, by casting out the Moon, we can better agree with Copernicus, then I will say that the Great Orb is not so thick all the way around that it covers the entire lunar heaven. Instead, I would say it protrudes sometimes upward and sometimes downward, so that the entire hemisphere of the Moon is either above or below the margins of the Great Orb—and sometimes, indeed mostly, less than a hemisphere stands out—entirely according as the body of the Earth itself

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the celestial spheres, specifically moving into the philosophical and physical relationship between the Earth and the Moon within the Copernican system.</meta>

# OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

It is a matter of law whether the center of the Moon’s sphere has either ascended or descended through the thickness of its own sphere. And by Hercules, I do not know which way the Cosmographic or even Metaphysical reasons more strongly incline. Indeed, it seems a fitting arrangement that there should not be any sphere in the heavens that bears such a knot, like a gem in a ring, whose protrusion would prevent the sphere from maintaining its most absolute roundness. But on the other hand, in assessing the figure of the sphere, what point is there in taking the Moon into account, since it does not properly belong to the Earth’s sphere in the same way as the wanderings of the other planets into the heights and depths—which are most conveniently demonstrated physically through <term>epicycles</term> <note>Small circles whose centers move around the circumference of larger circles; used in pre-Keplerian astronomy to explain complex planetary motions.</note>—belong, I say, to each of their spheres? 

For it is the Earth to whom that third sphere from the Sun is owed; she herself, by her own rowing <note>original: "remigio" — a favorite metaphor of Kepler’s for the Earth's active movement through space</note> among the other planets, circles the Sun; she performs her variations through herself and through her own epicycles, using no service of the Moon for this purpose, as the teachings of Copernicus show. The Moon, however, occupies this tiny little cottage around the Earth, as if by prayerful request or as a tenant. The Moon follows, or rather is dragged, wherever the Earth walks and with whatever variation. Imagine the Earth standing still: the Moon would never find its way around the Sun, let alone complete a circuit. 

For it runs to and fro, enclosed in narrow spaces around the Earth as a servant of light and moisture to the Earth, just like a <term>steward</term> <note>original: "Atriensis" — a servant who managed the household or hall</note> around a master, or like those who walk about on a ship, yet do not advance in their journey by their own fatigue unless the great force of the waters carries them forward, even while they are at rest. And just as the Moon was allotted its space and motion from the Earth's sphere, so too has it acquired many conditions of the terrestrial globe—namely, continents, seas, mountains, air, or things corresponding to these in some way—as Mästlin <note>Michael Mästlin (1550–1631), Kepler’s professor and an early supporter of the heliocentric theory.</note> proves with many conjectures, and I have some of my own. For this reason alone, Copernicus seems more plausible, for he grants the same fellowship of place and motion to these two bodies. 

And certainly the <term>human-loving</term> <note>original Greek: "φιλάνθρωπος" (philanthrōpos)</note> Creator seems to have finally clothed the Earth with this lunar sphere because He wanted to attribute to it a position similar to the position of the Sun: so that if she herself were the center of some sphere (just as the Sun is the center of all things), she could be regarded as a kind of Sun; because of this, she was commonly held to be the common center of the whole universe.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There is altogether, to play once again with an **Allegory**, a certain man who is like a God in the world, and the Earth is his domicile; just as God, if any <insert>bodily</insert>... <meta>The text cuts off here; the catchword "poreum" suggests the next word is "corporeum" (bodily).</meta>

<summary>Kepler argues that the Moon is not an independent planet but a "servant" to the Earth, sharing its motion and physical characteristics. He suggests the Creator gave Earth a satellite so it would possess a solar-like status as a center of motion, justifying why humanity long mistook Earth for the center of the universe.</summary>

### CHAPTER XVI.

[cor]poreal, certainly the Sun is that inaccessible light. Therefore, just as man responds to God, so the Earth must respond to the Sun. The proof of this matter is that the proportion of the Earth's globe to the orbit of the Moon is nearly the same as the proportion of the Solar globe to the average distance <note>original: "digressionem" — referring to the elongation or distance Mercury travels from the Sun</note> of Mercury from the Sun.

Nor indeed should it be feared that the lunar orbits might be crushed or destroyed by the proportions of neighboring bodies if they are not hidden and enclosed within the sphere itself. For it is absurd and monstrous to place these bodies in the heavens clothed in some kind of material that does not allow passage to another body. <margin>Truth 6</margin> Certainly, many do not fear to doubt whether such "Adamantine spheres" <note>An ancient concept that the planets were carried on hard, transparent, unbreakable crystal shells.</note> exist in the heavens at all; or whether the stars are transported through the fields and ethereal air, free from the shackles of spheres, by a certain divine power, with an intellect governing their courses through the proportions of Geometry. Truly, no weight will make the mover's steps uncertain or stumbling, so that it might ever wander from its circle. For no point, no center, is heavy. Indeed, all things follow a center of the same nature as the body. Nor does a center acquire weight from the fact that it attracts others to itself, or is desired by them—no more than a Magnet becomes heavier while it is actually pulling iron. 

Or this very Earth, which we (along with **Copernicus**) have determined is being carried—by what levers, what chains, what celestial Adamant is it fixed into its orbit? Namely, by that which all humans all around on the surface of the Earth breathe (fermented and mixed with vapors): the air, which we penetrate with our hands and bodies, yet we do not split it apart or move it aside, since it is the vehicle for celestial influences into the intermediate bodies. For this is the heaven in which we live, move, and are—ourselves and all worldly bodies. 

Though why is there need for so many words? For even if the little orbit of the Moon protrudes above the Earth's sphere: what is there about the <term>Dodecahedron</term> <note>A twelve-sided Platonic solid composed of pentagons; Kepler used these shapes to explain the spacing between planetary orbits.</note> or <term>Icosahedron</term> <note>A twenty-sided Platonic solid composed of triangles.</note> that would prohibit its passage? You saw above in Chapter XI where the plane of the <term>Zodiac</term> <note>The path the sun and planets appear to follow through the stars.</note> cuts these two bodies, that no angle and no center of a face is encountered. Instead, there exists from that section a "decangulum" <note>A ten-sided figure.</note> on both sides, for which the perpendicular line falling from the center to the side is far greater in the <term>Dodecahedron</term> (relative to the radius of the inscribed sphere) and far shorter in the <term>Icosahedron</term> (relative to the radius of the circumscribed sphere). And it is so long indeed that not only that little lunar path, but something far greater extending above the orbit could pass through that middle way and between those decagons.

# CHAPTER XVII.

->*Another note regarding Mercury.*<-

You will wonder at this even more—since I promised that I wanted to inscribe the planets within the [Platonic] solids themselves—why I have not inscribed Mercury within the **Octahedron**, but instead allowed it to wander in a certain circle beyond the inscribable sphere, extending to the width of the Octahedral square. For above, in chapters 13 and 14, instead of 577 (the number of the inscribed sphere), I used 707, the number of the circle inscribed in a square. <note>In Kepler’s model, these numbers represent ratios of the planetary spheres; 577 relates to the sphere touching the faces of an octahedron, while 707 relates to a larger circle passing through its square cross-section.</note> I will state the reason. First, because its distance <note>original: "digressio" — the elongation or orbital distance from the Sun</note> from the Sun is so great that it could by no means suffer such narrow confinement. Second, because both the Octahedron among the solids and the motion of Mercury among the planets share something peculiar and common with one another. For only in an **Octahedron** stood upright upon a vertex <note>original: "super angulum erecto"</note> does it happen that a square with straight sides shows a path for a larger circle to pass through the middle than the inscribed sphere would allow. This happens in no other solid, no matter how it is rotated. For in all others, the sides will always pass through the middle in a transverse and obstructed way.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a circular cross-section with an inscribed diamond shape representing an octahedron. The vertical axis is labeled X at the top and H at the bottom. A central horizontal line contains points Q, P, O, and S. An inner square or diamond is defined by points R, V, T, and I. A circle is drawn through the points R, V, T, and I, and another larger circle or arc passes through Q and S, extending beyond the inner circle.</image-desc>

In this diagram, the four outermost lines are the four perpendiculars of as many planes in the Octahedron. **R, I, T, V** are the centers of those planes, determining the size of the inscribed sphere, of which you see the "Great Circle" here. If this sphere is understood to be rotated upon the points at **X** and **H** (the two vertices <note>original: "angulos"</note> of the figure), it will find in the **P** quadrant from the poles all around a certain width greater than the semi-diameter of the sphere **OI** or **OT**—namely, **OQ**. The difference between them is **PQ**. And such is the width of the circle which, running out beyond the sphere like some Horizon in an **armillary sphere**, <note>A model of the celestial sphere consisting of a spherical framework of rings centered on Earth or the Sun.</note>

<meta>catchword: armillary,</meta>

<summary>Kepler explains why Mercury's orbit does not fit perfectly within the sphere inscribed in an octahedron, arguing that the solid's unique geometry allows for a wider circular path that accommodates the planet's observed movements.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Octahedron, Johannes Kepler, Copernicus, geometry, orbital distance, armillary sphere, Platonic solids</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Mercury’s unique placement within the geometric model of the universe. The previous page ended midsentence, describing how a square cross-section within an upright octahedron provides a path for a larger circle to pass through.</meta>

h the middle of the **Octahedron**. For *q* and *s* are the midpoints of two sides, and thus they are closest to the orbit.

In the same way, if a certain animate planet were ordered to run through the middle of the **Octahedron**, and to observe two angles as poles and the width of the inscribed [square] as its course: by Hercules, it would be no wonder if, enticed by that spaciousness where no boundaries blocked it throughout its whole circuit, it were to stray off course at times—just like that famous **Phaethon** <note>In Greek mythology, Phaethon lost control of the Sun-chariot, veering off the proper path through the heavens.</note>—until such a time as it was repelled by the side it encountered. What I have said in jest, the experts <note>original: "Artifices" — Kepler refers to the master astronomers such as Ptolemy or Copernicus.</note> seriously claim happens to **Mercury**. For while all other planets in their individual revolutions describe circles of the same width (for as much as they move away from the Sun on one side, they approach it by the same amount on the other), **Mercury** alone has obtained from the experts the reputation of being said to describe sometimes a larger circle and sometimes a smaller one; and he possesses this as a sheer privilege. For they say that it approaches and recedes from the center of its orbit *o* along a straight line *y z*, where the radius <term>semidiameter: the distance from the center of a circle to its edge</term> *o y* describes a much smaller circle than *o z*. 

A small geometric diagram integrated into the right side of the text block. It shows a horizontal line with three points labeled o, y, and z. Two circular arcs centered at point o pass through y and z respectively. Between the two arcs on the right side is a small Roman numeral I.

For he has been allotted all other inequalities equally with the others, and has exchanged none of them for this deviation. And while the eccentricities <term>eccentrotetes: the degree to which an orbit deviates from being a perfect circle</term> of the others all decrease—if not proportionally, then at least in such a way that the eccentricity of a smaller [planet] is always smaller—**Mercury** alone has an enormous one, namely ten times that of **Venus**, although as an inferior planet even less would be expected of it. Therefore, even if I have not yet reconciled that private inequality with this difference between the circle and the sphere—nor perhaps can they be reconciled exactly <note>original: "ad amussim" — literally "by a carpenter's rule," meaning with perfect precision.</note> as they have been reported by the experts—nevertheless, I do not doubt that the Creator looked to the rule of this shape in assigning motions to **Mercury**. Because of this, Astronomy, the views of **Copernicus**, and these very **five [Platonic] bodies** seem more and more divine to me.

Let others, who wish to, seek the causes of the other eccentricities from their own respective bodies. For since these deviations were not granted to each planet by God at random and without cause, the investigation of these causes must not be despaired of.

Furthermore, let the variation of **Mercury** be adapted to the **Octahedron**, here

this could be handled as follows. Let the proportion of the eccentricity <note>original: "eccentr."</note> of **Mercury** to the mean distance from the **Sun** <note>original symbol: ☉</note> be taken as certain; for example, because in **Copernicus** the longest distance (as you see in Table V, Chapter XV) is 488, the shortest is 231. Therefore, the mean will be 360, and the total thickness 257. This thickness would then be corrected proportionally; since the circle of the **Octahedron** grants no more than 474 for Copernicus's number of 488, the thickness in this proportion will be 250, and the corrected mean distance 349. Now see what the orbit in the **Octahedron** allows, namely 387. Therefore, the difference between 387 (the highest point of the orbit) and 349 (the mean) is 38, and double that is 76: the thickness of the orbit in the manner of the others—greater indeed than that of **Venus**, yet not so monstrous. The remaining difference between the highest point of the orbit (387) and the highest point of the circle (474), which is 87, is owed to the peculiar wandering <note>original: "exorbitationi"</note> of **Mercury**. Whether this line of reasoning <note>original: "ἐπιχείρημα" (epicheirema)</note> should be cast aside, or reconciled with the assumed <note>original: "ὑποθέτῳ" (hypotheto)</note> form of the motions in **Mercury**, or whether a new account of the motions should be established, let the experts <note>original: "Artifices"</note> consider. For the errors of this star are not so well investigated that its orbit does not require correction.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

->*Concerning the discrepancy of the equations of the center <term>prosthaphaereses: geometric corrections used to calculate the difference between a planet's mean position and its true position</term> resulting from the solids compared to the Copernican figures in general, and concerning the precision of Astronomy.*<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'S' containing a landscape scene with buildings and trees.</image-desc>
ABOVE, in chapters XIV and XV, when I seemed to be held by some evidence of falsehood regarding the distances which **Copernicus** produced—distances different from these geometric ones—I appealed to the equations of the center <term>prosthaphaereses</term> of the apogee <note>original: "ἀπογείου" (apogei) — the point in an orbit furthest from the center</note>. Nor did I plead against condemnation if my own figures receded somewhat from the Copernican ones. But after I presented similar arcs—the equations of the center <term>prosthaphaereses</term> derived from the elongations from the **Sun**—as witnesses before this judgment near the end of Chapter XV, they seemed to testify against me. For there was no planet that retained the arc assigned to it by **Copernicus**. From **Saturn**, I took away 4 minutes and 1 second. From **Jupiter**, 6. To **Mars**, I added 3 minutes and 0 seconds. But from **Venus**, I took away a monstrous amount: 2 degrees, 1 minute, and 8 seconds; and from **Mercury**, 6 minutes and 1 second. Therefore, those who wish to examine everything more exactly will think that because the calculation of the solids does not agree perfectly <note>original: "ad vnguem" — literally "to the fingernail," a Roman idiom for precision</note> with the decrees of **Copernicus** and with his numbers, all my labor has been wasted. Unless I object to the contrary, my...

<meta>This page continues the defense of Kepler’s geometric model, specifically addressing the minor discrepancies between his predicted planetary distances (based on Platonic solids) and the observed data recorded in the Copernican-based Prutenic Tables. ...I would have lost my case by an apt judgment. And indeed to **Physicists** or **Cosmographers**, whose persona I take on in this little book, I owe no account of this difference. For even if they borrow the arguments for their views from **Astronomers**: they do not recall them to calculations as subtly as **Astronomers** do; nor are they so sharp-sighted or fussy as to be moved by this slight difference. Therefore, I have won my case before the **Cosmographers**.

But although I rightly fear the common crowd of **Astronomers**: yet since it is fitting that **Experts** <note>original: "Artifices"</note> preside with judgment, I do not despair, nor do I give up victory against them. And first, I bid them hope well regarding calculation. For even if sometimes the difference is a bit large, let them remember that the numbers were excerpted from the most evident places of the entire circle, and from the meeting of all inequalities. For the discordance of the positions assigned by the [polyhedral] bodies and by **Copernicus** to the **Planets** is not so great throughout the whole circle, nor is it equal in all revolutions. And I think thus, even if the **Prutenic [Tables]** <note>These were astronomical tables published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551, based on Copernicus's work, and were the standard for the era.</note> were most certain, and these errors were most truly committed through this interpolation of the bodies: such a neat <term>argument <note>original Greek: ἐπιχείρημα (epicheirema)</note></term> could not be rightly rejected because that error would be in the smallest matters. And yet, not only is it uncertain by whose fault this difference exists: but on the contrary, there is great suspicion and many arguments that the calculation itself and the **Prutenic Tables** are at fault; so much so that it would have been a great conjecture against me if I had agreed entirely with the numbers of **Copernicus**.

Let this be the first of those arguments, that the **Prutenic** calculation not rarely fails in collecting the positions of the **Planets**. **Copernicus** indeed restored many things for us in the collapsed science of motions: and **Astronomy** is much purer in our memory than in that of our fathers. Nevertheless, if we look deeply into the matter itself, we will certainly be forced to confess that we are not much closer to that blessed and desirable perfection than old **Astronomy** is from today's. The way is long, and there are various windings toward this truth. The ancients showed it to us, our ancestors entered upon it, we have surpassed them and stand at a closer step, but we have not yet reached the goal. I do not say these things in contempt of **Astronomy**: *It is something to proceed this far, if it is not given to go further:* <note>A quote from Horace's Epistles (1.1.32).</note> but for this reason, lest anyone rashly decide something more serious regarding this discord, and while he attacks me, and these [bodies]—

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s defense of his geometric model. Having argued that his model is more "physical" than previous calculations, he now addresses the specific numerical discrepancies by questioning the accuracy of the existing astronomical data—specifically the Prutenic Tables—and the difficulties inherent in establishing planetary eccentricities.</meta>

# THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

bodies: let it insult the very foundations of Astronomy. I appeal to the observations of all Experts <note>original: "Artificum"; referring to practicing astronomers and instrument makers</note>: from which it can be seen how great a difference often exists between the true location and that which the calculation indicates, which sometimes increases to as much as 2 full degrees of longitude. Since this is the case, it is beneficial for me to depart somewhat from **Copernicus's** numbers: and henceforth it is left to the judgment of diligent observers which arcs correspond more closely with the heavens, mine or the Copernican ones.

The second argument, by which I transfer the blame for this difference onto the **Prutenic [Tables]** <note>The standard astronomical tables of the time, calculated by Erasmus Reinhold based on Copernican theory</note> themselves, is offered to me by the suspect **eccentricities** <note>The measure of how much a planet's orbit deviates from a perfect circle</note> of the Planets: this suggests that although my arcs are not entirely perfect and certain (as I am forced to admit), they have contracted the fault from the contagion of these eccentricities. If the [polyhedral] bodies were constructed upon the spherical surfaces of the mean distances of the planets, so that the same surface touched the centers of the circumscribed body and the corners of the inscribed body: then I would have no concern with the thickness of the spheres, which the eccentric paths of the Planets require. However, since that could not be done; and the cause of the eccentricities, as well as the differences, has not yet been similarly explored: it was necessary for me to borrow the thicknesses of the spheres from **Copernicus**, as if they were certain: though it is generally admitted they are not the most certain. 

For although the whole history of celestial motions is reached by a slippery path, through long and difficult observations: this appears especially in establishing the eccentricities and the locations of the **Apogees** <note>The points in an orbit farthest from the center of motion</note>. The Solar (or terrestrial) eccentricity ought to be the most accurately held of all. For the Earth is the closest of the stars to us inhabitants, and is carried by fewer motions than the others. Indeed, in constructing the world through the intervening bodies, we saw above in Chapter XV how much momentum the addition or removal of the lunar <term>subtraction <note>original Greek: ὑφαίρεσις (hyphairesis), a technical term for a reduction or taking away</note></term> brings to tightening or loosening all the spheres; this exceeds the thickness of the terrestrial sphere by only a very small portion. Here, then, is this sphere, which ought to have been measured most certainly, and was likely possible to be; *here, I say, see in what great difficulty it is found according to **Copernicus**, who himself in Book 3 of the 'Revolutions', Chapter 20, complains that we are forced to reason about great things through certain very small and barely perceptible details, because sometimes 5 or 6 degrees pass by under the difference of one single **scruple** <note>A tiny unit of measurement, 1/60th of a degree</note>, and a moderate error propagates itself immensely.* How much worse, therefore, will the thicknesses

<meta>catchword: of the orbits</meta>

<summary>Kepler defends the discrepancies in his model by questioning the accuracy of the Prutenic Tables and the difficulties astronomers face in measuring orbital eccentricities. He cites Copernicus's own admissions about how tiny observational errors in "scruples" can lead to massive errors in calculating planetary positions.</summary>

<keywords>Copernicus, Prutenic Tables, eccentricity, apogee, planetary spheres, observational error, astronomical calculation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 18, where Kepler defends his polyhedral model against numerical discrepancies found in existing astronomical tables. He argues that the fault lies not in his geometric theory, but in the imprecise measurements of planetary orbits (specifically their "thickness" and eccentricity) provided by his predecessors.</meta>

# CHAPTER XVIII.

...of the orbits, both those farther from us and those subject to more variations <unclear>of motion</unclear>. But if either that thickness <note>original Greek: πῆχυς (*pêchys*), literally "cubit," used here to mean the radial depth or "width" of a planetary sphere</note> of the orbits were most certainly <unclear>investigated</unclear>, or at least the probable causes were revealed as to why such great dimensions were assigned to each by the Creator: then I promise that I would produce from these [polyhedral] bodies planetary arcs consistent in all respects with the motions. For I believe that whatever still hinders us, following the discovery of this proportion of the heavens, from reaching an exact knowledge of the motions, all of that should be attributed to the faults of the eccentricities <term>eccentricity: the measure of how much a planet's orbit deviates from a perfect circle</term>. Once these are removed, I think these five solids will be of great help to Experts <note>original: "Artificibus"</note> for the correction of the motions, which many are currently contemplating everywhere.

What moved me to make this promise regarding the eccentricities is also this: that everywhere a dispute arises over a particle smaller than the thickness <note>original Greek: πῆχυς</note> of the entire orbit. For if you take away from all six orbits their new thickness, or assign double to each: you will see the world and all the corrections <note>original Greek: προσθαφαιρέσεις (*prosthaphaireses*); the mathematical adjustments added to or subtracted from a planet's average motion to find its true position</note> either collapse and increase immensely there, or here be pulled apart and diminished. Thus the truth lies between nothing and double, nor should it be feared that the Expert has too much license to change the eccentricities if someone attempts to fit them to these geometric figures. And so this is the second reason that can excuse me for the discord between my numbers and those of **Copernicus**.

The third reason is provided to me by the numbers of the **Prutenic [Tables]** <note>The standard astronomical tables calculated by Erasmus Reinhold based on Copernicus’s work</note> themselves, which are even now rough, and not so precisely expressed that one might not occasionally, with good leave, depart from them by half a degree. **Reinhold** indeed arranged everything most diligently in the Prutenic [Tables]. But I would not want anyone, baited by this appearance of scrupulousness, to loathe the somewhat coarse numbers in Astronomy; let him judge the matter more exactly. That minute and scrupulous care of that greatest of men [Reinhold] is either for the sake of the certainty of the calculation, or it is not necessary in the fractional parts of the numbers; for he took the whole numbers themselves, which he divided so scrupulously, from **Copernicus**, just as he found them.

And indeed, how flexible **Copernicus** himself is in accepting whatever numbers happen to come his way and suit his purpose: the diligent reader of **Copernicus** will experience this for himself. He does not reject numbers which, through different operations by the force of demonstration, ought to agree perfectly, even though they disagree by a few minutes <note>original: "scrupulis", meaning small units or minutes of a degree</note>. He reads observations in **Walther**, in **Ptolemy**, and elsewhere in such a way...

<summary>Kepler argues that discrepancies between his model and current astronomical data are due to inaccuracies in the calculated eccentricities and the "coarseness" of the Prutenic Tables. He asserts that his five polyhedral solids will eventually help refine these measurements.</summary>
