<meta>This page continues Chapter 18, where Kepler argues that the numerical discrepancies in his model are not due to his theory being wrong, but because the great astronomers of the past (like Copernicus and Ptolemy) often simplified their data or inherited errors in their pursuit of a functional system.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>

->**OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY**<-

so that he might use them more conveniently to construct a calculation; hence, he felt no religious scruple about neglecting or changing hours in time, or quarters of a degree in arcs, and sometimes even more. In some places, as in the altered eccentricity <term>eccentricity: the amount by which an orbit deviates from a perfect circle</term> of Mars and Venus, he even accepts sines that differ from the truth, simply because they point a finger slightly toward the results he desires. Many things which, by his own confession, ought to have been corrected, he takes whole and untouched from Ptolemy, while changing other similar points; and upon these, he later builds the foundations of the new Astronomy. Maestlin has given me many proofs of all these things, which for the sake of brevity I omit to write down here. And so Copernicus might rightly seem to incur blame, were it not that he did it deliberately, because it was better to have an astronomy that was in some way imperfect than none at all.

For indeed, such difficulties will occur as long as the stars run their course. To overcome them and to aspire to the establishment of science without hindrance and with the minimum of loss, as Copernicus dared, is the mark of a brave man; it is for the lazy to flee them, the timid to despair, and to throw away all this care. Just as Copernicus himself neither hides these recently listed errors <note>original Greek: σφάλματα (*sphalmata*)</note> regarding his work, nor confesses them with shame. He shields himself with the example of Ptolemy and the ancients, excuses himself by the difficulty of observing, and everywhere leads the way for others by example in despising these tiny defects when confirming noble discoveries. For if this had not been done before, Ptolemy would never have published that *Great Treatise* <note>original Greek: μεγάλην σύνταξιν (*megalēn syntaxin*), better known by its Arabic title, the *Almagest*</note>, Copernicus his books *On the Revolutions* <note>original Greek: τῶν ἀνελικτικῶν (*tōn aneliktikōn*)</note>, nor Reinhold the *Prutenic Tables* for us.

In the fourth place, that table of Maestlin’s inserted in Chapter XV provides me with no small excuse. When Copernicus borrowed the eccentricities of the Planets from Ptolemy, he suspected nothing less than this divine proportion of the heavens; so it is no wonder if someone is greatly amazed that he came so close to it. Nor did he think that necessity would one day compel us to inquire into the distances from the Sun and the locations of the aphelia <note>original Greek: ἀφηλίων (*aphēliōn*), the points in an orbit furthest from the Sun</note>. What wonder, then, if in this restoration to the life and analysis <note>original Greek: ἀναλύσει (*analysei*)</note> of the world, many rough parts are found, since the craftsman did not look at the smallest details? It is as if in a small painting, which hardly expresses an entire face to the senses: if someone were to look for the true proportion of the eye or the pupil, they would necessarily be deceived. For the painter neglected this because of its smallness, content if those things which are more evident, provided—

<meta>catchword: provided—</meta>

<summary>Kepler defends the numerical inaccuracies in his model by explaining that even great astronomers like Copernicus and Ptolemy used "convenient" numbers and inherited errors rather than perfect measurements. He argues that scientific progress requires the courage to move forward with imperfect data rather than abandoning the work entirely.</summary>

# CHAPTER XVIII.

I

...as long as they represented the values. Thus, although I approached this analysis <note>original Greek: ἀνάλυσιν (*analysin*)</note> by the best possible reasoning—driven by the force of demonstration and the nature of the matter at hand—I would not want anyone to persuade themselves that they have obtained absolutely certain numbers from it. For it is possible that this very trimming of the data was a cause of further error. Behold, there are significant signs of this. **Copernicus** attributes the cause of the changing <term>eccentricities: the measure of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle</term> of Mars and Venus to the changes in the Earth's orbit. Therefore, their true eccentricity from the Sun does not change; you have the visual proof in the table. But if this is so, it was necessary to derive the eccentricities from the Earth as they were in **Ptolemy’s** age and in our own, and from both sets of data conclude the same eccentricity from the Sun. Yet consult the calculation, and you will see this does not happen as it should. For the aphelion <note>original Greek: ἀφήλιοι (*aphelioi*); the point in an orbit farthest from the Sun</note> eccentricities also come out discrepant from each other. Let the same be said of the locations of the aphelia, because these things are mutually connected; and that is one point.

Next, it is easily gathered from a glance at the table that since both the aphelia <note>original Greek: ἀφήλια (*aphelia*)</note> and the apogees <note>original Greek: ἀπόγεια (*apogeia*); the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note> proceed at unequal rates, a great diversity of eccentricities <note>original Greek: ἐκκεντροτήτων (*ekkentroton*)</note> will emerge from this over the course of centuries. Today the <term>apsides: the points in an orbit where a planet is closest or farthest from the body it orbits</term> of Saturn and the Earth are nearly joined; therefore, Saturn's distance from the center of the Earth's sphere is less than its distance from the Sun by the total eccentricity of the Earth. When they are separated by a quarter-turn, both distances from the Sun and the Earth will be equal. Indeed, for **Copernicus**, his Saturnian eccentricity will increase until the apsides of Saturn and Earth are opposed to each other. Even if the world will not last until that event, nevertheless, if **Astronomy** were perfect, it ought to employ such hypotheses that would suffice as if for an eternal world. Yet **Copernicus** mentions none of these things, nor does **Reinhold** <note>Erasmus Reinhold (1511–1553), the astronomer who produced the Prutenic Tables based on Copernican theory.</note>. Therefore, their numbers are not most perfect, nor do they explain the entire spheres of the planets to us, in a way that we might understand how those wild motions could occur.

When these and similar matters disturbed me not a little, and I remained stuck without a plan—like one who does not know how to put the scattered wheels of a clockwork mechanism back in order—**Maestlin** <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), Kepler's teacher and a leading astronomer of the era.</note> consoled me, or rather discouraged me from these subtleties. He used to say that we cannot exhaust all the treasures of nature; that "an evil well-settled should not be moved"; and that this rupture in the human body, as it were, should rather be endured and supported by certain reliefs than that the patient should be thrown into immediate danger of his life by such a refined anatomy. For—

<meta>catchword: bore</meta>

<summary>Kepler admits that discrepancies in planetary eccentricities remain unresolved because the Copernican and Ptolemaic systems do not perfectly account for long-term orbital changes. He recounts how his mentor Maestlin advised him to accept these imperfections rather than risk undermining the entire science of astronomy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's discussion on the limits of observational precision. Kepler is quoting a letter from Georg Joachim Rheticus, the sole pupil of Nicolaus Copernicus, to explain why Copernicus did not seek absolute numerical precision in his data.</meta>

### COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

...[he] brought to me the example of Rheticus, and his attention to detail which was similarly as curious as mine, and Copernicus rebuking him on his own behalf. There is a letter by Rheticus prefixed to the *Ephemerides* <note>A table giving the calculated positions of celestial bodies at regular intervals</note> of the year 1551, which is not commonly available and helps this whole chapter wonderfully in many places; I will add the principal parts from it here as a finishing touch <note>original Latin: *colophone*</note> to this chapter. *Thus Rheticus says to the reader, among other things:*

*He (Copernicus) wanted his inquiries to be moderate, not excessive. Therefore, by design—and not out of laziness or the weariness of exhaustion—he avoided those minute divisions of degrees which some even affected, and which some even demand, such as the precision of Purbach in his tables of Eclipses. You might see some people putting all their care into these things, so that they scrutinize the locations of the stars quite scrupulously; yet while they gape at secondary, tertiary, quaternary, and quinary minutes <note>These are successive divisions of a degree by sixty: minutes, seconds, thirds, etc.</note>, they meanwhile pass over whole degrees and do not notice them; and in the timings of the phenomena <note>original Greek: τῶν φαινομένων (*tōn phainomenōn*)</note> they often wander off by hours, and even sometimes by whole days. This is surely what happens in Aesop's fables to the man who, ordered to bring back a lost ox, strives to catch certain small birds while doing so; he neither captures them nor recovers the ox itself.*

*I remember when I too was driven by youthful curiosity and desired to reach, as it were, the inner sanctum of the stars. And so I sometimes even quarreled about this inquiry with that best and greatest man, Copernicus. But he, while indeed being delighted by the honorable desire of my mind, used to rebuke me gently <note>original Latin: *molli brachio*, literally "with a soft arm"</note> and urge me that I should also learn to "take my hand off the table" <note>A classical idiom meaning to stop over-refining a work or to know when it is finished</note>. "If I," he said, "can bring the truth to within a sixth of a degree—which is ten minutes—I will exult in my heart no less than we hear Pythagoras did upon discovering the logic of his rule <note>A reference to the Pythagorean theorem</note>." While I was marvelling and saying that one must strive for more certain results, he demonstrated that it would be reached only with difficulty, for three principal reasons among others.*

*He said the first of these was that he noticed most observations of the ancients were not pure, but were adapted to whatever doctrine of motions each individual had specifically established for himself. Therefore, singular attention and industry are needed so that those things to which the observer's opinion had added nothing (or very little), or from which it had taken away, might be separated from the corrupted ones. He said the second cause was that the locations of the <term>fixed stars: stars that appear not to move relative to each other, as opposed to "wandering stars" or planets</term> were not sought by the ancients further than to a sixth of a degree <note>original: *sextantes partium*</note>. And yet the positions of the wandering stars <note>the planets</note> must be taken chiefly according to these; he excepted a few cases in which the recorded declination of a star from the equinoctial <note>the celestial equator</note> helped the matter, because from this the star's position could be established more certainly. He mentioned this third cause: that we do not have such authors as Ptolemy had after the Babylonians and Chaldeans—those lights of the art: Hipparchus, Timocharis, Menelaus, and the others, upon whose observations and precepts we too might rely and trust. Indeed, he preferred to rest in those,*

<summary>Kepler quotes a letter from Rheticus describing Copernicus's philosophy on observational accuracy, noting that Copernicus aimed for a precision of ten minutes of arc rather than impossible perfection due to the unreliability of ancient data.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Copernicus, Georg Joachim Rheticus, observational precision, fixed stars, Purbach, Pythagoras, ancient astronomy, errors in data</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the quote from Georg Joachim Rheticus regarding Copernicus’s attitude toward observational precision, then transitions into Kepler’s own analysis of planetary discrepancies in Chapter XIX.</meta>

->### CHAPTER XVIII.<-

...[truths] of which he could profess the truth, rather than to display the sharpness of his intellect in the doubtful subtlety of ambiguous things. He felt his indications would surely be no further—or indeed perhaps even closer—to the truth than a sixth or a fourth part of one degree <note>original: *sextante, aut quadrante partis vnius*; approximately 10 to 15 minutes of arc</note>. Far from being sorry for this lack of precision, he rejoiced greatly that he had been able to proceed this far through a long span of time, with immense labor, the greatest exertion, and singular zeal and industry. Indeed, he left Mercury "in the middle as common," following the Greek proverb <note>original: *relinquebat in medio communem*; meaning he left the matter undecided or for others to solve</note>, because he said he had not observed enough of it through his own study, nor had he received anything from others that could greatly help him or that he could fully approve. Advising, suggesting, and instructing me in many things, he especially urged me to devote effort to the observation of the fixed stars, particularly those that appear in the zodiac <note>original: *signifero*</note>, because the meetings of the wandering stars <note>planets</note> with these could be noted, and so forth. Thus far from the letter of *Rheticus* are those things which were relevant to the matter. 

What do you think now, friend Reader, of *Copernicus*? If he had been advised of this business, and had discovered how close he was to it with his own calculations, what do you think he would not have attempted? What labor would he not have undertaken to reconcile the celestial bodies with their orbits? And if this were granted, what harmony, what perfection might we not hope for? In which matter, what others, and what *Maestlin* <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler's teacher and a prominent astronomer</note> himself might one day provide, God willing, time will tell. Meanwhile, I would not wish for anyone to pronounce judgment against me rashly; let him bear this delay of the dispute with a calm mind.

---

### CHAPTER XIX.

#### On the remaining discrepancy of the individual Planets in particular.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'H' featuring floral scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>
These, therefore, were the matters in general that are able to lighten my case. Now let us see in particular whether anything further can be excused. Let us take our beginning from *Saturn*. In its case, a large addition has been made to its <term>distance</term> <note>original Greek: $\dot{\alpha}\pi\text{o}\sigma\tau\acute{\eta}\mu\alpha\tau\iota$ (*apostēmati*)</note>: but this nevertheless caused a difference in the <term>prosthaphæresis</term> <note>original Greek: $\pi\rho\text{o}\sigma\theta\alpha\phi\alpha\iota\rho\acute{\epsilon}\sigma\epsilon\omega\varsigma$ (*prosthaphaireseōs*); the geometric correction used to find a planet's true position</note> of no more than 41 minutes. For just as its immense distance provides the easiest cause for error in observation, so an error in distance, however clear, produces a smaller and slighter difference in the <term>prosthaphæresis</term> than one might expect. And yet, that *Astronomers* have not measured the motions of this star with total certainty could be seen this past winter alone—

<sig>I 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: reerat.</meta>

<summary>Kepler concludes a quote from Rheticus explaining Copernicus’s acceptance of minor observational errors and begins a detailed analysis of discrepancies in the orbits of individual planets, starting with Saturn.</summary>
<keywords>Copernicus, Rheticus, planetary motion, Saturn, prosthaphæresis, distance, observational error, Maestlin</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s analysis of planetary observations, moving from the philosophical justifications of Copernicus to a detailed comparison of contemporary observations against calculated tables. Kepler uses the discrepancies to argue that his geometric model is as accurate as the best available data allows.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
-># THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY<-

it was in reality. For on the 2nd (or 12th) of November in the year 1594, Saturn was seen exactly between the neck and the heart of Leo, where it ought to have been according to calculation on the 21st (or 31st) of the previous October. The difference in longitude was 37 minutes, more or less. But if the discrepancy of its <term>prosthaphairesis</term> <note>original: *προσθαφαιρέσεως*; an astronomical term for the "addition-subtraction" or correction applied to a planet's mean motion to find its true position</note> from Copernicus does not exceed this amount, provided the distance is corrected, let astronomers consider themselves abundantly satisfied.

In Jupiter, nothing can rightfully be desired. For it has a small difference, indeed less than a sixth of a degree.

However, the fact that in Mars there is an excess of half a degree is no wonder, nor does it disturb me; rather, it moves me that the difference is not greater. For *Maestlin* <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler's professor and mentor</note> testifies in the preface of the *Ephemeris* for the year 1577 that the errors of this star <note>planet</note> from the calculation cannot be confined within the narrow limits of two degrees.

Now, as for the inferior planets Venus <term>♀</term> and Mercury <term>☿</term>, although they seem to offer some advantage over the superior planets—because it is easier to measure their orbits from maximum elongation than from <term>acronychial observation</term> <note>original: *ἀκρονυχίᾳ*; an observation of a planet when it rises at sunset, essentially at opposition</note>—nevertheless, the method of observation itself is suspect to me. Although I leave this to be more correctly judged by astronomers: namely, whether in these planets they are sometimes deceived by the density of vapors and physical parallax, which neither the Sun nor the Moon escapes. Certainly *Maestlin*, in his *Disputation on Eclipses*, thesis 58 regarding Venus, affirms that not rarely its distance from the Sun near the horizon has appeared notably smaller than the true distance. How much more can this be said of Mercury, which is almost always hidden under the rays of the sun; and although it sometimes emerges, it never presents itself to our sight except near the horizon through the intervening mass of exhalations <note>atmospheric vapors or mists</note>. And although the fixed stars assist in the case of Venus, appearing both simultaneously and nearby, Mercury remains more frequently at fault, for it is rarely seen itself, and even more rarely are fixed stars seen near it. And since these things happen today, it is believable they could have happened to the ancient practitioners, however great they were. For the fact that they do not warn the reader about it increases the suspicion regarding the faulty dimensions of these planets. For this is a sign that it was neither noticed nor corrected by them if any fault existed because of it. Therefore, in reading the ancients, I think it must be especially considered whether the instruments and methods of the individual observations cited could have been liable to this error.

<meta>The catchword "Deinde" indicates the next page begins with "Then" or "Furthermore."</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
->### CHAPTER XVIII. 67<-

Then, not without reason, I fear that many things in the calculation of the hypotheses for these two Planets still remain uncertain. Copernicus (as is gathered from what was just cited from Rheticus, and below from the letter of Maestlin) followed the decrees of Ptolemy more than the necessity of observations in correcting the theories of the inferior planets <note>The "inferior" planets are Venus and Mercury, whose orbits are inside Earth's.</note>. Regarding this matter, so that he might be less criticized, Rheticus brought it up in his *Narratio* <note>The *Narratio Prima*, the first published account of Copernican theory</note>: where he advises that one must follow the footsteps of the ancients most religiously, nor should anything be easily changed until the extreme necessity of observations compels it. Therefore, because such exquisite observations could not be obtained, that was perhaps a sufficiently great cause for that most prudent Artist <note>Referring to Copernicus as the "Artifex" or craftsman of his system</note> to attempt nothing further regarding these Planets beyond adapting them to his own principles.

Regarding the fact that you see a great diversity of arcs <note>Discrepancies in the angular measurements</note> in Venus, attribute the fault for that matter—among the other things I have premised in general (which I would like you to remember well)—also to these obstacles just alleged; and you will easily overcome the magnitude of the discord with your equanimity, if you have weighed each point well. In this matter, it will be a great consolation to you: that the Copernican number is a mean between the arcs resulting from the inclusion and from the omission of the Moon. For if you stuff the **Great Orb** <term>Great Orb</term> <note>original: *orbem magnum*; the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun</note> with the system of the Moon: the **Icosahedron** <note>The twenty-sided Platonic solid that Kepler places between the orbits of Earth and Venus</note> moves Venus further away from the earth than Copernicus reported; but if, by removing the Moon, you make the Great Orb thinner: the figure allows Venus to come too close, and permits it to be larger than it is in Copernicus. Therefore, something smaller than the Moon could help the matter, if Copernicus is to be maintained.

But regarding **Mercury** so much has already been said, and more can be said: that I think you, fair Reader, even if something more were missing, would digest and excuse it. Nor does the diversity of its motion seem worthy to me of moving a great dispute. Although it behaves better than Venus: for it produces a difference of only one degree, which is a marvel; so consistently is it of a deceptive nature. Certainly this is the one planet that most prostrates the reputation of Astrologers, and throws all reasoning of meteors <note>In the 16th century, "meteors" referred to all atmospheric phenomena, including weather</note> into confusion. And indeed in predicting winds (which it most certainly stirs up whenever it is in suitable places) it often wanders by such a constant number of days: that I am nearly able to correct its circle, which is erroneously produced in the *Ephemerides* <term>Ephemeris</term> <note>A table giving the predicted positions of celestial bodies for every day of a given period</note>. Therefore, if I saw any Astronomer too anxiously...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion on the difficulties of modeling the orbit of Mercury. Following the previous page’s mention of Venus, Kepler now introduces evidence from his teacher, Michael Maestlin, regarding the inconsistencies found in the works of Copernicus and Reinhold concerning Mercury.</meta>

# OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

devote himself to investigating the errors of this planet <note>Mercury</note>, I would warn him that he might better spend that time by looking instead at the Earth and the Moon that circles it—that most luminous <note>original: "ἐναργέστατον" (enargestaton), meaning most vivid or distinct</note> star. We touch the Earth with our feet and reach the Moon most closely with our eyes; let him, I say, observe these stars instead. Let him refine the errors we still make regarding their motions and eclipses; only then should he transfer his efforts to Mercury. Meanwhile, if errors regarding the motions of the Earth and Moon are worthy of pardon, much more will errors regarding Mercury deserve it, as it is both further from us and almost always hidden under the Sun.

And here again, so that I may proceed with the chapter, I shall add as a concluding summary a part of a letter which Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), Kepler’s former professor and a significant astronomer who taught him the Copernican system</note> sent to me. I do this for two reasons: first, because he warns you of a necessary matter; second, because he confirms this chapter throughout. He writes as follows:

<margin>These numbers are in Table V, which is in Chapter XV, for Mercury.</margin>
*Mercury is so wondrous that it almost deceived even me. This is no surprise, as I notice it was also quite troublesome for Copernicus and Reinhold <note>Erasmus Reinhold (1511–1553), the astronomer who calculated the Prutenic Tables based on Copernicus's work</note>. Copernicus confesses this about himself: "This star," he says (in Book 5, Chapter 30), "has tortured us with many detours and labors so that we might scrutinize its motion." Hence, besides the fact that he records no observations of his own made of Mercury, but borrows them from Bernhard Walther of Nuremberg <note>Walther (1430–1504) was an influential observer whose data Copernicus relied upon</note>, he is also inconsistent with himself in establishing the position of its apogee <note>The point in a planet's orbit where it is furthest from the Sun (or, in older systems, the Earth)</note>. For that position which he finds (in Chapter 26) to be at 10 degrees of Libra—in the early years of Antoninus, around the year of Christ 140, according to the observations of Ptolemy—and at 183 degrees, 20 minutes under the starry sphere measured from the first star of Aries: this same 183 degrees, 20 minutes he places (in Chapter 29) at the 21st year of Ptolemy Philadelphus <note>A Hellenistic King of Egypt; Ptolemy used his records for ancient observations</note>, just as if this apogee of Mercury had remained unmoved under the sphere of fixed stars for the 400 intervening years. Yet (at the end of Chapter 30), it seems to him that it moved by one degree every 63 years; he adds, however: "provided it was uniform." That Reinhold was stuck in these same difficulties is revealed by the calculation of the Prutenic Tables, which argues that Reinhold assumed the same position for this apogee at the time of Philadelphus as Copernicus did, namely: 183 degrees, 20 minutes from the first star of Aries. But at the time of Ptolemy, it falls in a place far removed from the manifest observations of Ptolemy and the restatements of Copernicus. For there its position is calculated not as 183.20, nor 10 degrees of Libra, but 188 degrees, 50 minutes under the starry sphere, and 15 degrees, 30 minutes of Libra. Therefore, those numbers of mine were indeed accommodated to the century of Ptolemy, but they do not agree in all respects with the calculation of the Prutenic Tables, as the others do, but rather with the observations of Ptolemy. For Copernicus both retained and followed those observations, and produced the same numbers from them. However, to our own age, or that of Copernicus...*

### CHAPTER XIX.

*...I did not wish to calculate these pure numbers, because they would become far different due to the diminished eccentricity <note>The measurement of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle</note> of the great orbit <note>the Earth's orbit</note>; and because in the work of Copernicus, they were not investigated or verified by any more recent observations. I would wish, however (as you may remember me saying to you in person), that Copernicus had taken new observations, rather than ancient ones, as the foundations for these dimensions. For that is a great and enormous assumption (Book 5, chapter 30, page 169b, line 7 from the end) when he says: "we think it must be granted that the proportions of the circles have remained the same from Ptolemy's time even until now." For the diminished eccentricity of the Earth itself demands other numbers. Nor is it true what Rheticus <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus (1514–1574), Copernicus's sole pupil and the first to publish an account of the Copernican system</note> says in his narrative, that in Mercury no change of eccentricity is felt, just as in Jupiter; for it does not enclose the side of the Sun's apogee <note>the point in an orbit farthest from the center</note> with its own apogee in a similar way. To this is added the fact that Ptolemy's observations are quite coarse and partial, which ought to have been corrected entirely with more precise ones. But it is now useless to complain about these things. In your own project, however, if these numbers correspond to you in any way, consider yourself to have performed your duty excellently; and you should congratulate yourself vehemently, just as Copernicus did with Rheticus in his letter, relying on the most certain hope that soon, by the occasion of these things which you have most ingeniously discovered, the rest of the matters which are still doubtful and trouble the assembly of astronomers not a little, will become very clear.*

## CHAPTER XX.

->*What the proportion of the motions is to the spheres.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative historiated initial 'A' showing a seated male figure in a classical landscape with buildings in the background.</image-desc>And thus far indeed that argument has been cleared, by which I think the greatest strength is brought to the renewed hypotheses: and it has been demonstrated that the distances <note>original: "ἀποστήματα" (apostemata)</note> of the spheres in the Copernican hypotheses use the proportion of the five regular solids. Let us see now whether by another argument deduced from the motions, both the new hypotheses and these very Copernican dimensions of the spheres can be confirmed; and whether a more certain ratio between the motions and the distances <note>original: "ἀποστήματα"</note> can be obtained from Copernicus than from the common hypotheses. In which matter, while I build the sizes of the spheres nearest to the Copernican ones from the well-known periodic <note>original: "περιοδικοῖς" (periodikois)</note> times of the motions, favor this beautiful endeavor, gentle Urania <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note>; your honor is now at stake.

<meta>This page continues Kepler's transition from quoting his teacher Maestlin to his own investigation into the mathematical laws governing the heavens. He is specifically exploring the relationship between a planet's distance from the Sun and the time it takes to complete an orbit.</meta>

# THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY
<meta>catchword: which—</meta>

First, everyone desires that the further any sphere is from the center, the slower the motion with which it proceeds. For nothing is more consistent with reason—as Aristotle witnesses in Book 2 of *On the Heavens*, chapter 10—than "that the movements of each [planet] occur in proportion to their distances." <note>original Greek: κατὰ λόγον γίγνεσθαι τὰς ἑκάστου κινήσεις τοῖς ἀποστήμασι</note> In that passage, although the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> brings forward a different reason from our purpose—namely, the interference caused by the rushing of the most swift "First Mover" <note>The *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere in the old geocentric model that was thought to drive the motion of all inner spheres</note>—yet by another reason he still fights on my side, and in his entire opinion he fights against Ptolemy and even against himself. 

For he is pleased that an equality of motion comes to all spheres from their movers, but that the inequality of their completion <note>their orbital periods</note> is caused by the spheres themselves. Thus, any particle of Saturn might be just as fast as the lowest sphere of the Moon by the force of that equal motion; but it then happens that, having acquired a larger space, and since it is no swifter than the others, it returns <note>completes its circuit</note> more slowly. And the Philosopher could not achieve this simpler equality within the tradition of the ancients <note>the Geocentric/Ptolemaic system</note>, because they were forced to assign equal completion times to three planets of unequal spheres—the Sun, Venus, and Mercury—and thus they always made the higher one in its sphere swifter than the lower one. 

In Copernicus, at first glance, such a proportion offers itself. For of the six moving spheres, the one that is narrower always returns more quickly. For Mercury’s course is three months, Venus's is seven and a half months, Earth's is a year, Mars's is two years, Jupiter's is twelve, and Saturn's is thirty years. But if you recall this to calculations—so that whatever the proportion of Saturn’s motion is to the circuit of its sphere, or its distance (for the proportion of the circles is the same as that of the semi-diameters), you make the same proportion for the other motions of each to its own sphere—you will find that a simple proportion of this kind does not hold. Take this table as an index of this matter.

See here for me, in the mean motions (known with sufficient certainty long before Copernicus thought of a fixed ratio of distances), see, I say, the same diversity that exists between the distances themselves, derived from the <term>hypotheses</term> <note>original Greek: προϋποθέσεσιν, meaning the underlying assumptions or foundations of a system</note> of Copernicus and from the five solids constructed by me: in both cases **Mars** (♂) is the smallest, then **Mercury** (☿), **Jupiter** (♃), and the **Earth**, and the largest is **Venus** (♀); in both cases **Jupiter** (♃) and **Mercury** (☿) are almost equal, as are the **Earth** and **Venus** (♀). Therefore, victory over the old world system has already been sufficiently secured for Copernicus.

But if we wish to approach the truth even more precisely and hope for any equality of proportions, one of two things must be established: either the **Moving Souls** <note>original: "Motrices animas." At this stage, Kepler believed planets were moved by "souls" rather than an impersonal force like gravity</note> are weaker the further they are from the Sun; or, there is one moving soul in the center of all orbits—namely, in the Sun—which incites each body more vehemently the nearer it is; in the more remote ones, it somehow faints due to the elongation and attenuation of its power. Just as the source of Light is in the Sun, and the beginning of the circle is in the place of the Sun (namely, the center), so now the life, motion, and soul of the world return to the same Sun: so that the fixed stars are at rest, the Planets have secondary acts of motion, and the Sun has the primary act itself. This is incomparably nobler than the secondary acts in all things, just as the Sun itself...

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument that the Sun is the physical and metaphysical center of the universe. He is moving from a general description of the Sun's majesty to a specific mathematical hypothesis: that the "moving force" of the Sun weakens with distance in the same way that light does.</meta>

# THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

...and in beauty of appearance, in effectiveness of power, and in splendor of light, [the Sun] far surpasses all others. Now, those noble epithets apply far more correctly to the Sun: Heart of the world, King, Emperor of the stars, Visible God, and so on. But the nobility of this matter requires a far different time and place <note>Kepler suggests that the theological and physical importance of the Sun deserves its own dedicated study elsewhere</note>, and it already appears clearly enough from the *Narration* of Rheticus <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus was the first disciple of Copernicus; his *Narratio Prima* was the first published account of the Copernican system</note>.

Now, however, we must reflect on the method of establishing this sought-after proportion. It was seen above that if the size of the orbit alone contributed to increasing the <term>periodic time</term> <note>original Greek: *periodikon*, the time taken for one full orbit</note>, then the difference between the motions and the mean distances would have been the same. That is, the proportion of the 88 periodic days of Mercury to the 225 days of Venus would be the same as the proportion of the radius of Mercury's orbit to that of Venus. But now, the weakness of the "moving soul" <note>Kepler uses the term *motricis animae*—at this stage in his career, he believed an actual "soul" or spirit in the Sun pushed the planets along, a concept he later replaced with physical force</note> in the more distant planet mixes with this proportion of motions. We must therefore investigate how things stand with this weakness.

Let us suppose then, what is very likely, that motion is dispensed from the Sun in the same way as light. The writers on optics teach by what proportion the weakening of light spread from a center occurs. For there is just as much light—or solar rays—in a large circle as there is in a small one. Since it is more crowded in the small one and thinner in the large one, the measure of this thinning must be sought from the proportion of the circles themselves, both for light and for the moving power. Therefore, by as much as Venus is more spacious than Mercury, the motion of the latter is stronger, or swifter, or faster, or more vigorous than the former—or by whatever word one chooses to express the matter. But the larger the orbit, the more time it also requires for a revolution, even if the force of motion were equal on both sides. Therefore, it follows from this that a single greater distance of a planet from the Sun acts twice to increase the period: and conversely, the increase of the period is double the difference of the <term>distances</term> <note>original Greek: *apostematon*</note>.

Therefore, half of the increase added to the smaller period ought to show the true proportion of the distances: so that the sum is as the distance of the superior planet, and the simple smaller period represents the distance of the inferior planet—namely, its own distance in the same quantity. For example: the periodic motion of Mercury (☿) is nearly 88 days; that of Venus (♀) is nearly 224 and two-thirds <note>original: *bes*, a Roman fraction for two-thirds</note>; the difference is 136 and two-thirds; half of this is 68 and a third <note>original: *triens*, a Roman fraction for one-third</note>. This joined with 88 makes 156 and a third. Therefore, [the ratio of the distances is] as 88 is to 156.

<meta>catchword: with</meta>

<summary>Kepler proposes that the Sun moves the planets via a "moving soul" that weakens with distance just like light. He derives a mathematical rule where the planetary period increases at twice the rate of the distance, providing a calculation for the relative distances of Mercury and Venus.</summary>

As you see, we have come closer to the truth. Yet, although I doubt whether that practice of divided differences followed in every respect the demonstrative method which the theorem had established, nevertheless another method of calculation—by which I return to the same numbers—commands me to believe that something is not entirely hidden in these figures. For because it is probable that the strength of motion is in proportion with the distances, it will also be probable that however much any planet exceeds the superior planet in strength of motion, by that much it is exceeded in distance. 

Let the distance and power of Mars be, for the sake of example, one unit. Therefore, by whatever small part of Martian power the Earth is stronger than Mars, it will lose that same part of the Martian distance <note>Meaning the Earth will be that much closer to the Sun than Mars is</note>. This is easily done by the <term>rule of false</term> <note>original: *regulam falsi*; a mathematical method of trial and error used to solve linear equations by making an initial guess and then correcting it</note>: for I assume the radius of the Earth relative to Mars to be as 694 is to 1,000. Therefore, I say, if the size of a circle marked by 1,000 is traveled through by the <term>moving power</term> <note>original: *vi motrice*</note> of Mars in 687 days, then a smaller circle, marked by 694, will be traveled through by that same Martian power in 477 days. 

Now, since it is certain that the Earth’s circuit is not 477 days, but 365 and a quarter days, I proceed by the <term>inverse rule</term> thus: 477 days would be consumed by the simple power of Mars; how much of the Martian power does the circuit consume in 365 and a quarter days through the same path that Mars would complete in 477 days? For there is no doubt that a stronger power than the Martian one is required. There results, therefore, beyond the whole Martian power, a further 306/1000 part of that same power. And the Earth is stronger than Mars by just that much: it must therefore also be that much closer to the Sun. Namely, if Mars has receded from the Sun by 1,000 (for the distance of the superior planet is always a whole value), the Earth will be closer by 306 of those parts; and by subtracting the 306 from the superior...

<note>original catchword: *inferiori* (the lower)</note>

He then proposes a physical explanation: planets move faster when closer to the Sun because the Sun's "moving power" is stronger there, using Earth and Mars to demonstrate the math.</summary>

# OF THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

...the lower 1000, the number initially set should result—namely 694—if that position was true. But if it were false, you would then operate according to the instructions of the rule <note>Kepler refers to the "Rule of False Position," a historical method of solving equations by testing a value and then adjusting the result based on the error produced</note> and draw out the true position.

You see that from this second theorem no numbers result other than the ones above; hence it is certain that both theorems differ in form, but in reality they coincide and rely on the same foundation—though I have never yet been able to investigate exactly how that occurs.

## CHAPTER XXI.

### What should be gathered from the deficiency.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' depicting a bird among foliage and flowers within an ornate border.</image-desc>

**SO**, then, this second argument holds: it has been proved by the authority of Aristotle that the new hypotheses <note>the Copernican heliocentric system</note> are superior, because through them the motions become proportional to the Copernican **distances** <note>original Greek: ἀποστήμασι (*apostēmasi*)</note> in two ways—both by the intensity of the force and by the speed of the return. This could in no way occur in the ancient tradition concerning the world <note>the geocentric system of Ptolemy</note>. And indeed, this should have been the sole intention of this treatise on motion.

However, it is not difficult for me to guess that there will be those who wish I had omitted this final part of the work. For they will say: "If you had established the true proportion of the heavens through the [Platonic] solids, the motions would certainly confirm it. For truth does not disagree with itself. Yet you see for yourself, Kepler, how much the motions and the solids—that is, the distances constructed from both sides—disagree with each other. Therefore, you expose a bare flank to the enemy; indeed, you strike yourself, and you do not need to be slaughtered by another’s sword."

To respond to these people, I first invert the reasoning and appeal to the judgment and conscience of them, and indeed of everyone: which argument do they think is more likely—the one concerning the solids, or this one concerning motion? It does not seem probable to me that anyone would say anything other than that this fitting of motions to the spheres is quite elegant and a wonderful **handiwork** <note>original Greek: χειρέργημα (*cheirergēma*)</note> of God the Creator. Accordingly, if faith must be placed in either argument, they would support this one over the solids as a more evident matter, even though the numbers still disagree somewhat from the Copernican ones. If I have gained the Reader’s agreement on this, I shall use it for the confirmation of the solids and as an excuse for that discord, since it is many times smaller than this dissonance in motion. For if the Reader, here because of the great elegance of the discovery...

<summary>Kepler defends his theory by arguing that his mathematical model of planetary motion, though slightly divergent from Copernican data, is a more "elegant" and likely reflection of divine design than previous systems. He anticipates criticism regarding the discrepancies between his geometric solids and observed planetary speeds.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Copernican system, planetary motion, Aristotelian authority, Platonic solids, divine handiwork, celestial physics, mathematical discrepancy</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from the previous page, completing the sentence about how an observer might overlook a large error in favor of a smaller one in a different context. Kepler is transitioning into Chapter XXI, where he addresses the discrepancies between his theoretical model (the five Platonic solids) and the observed distances of the planets as recorded by Copernicus.</meta>

[a] large error he willingly ignores; he will tolerate a small error there much more easily. For that diversity regarding the bodies <note>The "bodies" refers to the five Platonic solids—the cube, tetrahedron, etc.—that Kepler believes dictate the spacing between planetary spheres</note> does not disturb the astronomical calculation at all; but that [diversity] regarding the motions introduces something slightly greater. And this is the first point: namely, the region has been set aside.

Next, since the bodies disagree with the motions, as is truly objected against me, I am certainly forced to confess that one of the two is in error. However, I believe the error can be demonstrated in such a way that it is not necessary to completely abandon either finding—neither the one concerning the proportion of motions nor the one concerning the proportion of the spheres. But which of the findings is at fault can easily be gathered from what has been said above. First, the <term>motory distances</term> <note>distantiæ motoriæ: distances calculated based on Kepler’s theory that a physical force from the Sun drives the planets</note> deviate further from the Copernican values than the <term>figural distances</term> <note>figurales: distances derived from the geometry of the Platonic solids</note> do. Second, if you compare the motory distances with the Copernican ones, one by one, and record the deficiencies, you will see a certain kinship between the deficiencies and the numbers themselves, and even with the bodies, except in the case of Mercury. Behold:

| | Copernican | Motory | Difference | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| <term>♄</term> &nbsp;&nbsp; <term>♃</term> | 572 | 574 | + 2 | Cube. |
| <term>♃</term> &nbsp;&nbsp; <term>♂</term> | 290 | 274 | — 16 | Tetrahedron. |

There is more [difference] in four of them, and less in the fifth. For out of the four, pairs of bodies are always similar <note>Kepler often pairs the Cube with the Octahedron, and the Dodecahedron with the Icosahedron, based on their geometric symmetries</note>; the fifth is solitary. Next, arrange Mercury, as it is variable, into order, and consider that something deeper than the middle thickness of its sphere should be reckoned as the middle distance—namely, as much as the thickness of the Octahedron's sphere is (which you heard above is more than the middle thickness)—and it will obtain 559 for the middle distance, not 500. Therefore, this will be the order of its numbers: Venus/Mercury 559 | 563 | + 4. Behold, in Saturn/Jupiter and Venus/Mercury the differences are smaller (namely 2 and 4), while in Mars/Earth and Earth/Venus they are larger (26 and 43), just as the bodies placed between them—the Cube and Octahedron in the former case, and the Dodecahedron and Icosahedron in the latter—are similar. And notice that where there is a large difference between the inscribed and circumscribed spheres, the difference in distances is small; conversely, where the inscribed distances are nearly equal, the motory distances differ from the Copernican ones by a large interval.

<meta>This page continues Chapter XXI of the Mysterium Cosmographicum. # THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

Since, therefore, there is a certain equality in this deficiency, and truly nothing ordered happens by chance, we must think that these numbers allude to the truth, even if they have not yet fully attained it. Indeed, something in the theorem itself can still be polished; or perhaps the theorem is correct, but neither operation has grasped its true meaning. Although I could have suspected this from the start, I did not want to deprive the reader of this opportunity and, as it were, a stimulus for attempting more. For what if we someday see that day when both these findings are reconciled? What if from here the ratio of the <term>eccentricities</term> <note>The degree to which a planet's orbit deviates from a perfect circle</note> could be drawn out? For the reason I hold so stubbornly to this theorem on motions is, among other things, that the proportion of one motory distance to another never wanders far from the whole Copernican system, but always points a finger toward something pertaining to the <term>thickness of the spheres</term> <note>In the older model Kepler is refining, planets didn't move on a single line but within a "shell" or "sphere" thick enough to accommodate their closest and furthest distances from the Sun</note>. And in this, there is also a certain equality that you might wonder at. So that you may see it, I explain to you the order of motory distances in parts where the Earth's average distance is 1000, and I set beside them the Copernican distances:

<meta>This page concludes Chapter XXI and begins Chapter XXII. Kepler continues to refine his theory of the five Platonic solids, admitting that while the numerical data (the "motory distances" derived from his physical theory of a moving force) does not perfectly match the Copernican data, the patterns suggest a physical reality rather than mere coincidence. He then transitions to a discussion on the "equant" and the actual mechanism of planetary motion.</meta>

### CHAPTER XXI.

This equality consists in this: that in the regions far from the Earth, the values come very close to the average distances; in the neighboring planets, Mars and Venus, the motory distance on both sides is closer to the Earth than the Copernican average.

You see also that nowhere is a body excluded from its place, nor is the order disturbed; at the very least, a gap is left open between the average distances to receive the body. So, if anyone wishes to accept these "motory" distances as perfectly demonstrated—though this is still doubted—he might perhaps remove the *method* of interposing the solids, but he would not remove the interposition itself. For the motory distances indicate, as it were, that two similar outer ratios exist between the averages, and two similar inner ratios exist between the average and the extreme: namely, the **dodecahedron** <note>A 12-sided Platonic solid</note> from the lowest point of Mars to the average of the Earth, and the **icosahedron** <note>A 20-sided Platonic solid</note> from the average of the Earth to the highest point of Venus. Let the **tetrahedron** <note>A 4-sided pyramid; the simplest Platonic solid</note> also enjoy its privileges and be placed between both extremes. But all these things should be judged in their proper place, for they are built from the uncertain numbers of motory distances, and intended for no other purpose than to stimulate others toward a reconciliation of these values; a path upon which I have led the way.

### CHAPTER XXII.

->*Why a planet moves uniformly around the center of the equant.*<-
<note>original: "Planeta cur super æquantis centro æqualiter moueatur." The equant was an imaginary point used by ancient astronomers to explain why planets seemed to speed up and slow down.</note>

You have just learned, Reader, to recognize even imperfect things; thus I fear less that you will reject this last and "cold" catastrophe. I wanted to report this last, both because I hold it in the last place and because it is tied to the motions and cannot be explained without Chapter XX, even though it properly belongs to Chapter XIV, as you were warned there.

<margin>See Table 4, Chapter 14.</margin>
When I submitted this figurative proportion of the heavens to the judgment of Mästlin <note>Michael Mästlin, Kepler's teacher and mentor</note>: he warned me about the **epicycles** <note>Small circles whose centers move along the circumference of larger circles (deferents), used to explain retrograde motion</note> of the superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, which Copernicus introduced in place of **equants** <note>Copernicus disliked the equant because it violated the principle of perfectly uniform circular motion; he replaced it with a second small circle</note>. These epicycles make the thickness of the <term>sphere</term> <note>The physical shell or region in space where the planet resides</note> twice as large as the planet’s ascent and descent requires. And in the inferior planets <note>Venus and Mercury</note> indeed there are other motions by which the planet is raised to the full height of that epicycle and descends to its full depth. For this reason, in those cases, Copernicus assumed an **eccentric of an eccentric** <note>An eccentric circle moving on another eccentric circle</note> instead of an **eccentrepicycle**; but for Mercury, he assumed a certain peculiar diameter through which it approaches and recedes from the Sun. Similarly...

<meta>catchword: ...ly</meta>

<summary>Kepler concludes his attempt to fit the Platonic solids into the gaps between planetary orbits and begins a technical discussion on the "equant," noting his mentor Mästlin's critique of Copernican epicycles.</summary>
<keywords>Platonic solids, dodecahedron, icosahedron, tetrahedron, motory distances, Mästlin, equant, epicycle, Copernicus, planetary spheres</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter XXII, where Kepler transitions from discussing the global arrangement of the Platonic solids to the specific physical causes of planetary speed and the thickness of their "orbs." He is addressing potential objections to his model, specifically regarding the physical space required for planetary motion.</meta>

# THE COSMOGRAPHIC MYSTERY

...[simi]larly, it is sometimes stretched much further from the Sun than any Star ever is. He <note>Likely referring to Tycho Brahe or a contemporary critic</note> thought, therefore, that a thickness should be granted to the orbs that would be sufficient for demonstrating their motions. To this I replied, first, that the whole business would have to be abandoned if the orbs became twice as thick: for too much would be taken away from the *prosthaphaireses* <term>original: προσθαφαιρέσεσιν; these are the geometric "equations" or corrections used to account for the difference between a planet's mean position and its actual observed position</term>. Next, nothing is lost from the miraculous nobility of this machinery, provided that the paths themselves, described by the small spheres of the planets, retain this proportion; regardless of what orbs they are imagined to occupy, whether large or small.

And I added what you find in Chapter XVI: that the "matter" of the geometric figures is nothing <note>Kepler argues the Platonic solids are archetypal patterns, not physical walls in space</note>; and thus it is not absurd to include the planetary bodies within the orbs in the same location. Indeed, this inequality of the path can be defended even without the existence of physical orbs. I see that the Noble and most excellent Mathematician Tycho Brahe, the Dane, is occupied with this same opinion. However, our own findings indicate the cause and the method of this more clearly.

Namely, if the cause of slowness and speed in the orbits of individual planets is the same as that which was in the whole world in Chapter XX, it works in this way: The eccentric path of a Planet is slow at its highest point <note>aphelion</note> and fast at its lowest <note>perihelion</note>. To demonstrate this, Copernicus assumed epicycles, and Ptolemy assumed equants <note>The "equant" was a mathematical tool used by Ptolemy to explain why planets seem to move at different speeds; Kepler is here trying to provide a physical reason (the Sun's power) for this phenomenon</note>. Let a concentric circle, therefore, be described equal to the eccentric path of the Planet; its motion will be uniform everywhere because it is equally distant from the source of motion. Therefore, in the half of the eccentric path rising above the concentric circle, the Planet will be slower, because it recedes further from the Sun and is moved by a weaker power; in the remaining part it is faster, because it is closer to the Sun and influenced by a stronger power. And it is easy for anyone to gather that this variation of motion is demonstrated through a small circle just as if the Planet truly moved in that small circle with uniform motion. You have the cause of this slowness; let us now see its measure:

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating planetary motion and orbits. It consists of multiple eccentric and concentric circles. A point 'A' is situated below the center of the larger circles, representing a focus or fount of motion. Points 'B', 'C', and 'D' are plotted on a vertical line above 'A'. The various circles and arc segments are labeled with letters: 'E', 'F' at the top; 'H', 'G' at the bottom; 'N', 'O' and 'P', 'Q' indicating specific arc lengths. Points around the outer circumference include 'I', 'K', 'R', 'L', 'M', and 'S'.</image-desc>

**A** is the source of the moving soul <note>Kepler's early term for the physical force, similar to gravity or magnetism, emanating from the Sun</note>, namely, the Sun. **B** is the center of the path **EFGH**, which the Planet travels, but with an unequal pace. Let **BD** be equal to **BA**, and **CB** be half of that. Since, therefore, **EF** is more remote from **A** than **NO** by the amount **AB**: it was fitting that the Planet in **EF** should be as slow as if it had receded twice as far from **A**, namely, by the amount **AD**, and ran around the center **D**. And on the other hand, since **HG** is closer to **A** than **PQ** by that same amount **AB**, it was fitting that the Planet in **GH** be as fast as if it were twice...

<summary>Kepler argues that planetary speed is governed by the Sun's power, which weakens with distance, explaining why planets move slower at aphelion. He aligns his physical theory with the mathematical observations of Tycho Brahe and the geometric models of Ptolemy and Copernicus.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Tycho Brahe, solar power, moving soul, eccentricity, equant, prosthaphaeresis, planetary speed, Copernicus, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter XXII, where Kepler is explaining the physical and geometric reasons for the varying speeds of planets (the "equant" problem). He is linking his theory of a "moving power" emanating from the Sun to the classical models of Ptolemy and Copernicus.</meta>

### CHAPTER XXII.

...had approached closer to *A*, certainly by the same amount *A D*. Therefore, on both sides, it is exactly as if it were moving around the center *D*. For above in Chapter XX, that was the proportion of motions to the orbs. Therefore, consider that the two causes which converged there throughout the entire circle are here inverted and mixed together. There, the entire circumference of that same orb, being larger and more remote, increased the period [the time taken to complete a circuit], and being smaller and closer, diminished it. Here, however, the circles *N O P Q* and *E F G H* are equal, and one part of this circle is further from, and the other part is closer to, the center *A*, the Sun. Consequently, the **moving power** <term>original: motrix virtus; Kepler's early concept of a force emanating from the sun that pushes planets, a precursor to his later thoughts on gravity</term> in *A* acts upon *E F* and *G H* just as if the planet were at *I K* in the former case, and at *L M* in the latter. Moreover, the common measure of both that slowness and this speed is found at *D*. Thus, a planet traveling along the path *E F G H* becomes slow, fast, and intermediate around *R* and *S*, just as if it were moving uniformly around the center *D* in the path *I K L M*. Now look at the master astronomers who established exactly the same thing. Specifically, **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Roman astronomer whose geocentric model dominated for over a millennium</note> made *D* the center of the **equant** <term>A mathematical point used to explain the observed non-uniform speeds of planets; the planet appears to move at a constant angular speed when viewed from this point</term>, and *B* the center of the planetary path. **Copernicus** <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), who proposed the heliocentric system</note> however, around center *C*—the midpoint between *D* and *B*—rotates an "eccentric of an eccentric" or an "eccentric-epicycle." Therefore, for him, the planet's path becomes as close as possible to *E F G H*, but the uniformity of motion is regulated around *D*, just as the intermediate orb between *E F G H* and *I K L M* is regulated around *C*.

You now have the reason why the center of the equant stands at a distance of **one-third** <note>Kepler is explaining the "bisection of eccentricity," a key geometric feature in planetary models that he is trying to justify physically</note> of the total eccentricity from the center of the eccentric. Specifically, let the entire world be filled with a **soul** <note>Kepler often used the term "anima" (soul) to describe the physical forces or governing spirits of the heavens before fully developing his theory of universal gravitation</note>, which sweeps along whatever it reaches—whether stars or comets—and does so with that swiftness required by the distance from the Sun and the strength of the power at that location. Then, let there be a specific soul in each planet, by whose "rowing" the star may ascend in its own circuit; and the same results will follow for the remote orbs.

And as for these matters regarding the **Equant**, I know some will be thrilled when they read this. For if astronomers marvel that **Ptolemy** assumed this same measure for the center of the Equant without proof, some will now marvel much more that there was a physical cause for this thing, yet Ptolemy did not even suspect it, even though he took the matter exactly as it is; it was as if, blind, he reached the required place by a divine nudge.

Nevertheless, I would like them to be warned that "nothing is blessed in every respect" <note>original: "nihil esse ex omni parte beatum"; a Latin idiom meaning no theory or situation is perfect or without its difficulties</note>. For in **Venus** and **Mercury**, that slowness and speed are not adjusted to the planet's departure from the Sun, but only to the motion of the Earth. And if anyone should use a different condition of motion as a pretext for this...

<meta>catchword: from motion</meta>

<summary>Kepler provides a physical justification for the "equant," a mathematical device used by Ptolemy, by arguing that a "moving power" from the Sun causes planets to change speed based on their distance. He credits Ptolemy and Copernicus for correctly identifying these proportions, even if they did not understand the underlying physical cause.</summary>
<keywords>Equant, Ptolemy, Copernicus, moving power, eccentricity, planetary speed, Venus, Mercury, soul of the world</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page concludes the technical discussion of the equant from Chapter XXII and begins Chapter XXIII, where Kepler moves from the mechanics of planetary motion to more speculative chronological and cosmological questions regarding the age and duration of the universe.</meta>

...from the motion of the superior bodies: finally, what cause would he provide for the annual motion of the Earth? For that motion required an **equant** <term>A mathematical device used by ancient astronomers to explain why planets seem to speed up or slow down; it was a point from which a planet appeared to move at a constant speed</term> neither in the system of Ptolemy nor in that of Copernicus. Therefore, let this uncertain dispute remain pending under the judgment of the Astronomer.

## CHAPTER XXIII.
### *On the Astronomical beginning and end of the World and the Platonic year.*

After the feast, after the weariness of satiety, let us come to the desserts. I propose two noble problems. The first concerns the beginning of motion; the second concerns the end. Certainly, God did not establish the motions at random, but from a certain specific beginning and an illustrious conjunction of the stars, and at the start of the **Zodiac** <note>The belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs, starting with Aries</note>, which the Creator fashioned through the inclination of the Earth, our dwelling place, because all things were made for the sake of man. Therefore, if the year of Christ 1595 is referred to the year 5572 of the world (which is commonly and by the most approved authorities reckoned as 5557), the creation will coincide with an illustrious constellation at the beginning of **Aries** <note>original: ♈</note>. For in the first year of the assumed number, on the 27th day of April, by the Julian calendar calculated backward, on the first day of the week—which is the day of the Creation of all things—at the eleventh hour of noon in **Prussia** <note>Kepler likely refers to the location of the observers or the tables he is using, such as the Prutenic Tables</note>, which is six in the evening in India, the face of the heavens is shown as follows by the **Prutenic calculation** <term>original: prutenico calculo; astronomical tables published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551 based on Copernican theory</term>.

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| Sun (☉) | 3 | Aries (♈) |
| Moon (☽) | 3 | Libra (♎) |
| Saturn (♄) | 15 | Aries (♈) |
| Jupiter (♃) | 10 | Aries (♈) |
| Mars (♂) | 24 | Gemini (♊) |
| Venus (♀) | 10 | Aries (♈) |
| Mercury (☿) | 3 | Aries (♈) |
| North Node (☊) | 18 | Virgo (♍) |

If you delay or advance the motions of Mars, Venus, and Mercury for a short time, they will come into related positions, and perhaps the **North Node** <note>original: ☊; the point where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> would be at 0° Libra near the Moon. **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a leading scholar of chronology</note> wrongly desires a **New Moon** <term>original: Nouilunium</term>. For the Moon, established into the power of the night, certainly shone on that first night. A more likely beginning is not supplied by the calculation over many years backward and forward. But if we follow reason, it is necessary to seek this beginning while the Sun is turning in **Libra** <note>original: ♎</note>, specifically with this face of the heavens,

<summary>Kepler transitions from the mechanics of planetary motion to "astronomical dessert," speculating on the exact date and celestial alignment at the moment of Creation. He uses the Prutenic Tables to calculate a possible birth chart for the universe in late April, placing several planets in Aries.</summary>

<keywords>Creation of the world, Platonic year, Prutenic Tables, Scaliger, zodiacal alignment, Aries, Libra, chronology, astronomical history</keywords>

### CHAPTER XXIII.

| | | |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Saturn (♄) | 0 | Aries (♈) |
| Jupiter (♃) | 0 | Aries (♈) |
| Mars (♂) | 0 | Aries (♈) |
| North Node (☊) | 0 | Aries (♈) |
| Moon (☽) | 0 | Cancer (♋) |
| Venus (♀) | 0 | Libra (♎) |
| Mercury (☿) | 0 | Libra (♎) |
| Sun (☉) | 0 | Libra (♎) |

The authority of the ancients holds that the World was created in Autumn, and reason itself—following Copernicus—suggests that the Earth should stand at the same beginning as the rest of the planets. Therefore, the superior planets <note>The "superiors" are the planets further from the Sun than Earth: Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</note> will appear in **Aries** (♈), while the inferior planets <note>The "inferiors" are the planets closer to the Sun: Venus and Mercury.</note> and the Sun will appear in **Libra** (♎). Since the Moon revolves around the Earth, it fits neither in Aries nor in Libra, so as not to disturb the triple grouping of the superiors and inferiors. And since the Sun was setting (for thus the world was created), the night is nowhere more rightly dominated than from the middle of the sky, which is **Cancer** (♋). Thus, the Moon could stand at the highest point of its **epicycle** <term>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one, used in pre-modern astronomy to account for planetary speeds.</term>. 

And because its orbit is adventitious <note>original: "aduentitius," meaning secondary or added on.</note>, let it also receive an added and peculiar position for its beginning. Its nobility and fame among men also concern its cycles, specifically the most powerful quarter of the lunations. I refer the **Head** <note>The North Node of the Moon, or "Caput Draconis."</note> to Libra and the **Tail** <note>The South Node of the Moon, or "Cauda Draconis."</note> to Aries, so that it sits in a rational position with the Moon, yet without an **Eclipse**; thus the Moon is at its maximum northern limit. Therefore, the Earth will also be visually placed in the middle among the stars, just as its sphere obtained a middle place among the spheres by the certain counsel of God, because all things were made for the sake of man. 

But if you were to place the Sun here in Aries as well: Mercury would be in Libra, and the Moon in Cancer, and the rest similarly. However, **mean motions** <term>The average or uniform speed of a planet, as opposed to its "true" or variable speed seen from Earth.</term> must be used, for it is fitting that these be true at the beginning of their course—namely, from their **apsides** <term>The points in an orbit where a planet is closest to or furthest from the body it orbits.</term>. This is the prize <note>original: "palma," literally the palm branch of victory.</note> set in our midst; if anyone attains this, or something like it, either by calculation or by the restoration of Astronomy, he alone "shall have Phyllis" <note>A literary allusion to Virgil’s Eclogues, meaning he will win the ultimate prize.</note>. So much for the beginning.

I have established with reason that there is no end to motion, and I will prove by a single postulate that there will be no **Platonic Year** <term>A "Great Year," the hypothetical cycle after which all celestial bodies return to the exact same positions relative to one another.</term>. For let it be granted that the **eccentricity** <note>The measure of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</note> is in a rational proportion with the sphere; therefore, the radii of the spheres will be mutually irrational to one another, because they relate as bodies inscribed within and circumscribed around the [Platonic] solids. These are irrational because they follow from the ratio of the diagonal of a square, and the section according to the extreme and...

<sig>L 2</sig>
<meta>The catchword "medi-" at the bottom suggests the next page continues with "medium ratio," referring to the Golden Ratio or "mean and extreme ratio."</meta>

<summary>Kepler proposes an ideal astronomical configuration for the moment of Creation, placing the planets in specific signs like Aries and Libra. He then begins a mathematical argument against the "Platonic Year," claiming that because planetary orbits are based on the geometry of the five Platonic solids, their relationships are irrational and will never perfectly repeat.</summary>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page concludes Kepler’s argument against the "Platonic Year"—the ancient idea that the universe eventually returns to its exact starting configuration—and transitions into a poetic and prayerful conclusion to the work.</meta>

# THE MYSTERY OF THE COSMOS

mean proportion, which are two examples of irrationals in Geometry. Now, however, the motions are in proportion with the rays <note>By "rays," Kepler refers to the semi-diameters or radii of the planetary orbits.</note>; therefore, the motions are irrational relative to one another, and thus they will never return to the same beginning, even if they were to last for infinite ages. This is because never, not even in an infinite division of time, would a common measure be found which, being repeated often enough, would establish a single terminal point and the goal of a **Platonic Year** <term>A theoretical cycle in which all heavenly bodies return to the same positions they held at the moment of creation; Kepler argues here that because planetary speeds are mathematically "irrational," they can never perfectly sync up.</term> for all motions. And now at last, it is pleasing to cry out with the divine Copernicus: *Truly, so great is this divine fabric of the **Best and Greatest** <note>original: "Opt. Max.," an abbreviation for "Optimus Maximus," a title for God borrowed from the Roman title for Jupiter.</note> Builder;* and with Pliny: *The world is sacred, immense, whole in the whole, or rather it is the whole itself, finite and yet like the infinite.*

*Now you, friend Reader, do not forget the end of all these things, which is the knowledge, admiration, and veneration of the most Wise Maker. For it is nothing to have proceeded from the eyes to the mind, from sight to contemplation, and from the visible course to the most profound counsel of the Creator, if you wish to rest here; and not, with one impulse and with all the devotion of your soul, raise yourself upward to the knowledge, love, and worship of the Creator. Therefore, purely and with a grateful heart, sing along with me the following Hymn to the Architect of this most perfect work.*

> *Jehovah, Sower of the World, and our eternal power,*
> *How great is your fame through all the circle of the lands?*
> *How great is your glory? Which, set above the walls*
> *Of heaven, flies admirable with shaken wings.*
> *The child recognizes you, and having rejected the full breast,*
> *Builds strong arguments with stammering lips at your dictation:*
> *Arguments by which the swollen enemy is confounded,*
> *The contemner of you, and the contemner of right and justice.*
> *But I, so that I may believe the Divinity in the spacious orb,*
> *Shall look up astonished at the structures of the vast heaven,*
> *The work of the Great Artist, the miracles of his strong right hand;*
> *How you have distinguished the five starry spheres by rules,*
> *Within which the Minister of Light and Soul <note>Kepler refers here to the Sun, positioned at the center of his system.</note> stands in the middle,*
> *By what law he moderates the reins of the eternal course,*

<meta>This page concludes the first edition of Kepler’s "Mysterium Cosmographicum." It transitions from the technical argument about the impossibility of a Platonic Year into a poetic doxology, or hymn of praise. Kepler draws heavily on the imagery of Psalm 8 to celebrate the human capacity to understand the heavens.</meta>

### CHAPTER XXIV.

*What changes the varied Moon undergoes, what labors <note>original: "labores"; in a classical context, this refers to the "struggles" or eclipses of the moon.</note> she endures,*
*And how many Stars you have scattered across the immense field of heaven.*

*O Greatest Builder <note>original: "Opifex"</note> of the World, by what reason did*
*The small, helpless, humble, and dweller of such a tiny clod of earth,*
*The Son of Adam <note>original: "Adamides"; a poetic way of referring to humanity.</note>, compel you to take up the care of his affairs?*
*You look upon the undeserving, you carry him on high; he is*
*All but the race of the Gods, so many honors you bestow,*
*And you encircle his magnificent head with a crown, and establish him*
*As King over the monuments of your own hands.*
*That which is above his head—the great orbs and their motions—*
*You subject to his intellect <note>Kepler emphasizes here that God gave humans the mathematical ability to understand the complex movements of the planets.</note>: whatever is created on the Earth <term>Tellure: The Latin name for the Roman goddess of the Earth, often used poetically to mean the world itself.</term>,*
*The cattle born for labor, and those fit for smoking altars,*
*And the generations of other wild beasts that inhabit the forests,*
*And the race of birds, which beats the air with light wings,*
*And the fish which swim through the stretches of the sea and the rivers,*
*All these you command him to rule with his power and mighty right hand.*

*Jehovah, sower of the World, and our eternal power,*
*How great is your fame through the whole circle of the earth?*

->THE END<-
