# ON THE BOOKS OF THE RE-
# VOLUTIONS OF THE MOST LEARNED
## MAN AND MATHEMATICIAN,
->the most excellent and Reverend Master Doctor Nicolaus Copernicus of<-
->Toruń, Canon of Warmia <note>original: "Vuarmacienſis"</note>, the First Account addressed to<-
->the most distinguished Master Johannes Schöner, by<-
->Master Georg Joachim<-
->Rheticus,<-

### TOGETHER WITH A PRAISE OF
->Prussia, written herein.<-

### ALCINOUS. <note>Alcinous was a 2nd-century philosopher; this quote emphasizes the need for intellectual independence when exploring new scientific truths.</note>
> ->"He who intends to philosophize must be free in his mind."<-
> -><note>original Greek: "Δεῖ δ' ἐλευθέριον εἶναι τῇ γνώμῃ τὸν μέλλοντα φιλοσοφεῖν."</note><-

## GEORG VÖGELIN,
## PHYSICIAN, TO THE READER.

> ->This little book contains things unknown to the men of old,<-
> ->and wonders for the minds of our own time.<-
> ->For by a new reasoning the order of the stars is sought,<-
> ->and the Earth now runs, though believed to stand still before.<-
> ->Let learned Antiquity be celebrated for the arts it discovered,<-
> ->but let not praise and honor be lacking for these new studies.<-
> ->These findings do not fear the judgment or the refinement<-
> ->of a skilled mind; only envy <note>original: "liuor"</note> can stand in the way.<-
> ->But let envy be gone; let these things be approved even by a few.<-
> ->It will be enough if they have pleased learned men.<-

## IN THE YEAR 1596.

<summary>This is the title page of the Narratio Prima, the first published summary of Copernicus’s revolutionary sun-centered solar system, written by his disciple Rheticus. It includes a poem by Georg Vögelin defending the "new" idea of a moving Earth against the weight of ancient tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Copernicus, Georg Joachim Rheticus, Narratio Prima, heliocentrism, astronomy, Toruń, Warmia, Johannes Schöner, Alcinous, scientific revolution</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of a preface written by Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), a celebrated mathematician and the university professor who introduced Johannes Kepler to the Copernican system. This preface introduces Kepler’s "Mysterium Cosmographicum" (The Secret of the Universe), which was often published alongside Rheticus’s "Narratio Prima" to provide a complete defense of the new sun-centered model of the universe.</meta>

# MASTER MICHAEL MAEST-
## LIN OF GOEPPINGEN,
### Greetings to the Honest Reader.

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut decorative initial 'R' containing detailed foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>**GEOMETRY AND Arithmetic are rightly called the Wings of Astronomy by Plato.** <note>original: "Alæ Astronomiæ"</note> For Geometry, working within the celestial body—which is a Magnitude <note>original: "Quantum"</note> that is spherical and moves regularly in a circular motion—does more than just find the paths of the stars from observations made at various times. It also explains the reasons for their apparent irregularities based on their regular movements, reveals their certain measurements, and demonstrates the sizes and heights of those sublime bodies. Arithmetic, moreover, by casting the parts of this physical Magnitude into numbers, measures those sizes and heights and constructs the tables from which the locations of all the stars, and indeed the entire face of the heavens, can be displayed for any given time. Although these matters are wonderful and difficult, our exceptional mathematician, **MASTER JOHANNES KEPLER**, teaches us to fly toward much higher things on the rowing-strokes of these same wings. Truly, the things that skilled astronomers have found up to this point are great; yet, until now, all astronomers have approached Astronomy only "from behind" <note>This refers to the "a posteriori" method: starting with observations and trying to work backward to find the cause or the math.</note>, and have taught how to track the movements as well as the sizes and distances from observations alone. However, whether any access to measuring these things "from the front" <note>The "a priori" method: starting from fundamental principles or laws to determine how the universe must be built.</note> stood open—or whether there could be any other geometric Rule <note>original: "Norma"</note> besides observations for examining the discovered numbers of movements and quantities—has not occurred even in a dream to any craftsman, not even the most experienced, until now. But now, our Kepler, by a most skillful discovery based on Geometry, provides a certain and finite number and order of the celestial circles or spheres. Most importantly, he provides the certain proportion of their sizes and movements relative to one another. Taking his starting point from a slightly higher level, he shows that God, the Creator, Greatest and Best, in the creation of the World, built, extended, arranged, adorned, and ordered the moving celestial spheres according to the proportion of the five regular geometric Bodies <note>Today these are known as the "Platonic Solids": the tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron.</note>, which are otherwise very well known to all Geometers. And he confirms this opinion not with logical games, nor with light, doubtful, or "old wives' tales" <note>original: "anilibus"</note>, much less with foreign conjectures violently forced to fit his purpose. Rather, he confirms it with genuine and most appropriate reasonings drawn from both the Nature of things and from Geometry, which cannot be contradicted. The most powerful of these is the most elegant and sweet harmony and the consistent agreement of the astronomical calculation (already produced from observations) with the distances <note>original: "diastematis"</note> of the five regular Bodies. For the planetary spheres are positioned in an order with such great gaps between them as the calculations clearly indicate—at least as far as has been provided or could be provided until now by those who realize that many things are still lacking in observations. These gaps match the specific distances between the spheres that circumscribe and the spheres that are inscribed within the Cube (or Hexahedron), the Pyramid (or Tetrahedron), the Dodecahedron, the Icosahedron, and the Octahedron. From this there- <note>The text ends mid-word with "igi-", the start of "igitur" (therefore).</note>

<summary>Michael Maestlin introduces Johannes Kepler’s groundbreaking theory that the spacing of the planets is determined by the five Platonic solids, moving astronomy from mere observation to a structural "a priori" science. He argues that this geometric arrangement reflects God's architectural plan for the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Maestlin, Johannes Kepler, Platonic Solids, Geometry, Arithmetic, Astronomy, a priori, celestial spheres, planetary distances, harmony of the spheres</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Michael Maestlin. He is defending the Copernican system against critics who find it "absurd" or "un-physical." Maestlin argues that traditional physics—based on the idea that heavy things fall to the center of the universe—is flawed because our experience of gravity is limited only to our tiny "speck" of an Earth, not the entire vastness of the cosmos.</meta>

-># PREFACE.<-

...at any time, whoever wishes to inquire more fully into the motions of the heavens and to repair and restore those things which are still lacking in astronomy, now has an open door from the start <note>original: "a priori"</note> through which he may enter; he has the most correct rule by which, as if by a touchstone <note>original: "Lydium lapidem" — a stone used to test the purity of gold and silver; a metaphor for a standard of truth.</note>, he may examine all his observations and his entire calculation. Justly, therefore, I congratulate our century on this most ingenious discovery of the most excellent mathematician **Kepler**, doubting not that through it we shall shortly see all of Astronomy restored.

But if anyone is offended—as not a few have been until now—by the "absurdity" of **Copernicus's** hypotheses, which have been unlawfully condemned by many and defamed beyond reason; and by the fact that **Kepler**, by this discovery of his, asserts along with **Copernicus** the immobility of the fixed stars at the edge and of the Sun in the center of the World <note>original: "Mundi" — in this period, "the world" often referred to the entire universe or cosmos.</note>, as well as the circular mobility of the Earth outside the middle: I ask that such a person first understand and examine the matter before passing judgment with premature prejudice. Let him read what **Copernicus** writes in Book I, Chapter 5 and the five following chapters; likewise what our **Kepler** writes in Chapter 1 of his *Forerunner* <note>original: "Prodromi" — referring to Kepler's "Mysterium Cosmographicum," which had the word "Prodromus" in its full title.</note>; also what **Rheticus** has in the following *Narration* <note>The "Narratio Prima," the first published summary of Copernican theory.</note>, where he enumerates the principal reasons why it was necessary to depart from the hypotheses of the ancient astronomers. And he will see: that the question concerning the location and perpetual rest of the Earth is by no means settled. To which I add this.

In the commonly used hypotheses, which prevail more by long-standing custom <note>original: "praescriptione" — referring to legal or traditional usage over time.</note> than by reason (therefore it is better and more advisable that they be proposed publicly, and that beginners <note>original: "tyrones"</note> be first instructed in them as things commonly known and for that very reason easier to learn: so too it is generally expedient to retain them in other disputes, unless one must enter into the inner sanctums of astronomy), the Earth is established as resting in the center. This is based on a very powerful argument taken from the weights of gravity and levity, because heavy things are said to be carried downward toward the Center of the World, and light things upward away from that same Center. But I ask you, from where do we get this experience of light and heavy things? And how far does our knowledge of them extend, so that from them we can certainly argue for the Center of the whole World? Is not the Earth and the air around the Earth the entire seat and home of everything that is heavy or light to us? But what is the Earth, what is the air surrounding it, in respect to the immense vastness of the whole World? They have the status of a point, or a tiny speck, or if something smaller could be named. This being so, do you not think a philosopher would say that it is a weak argument to build a case for the whole World from a particle, or from this tiny speck? We cannot, therefore, be certain about the center of this most spacious World from those things that seek this tiny speck or flee from it. These heavy and light things of ours seek their own proper place, which according to the philosopher <note>Usually referring to Aristotle.</note> is the "perfection of a thing," assigned to them by Nature. It is believable, as **Copernicus** learnedly discusses in Book I, Chapter 9, that this same tendency exists also in the Sun, the Moon, and the other wandering lights <note>The planets.</note>, so that by its efficacy they remain in that roundness in which they present themselves. If that place happens to be the center of the World as well, it happens only incidentally <note>original: "per accidens" — a philosophical term for a property that is not essential to the thing's nature.</note>. Indeed, the astronomical reasons of **Copernicus** do not proceed from a particle—and a most minute one at that—to the whole; but on the contrary, from the whole to the parts.

Furthermore, from the very methodology of the common hypotheses and those of **Copernicus**, it is easily recognized which deserve more faith. For **Copernicus's** hypotheses so count, arrange, connect, and measure the order and magnitude of all the orbits and spheres that nothing in them can be changed or transposed without the confusion of the entire Universe; indeed, all doubt concerning their position and sequence remains far excluded. On the contrary, in the common hypotheses, the number of spheres is uncertain. For some...

<sig>M</sig>
<meta>catchword: nine,</meta>

<summary>Maestlin defends the Copernican and Keplerian models, arguing that the traditional Aristotelian argument for a central Earth—based on gravity—is logically flawed because it applies local physics to the entire universe. He praises the Copernican system for its mathematical interconnectedness, where no part can be moved without collapsing the whole.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Nicolaus Copernicus, Rheticus, Heliocentrism, Gravity, Cosmology, Logic, Aristotelianism</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Michael Maestlin. He is building an argument against the traditional geocentric (Earth-centered) model by highlighting the mathematical and physical contradictions found in the works of ancient and medieval astronomers.</meta>

-># PREFACE.<-

...which some count as 10 spheres, others 11, and the number is not yet agreed upon. The order of these spheres is likewise uncertain: no defined distance can be given, much less demonstrated, except for the **Sun** and the **Moon**. Regarding **Venus**, **Mercury**, and the **Sun**, the dispute has not yet been settled, nor will it ever be. **Regiomontanus** <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg (1436–1476), a highly influential German mathematician and astronomer.</note> in Book 9, Chapter 1 of his *Epitome of Ptolemy's Almagest*, confesses that which of them, **Venus** or **Mercury**, is situated above the other cannot be grasped with any certainty. And although **Proclus** <note>A Greek Neoplatonist philosopher who also wrote on astronomy.</note> asserts in his *Hypotheses of Astronomy* that **Mercury** was seen passing beneath **Venus**, another much more serious question arises concerning the inexcusable **penetration of these planetary spheres** <term>penetratione: the physical overlapping or intersecting of the solid celestial spheres that were thought to carry the planets.</term>, which the proportion of their epicycles and eccentricities <note>Geometric tools used in ancient astronomy to explain the complex paths of planets.</note> absolutely demands, as supported by calculation and observations. Neither **Albategnius** <note>Al-Battani (c. 858–929), a pioneering Arab astronomer.</note> in Chapter 5, nor **Alfraganus** <note>Al-Farghani (9th century), another major figure in Islamic astronomy whose works were translated into Latin.</note> in Difference 21, nor other great Mathematicians, however much they struggle, are able to defend this. For the invincible demonstrations of the Physicists do not admit such penetrations; furthermore, the certainty of Geometry (which is the rule of truth in all things regarding magnitude) contradicts the crowding of the spheres. To these points I add that just as the distances of the spheres are doubtful, so too is no order certain. For with the exception of the **Sun** and the **Moon**, for the others it is the same—to speak by a most paradoxical <note>original: "παραδοξωτάτῳ" — Greek for "most contrary to expectation."</note> example—whether you place **Saturn** above **Mercury**, or **Mercury** above **Saturn**.

What shall I say regarding the most rapid and immeasurable velocity of this so vast mass of the World <note>original: "Mundi" — referring to the entire cosmos.</note> as it turns itself daily? Here first: the unspeakable speed surpasses all belief. **Albategnius** estimates the radius of the Sphere of fixed Stars at 19,000 Earth-radii; **Alfraganus** at 20,110; and indeed, it was agreed that it is not smaller, but far larger. From this, therefore, arises such a swiftness of the sphere that any star positioned in the middle of the circles between the poles of the world would be swept along more than twelve hundred German miles <note>A German mile (Leuge) was approximately 7.5 kilometers, making this speed incredibly high.</note> in a single **second scruple** <note>original: "scrupulo secundo" — 1/3600th of an hour, or what we now call a second.</note>, or the 3600th part of one hour. In this tiny span of time, hardly anyone could utter three or four words, even if speaking with headlong speech. It is necessary, moreover, for the highest heaven or **First Mover** <term>primo mobili: the outermost sphere in the geocentric model that was thought to drive the rotation of all other spheres.</term>, a sphere far higher, to possess a much more rushed course. But who will believe these things? Next: What is this impotence of Nature, which could infuse such a mind-incomprehensible velocity into an immense celestial body, yet could not communicate any of this motion to a mere little point of the World—namely, the earthly body? How can it be that while the whole system of the World is driven around—with no sphere of it excepted, not even the sphere of the fiery element (if there is one), nor the upper region of the air—this one little point is not turned? Therefore, it is much more probable and more consistent with reason that, with this immense world being freed from this daily rush, only this little globe [the Earth] moves with that motion. For it was easier for Nature to bestow this motion upon the Earth, whereby in one **scruple** <note>original: "scrupulo" — here meaning a minute, or 1/60th of an hour.</note> or sixtieth part of an hour, it traverses a quarter of one German mile in its great circle; the flight of clouds often equals this speed, not rarely surpasses it, and the fall of a lightning bolt incomprehensibly defeats it. I pass over countless other things, not a few of which **Copernicus**, and **Rheticus** in his *Narration*, and our **Kepler** in his *Forerunner* recount.

Some most excellent modern Mathematicians attempt to apply some remedy to these weaknesses; and they indeed place the Earth, according to the ancient hypotheses, immobile in the middle of the lunar and stellar spheres, and even of the whole Universe; but they declare the **Sun** (following **Copernicus**) to be the center of the other planets, though still mobile. It is truly a great thing, and those who establish this are not to be deprived of their praise. But in this...

<meta>catchword: hypo-</meta>

<summary>Maestlin argues that the geocentric model is physically impossible due to the absurd speeds required for the outer heavens to rotate daily and the geometric "penetration" of overlapping planetary spheres. He concludes that it is far more rational for the tiny Earth to rotate than for the entire vast cosmos to do so.</summary>
<keywords>celestial spheres, planetary order, planetary distance, velocity of the heavens, German mile, rotation of the Earth, Regiomontanus, Alfraganus, Albategnius, Copernicus, Kepler, Tychonic system</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter, likely written by Michael Maestlin, defending the Copernican system as refined by Kepler.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>

-># PREFACE.<-

In their attempts to amend the hypotheses, they do nothing but patch an old and worn-out garment with a new piece of cloth, which usually results in an even greater tear. For by this arrangement, the centers of motions and the driving forces are surely dissolved and pulled apart; the other motions and spheres (or whatever it is that possesses the character of spheres) are entangled in many more intricate difficulties, and nothing is joined with another according to reason, or with any proportion of magnitudes, motions, and order. Against these I oppose this single discovery of our <term>Kepler</term> <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630), the astronomer who discovered the laws of planetary motion.</note>. In this work—to say nothing of other matters for now—the most elegant order of the parts of the World, as well as the most beautiful and irrefutable proportion of magnitudes and motions, consistent with the five regular solids, is most ingeniously demonstrated in support of the opinion of the more recent <term>Copernicus</term> and the most ancient mathematician <term>Aristarchus</term> <note>Aristarchus of Samos (c. 310–230 BCE), the first known proponent of a heliocentric model.</note>. These things can never be found or hoped for in the ancient hypotheses, nor in the amendments of the more recent ones. To whom, therefore, shall we think it safer to trust? To him who, wishing to avoid some apparent absurdities, plunges into more serious ones, which they nevertheless prop up with wobbling spindles and say nothing with reason? Or truly to him who asserts nothing without reason, confirms everything solidly, and solidly refutes those things which indeed seem absurd? <term>Plato</term> is a friend, <term>Socrates</term> is a friend, but <term>Truth</term> is a greater friend. <note>original: "Amicus ergo Plato, amicus Socrates, magis tamen amica Veritas." A famous proverb emphasizing that objective truth outweighs personal loyalty to teachers.</note>

I have thought it necessary, kind Reader, to subjoin these things to <term>Kepler's</term> learned <term>Forerunner</term> <note>original: "Prodromus." Referring to Kepler's first major work, the *Mysterium Cosmographicum* (1596).</note>, which contains an explanation of the most abstruse mysteries of Nature, discovered or noticed by no one until now. I rely, as I said before, on the certain hope that by the occasion of this mystery (I have long since despaired of the other ancient and recent hypotheses), we shall shortly have <term>Astronomy</term> so polished—if indeed any explanation and more perfect cleansing and form of it can be hoped for and expected—that it will be as brilliant as possible.

However, since in this <term>Forerunner</term>, Master <term>Kepler</term> often appeals to the <term>Narration</term> of <term>Georg Joachim Rheticus</term> <note>Rheticus (1514–1574) was the sole pupil of Copernicus and the first to publish an account of his heliocentric theory.</note>, which he wrote to <term>Schöner</term> in the year 1539 while living with <term>Copernicus</term>, before <term>Copernicus</term> had published his books on the <term>Revolutions</term>; and since that <term>Narration</term> is not in everyone's hands, I have judged it absolutely necessary to join it, together with the <term>Praise of Prussia</term> written by the same <term>Rheticus</term>, to this <term>Forerunner</term> of Master <term>Kepler</term> (consulting him, yet because he is absent, without his consultation). I did this all the more because I saw that from these two writings, a great part of the <term>Forerunner</term>—where the style has cut some things short for the sake of brevity—would receive a great deal of light. Furthermore, <term>Rheticus</term> here explains many rather obscure passages in <term>Copernicus's</term> own books from professional expertise; hence this <term>Narration</term> and <term>Praise</term> can be held in place of a brief <term>Commentary</term> on <term>Copernicus</term>.

These are the reasons for the addition of this <term>Narration</term>, along with <term>Rheticus's Praise of Prussia</term>. Lest our own labor also be lacking in achieving these goals more easily, it seemed best to revise both the <term>Narration</term> and the <term>Praise</term> with as much diligence as possible, and to add <margin>marginal annotations</margin> as well as the <term>diagrams of demonstrations</term> which <term>Rheticus</term> had undoubtedly included, but which, through I know not what thoughtlessness, had been omitted in the printed copies. If, however, anything in these same writings is considered somewhat foreign to our purpose, let the prudent Reader leave them in their place. We have considered it a matter of our integrity that those other things which we found in the copy published at <term>Basel</term> in the year 1541, including the Title and Preface, should be faithfully rendered, even if they do not serve our principal goal.

<meta>This page concludes the preface by Michael Maestlin and begins the introductory letter by Achilles Pirmin Gasser, establishing the historical context for the publication of Johannes Kepler’s early work and its connection to Copernican theory.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

It would have been desirable, however, if that second Narrative, which the Author here promises several times, had also been released; or if perhaps it was indeed written but lies hidden somewhere (I have not seen it published, nor have I learned of anyone else who has seen it), it would be better for it to be granted for public use than to be left to be gnawed by moths in secret places. The same wish might be made regarding the Commentaries of Erasmus Reinhold <note>A leading astronomer of the generation before Kepler, known for his "Prutenic Tables" based on Copernicus.</note> on those same Books of Copernicus, had not the untimely death of that man cut short the work he had begun, as well as many other most useful labors for the mathematical Commonwealth. You, best Reader, enjoy these present works until our Kepler sends forth his entire Uranic or Cosmic work, of which we have here the Forerunner <note>original: "Prodromum"; referring to Kepler's *Mysterium Cosmographicum*.</note>. Farewell. Written at Tübingen in our Study, on the Calends of October, in the Year of Salvation 1596.

# TO THE MOST LEARNED
MAN, MASTER GEORG VOGELIN
OF CONSTANCE, Phi-
losopher and Physician, a Friend like a Brother, Achilles
P. Gasser of Lindau sends
greetings.

<image-desc>Large decorated initial 'E' with intricate foliage design.</image-desc> I SEND TO YOU, MOST EXCELLENT
MAN, <gloss>as if adding a Word to my own Word</gloss> <note>original Greek: "ὥσπερ πρὸς τῷ ἐμῷ Λόγῳ Λόγον"</note>, this little Book, which is not only new and unknown to our people, but also to you, unless I am completely mistaken, admirable and in every respect <gloss>now most clearly pure</gloss> <note>original Greek: "νῦν ἄκρατον ὀφάτατον"</note> to the point of amazement. Georg Joachim Rheticus <note>The sole pupil of Copernicus and the man responsible for the first publication of Copernican theory.</note>, Master of Liberal Arts and sometime Professor of Mathematics at Wittenberg, a fellow citizen and my dearest friend, sent this to me from Danzig <note>original: "Gedano"</note> in recent days, together with a letter most full of these matters. Although this Book does not correspond to the method of teaching customary until now, and may be considered contrary to the usual theories of the Schools on more than one theme—and (as the Monks would say) even heretical—it nevertheless seems to offer a new and most true restoration of Astronomy, indeed <gloss>a beautiful rebirth</gloss> <note>original Greek: "τῷ καλὴν γεννησίαν"</note> beyond doubt, especially...

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle (Nuncupatoria) addressed to George Donner, discussing the reception of Nicolaus Copernicus’s new astronomical theories.</meta>

->DEDICATORY<-

page 93

since he boasts of the most evident decrees concerning such propositions, over which you know there has long been controversy across the whole world—not only among the most learned **Mathematicians**, but even the greatest **Philosophers**, and not without "sweat," as they say. Namely, these concerns the number of the **celestial spheres**, the distance of the **Stars**, the governance of the **Sun**, both the position and the circles of the **Planets**, the fixed length of the Year, the known points of the **Equinoxes** and **Solstices**, and finally the location and motion of the **Earth** itself, along with similar most arduous matters. While this man <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> claims he will faithfully demonstrate the reasoning and decisions of all these things through various proofs he has recently devised, I do not see why that argument should be rejected, torn apart, or despised by the learned of our age. For even among those only moderately imbued with mathematics, and even among the (so to speak) makers of almanacs <note>original: "ephemeridistas," those who calculate daily celestial positions</note>, it is well known that astronomical matters—which are nonetheless believed to be the most certain of sciences due to the infallible rectitude of the compass and calculation—are today limping in more than one area, both in the measurement of time and the observation of motions; nor do they always square perfectly <note>original: "ad amussim," according to the mason's rule</note> with what **Geometry** specifically professes. Therefore, dearest **George**, since we feel ourselves being freed from many difficulties in the study of the heavens <note>original: "vrania," referring to Urania, the muse of astronomy</note>, and moreover, the most abstruse knots are being opened for us, I ask that you read this sent booklet diligently. Once read, judge it more sharply; once judged, come now, uniquely commend it to all cultivators of mathematics, but especially to your neighbors. Offer it to be unrolled <note>read</note> from time to time, so that in such a way not only may a second **Account** <note>original: "Narratio." This refers to the *Narratio Prima*, the first published summary of Copernicus’s theory</note> be sent out more maturely, but that this entire and nearly Divine work (whose scope <note>original: "περιοχὴ" (periochē)</note> these Accounts show as if they were indexes) might become better known, be loved, and be demanded with more frequent prayers from the Author himself—a man, without doubt, of incomparable learning and of **Herculean**, or rather **Atlantean** labor. May the whole work finally be communicated to us through the instigation, effort, and diligent spurring of my most respected friend, the writer of these present words. This is what I especially wish to be cared for by this inscription: namely, through you, a most true seeker of **physical** things, and those like you...

<sig>M 3</sig>

<summary>The author defends Copernicus's new astronomical findings against traditional skepticism, arguing that current models of the heavens are "limping" and inaccurate. He urges George Donner to share this "divine work" to encourage the publication of Copernicus's full research.</summary>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory address or preface by A. Cassar, likely following a dedication to a patron or the learned public.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>A. CASSARI</header>

*...to provide an opportunity for the most honorable **followers of this discipline**, so that with worthy gratitude, an abundance of growth may be granted to the **younger students**, and to the **elders** <note>original: "Maioribus"</note> an occasion for unearthing the truth against the scrutiny of common eyes, as liberally as it is most fruitfully given. For you see clearly what this profession requires, and what—and how magnificent—this table of contents promises. Therefore, as is your custom, turn your mind toward these noble things, so that you may continue to take up and receive this **little book** <note>original: "Libellum"</note> in such a way that we do not later grieve or sadly complain that we have been deprived and utterly cheated of a complete and most splendid banquet—of which we here provide a very sumptuous taste—as if a most sweet morsel had been snatched from hungry jaws. Fare well, my friend, and in loving me, laugh at the common judgment in this business; for there is no doubt that this novelty, being without malice, will one day be both pleasing and useful to all learned men.*

->At Feldkirch in Rhaetia <note>A region in the central Alps, modern-day Austria/Switzerland</note>, in the year 1540 since the birth of **Christ the Savior**.<—

<meta>This page begins the 'Narratio Prima' (First Account), the first published summary of Nicolaus Copernicus’s revolutionary heliocentric theory. Georg Joachim Rheticus, a young professor from Wittenberg, writes this as a letter to his mentor, Johannes Schöner.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

## TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS MAN,
Master Johannes Schöner, as to his own revered father, Georg Joachim Rheticus sends his greetings.

<image-desc>Decorated initial 'P' at the beginning of the first paragraph.</image-desc>
On the fourteenth of May <note>original: "PRIDIE IDVS MAIAS"</note> I sent a letter to you from Poznań, in which I informed you of my undertaken journey into Prussia; and I promised that I would signify to you as soon as I could whether the outcome responded to the rumors and to my own expectations. And although I have as yet been able to devote scarcely ten weeks to thoroughly learning the Astronomical work of the Master Doctor himself to whom I have come—partly because of the somewhat poor health of *Nicolaus* <gloss>Copernicus.</gloss> and partly because, having been most honorably invited by the Most Reverend Lord Master Tiedemann Giese, Bishop of Chełmno <note>original: "Culmensi"</note>, I set out for Lubawa <note>original: "Lobaumiam"</note> together with my Teacher and rested from my studies for several weeks—nevertheless, so that I might finally fulfill my promises and satisfy your wishes, I shall show with what brevity and clarity I can what my Teacher thinks concerning these things which I have learned.

<margin>Regiomontanus was extinguished by poison at Rome in the year 1476, having scarcely completed his 40th year.</margin>
In the first place, I would wish you to be certain, most learned Master Schöner, that this man whose work I now use is not inferior to Regiomontanus <note>A famous 15th-century German mathematician and astronomer.</note> in any kind of learning or in skill in astronomy. However, I more willingly compare him with Ptolemy <note>The 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose Earth-centered model had dominated for 1,400 years.</note>, not because I esteem Regiomontanus less than Ptolemy, but because my Teacher has this good fortune in common with Ptolemy: that, with the help of Divine mercy, he has completed the restoration of astronomy which he undertook; whereas Regiomontanus (alas, cruel Fates!) departed from life before his pillars were set up. <note>A metaphor for completing a monumental work.</note>

<margin>The Books of the Revolutions by Nicolaus Copernicus.</margin>
The Master Doctor, my Teacher, has written six Books in which, in imitation of Ptolemy, he has embraced the whole of Astronomy by teaching and demonstrating individual points through mathematical and Geometric methods.

The First Book contains the general description of the universe and the foundations by which he saves <note>A technical term in astronomy meaning to provide a mathematical model that matches observed phenomena.</note> the observations and appearances of all ages.

<meta>Catchword: saves-</meta>

<meta>This page continues the description of Nicolaus Copernicus’s monumental work, "On the Revolutions of the Heavenly Spheres," detailing the contents of its six books and providing biographical context regarding Copernicus's education and career.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
-># NARRATION<-

...contains the things he is about to undertake. To these, he adds as much of the doctrine of sines <note>trigonometry</note>, plane triangles, and spherical triangles as he deemed necessary for his work.

The **Second Book** concerns the doctrine of the primary motion <note>the apparent daily rotation of the heavens</note>, and those things which he thought should be said in this place regarding the fixed stars.

The **Third Book** is on the motion of the Sun. And, because experience taught him that the length of the year—measured from the equinoxes—also depends on the motion of the fixed stars, he shows in the first part of this book, with true reason and indeed divine ingenuity, how to investigate the motions of the fixed stars and the changes of the solstitial and equinoctial points.

The **Fourth Book** is on the motion of the Moon, and on Eclipses.

The **Fifth Book** is on the motions of the remaining planets.

The **Sixth Book** is on Latitudes.

I have thoroughly learned the first three books; I have conceived a general idea of the fourth; and I have first grasped the hypotheses of the remaining books in my mind. As far as the first two are concerned, I thought nothing needed to be written to you; partly by my own specific judgment, and partly because the doctrine of the primary motion departs in no way from common and accepted reasoning—except that he has reconstructed from scratch the tables of declinations, right ascensions, ascensional differences, and the rest pertaining to this part of the doctrine, so that they can be adapted to the observations of all ages through proportional parts. Therefore, those things which he delivers in the third book, along with the hypotheses of all the remaining motions, I shall—God willing—clearly recite to you, as far as I am able to achieve at present according to the slenderness of my talent.

<margin>They report **Nicolaus Copernicus** was born in the year 1473, on the 19th of February, at 4 hours and 48 minutes in the afternoon, on the Friday before the Feast of the Chair of Saint Peter. Therefore, **Francesco Giuntini** <note>original: "Franc. Iunæ:"</note> is in error, who writes that he was born in the year 1472 on the 29th of January. He died, however, in the year 1543 on the 19th of January <note>historical records usually cite May 24</note> at the age of 70, in which same year he had seen to the publication of those books of his *Revolutions*.</margin>

<margin>When my Lord Doctor <note>Copernicus</note> was at Bologna, not so much a pupil as an assistant and witness to the observations of the most learned man **Domenico Maria** <note>Domenico Maria Novara da Ferrara</note>; and at Rome, around the year of our Lord 1500, being more or less twenty-seven years old, he was a Professor of Mathematics before a great crowd of students and a circle of great men and masters in this kind of learning; then here in **Warmia**, devoting himself to his own studies, he had noted observations with the greatest care. From the observations of the fixed stars, he chose that one which he made in the year of our Lord 1525 concerning **Spica Virginis** <note>the brightest star in the constellation Virgo</note>.</margin>

<header>FIRST</header>

<margin>On the motion of the Fixed Stars.</margin>
...he held. Furthermore, he determined that it <note>the star Spica</note> was distant from the autumnal point by approximately 17 degrees and 21 minutes, since he found its southern declination to be no less than 8 degrees and 40 minutes. Then, comparing all the observations of the Authors with his own, he found that the revolution of the Anomaly—or the circle of diversity—was complete, and that we, in our age, are in the second revolution since the time of Timocharis <note>An Alexandrian astronomer, c. 320–260 BCE</note>. Therefore, he geometrically established the mean motion of the fixed stars and the equations of the diverse motion. For since the observation of *Spica* by Timocharis in the 36th year of the first period of Callippus <note>A Greek astronomer who improved the Metonic cycle</note>, when compared with the observation of the 48th year of the same period, teaches us that the stars in that age proceeded one degree in 72 years; and then from Hipparchus to Menelaus they always completed one degree in a hundred years, he concluded for himself that the observations of Timocharis fell into the last quadrant of the circle of diversity, in which the motion appeared to be a diminished mean. However, in the intermediate time between Hipparchus and Menelaus, the motion of diversity was at its slowest point. Indeed, the observations of Menelaus compared with those of Ptolemy show that the stars then moved by one degree in 86 years; therefore, Ptolemy's observations were made while the motion of the anomaly was in the first quadrant, and the stars were then moved by a slow motion that was added to, or increased. Furthermore, because from Ptolemy to Albategnius <note>original: "Albategnium," referring to the Arab astronomer Al-Battani, c. 858–929 CE</note> 66 years correspond to one degree, and our observations compared with those of Albategnius show that the stars, by their diverse motion, again complete one degree in 70 years—but compared to his other observations made in Italy, that observation which I mentioned above shows the fixed stars progressing again by one degree in 100 years by their diverse motion. It is also clearer than the sun that from the time of Ptolemy to Albategnius, the motion of diversity passed the first mean limit, and the entire quadrant of the added mean, and around the times of Albategnius it was at the point of highest velocity. From Albategnius to us, the third quadrant of the diverse motion has been completed, and meanwhile the stars progressed with a diminished fast motion. Having passed the other limit of mean motion, in our age the anomaly has again reached the fourth quadrant of the diminished mean motion; consequently, the diverse motion is now again approaching its slowest limit. However, in order for the Master <note>original: "D. Præceptor," referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> to reduce these things to a certain calculation, so that they might agree in order with all observations, he established the diverse motion...

<margin>Albategnius, Chapter 51.</margin>
<margin>From the year 1525 to the year 1525. <note>Likely a reference to the span of contemporary observations used for comparison.</note></margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the precession of the equinoxes and the variation in the length of the solar year, building on the observations of ancient and medieval astronomers.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

->ACCOUNT<-

<margin>Copernicus, Book 3, Chapter 6.</margin>
...is completed in 1717 Egyptian years <note>The "Egyptian year" was a standard calendar of exactly 365 days used by astronomers for calculation</note>, and that the maximum equation <note>The correction applied to an average motion to find the true position</note> is nearly 70 minutes. Furthermore, the mean motion of the stars in an Egyptian year is nearly 50 seconds, and a full revolution of this mean motion will be completed in 25,816 Egyptian years.

<margin>General consideration of the year from the equinox. Ptolemy, Book 3, Chapter 2. Al-Battani, Chapters 27, 28. Copernicus, Book 3, Chapter 13.</margin>
This ratio of the motions in the fixed stars is also confirmed by the annual quantities observed from the equinoctial points. It is established with certainty why, from Hipparchus to Ptolemy, a full day (minus a twentieth part of a day) was lost; from Ptolemy to Al-Battani <note>original: "Albategnium," a 9th-century Arab astronomer</note> nearly 7 days; and from Al-Battani to his own observations, which he held in the year of our Lord 1515, nearly 5 days. These discrepancies did not occur entirely through the fault of the instruments, as has been believed until now, but by a certain and self-consistent logic everywhere. Therefore, the uniformity of motion is by no means to be taken from the equinoxes, but from the fixed stars, as the observations of all ages testify with wonderful agreement regarding the motions of the Sun and Moon, as well as the rest of the planets. Because from Timocharis to Ptolemy the stars proceeded with a very slow motion, only a three-hundredth part of a day was added to the 365 days and a quarter; but from Ptolemy to Al-Battani, because they were fast, it is accepted that a one-hundred-and-sixth part of a day was subtracted from the quarter-day. In our age, if observations are compared to those of Al-Battani, it is clear that a one-hundred-and-twenty-eighth part of a day is missing from the quarter-day. Therefore, a greater quantity of the year from the equinoxes seems to correspond to a slow motion, and a smaller quantity to a fast motion; and an increase in the year corresponds to a decreasing velocity, so much so that if the quantity of the year from the equinoxes is accurately examined in our age, it almost agrees with Ptolemy again. Hence, it must be established that the equinoctial points move in precedence <note>retrograde or westward motion</note>, just as the nodes do in the case of the Moon, and that the stars do not advance according to the sequence of the signs <note>the zodiacal order</note> at all. One must imagine, therefore, a "mean equinox" which proceeds from the first star of Aries in the stellar sphere, by placing the fixed stars behind by a uniform motion; and that on both sides of this mean equinox, the "true equinox" itself departs by a diverse and regular motion. The radius of this divergence, however, does not much exceed 70 minutes. In this way, a certain logic for the quantity of the year from the equinoxes has existed for individual ages, and can still be detected today; furthermore, this logic corresponds most exactly, almost to the minute, to the observations of the fixed stars by all skilled practitioners.

In order that I might provide you with some taste of this matter, most learned Schöner <note>Johannes Schöner (1477–1547), a noted German astronomer and mathematician to whom this letter is addressed</note>, behold, I have calculated for you the true precessions of the equinoxes <note>Precession is the slow, wobbling shift of the Earth's axis that changes the positions of the stars over centuries</note> at certain times of observation.

| Year | True Precession | Time |
|:-----|:-------------:|:--------|
| | Deg. Min. | |
| Before the birth of the Lord | 295 | 2 20 | Timocharis |
| Of the Lord | 128 | 4 0 | Hipparchus |
| After the birth of the Lord | 139 | 6 40 | Ptolemy |
| | 880 | 18 10 | Al-Battani |
| Of the Lord | 1076 | 19 37 | Arzachel |
| | 1525 | 27 21 | Our Time |

Ptolemy's precession, when subtracted from the positions of the stars recorded in Ptolemy's work, leaves the distance they stand from the first star of Aries. Then, the precession of Al-Battani <note>original: "Albategnij"</note> added to this shows the true place of observation. This is done similarly in all other cases. These values correspond most precisely to the observations of all skilled craftsmen, even where individual minutes are noted, or are obtained from recorded declinations, or from the motion of the Moon reduced to a greater precision, as our own observations compared with those of the Ancients teach us. For, as you see, having neglected a few minutes, they recite at least a half, a third, or a fourth part of a degree, etc. However, these do not satisfy the movements of the <term>apsides</term> <note>The points in an orbit where a planet is nearest or farthest from the body it orbits</note> of the planets; therefore, it was necessary to assign a particular motion to them, as will be clear from the Theory of the Sun.

<margin>The sidereal year.</margin>
<margin>Chapter 27.</margin>
Furthermore, since he had perceived that the equality of motion must be taken from the fixed stars, he investigated most diligently the <term>sidereal year</term> <note>The time it takes for the Earth to orbit the Sun relative to the fixed stars, rather than the seasons</note>, which he found to be 365 days, 15 minutes, and nearly 24 seconds, and that it has been constant from the time that established observations were made. For as for the fact that, according to Al-Battani, the Babylonians set it at three seconds more, and Thabit <note>original: "Thebit," referring to Thabit ibn Qurra</note> at one second less, these differences can be attributed—without insult to their instruments or observations, which as you know can by no means be <note>original: "ἀκριβέσταται" (akribestatai)</note> most precise—either to the diversity of the Sun's motion, or even to the fact that the most ancient observers, not having a certain method for eclipses, did not account for the differences in the Sun's appearance in their observations...

can be attributed to those who neglected these matters. However, this error—spanning the entire time from the Babylonians to us—is by no means to be compared with that error of 22 seconds of a day found between Ptolemy and Albategnius <note>Al-Battani, a 9th-century Arab astronomer</note>. That it was necessary for less than a twentieth part of a day to fall away between Hipparchus and Ptolemy, yet nearly seven days to be missing between the latter and Albategnius, I have gathered for you, most learned Master Schöner, with the greatest pleasure from the aforementioned calculation of the motions of the stars and from the Master Preceptor’s <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> own treatise, *On the Motion of the Sun*, as you shall see shortly.

<margin>On the change of the obliquity of the Ecliptic.</margin>
My Master Doctor and Preceptor found that the change in the maximum declination <note>The tilt of the Earth's axis relative to its orbit</note> follows this rule: while the motion of the diversity of the fixed stars completes one cycle, half of the change in obliquity occurs. Therefore, he established that a full revolution of the change in obliquity takes place in 3,434 Egyptian years. It is established that in the times of Timocharis, Aristarchus, and 
<margin>Ptolemy, Book 1, Chapter 11 of the Almagest.</margin>
Ptolemy, the change in obliquity was at its slowest variation, so much so that they believed the maximum declination to be unchangeable, always <gloss>11/93</gloss> parts of a great circle <note>Approximately 23 degrees and 51 minutes</note>. After these men, Albategnius recorded it in his age as nearly 23 degrees and 35 minutes. Then Arzachel <note>Al-Zarqali, an 11th-century Andalusian astronomer</note>, nearly 190 years after 
<margin>Copernicus, Book 3, Chapter 6.</margin>
him, recorded 23 degrees and 34 minutes. Prophatius the Jew <note>Jacob ben Machir ibn Tibbon</note>, 230 years after him again, recorded 23 degrees and 32 minutes. In our own age, however, it appears no greater than 23 degrees and 28 and a half minutes. Hence, since it is clear that in the 300 years before Ptolemy, the motion of the change in obliquity was at its slowest, but from him to Albategnius, over nearly 750 years, it decreased by 17 minutes, and from Albategnius to us in 650 years it decreased by at least 7 minutes, it follows that the change in obliquity occurs just like the departure of the planets from the ecliptic: by a certain motion of libration <note>An oscillation or swinging motion</note>, or along a straight line. It is the nature of this motion to be fastest in the middle and slowest near the extremes. Therefore, around the time of Albategnius, the equinoctial pole (or the pole of the ecliptic) was nearly in the middle of this motion of libration, but in this century it is near the other slowest limit, at which point the greatest approach of one pole to the other occurs. But we have posited above that the motions of the fixed stars, and the diversity of the annual length from the equinoxes, are preserved by the motion of the equinoctial [pole]; and the vertices of the earth are the poles of this [motion], from which the elevations of the poles <note>Latitude</note> are taken. You see therefore, most learned Master Schöner, to advise you in passing, what kind of

By comparing data from ancient Greeks, medieval Arabs, and contemporary observations, he demonstrates that this "libration" is currently slowing down as it reaches its limit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical defense of the Copernican system, specifically addressing the shifting measurements of the Earth's tilt and the Sun's orbit. The "Teacher" referred to throughout is Nicolaus Copernicus.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>FIRST [PART].</header>

hypotheses, or theories of motions, require observations; indeed, you shall hear even clearer testimonies. Furthermore, the Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> assumes the minimum obliquity <note>The tilt of the Earth's axis relative to its orbit</note> will be 23 degrees and 28 minutes, the difference of which from the maximum is 24 minutes. From these, he has geometrically established a table of proportional minutes, so that the maximum obliquity of the ecliptic can be derived for all ages. Thus, the proportional minutes were 58 in the time of Ptolemy, 24 in the time of Al-Battani <note>original: "Albategnij", a 9th-century Arab astronomer</note>, 15 in the time of Arzachel <note>original: "Arzahelis", an 11th-century Spanish-Arab astronomer</note>, and 1 in our own age. By applying these to the 24 minutes of difference as a proportional part, it is clear that a certain rule for the change in obliquity has been discovered.

<margin>On the change of the Sun's eccentricity.</margin>
In the motion of the Sun, since every difficulty concerns the unstable quantity of the year's flow, we must first speak of the change in the apogee <note>The point in the orbit farthest from the center</note> and the eccentricity, so that we may establish all the causes of the inequality of the year; which the Master shows to be regular and certain, having adopted theories suited to this purpose. Although Ptolemy established the apogee of the Sun as fixed, he preferred to accept the common opinion rather than trust his own observations, which perhaps differed little from the common view. But as a certain conjecture is drawn from his own account, it is established that the eccentricity around the time of Hipparchus—namely, 200 years before Ptolemy—was 417 such parts as the radius of the eccentric circle is 10,000. In Ptolemy's age, however, it was 414 of the same parts. In the time of Arzachel (to whom our Regiomontanus <note>A famous 15th-century German astronomer</note> also gave greater credit), it is established from the maximum equation to have been nearly 346, but in our time it is 323; for the Master affirms that he has found the maximum equation to be no greater than 1 degree and 50 and a half minutes.

<margin>On the motion of the Sun's Apogee.</margin>
Then, when he most diligently weighed the motions of the apsides <note>The points of greatest or least distance in an orbit</note> of the Sun and the remaining planets, he first discovered (as you also see from what was said before) that the apsides proceed by peculiar motions beneath the sphere of the fixed stars. It is no more fitting to affirm that the apparent motions of the fixed stars and the apsides, as well as the change in obliquity, depend on a single cause, than if one of your craftsmen who produce *automated machines* <note>original Greek: "τὰς αὐτομάτως" (tas automatous), referring to clockwork or mechanical models</note> should attempt to represent the motions and appearances of every single planet with one and the same mechanism; or if someone should presume to defend the idea that the foot, the hand, and the tongue perform all their actions by the same muscle and the same motive force. Therefore, the Master attributes two motions to the apogee of the Sun—namely, a mean and a varying motion—by which it moves beneath the eighth sphere,

<meta>catchword: [moves] ...that,</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the historical variations in the Earth's axial tilt (obliquity) and the Sun's orbital eccentricity, citing astronomers from Ptolemy to Copernicus. It argues that these astronomical changes are governed by distinct, complex mechanical laws rather than a single universal cause.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the astronomical narrative regarding the motions of the Sun and the shifting of the celestial spheres, specifically focusing on the eccentricity of the solar orbit.</meta>

# NARRATIVE

<page-num>100</page-num>

To these things is added the fact that, since the true equinox <note>The point where the celestial equator and ecliptic intersect</note> moves toward the preceding signs with both a uniform and a varying motion, the apogees <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note> of the Sun and the remaining planets are displaced, just like the fixed stars. Therefore, so that the observations of all ages might correspond to one another in a consistent law, he was compelled to distinguish these three motions from one another.

<margin>See below in the appendix: The Diagram of the Theory of the Sun.</margin>To understand these things, you must assume the maximum eccentricity to be 417 parts and the minimum to be 321; the difference is 96 parts, which is the diameter of a small circle. The center of the eccentric <note>A circular orbit where the Earth is not at the exact center</note> moves from east to west along the circumference of this small circle. Therefore, from the center of the world to the center of this small circle, there will be 369 parts. All these parts, as was just said, are of the same scale as the radius of the eccentric, which is 10,000 parts. You have here the mechanism which he investigated from the three previously mentioned eccentricities, in exactly the same way that the uniform motions of the Moon are corrected from three lunar eclipses—certainly a divine discovery.

<margin>The motion of the center of the eccentric in the small circle.</margin>Furthermore, he established that the center of the eccentric completes its revolution at the same speed with which every variation in the change of the obliquity <note>The tilt of the Earth's axis</note> returns. And this matter is truly worthy of the highest admiration, because it is completed with such a great and wonderful harmony.

<margin>B</margin>Almost 60 years before the birth of the Lord, the eccentricity was at its maximum, and at that same time, the maximum declination <note>The angle of the Sun above the celestial equator</note> of the Sun also occurred. By the same ratio and in no other way, the rest has also decreased. Often, amidst the varying fortunes of my own affairs, this and other such "games of Nature" <note>original: "Naturæ lufus"</note> bring me relief and most sweetly soothe my troubled mind.

<margin>The Monarchies of the World change according to the motion of the center of the eccentric.</margin>I will also add a certain prophecy. We see that all Great Monarchies have begun when the center of the eccentric was in some significant position on this small circle. Thus, when the Sun's eccentricity was at its maximum, the Roman Empire declined toward a Monarchy <note>The transition from Republic to Empire</note>; and as that eccentricity decreased, so too did the Empire fail as if growing old, and eventually vanished. When the center reached the quadrant—the middle boundary—the Law of Muhammad <note>The rise of Islam</note> was brought forth; thus another great Empire began and grew very rapidly in proportion to the motion. Now, in 100 years, when the eccentricity will be at its minimum, this Empire too will complete its period, so that around these times it is at its highest peak, from which...

<sig>equally</sig>

and just as swiftly, God willing, it will fall with a heavier slip. However, when the center of the <term>eccentric</term><note>A circle in ancient astronomy whose center is displaced from the Earth to account for varying planetary speeds.</note> reaches the other middle boundary, we hope for the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ. For it was at this location that the creation of the world occurred. For in this manner, the greatest changes of the entire history of the World are seen as if inscribed upon this circle. Furthermore, while the center of the eccentric descends toward the center of the universe, it is consistent that the center of the small circle proceeds according to the sequence of the signs <note>original: "secundum signorum consequentiam," meaning moving in the natural order of the Zodiac</note> by nearly 25 seconds in each Egyptian year. And because the center of the eccentric moves from the greatest distance in a preceding direction, the <term>equation</term><note>A correction factor used to reconcile observed and mean positions.</note> corresponding to the movement of the <term>anomaly</term><note>The angular distance of a planet from its perihelion or apogee.</note> of the proposed time is subtracted from the mean motion until the semicircle is completed; in the remainder, however, it is added, so that the true motion of the <term>apogee</term><note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> may be obtained. Moreover, the maximum equation between the true and mean apogee, geometrically derived as is fitting from the aforementioned points, is 7 degrees and 24 minutes; the rest, as is customary, are established according to the ratio of the center of the eccentric in this small circle. We have certain knowledge of the diverse motion, which consists of three given locations; regarding the mean motion, there is some doubt, because we do not have the true position of the Sun's apogee under the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The sun's apparent path across the sky.</note> for those three locations, and this is due to the error that occurred between Albategnius and Arzachel, as our countryman Regiomontanus reports in Book 3, Proposition 13 of the *Epitome*.

<margin>An error is believed to have been committed between the observations of the Sun's apogee by Albategnius and Arzachel.</margin>
Albategnius <note>A 9th-century Arab astronomer also known as Al-Battani.</note> uses the mysteries of astronomy too freely, as can be seen in many places; if he did this also in the establishment of the Sun's apogee—even if we grant that he surely had the certain time of the equinox—it is nevertheless impossible, as Ptolemy also testifies, to establish the times of the solstices precisely with instruments. Indeed, a single minute of <term>declination</term><note>The celestial equivalent of latitude.</note>, which certainly easily escapes the senses, can defraud us in this place by nearly four degrees, to which four days correspond...

<page-num>102</page-num>
-># NARRATIVE<-

days, how could he establish the position of the Sun's apogee <note>The point where the Sun is furthest from the Earth in the geocentric model</note>? If he proceeded through the intermediate places of the ecliptic, as Regiomontanus <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg (1436–1476), a celebrated German astronomer</note> relates in the 14th proposition of that same third book, he used an argument of very little certainty. Therefore, if he erred, let him blame himself, for he chose eclipses occurring not near the apogee, but near the middle longitudes of the Sun's eccentric orbit, where an apogee placed six degrees away from its true location could induce no notable error in the eclipses.

Arzahel <note>Al-Zarqali (c. 1029–1087), an Arab mathematician and astronomer</note>, according to Regiomontanus, boasted that he possessed 402 observations, and from these he established the location of the apogee. We concede that with such diligence he might indeed have found the true eccentricity; but since it is not clear that he took into counsel the eclipses of the Moon near the apsides <note>The highest or lowest points in an orbit</note> of the Sun, it appears he should be no more believed in the establishment of the highest apogee than Albategnius <note>Al-Battani (c. 858–929), a famous Arab astronomer</note>. Here you see with how much labor my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> had to strive to establish the mean motion of the apogee.
<margin>In the year 1515, the Sun's apogee was found at 5 degrees and 40 minutes of Cancer.</margin>
He himself, for nearly 40 years in Italy and here in Warmia <note>A region in Poland where Copernicus lived and worked</note>, observed the eclipses and the motion of the Sun, and he chose this observation by which he established that in the year of our Lord 1515, the Sun's apogee occupied 6 degrees and two-thirds of Cancer. Then, examining all the eclipses in Ptolemy and comparing them to his own, which he himself observed most diligently,
<margin>The mean motion of the Sun's apogee.</margin>
he established that the annual mean motion of the apogee is nearly 25 seconds relative to the fixed stars, and nearly 1 minute and 15 seconds relative to the mean equinox. And by this reasoning, through both the mean and diverse motion, and applying the true precession of the equinoxes, it is gathered that the true
<margin>The true motion of the same.</margin>
location of the apogee at the true equinox was, in the time of Hipparchus <note>Greek astronomer, 2nd century BCE</note>, at 63 degrees; in Ptolemy's time, at 64 and a half degrees; in Albategnius's time, at the 76th degree and a half; in Arzahel's, at 82; but in our age, all things agree with experience.
<margin>The Alphonsine calculation errs too much in placing the Sun's apogee.</margin>
These results certainly fit better than the Alphonsine Tables <note>13th-century astronomical tables named after Alfonso X of Castile</note>, by which the Sun's apogee is established to have been at 13 and a half degrees of Gemini in Ptolemy's time; in our time, at the beginning of Cancer. We come two degrees closer to the opinion of Arzahel. The computation of Albategnius's location of the apogee according to those tables exceeds the truth by 1 degree; we, not without merit, fall short of it by 6 degrees. For the Doctor, my Master, can by no means depart from Ptolemy and his own observations, both because he saw and grasped his own with his own eyes, and also because he perceives that Ptolemy examined the motions of the Sun and Moon with the greatest diligence and by means of eclipses, and established them as accurately as was possible.

<header>FIRST PART.</header>

However, the fact that we are forced to differ from him <note>referring to Ptolemy</note> by nearly one degree is taught to us by the motion of the apogee <note>the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth/Sun</note>, which he himself thought to be fixed; for this reason, he also applied less care in examining it in this location.

<margin>Tables of the Sun's motion.</margin> You have here the opinion of my Lord Teacher <note>Nicolaus Copernicus</note> regarding the motion of the sun. He has therefore composed tables by which, for any proposed time, one may gather the true position of the solar apogee, the true eccentricity, the true equations, the equal motions of the Sun relative to the fixed stars, and the mean equinoxes; from these, he collects the true position of the Sun corresponding with the observations of all ages. From this it is manifest that the Tables of Hipparchus, <margin>The Tables of others are only temporary.</margin> Ptolemy, Theon, Albategnius, Arzachel, and the Alphonsine tables—which were partially forged from these—are only temporary, and can last at most 200 years, until a notable diversity in the length of the year, the eccentricity, the equation, etc., occurs. <margin>The Copernican tables are perpetual.</margin> This happens by a similar certain reasoning in the motions and appearances of the remaining Planets. Not without merit, therefore, can the Astronomy of my Lord Doctor and Teacher be called "perpetual," as the observations of all ages testify, and as the observations of posterity will undoubtedly confirm. Furthermore, he computes his motions and the positions of the apsides <note>the points of greatest or least distance in an orbit</note> from the first star of Aries, since the equality of motions is measured from the fixed stars; then, by adding the true precession, he collects and establishes how much the true positions of the planets differ from the true equinox in each individual age.

<margin>Pico della Mirandola.</margin> If such a doctrine of celestial matters had existed a little before our age, Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, who wrote a famous critique of astrology</note> would have had no occasion in his eighth and ninth books for attacking not only astrology, but also astronomy. For we ourselves see daily how notably the common calculation <note>the traditional Alfonsine tables</note> deviates from the truth.

<margin>Special consideration of the length of the year from the equinoxes.</margin> Many people, in the reform of the Calendar, enumerate the various lengths of the year established by Authors, but they do so confusedly; nor do they determine anything for certain, which is truly surprising among such great Mathematicians. <margin>It is surprising that this is still done by many today.</margin>

<margin>Four causes of the unequal motion of the Sun and the revolving Year.</margin> You see, however, most learned Master Schöner, four causes from the aforesaid for the unequal motion of the Sun from the equinoxes: the inequality of the precession of the equinoxes; the inequality of the motion of the Sun in the ecliptic; the decrease of the eccentricity; and finally, the progression of the apogee for a twofold reason. For these same reasons, the year measured from the equinoxes is by no means <note>original: "minimè"</note>

...could be equal. Indeed, Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, 2nd-century astronomer</note> can easily be forgiven for assuming that equality should be taken from the equinoxes, since he established that the fixed stars move uniformly forward and that the position of the apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note> was fixed, nor could he perceive that the eccentricity of the Sun decreases. However, how others wish to excuse themselves, I do not see. For even if we were to grant them that the stars and the Sun's apogee are carried by the same motion in the order of the signs, and that therefore nothing changes in reality regarding the time from the true equinox—but rather that every variation happens because of a defect in the instruments (which, however, would be most absurd to say in our age)—even if the progress of the Sun's apogee changes the length of the year very little: nevertheless, it does not follow that the Sun always returns to the true equinox regularly in an equal time, just as we say the Moon regularly moves away from the mean apogee of the Epicycle <note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</note> and returns to the same in an equal time, as the most learned Marco Beneventano <note>An Italian mathematician and monk, d. 1524</note> reports according to the opinion of the Alfonsines <note>Followers of the Alfonsine Tables, medieval astronomical data</note>. For since we certainly cannot deny that the eccentricity of the Sun changes, let them see for themselves how they can affirm that the length of the year observed from the equinox does not change due to the mutation of the angle of diversity from the mean motion. I certainly congratulate the republic and all students, whom the labor of my Lord Doctor Preceptor <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> is destined to benefit, that we now possess a certain account of the year's diversity.

<margin>Reconciliation of the diversity of the year between Hipparchus and Ptolemy.</margin>But so that you may more easily perceive all these things in your mind, most learned Master Schöner <note>Johannes Schöner, the recipient of this letter</note>, behold, I set it before your eyes in numbers, so that I may finally respond to those things which I promised above. Let the Sun be at the point of the mean vernal equinox, at which time of the observation of the autumnal equinox made by Hipparchus <note>Greek astronomer, 2nd century BCE</note>, in the 147th year before the birth of the Lord, it preceded the first star of Aries by 3 degrees and 29 minutes: let the Sun proceed from that same point of the eighth sphere, so that in a sidereal year (namely, <margin>Magnitude of the sidereal year.</margin>365 days, 15 minutes, and nearly 24 seconds <note>original: "365. dieb. 15. minutis, 24. secundis ferè"</note>) it returns to the same point. Because, however, the mean equinox moves to meet the Sun during the sidereal year by nearly 50 seconds, it happens that the Sun reaches the mean vernal point sooner than the place from which it departed, where <margin>Magnitude of the equal tropical year.</margin>evidently the Sun and the mean equinox were joined in the same point of the ecliptic. Therefore, the year measured from the mean equinox is smaller than the sidereal year, which according to our hypotheses is nearly 365 days, 14 minutes, and 34 seconds.

is gathered to be nearly so. But if we inquire how many days and parts of a day—with respect to the mean equinox <note>The "mean equinox" refers to a theoretical point moving at a uniform rate, used as a baseline for astronomical calculations.</note>—increase in the 285 years that passed between Hipparchus and Ptolemy, we shall find nearly 69 days and 9 minutes. Therefore, 2 days and 6 minutes would be missing if we assume that a quarter part of a day increases in each individual year. Let us therefore weigh the remaining causes as well, until we find only one day, minus a twentieth part of a day, to be missing. At the time of Hipparchus's observation, the true equinox preceded the mean equinox according to the precedence of the signs <note>This refers to the retrograde motion of the equinoxes, known as precession.</note> by nearly 21 minutes of the starry ecliptic, at which point the Sun then was; but in the time of Ptolemy, the true equinox followed the mean one by nearly 47 minutes. Therefore, when the Sun in Ptolemy's time had reached the 21st minute before the point of the mean equinox (where it had left the true equinox in Hipparchus's time), it was not yet the true equinox, nor even when it reached the mean equinox; but after it transcended that point by forty-four minutes, 
<margin>Book 2, chapter 19.</margin>it fell "into the center of the earth," as Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman naturalist.</note> says—namely, into the place of the true equinox. Therefore, there were 1 degree and 8 minutes for the Sun to ascend, which arc it completed in 1 day and 8 minutes of true motion. I keep this to the side, and I weigh how much the angle of diversity <note>The "angle of diversity" likely refers to the equation of time or the difference between mean and true solar motion.</note> decreased in this place, and I find that nearly one minute of a day corresponds to it. It is clear, therefore, from the days computed from the mean equinox, that a time of 1 day and 9 minutes is added; wherefore Ptolemy also correctly reported that between his own observation and that of Hipparchus, from true equinox to true equinox, there are 285 years, 70 days, and 18 minutes: and accordingly, 57 minutes of a day are missing, which is also made known from the subtraction of 1 day and 9 minutes from the 2 days and 6 minutes previously found missing with respect to the mean equinox.

<margin>The annual diversity between Ptolemy and Al-Battani.</margin>But let us speak of the deficiency of 7 days between Ptolemy and Al-Battani <note>original: "Albategnium"; a famous 9th-century Arab astronomer.</note>, which is notable for that reason, because the interval of time is greater—namely, 743 years—wherefore all the causes will be more conspicuous. In the time of Ptolemy, the mean equinox preceded the very first star of Aries by nearly 7 degrees and 28 minutes in the precedence of the signs. However, with the mean equinox subsequently moving to meet the Sun, as was said, it happened that in the intervening years between Ptolemy and Al-Battani, nearly 180 days and 14 minutes increased through additions with respect to the mean equinox. Therefore, there will be missing...

5 days, 31 minutes; if we apply this to the mean equinox <note>The "mean" equinox refers to a theoretical point moving at a constant speed, as opposed to the "true" equinox which accounts for celestial irregularities.</note>, let us compare it to that which results when one day is collected every four years. Furthermore, in the time of Ptolemy, the true equinox had left the mean equinox by 47 minutes in the order of the signs <note>original: "in signorum consequentiam" — meaning moving forward through the zodiac</note>; however, in the age of Al-Battani <note>Albategnius (c. 858–929 AD), a famous Arab astronomer</note>, the true equinox was 22 minutes before the mean equinox, in the preceding order of the signs. Therefore, the Sun arrived at the true equinox earlier than at the mean, or where it had left the true equinoctial point, which is contrary to the previous example. Thus, as much time as corresponds to 1 degree and 9 minutes will be subtracted from the days with respect to the mean equinox: and it will be added to the remainder, namely 5 days and 31 minutes. And because we must proceed in the same way with the difference of the angle of diversity due to the decrease in eccentricity <note>The "eccentricity" refers to how much the Earth's orbit deviates from a perfect circle</note>, to which 30 minutes of a day correspond, one day and 30 minutes will be subtracted from the average time due to the change in the angle of diversity and the unequal motion of precession—with the other two causes of the Sun's unequal motion mixed in. The true addition from the time of Ptolemy to the time of Al-Battani's observation will emerge as 178 days and 44 minutes. But that same decrease, added to 5 days and 31 minutes, shows that 7 days and 1 minute have fallen away, which was what needed to be shown.

->Diagram of the Anomaly of the precession of the Equinoxes<-
->and the unequal magnitude of the<-
->Tropical year.<-

**AB** is the Ecliptic <note>The Sun's apparent path across the sky</note>. **A** is the first star of Aries. **C** is the mean equinox, or the intersection of the mean equinoctial and the ecliptic. Its precession from **A** is uniform. **DE** is the diameter of the circle of the anomaly of the equinoxes, through which the true equinoctial point moves back and forth by libration <note>A slow "rocking" or oscillation</note>. **F** is the location of the true equinox, or the intersection of the true equinoctial and the ecliptic, in the time of Hipparchus; **G**, however, is in the time of Ptolemy, and **H** in the time of Al-Battani. **CF** is 21 minutes <note>original: "scr." for scrupula, an old term for minutes of a degree</note>. **CG** is 47 minutes. **GH** is 22 minutes. **FG** is 68 minutes, but **GH** is 69 minutes, of which **CE** or **CD** is 70 minutes. Reinhold <note>Erasmus Reinhold (1511–1553), who calculated the Prutenic Tables based on Copernicus's work</note> in the Prutenic Tables calculates **CE** or **CD** as 1 degree, 11 minutes, 22 seconds, and 30 thirds.

Such a great labor it was to restore the motions of the fixed stars and the Sun by such a method, so that from the connection of their motions, the true ratio of the annual quantity from the equinoxes could be gathered. Therefore, God has given a kingdom in astronomy without end to that most learned man, my Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note>, which may the Lord deign to govern, protect, and increase for the restoration of astronomical truth, Amen.
