# ->FIRST PART<-

<margin>*Concerning the theories of the remaining planets.*</margin>

I had briefly intended for you, most learned Master Schöner, to write out the entire treatment of the motion of the Moon and the remaining planets, just as I did for the fixed stars and the Sun, so that you might understand what benefits are destined to flow—as if from a most abundant spring—from the books of my Teacher <note>The "Teacher" refers to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note> to students of mathematics and to all posterity. However, when I saw that the work was growing too large for the present circumstances, I decided that a separate Narrative should be established concerning this matter. Therefore, what I deem necessary to precede this, as if to prepare the way, I shall set forth in this place; I will intersperse certain general remarks regarding the hypotheses of the motion of the Moon and the other planets, so that you may conceive a greater hope for this entire Work, and perceive what necessity compelled him to adopt other hypotheses, or theories. Since at the beginning of our Narrative I mentioned that my Teacher...

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the necessity of reconciling ancient observations with modern mathematical rigor. The catchword "fumpti" (taken) connects to the ongoing argument regarding the assumptions made by previous astronomers.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
# NARRATION

<margin>Other hypotheses had to be assumed by Copernicus, because he saw that the common ones did not satisfy the permanence of the motions.</margin>...to accommodate the regularity of the motions, which was known to him from the small amount of time elapsed since the observations, quite elegantly. Therefore, he rightly and prudently chose those hypotheses which seemed more consonant both with reason and our senses, and which the greatest craftsmen before him had used, as this was more plausible. However, since the observations of all craftsmen, and heaven itself, and mathematical reasoning convince us that the hypotheses of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose geocentric model placed Earth at the center.</note> and the common ones are by no means sufficient to demonstrate the perpetual and mutually consistent connection and harmony of celestial things, nor to collect them into tables and rules, it was necessary for my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note> to devise new hypotheses. By assuming these, he could deduce such accounts of the motions through geometry and arithmetic with good consequence, just as the Ancients and Ptolemy once perceived when raised on high <gloss>with the divine eye of the soul</gloss> <note>original: "τῷ θείῳ ψυχῆς ὄμματι"</note>; and such as diligent observations today teach those who gather the traces of the Ancients in the sky. Thus, indeed, students will see in the future what use Ptolemy and the rest of the ancient authors have, whereby they may recall them as if they had been excluded from the schools until now, and restore them to their former honor, as if they had returned by <term>postliminium</term> <note>A legal term referring to the restoration of rights to a person returning from captivity.</note>. As the poet says, *There is no desire for the unknown*: therefore it is no wonder why Ptolemy, along with all Antiquity, has hitherto lain neglected in the shadows, a fact which you, most excellent Master Schöner <note>Johannes Schöner, the noted mathematician and geographer to whom this letter is addressed.</note>, have no doubt often grieved over, along with other good and learned men.

The calculation of eclipses alone seems to protect the honor of astronomy among the unskillful masses. Yet we see daily how much this differs today from common calculation, both in time and in the predicted magnitude. Since we should by no means reject the most accurate observations of Ptolemy and other excellent authors as false and rejected when establishing astronomical tables—as we see some people doing—unless we detect that some manifest error has crept in as the ages have passed: for what is more human than to be mistaken and deceived sometimes, even by the appearance of right, especially in these most difficult, most abstruse, and by no means obvious matters? In demonstrating the motion of the Moon, my Master assumes theories and accounts of motions of such a kind that it may appear that the most excellent ancient philosophers were by no means blind in their observations. For which reason, just as above regarding the year from the equinoxes...

<summary>The author argues that Copernicus developed new hypotheses not to spite the ancients, but to restore the harmony of celestial motions that Ptolemy's system could no longer explain. He emphasizes that these new theories actually validate the accuracy of ancient observations which had fallen into neglect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial mechanics, specifically addressing the discrepancies in the observed size and distance of the Moon compared to traditional Ptolemaic models.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>FIRST PART.</header>

Just as we have shown the increase and decrease of the [planetary] distance to be regular, so too, from a diligent examination of the motions of the Sun and Moon, it can be deduced what the true distances of the Sun, Moon, and Earth from one another were in each age; and what the ratios of the diameters of the Sun, Moon, and the shadow <note>The Earth's shadow during an eclipse.</note> were found to be at different times, so that a certain account of the parallax <term>diversity of aspects: the apparent shift in position of a celestial body when viewed from different points</term> of the Sun and Moon might also be maintained. Our countryman Regiomontanus <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg (1436–1476), a celebrated German mathematician and astronomer.</note> says in Book 5, Proposition 22 of the *Epitome*: *But it is a marvel that in quadrature <note>When the Moon is at a 90-degree angle from the Sun, such as a half-moon.</note>, the Moon does not appear so large when it is at the perigee of the epicycle <note>The point in its orbit closest to Earth.</note>; for if it were fully illuminated, it ought to appear four times the size it appears at opposition <note>Full Moon</note> when it is at the apogee of the epicycle <note>The point furthest from Earth.</note>.* Timochates and Menelaus <note>Ancient Greek astronomers whose observations were used by Ptolemy.</note> also perceived the same, as they always use the same lunar diameter in their observations of the stars. But experience also taught my Master <note>A reference to Nicolaus Copernicus, the author's teacher.</note> that the variations of parallax and the size of the Moon's body at every distance from the Sun differ little or not at all from those which occur at conjunction <note>New Moon</note> and opposition; thus it is manifest that the Moon cannot be assigned an eccentric orbit <note>A circular orbit where the Earth is not at the center.</note> as is commonly accepted.

He proposes, therefore, that the Moon's orbit encompasses the Earth with its adjacent elements, the center of whose carrying sphere <term>deferent: the large circle along which the center of a smaller circle (epicycle) moves</term> is the center of the Earth, upon which the center of the Moon's epicycle is carried uniformly. <margin>See below in the appendix on the Theory of the Moon.</margin> However, that second variation which the Moon appears to have relative to the Sun, he resolves in this way: He assumes the body of the Moon is moved by an epicycle upon a homocentric <note>Sharing the same center.</note> epicycle. That is, to the first epicycle—which appears in the middle time between conjunctions or oppositions and quadratures—he attaches another small epicycle carrying the body of the Moon. He demonstrates that the proportion of the diameter of the first epicycle to the diameter of the second is as 1097 to 237. <margin>Book 4, chapter 8.</note> Furthermore, the logic of the motions is as follows: The inclined circle <note>The Moon's orbit, which is tilted relative to the ecliptic.</note> maintains its ratio of motion as before, except that it derives its uniformity from the fixed stars. The deferent, which is also concentric, moves regularly and uniformly upon its own center (namely, the Earth), likewise departing uniformly and regularly from the line of the mean motion of the Sun. The first epicycle also revolves uniformly upon its own center, carrying the center of the small second epicycle—in the upper part toward the preceding signs <note>Westward/Retrograde</note>, and in the lower part toward the following signs <note>Eastward/Prograde</note>. He proposes that this motion is equal and regular from the true apogee, which is shown in the upper part of the first epicycle by a line projected from the center of the Earth through its center to the circumference. The Moon, however, in the circum—

<summary>The author discusses the flaws in the Ptolemaic lunar model, specifically that the Moon's apparent size does not change as drastically as the old theory predicts. He introduces Copernicus's revised model using a system of two epicycles on a concentric deferent to better match observations.</summary>
<keywords>Regiomontanus, Copernicus, lunar theory, epicycle, deferent, quadrature, parallax, lunar diameter, astronomy</keywords>

<page-num>110</page-num>
-># NARRATION<-

The circumference of the small and second epicycle <note>A smaller circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger circle</note> also moves regularly and uniformly, departing from the true apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the center</note> of the small epicycle; this is clearly shown by a line extending from the center of the first epicycle through the center of the second to its own circumference. And this is the rule of its motion: that the Moon itself revolves twice in this smaller epicycle during one period of the carrying circle <note>The deferent</note>, so that in every conjunction and opposition <note>New and Full Moons</note> the Moon is found in the perigee <note>The point closest to the center</note> of the small epicycle, but in the quadratures <note>The quarter moons</note> it is found in its apogee. This is the mechanism, or hypothesis, by which the Master Preceptor <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> excludes all the aforementioned inconsistencies, and which he demonstrates to the eye to satisfy all appearances, just as can also be gathered from his tables.

<margin>Book Five.</margin>
Furthermore, most learned Master Schöner, just as you see us here liberated from the equant <note>A mathematical device used by Ptolemy to explain planetary speed changes, which Copernicus found physically impossible</note> in the case of the Moon, and having adopted such a theory that corresponds to experience and all observations: so also in the remaining planets he removes the equants, assigning to each of the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> only one epicycle and an eccentric <note>A circular orbit whose center is offset from the Earth/Sun</note>, both of which move uniformly about their own centers, and the planet makes equal revolutions in the epicycle with the eccentric. For Venus and Mercury, however, he assigns an "eccentric of an eccentric." He teaches, however, that the motions of the former can similarly be delivered through an eccentric of an eccentric, and the revolutions of the latter through eccentric-epicycles. For that the planets are seen every year as direct, stationary, retrograde, near to, and remote from the earth, etc., he demonstrates can happen through another regular motion of the terrestrial globe, beyond those established above, which is: that the Sun occupies the center of the universe, while the Earth is carried around in the place of the Sun in an eccentric circle, which he has seen fit to call the **Great Orb**. And truly it is something divine, that from the regular and uniform motions of the single terrestrial globe, a certain system of celestial things must depend. But first, he assumed that many appearances in the sky could happen, or certainly be most conveniently saved <note>A term meaning to provide a mathematical explanation for observed phenomena</note>, by the mobility of the earth; the indubitable precession of the equinoxes (as you have heard) and the change in the obliquity of the ecliptic <note>The tilt of the Earth's axis</note> led him to depart from the ancients.

<margin>Principal reasons why one must depart from the hypotheses of the Ancient Astronomers.</margin>
Next, because that same decrease in the eccentricity of the Sun is noticed in like manner and proportionally in the eccentricities of the remaining planets.

Afterward, because the planets [have] the centers of their carrying circles around the Sun,

<page-num>III</page-num>
<header>FIRST</header>

->Indeed, it appears that the Sun holds the center of the universe.<- That the most ancient thinkers felt the same (to say nothing of the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras who suggested the Earth might move</note> for now) is sufficiently clear from what **Pliny** says: that **Venus** and **Mercury** do not depart further from the **Sun** than certain prescribed limits. He was undoubtedly following the best authors, who held that these planets have their orbits <term>apsides: the points in an orbit furthest from or nearest to the center</term> turned around the Sun; hence it was necessary that the mean motion of the Sun should also apply to them.
<margin>Witnesses of this are found in the abundance of writings "On the Heavens"</margin>
<margin>Pliny, Book 2, Chapter 17.</margin>

Furthermore, when he says that the course of **Mars** is unobservable, and beyond the other difficulties in correcting the motions of Mars, there is no doubt that it sometimes admits a greater diversity of aspect <note>parallax</note> than the Sun itself; thus it seems impossible for the Earth to occupy the center of the world. Moreover, although this same fact is easily gathered from the relationship of **Saturn** and **Jupiter** to us in their morning and evening rising, it is especially and most greatly noticed in the variation of the risings of Mars. For because the star of Mars has a very dim light, it does not deceive the sight as much as Venus or Jupiter; rather, it reflects a change in magnitude in proportion to its distance from the Earth. Consequently, since Mars in its evening rising appears to equal the star of Jupiter in size—so that it is distinguished only by its fiery glow—yet in its appearance and disappearance <note>rising and setting</note> it can hardly be distinguished from stars of the second magnitude, it follows that it approaches closest to the Earth at its evening rising, and conversely is as far away as possible in the morning. This certainly cannot happen by the logic of an <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</term>. It is therefore clear that another location must be assigned to the Earth to restore the motions of Mars and the other planets.

Fourthly, my **Lord Teacher** <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> saw that by this single reasoning it could conveniently happen that—as is most proper to circular motion—all revolutions of the circles in the world would move equally and regularly about their own centers, and not about centers belonging to others.

Fifthly, since it must be established by Mathematicians no less than by Physicians, as **Galen** repeatedly emphasizes: <gloss>Nature does nothing in vain</gloss> <note>original Greek: Μηδὲν εἰκῆ τω φύσιν ἐργάζεθαι</note>; and: <gloss>Our Creator is so wise that each of the parts ordained by Him possesses not just one use for its time, but often two, three, or more</gloss> <note>original Greek: οὕτως εἶναι τὸν δήμιον ὃν ἡμῶν σῶφον...</note>. Therefore, since we see that by this single motion of the Earth, an almost infinite number of appearances are satisfied, why should we not attribute to God, the founder of nature, that same skill which we see common clockmakers possess?
<margin>These words are in Book 10 of "On the Use of Parts."</margin>

<meta>catchword: heavenly</meta>

...who most studiously take care not to insert any small wheel into an instrument that is either superfluous or whose function could be more conveniently supplied by another wheel with a slightly changed position. And what would move my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> so that he, as a Mathematician <term>Mathematicus: at this time, the term encompassed astronomy, geometry, and the study of the heavens</term>, would not assume a suitable reasoning for the motion of the terrestrial globe? Especially when he saw that by assuming such a hypothesis for establishing a certain doctrine of heavenly matters, a single eighth sphere—and that one immobile—would suffice for us; with the Sun immobile in the center of the universe; while in the motions of the remaining planets, eccentric-epicycles, or eccentric-eccentrics, or epicycles of epicycles would be enough.

To these points is added the fact that the motion of the Earth in its own orbit accounts for the "arguments" <note>In astronomy, the 'argument' is the angle or parameter used to determine a planet's position</note> of all the planets except for the Moon. This single motion alone appears to be the cause of all the diversity of motion which is seen in the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> in relation to the Sun, and in Venus and Mercury around the Sun. Finally, this motion ensures that each of the planets is content with at least a single deviation in the latitude of the planet's carrying-circle; thus, the motions of the planets principally demand such hypotheses.

Sixth, and lastly, this especially moved my Teacher, the Doctor: he saw that the chief cause of all uncertainty in Astronomy was that the practitioners of this science (I say this with the pardon of the divine Ptolemy, the parent of Astronomy) recalled their theories and methods for correcting the motion of heavenly bodies too loosely to that rule which warns that the order and motions of the heavenly spheres consist of a most perfect system. For even if we grant them their honor most amply (as is only fair), it was nonetheless to be wished that in establishing the harmony of motions they had imitated Musicians. Musicians, when one string is either tightened or loosened, form and temper the sounds of all the others with the greatest care and diligence until they all together return the desired concord, and no dissonance is noted in any of them. If Albategnius <note>The Latinized name of Al-Battani, a 9th-century Arab astronomer</note>, to speak of him for a moment, had followed this in his work, we would undoubtedly have a more certain account of all motions today. For it is likely that the Alfonsine tables <note>13th-century astronomical tables sponsored by Alfonso X of Castile</note> took a great deal from him; and if this one thing had been neglected, at some point (if the mind is willing to admit the truth) the ruin of all Astronomy would have been feared. In the common principles of Astronomy, it was indeed possible to see that all appearances were related to the mean motion of the Sun...

<summary>The author argues that Copernicus's heliocentric model simplifies the heavens by replacing complex planetary "wheels" with the Earth's motion and compares the necessary harmony of astronomical systems to the tuning of a musical instrument.</summary>

# NARRATION

You see, therefore, what kind of hypotheses for saving the movements <note>original: "saluandos motus" — a technical term in astronomy meaning to create a mathematical model that matches observed data</note> my Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> had to assume, once these principles were established.

<margin>Transition to the enumeration of the new hypotheses of the whole of Astronomy.</margin> I interrupt your thoughts, most distinguished Sir, for I see that while you hear the reasons for renewing the hypotheses of Astronomy—investigated by my Teacher with excellent learning and the greatest zeal—you are thinking to yourself what the suitable method for the hypotheses of this reborn Astronomy will finally be. * Furthermore, you, along with other true mathematicians and all good men, judge that those kind of people <margin>He means those who deny epicycles and eccentrics.</margin> who attempt to lead all the stars around in the ether at their own whim, as if they had been cast into chains, are worthy of pity rather than hatred. And since you are not ignorant of the place that hypotheses, or theories, hold among astronomers, and how much the <term>Mathematician</term> <note>One who focuses on the geometry of planetary paths</note> differs from the <term>Physicist</term> <note>One who focuses on the physical reality and causes of the heavens</note>: I feel that you also determine that one must follow where the observations and the testimonies of heaven itself draw and redraw us; and that every difficulty must be overcome by enduring it, with God as guide, and with Mathematics and indefatigable study as companions.

Therefore, if anyone determines that they should look toward the highest and principal goal of Astronomy, they will join us in giving thanks to my Teacher, and will consider that that saying of Aristotle pertains to them: <note>original Greek: "Τὰς μὲν ἀκροάσεις ἀνάγκας..."</note> "When one seeks out the truth, one must then hold to what has been discovered." <margin>Book 2, On the Heavens.</margin> And since Aristotle confirms for us, by the example of Callippus and his own, that the causes of the <note>original Greek: "τῶυ φαινομένων"</note> appearances <note>The observed movements of stars and planets</note> must be assigned, and that Astronomy must be restored according as the diverse motions of the celestial bodies present themselves: <margin>Book 21, Metaphysics.</margin> I would hope that not even Averroes <note>A 12th-century philosopher known for his commentaries on Aristotle</note>—that not-entirely-merciful critic <note>original: "Aristarchum" — used here as a metaphor for a harsh critic</note> of Ptolemy—would receive my Teacher's hypotheses too bitterly, if only he were willing to look upon the study of nature with fair eyes. It is so far from the case that I would think Ptolemy so addicted and sworn to his own hypotheses (if he were granted to return to life) that, in order to build up a certain doctrine of celestial things, where he found the royal road blocked by the ruins of so many centuries and made impassable, he would not seek another path over lands and seas, since he could no longer climb through the air and the open sky to the desired goal. For what else should I decide about that man, whose words are these? <margin>Book 9, Ethics. These things satisfy those who cared to climb elsewhere when the house was falling.</margin> <note>original Greek: "οὔτε τὰ ἀποδείκτους..."</note> "Neither can those things assumed without proof be found without some path and attention, if once they are found to be in harmony with the appearances, even if the manner of their comprehension is difficult to accept. Since, in general, none of the first principles is by nature easy to explain..."

<summary>The narrator defends the necessity of Copernicus's new astronomical hypotheses, arguing that even ancient authorities like Aristotle and Ptolemy would prioritize observed celestial data over outdated mathematical models.</summary>
<keywords>hypotheses, Copernicus, Aristotle, Ptolemy, mathematical models, celestial motion, Averroes, astronomy vs physics</keywords>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>FIRST PART.</header>

<margin>Book 1 of the Ethics.</margin>
<margin>Book 2 of the Politics.</margin>
<margin>The distribution of the Universe.</margin>
<margin><note>original Greek: "μᾶλλον. α. τὸ ἐλάττον"</note> The greater and the lesser.</margin>
<margin>The order of the spheres of the World.</margin>
<margin>The highest starry sphere, fixed and immobile.</margin>

->nature allows:<- How modestly and prudently Aristotle speaks concerning the doctrine of celestial motions can be seen throughout his books. He says elsewhere, <note>original Greek: "πεπαιδευμένῳ γάρ ἐσιν..."</note> *for it is the mark of an educated man to look for precision in each class of things just so far as the nature of the subject admits.* Since, however, in both Physics and Astronomy, the process moves for the most part from effects and observations toward first principles, I certainly maintain that Aristotle—having heard the reasons for these new <term>hypotheses</term> <note>provisional explanations or models of the heavens</note>—would have most diligently examined the disputes concerning the heavy, the light, circular motion, and the motion and rest of the earth. Thus, without a doubt, he would candidly have confessed what had been demonstrated by him in these matters, and what had been assumed as a principle without demonstration. For this reason, I believed he would have supported the Master, my Teacher, especially since it is well established that Plato most rightly said (as it is reported), <note>original Greek: "τὸν Ἀριστοτέλην ἀποδέιξας εἶναι φιλόσοφον"</note> *Aristotle has proven himself a philosopher.* On the other hand, if he were to break out into harsh words—though I cannot persuade myself otherwise—he would surely exclaim and deplore the condition of this most beautiful part of philosophy with these words: <note>original Greek: "πάνυ ἀλλ᾽ ἔλεγκται..."</note> *It is entirely refuted; they dream about geometry and its related studies regarding this place, beyond which it is impossible for them to see, as they leave these things unmoving and are unable to give an account of them.* And he would add: <note>original Greek: "πολλῶν τοῖς ἀθανατοῖς..."</note> *One must give great thanks to the immortal gods for knowing such a reason from the natural philosophers.*

But indeed, since these matters belong not so much to this place as to some other disputation, I shall proceed to relate in order the remaining hypotheses of the Master, my Teacher, freely and so that some light may be added to those things we said above. * Aristotle says, it is most true that that which exists is the cause for subsequent things being true. Thus, when my Teacher determined that he must assume such hypotheses as would contain the causes for the observations of previous ages to be confirmed as true—and as is to be hoped, would be the causes so that in the future all astronomical predictions of the <term>phenomena</term> <note>the observed appearances of the stars and planets</note> would be found true—he first, having overcome no small labors, established by hypothesis that the **Starry Sphere**, which we commonly call the eighth, was created by God for this purpose: to be that domicile which by its embrace would encompass the whole nature of things. Therefore, He created it as the place of the universe, fixed and immobile. And since motion is not perceived except by comparison to something fixed, just as a ship...

<summary>The author argues that Aristotle would have embraced new astronomical models (like the Earth's motion) if presented with modern observations, and describes the fixed outer sphere of stars as the immobile "domicile" of the universe.</summary>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>NARRATIVE</header>

<margin>Why the Creator willed that the sphere be marked with so many stars on all sides.</margin>
Just as those sailing the circle of the sea, to whom no lands are any longer visible, see only the sky and water on all sides, and feel no motion of the ship when the sea is calm from winds—even though they are carried with such speed that they may traverse several great miles in an hour: for this reason, God marked that sphere <note>The "sphere" here refers to the firmament or the outermost shell of the universe.</note> with stellar globules for our sake, so that by observing them—fixed as they are in their place—we might perceive the positions and motions of the other spheres and the planets contained within.

<margin>The Sun in the middle of the World, immobile.</margin>
Next, and consistent with these facts, God placed the Sun—His administrator in nature and the King of the entire universe—in the middle of this theater, conspicuous in its Divine majesty,

<margin>Pontanus, Book 1 of *Urania*.</margin>
->To whose rhythms the Gods are moved, and the world<-
->Receives its laws, and keeps its prescribed covenants.<-
<note>original: "Ad cuius numeros & Dij moueantur, & orbis Accipiat leges, præfcriptaǵ, fœdera feruet." Giovanni Pontanus was a 15th-century humanist poet who wrote about the heavens.</note>

<margin>Below the Sphere of the stars: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars.</margin>
The remaining spheres are distributed in this manner: the first place below the firmament, or the sphere of the stars, is allotted to the sphere of Saturn, within which Jupiter is contained, and then Mars. The Sun, however, is surrounded by the sphere of Mercury, and then the sphere of Venus, whereby the centers of the five planetary spheres are found near the Sun.
<margin>Around the Sun: Mercury, Venus.</margin>
But within the concave surface of the sphere of Mars, and the convex surface of Venus, since a sufficiently large space remains, the globe of the Earth with its adjacent elements, surrounded by the Lunar sphere, is carried around by a certain **Great Orb** <note>The "Great Orb" (Orbis Magnus) is the term used by Copernicus for the Earth's annual orbital path around the Sun.</note> which encompasses within itself the spheres of Mercury and Venus, as well as the Sun; thus, the Earth has its motions just like one of the stars among the planets.
<margin>Within Mars, the Great Orb containing the Earth with the elements and the sphere of the Moon.</margin>

That this order and arrangement of the Spheres of the World was not first devised by Copernicus, but was handed down by ancient Philosophers, is witnessed by Archimedes in his book *The Sand Reckoner*, in which he writes concerning Aristarchus: *"Aristarchus of Samos, refuting these things in the writings of the Astrologers (concerning the common hypotheses in which the Earth is placed as the center of the World), published certain positions from which it follows that the World is many times larger than it is said to be. For he posits that the Fixed Stars and the Sun remain immobile; but that the Earth is carried around the Sun according to the circumference of a circle which is situated in the middle of the course. He posits that the Sphere of the fixed stars, situated around the same center as the Sun, is of such great magnitude that the circle in which he posits the Earth is carried has the same proportion to the distance of the fixed stars as the center of a sphere has to its surface (that is, a proportion that is not at all perceptible), etc." It is manifest from Ptolemy (Book 3, Chapter 2) that Aristarchus flourished about the 44th year after the death of Alexander the Great—that is, about the 280th year before the birth of Christ, or 1,876 years before our present age.*

<summary>This page describes the heliocentric (Sun-centered) arrangement of the universe, explaining that the stars serve as fixed reference points for human observation. It credits the ancient Greek astronomer Aristarchus of Samos with originating the theory long before Copernicus.</summary>

As I more diligently weigh this distribution of the entire universe according to the opinion of my Master <note>likely referring to a specific teacher or influential astronomer like Copernicus or Kepler, depending on the manuscript's context</note>, I understand that Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author and natural philosopher</note> felt both clearly and rightly when he said: *To investigate the outside of the World, or the heavens, by whose circumference all things are covered, is neither of interest to men, nor can the human mind grasp such a conjecture.* And he adds: *It is sacred, immense, all in all, or rather itself the whole, finite and yet like the infinite, etc.* <note>original Latin: "Mundi, seu cæli... finitus & infinito fimilis."</note> For when we follow my Master, there is nothing outside the concavity of the starry sphere for us to investigate.

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>THE NARRATION</header>

<margin>All of Nature is enclosed within the Starry Heaven.</margin>
...[we shall know], unless it be as much as the Holy Scriptures <note>original: "Sacræ literæ"</note> have willed us to know of these things; likewise, the way to establish anything outside of this concave shell will be closed off. Therefore, we shall admire and contemplate all this remaining Nature—as if it were something most holy, enclosed by God within the starry heaven—with thanksgiving. To scrutinize and understand this Nature, He has enriched us in many ways with infinite instruments and gifts, and has made us fit for the task; and indeed, we shall progress as far as He Himself has willed, nor shall we attempt to transgress the limits established by Him.

<margin>The World is immense, and like unto the infinite.</margin>
Furthermore, that the world is immense and truly like unto the infinite, even regarding its concave interior, is generally admitted from the fact that we see all the stars twinkle—except for the planets, even Saturn, which, being nearest to their heaven, is carried in the greatest circle. But this same fact is far more manifest from the hypotheses of my Lord Teacher <note>A reference to Nicolaus Copernicus, the author's mentor.</note> through demonstrations <note>original: "ἀποδείξεις" (apodeixeis)</note>. For since the Great Orb <note>The "Orbis Magnus," or the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun.</note> carrying the Earth has a perceptible ratio to the orbs of the five planets—from which it is demonstrated that every diversity of appearances in these planets arises through their relationship to the Sun—and since every horizon on Earth intersects the orb of the stars into equal parts, as a great circle of the universe would, and the orbs of their revolutions are proved to have equality from the fixed stars: it is clear enough that the orb of the stars is most like unto the infinite. This is because the Great Orb, when compared to it, vanishes, and all the phenomena <note>original: "τὰ φαινόμενα" (ta phainomena)</note> are observed no differently than if the Earth sat in the middle of the universe.

Furthermore, although the symmetry and connection of the motions and orbs—which are preserved by assuming the aforementioned hypotheses—are admirable and worthy both of God the Creator and of these divine bodies, I would assert that they can be more quickly conceived by the mind (because of the affinity it has with heaven) than they can be expressed by any human voice. Just as in demonstrations, these most pleasant things are accustomed to be imprinted on our minds not so much by words as by perfect and absolute ideas, so to speak. Nevertheless, even in the general contemplation of the hypotheses, one can 
<margin>Things which are desired in many ways in the common hypotheses, and about which...</margin>
see how an ineffable harmony and a consensus of all things offers itself. For besides the fact that in the common <note>The "vulgar" or traditional Ptolemaic geocentric system.</note> hypotheses there appeared to be no end to the fashioning of spheres, the orbs—whose immensity could be perceived by no sense or reason—were led around by the slowest and swiftest motions; and others [were led] by the supreme mobile...

He praises the Copernican system for its mathematical harmony and "ineffable" elegance compared to traditional geocentric models.</summary>

<header>FIRST PART.</header>

<margin>There is much dispute: this matter is most correctly handled by Copernicus.</margin>...they established that all the lower spheres were swept along by the diurnal motion <note>The daily rotation of the heavens</note>, even though a massive crowd of disputes has been stirred up concerning this matter—by what reasoning a superior sphere should have authority over an inferior one—and they have not yet provided a settled answer. Others, such as Eudoxus and those who followed him, assigned to each planet its own sphere, by whose motion it would be carried around the Earth once in a natural day. Furthermore, immortal Gods! What a clashing of swords, what a great lawsuit there has been up to this point concerning the position of the spheres of Venus and Mercury, and how they are to be placed in relation to the Sun. Truly, the case is still before the judge <note>A Latin legal idiom, *sub iudice*, meaning the matter is undecided</note>, and who does not see that it is quite difficult, and even impossible, for it ever to be settled if those common hypotheses remain established?

What, likewise, would stand in the way if someone were to place Saturn below the Sun, provided the ratio of the spheres and the epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle or orbit formerly used to explain the complex movements of planets</term> to one another were preserved? For in those same [old] hypotheses, the common dimension of the planetary spheres relative to one another has not yet been demonstrated, by which each sphere might be geometrically circumscribed in its own place. Indeed, I shall pass over in silence here what great tragedies the detractors of this most beautiful and sweet part of philosophy have stirred up on account of the size of the epicycle of Venus, and because—once equants <term>equant: a mathematical concept used by Ptolemy to account for observed changes in planetary speed</term> were assumed—the motions of the celestial spheres about their own centers were rendered unequal. In the hypotheses of my Divine Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note>, however, once the sphere of the stars has been established as the boundary (as has been said), the sphere of each planet, proceeding uniformly with the motion assigned to it by nature, completes its own period; it suffers no force from a superior sphere that would sweep it in a different direction. Add to this that the larger circuits are completed more slowly, and those nearer to the Sun—from which one might say the principle of motion and light originates—complete their revolutions more swiftly, as was fitting. Therefore, Saturn, snatching up its path freely beneath the ecliptic, completes its revolution in 30 years; Jupiter in 12; Mars in two. The center of the Earth, however, determines the length of the year relative to the fixed stars. Venus traverses the zodiac in seven and a half months. Mercury, truly surrounding the Sun in the smallest sphere, surveys the world in 88 days.

<margin>Only six moving spheres.</margin>
<margin>The Great Orb is the measure of the other spheres.</margin>And so there are only six moving spheres surrounding the Sun, the center of the universe, of which the "Great Orb" <note>The Earth's orbit around the sun</note> carrying the Earth is the common measure: just as the spheres of the Moon, and likewise the distance of the Sun from the Moon, etc., are measured by those things which proceed from the center of the terrestrial globe.

<margin>Regarding this group of six spheres, you have a clearer and more specific cause explained above on page 24.</margin>

And indeed, who could have chosen another number more suitable and worthy than the number six? Or by what other number could one more easily persuade mortals that this entire universe was divided into its spheres by God the Founder and Craftsman of the world? For that number is highly celebrated both in the sacred oracles of God and by the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras who believed numbers were the fundamental reality of the universe.</note> and other Philosophers. What, moreover, could be more fitting for this craftsmanship of God than that this first and most perfect Work should be enclosed within the first and likewise most perfect number? <note>Six was considered a "perfect number" in antiquity because it equals the sum of its divisors: 1+2+3=6.</note> To this is added that the celestial harmony is thus perfected by the aforementioned six moving spheres, where all spheres succeed one another in such a way that no vastness of space is left between them; and each one, enclosed by geometry, maintains its place in such a manner that if you should attempt to move any one of them from its place, you would simultaneously dissolve the entire system.

<margin>Which motions belong to the Great Orb and those things adhering to it.</margin>

But having tasted these generalities, let us proceed to the enumeration of the circular motions <term>circular motions: the paths of celestial bodies, which were traditionally believed to be perfect circles</term> which belong to the individual spheres and the bodies adhering to and resting upon them. First, however, we shall speak of the hypotheses of the motions of the terrestrial globe on which we dwell. Since my Lord Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note>, following Plato and the Pythagoreans—the greatest Mathematicians of that divine age—judged that circular motions should be assigned and attributed to the spherical body of the Earth to explain the causes of the appearances <note>original: "τὼν φαινομένων" (the phenomena). This refers to the "saving the appearances" tradition in astronomy.</note>, and since he saw (as Aristotle is also said to have held) that if one motion is attributed to the Earth, other motions would likewise belong to it in imitation of the stars, he judged it must be assumed that it is moved by three principal motions. For first, having assumed the universal distribution of the world as was just said, he established that the Earth, enclosed within the sphere of the Moon between its own poles, like a little sphere in a lathe—the divine power so ordaining—produces day and night and one face of the sky after another for mortals, according as it turns itself toward the Sun, by the motion of the globe itself from west to east.

<margin>Three motions of the Earth.</margin>

<margin>1. Daily.</margin>

<margin>2. Annual.</margin>

In the second place, he established that the center of the Earth, with the things resting upon it—namely the elements and the lunar sphere—is carried around by the Great Orb <note>The "Orbis Magnus," or the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun.</note>, of which we have already made mention once or twice, uniformly in the plane of the ecliptic <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky, now understood as the plane of Earth's orbit.</note> following the sequence of the signs of the Zodiac.

<margin>3. Of Declination.</margin>

Thirdly, that the Earth has an equinoctial <note>The celestial equator.</note> and an axis with an inclination changeable relative to the plane of the ecliptic, and that it is reflected against the motion of the center in such a way that wherever the center is...

<header>FIRST PART.</header>

The center of the earth, the <term>equinoctial</term><note>The celestial equator.</note>, and the poles of the earth—due to the specific inclination of the earth's axis and the immense size of the stellar sphere—always look toward almost the same parts of the universe. This occurs because as much as the center of the earth is carried forward by the <term>Great Orb</term><note>The Earth's annual orbit around the Sun.</note>, the extremities of the earth's axis (the poles) are understood to move almost an equal amount backward each day. They describe small circles around the axis and poles that are equidistant from the axis and poles of the Great Orb, or the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The apparent path of the Sun, which Copernicus identifies as the plane of the Earth's orbit.</note>. To these motions, when we add—according to the opinion of my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note>—the <margin>There is also the motion completed by the two oscillations of the earth's poles.</margin> two oscillations of the earth's poles, and likewise the two motions by which the center of the Great Orb proceeds with both uniform and varying motion under the ecliptic, along with what was said above regarding the motions of the Moon around the center of the earth, we shall have, most learned Master Schöner, the true system of hypotheses. This allows us to derive the entire doctrine which the <term>Moderns</term><note>Contemporary astronomers of the 16th century.</note> call the "Primary Motion," and everything we know concerning the various motions of the starry sphere. It also allows us to assign the causes for what has been observed regarding the motions and <term>passions</term><note>Phenomena or changes, such as eclipses or irregularities.</note> of the Sun and Moon through diligent observations by craftsmen over the past two thousand years. Indeed—as must be discussed more fully later—we should not pass over in silence that the motion of the Great Orb produces the observed appearances in the remaining five planets. So much learning is contained within such few motions, as if within a single orb. 

<margin>Copernicus's hypotheses are consistent with common theories regarding the doctrine of Daily Motion.</margin> In the doctrine of the primary motion, nothing needs to be changed. For whatever property belongs to those things positioned at the maximum declination relative to one another, the same reasoning will be used to investigate the declinations of the remaining parts of the ecliptic, the <term>right ascensions</term><note>The distance of a point east of the First Point of Aries.</note>, the ratio of shadows and <term>gnomons</term><note>The part of a sundial that casts a shadow.</note> across the whole world, the lengths of days, <term>oblique ascensions</term><note>The arc of the celestial equator between the first point of Aries and the point of the equator that rises with a celestial body.</note>, the rising and setting of stars, and so on. However, there is this difference between these hypotheses and those of the Ancients: in these, contrary to what was prescribed by the Ancients, <margin>Besides the Ecliptic, the other circles of the primary motion are properly described on the earth.</margin> no circle besides the ecliptic is properly described by the imagination within the starry sphere. The rest—such as the equinoctial, the two tropics, the arctic and antarctic circles, Horizons, meridians, and all other circles pertaining to the doctrine of the primary motion (vertical circles, altitude circles, parallels, <term>colures</term><note>Two principal meridians of the celestial sphere.</note>, etc.)—are properly designated on the globe of the earth and are projected into the heavens by a certain relationship. Furthermore, regarding those things which appear to happen around the Sun, besides the appearance of the daily revolution around the earth (which it shares with all the stars and the other planets)...

-># NARRATION<-

<note>This text is from the *Narratio Prima* (First Account) by Georg Joachim Rheticus, the first published summary of Copernicus’s heliocentric theory.</note>

The Earth shares in common, and those things which Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer who championed the geocentric (Earth-centered) model.</note> and more recent astronomers attributed to the Sun's own motions, also happen to it—namely, those things which are found to occur regarding the changes of the solstitial and equinoctial points, the distances of the stars from them, and the variations of the apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the center.</note> from the fixed stars. All these things present themselves to our eyes just as if the Sun and the sphere of the stars were moving. For the way they are commonly believed to emerge or rise in the east, and gradually be elevated above the horizon until they reach the meridian—from which they descend in like manner, then pass through the lower hemisphere and complete their daily revolutions day by day—has sufficiently evident causes from the **first motion**, which my Master <note>Rheticus refers to Nicolaus Copernicus as "Dominus Praeceptor" (Lord Teacher/Master).</note> attributes to the Earth according to Plato.

<margin>By the second motion of the Earth, the Sun appears to move through the zodiac.</margin>
Furthermore, that the Sun appears to us to progress according to the sequence of the signs, and that we persuade ourselves it describes the ecliptic <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky.</note> with such a motion and establishes the annual time, can happen through the **second motion** which my Master attributes to the Earth. For while the Earth is carried in its great orbit and stays between the stars of Libra and the Sun, we—who indeed think the Earth is at rest—will judge the Sun to hold the stars of Aries; for a line cast from the center of the Earth through the Sun into the sphere of the stars will fall upon the constellation of Aries. Then, as the Earth progresses toward Scorpio, the Sun will seem to seek Taurus, and in this manner pass through the zodiac, even though we establish that this motion belongs to it while it is actually at rest. And the sidereal year will be the time in which the center of the Earth (or the Sun in appearance) revolves once from the same star back to the same star.

<margin>The third motion of the Earth is the cause of the changes of the parts of the year on Earth.</margin>
The **third motion** of the Earth produces the fixed and ordained successions of the seasons in the whole world; for through this it happens that the Sun and the remaining planets seem to be carried in a circle oblique to the equinoctial <note>The celestial equator.</note>. The same relationship of the Sun to every region of the Earth occurs as would have happened if, by hypothesis, the Earth occupied the center of the universe and the planets moved in an oblique circle. For since the plane of the equinoctial—on account of the motion of its poles, as was said—is reflected and declines from the plane of the ecliptic in comparison to the Sun, or as the Greeks say, <gloss>obliquity and inclination</gloss> <note>original: "λοξότης, & ἔγκλισις"</note>, the same declination of the equinoctial from the ecliptic returns under almost the same places of the ecliptic, and the poles of the daily revolution always remain under nearly the same position of the starry sphere. Then, in the greatest declinations of the equinoctial from the plane of the ecliptic...

<margin>The Equinoctial and Ecliptic are oblique. From this, the other circles of the Earth are inscribed.</margin>
<sig>To the Su—</sig>

<header>FIRST PART.</header>

A line extending from the center of the Sun toward the center of the Earth cuts through the globe of the Earth—which is rotated by its daily revolution—in a conical section, and thus describes the tropics.
<margin>The Tropics</margin>
Furthermore, the plane of the equator is divided by the equinoctial line into two hemispheres.
<margin>The Equinoxes</margin>
But the remaining divisions of the day on Earth are marked according as the reflection and declination (or, as Ptolemy calls it, the *protrusion and inclination* <note>original: "λὸ ἔξωσις κὴ ἔγλισις" (lo exosis kai enclisis). These are technical terms for the tilting of the planetary orbits.</note>) of the equator present themselves to the Sun.
<margin>The remaining divisions of the day on Earth are marked according as the reflection and declination (or, as Ptolemy calls it, the protrusion and inclination) of the equator present themselves to the Sun.</margin>
The Arctic and Antarctic circles, however, are described by the points where the horizons are tangent.
<margin>The Arctic and Antarctic circles.</margin>
But the polar circles, which for my Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note> are equidistant from the poles of the ecliptic <note>The ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun across the sky.</note>, are traced around the poles of the equator.
<margin>The polar circles.</margin>
Moreover, a great circle of the terrestrial globe passing through the poles of the equator and the aforementioned poles equidistant from the ecliptic will be the colure of the solstices; and another circle intersecting this one at right angles at the poles of the equator will take the place of the colure of the equinoxes.
<margin>The Colures <note>The colures are two principal meridians of the celestial sphere.</note></margin>
And in this manner, either the specific circles of any given location, or any number of others, are easily understood to be inscribed upon the Earth, and from there related to the sky extended above.

<margin>By what reasoning Copernicus demonstrates the apparent motion of the Sun.</margin>
Furthermore, since for the sake of mastering observations the globe of the Earth has moved out into the circumference of an eccentric circle, the Sun has taken its place in the middle of the universe. In common hypotheses <note>Referring to the traditional Ptolemaic/geocentric system.</note>, the center of the eccentric was between the center of the entire universe (which in those systems was the Earth) and the stars of Gemini in our age. Conversely, in my Teacher’s hypotheses, the center of the "Great Orb" <note>The *Orbis Magnus*, the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun.</note>—which at the beginning of our Narration we understood to be the center of the eccentric—is found between the Sun (the center of the universe for my Teacher) and the stars of Sagittarius. The diameter of the Great Orb falling upon the center of the Earth represents the line of the Sun's mean motion. Since the line projected from the center of the Earth through the center of the Sun into the ecliptic determines the true position of the Sun, it is not obscure how the Sun, according to the tradition of Ptolemy and the more recent astronomers, is thought to move unequally beneath the ecliptic, and how the angle of diversity from the mean motion is investigated geometrically. When the Earth is at the highest apsis <note>The point furthest from the Sun; the aphelion.</note> of the Great Orb, the Sun is believed to occupy the position of the apogee in the eccentric; and conversely, when the Earth lingers in the lowest apsis, the Sun is seen in its perigee.
Truly, however, the reason why the fixed stars seem to move away from the equinoctial and solstitial points, and why the maximum obliquity of the Sun varies, etc., which at the beginning of the Narration [was explained] from my Teacher's reasoning...
<margin>Apparent motion in the Fixed Stars: and the variation of the obliquity of the ecliptic.</margin>

# NARRATION
<page-num>114</page-num>

<margin>The third motion of the earth, or rather the investigation of it.</margin>
I have derived this from Book III of the author <note>referring to Copernicus</note>, based on the motion of declination which we proposed generally. My Teacher <note>likely referring to Copernicus or a primary instructor of his system</note> concluded that this depends on two librations <term>libration: a slow oscillation, like the swinging of a balance</term> meeting one another. From the poles—the poles of the ecliptic <term>ecliptic: the sun's apparent path across the sky</term>—which are equidistant, as was said not long ago, let 23 degrees and 40 minutes of a great circle be counted on both sides. There, let two points be marked to represent the poles of the mean equinoctial, and as is fitting, let two colures <term>colures: great circles that intersect at the celestial poles</term> be designated to distinguish the solstices and the mean equinoxes. These things should certainly be conceived for the sake of learning and sketched upon a small disk containing the globe of the earth; by its uniform motion, the third motion—which is indeed attributed to the earth—occurs. 

Now, with the center of the earth situated between the Sun and the stars of the constellation Virgo, let the mean equinoctial be reflected or slanted toward the Sun. Let the line of the true position of the Sun pass through the common section of the plane of the ecliptic, the mean equinoctial, and the colure distinguishing the mean equinoxes. This is done so that it is the mean vernal equinox and simultaneously the true vernal equinox, where the ratio of the motions will require it to be so, as will become clearly established from what follows. 

From this place, as the center of the earth proceeds with equal motion relative to the fixed stars by 59 minutes, 8 seconds, and 11 thirds <note>original: "59. min. 8. fecun. 11. ter." — these are subdivisions of degrees used for precise astronomical measurement</note> each day, the mean vernal point completes the same amount in precedence <note>moving westward</note> over the center of the earth, and traveling with a slightly faster pace, it describes a larger angle by nearly 8 thirds. And this is the reason why, a little earlier, we said the motion of declination was nearly equal to the equal motion of the center of the earth relative to the fixed stars. 

But as the angle increases—which is marked from the mean equinoctial point over the center of the earth (according to the rule already established)—before the center of the earth returns again to the place of the ecliptic from which it departed, the line of the true position of the Sun will fall upon the mean equinox. Thus, the stars will appear to us to move forward in consequence <note>moving eastward</note> by some mean or equal motion, according to the rate of anticipation.

<margin>The mean equinox, or equal precession.</margin>
This anticipation, as I said at the beginning, is nearly 50 seconds in an Egyptian year, and in 25,816 Egyptian years, it grows into a full revolution. It is evident, therefore, what the mean equinox is, what equal precession is, and how these things can be placed before the eyes as if by a mechanical instrument.

<margin>How the Librations occur. Copernicus Book 3, Chapter 4.</margin>
Regarding the deliberations: Let there be a determined straight line AB, for example 24 minutes; let this be divided into two equal parts at point C. Then, with one foot of the compass placed at C, let a circle DE be described with the extension CD.

<header>FIRST PART.</header>

## FIRST PART.

<margin>Oscillation.</margin>
<margin>Motion along a diameter.</margin>
toward A, 6 minutes (that is, a fourth part) and let two small circles <note>original: "circelli"</note> of the same size be fashioned from this other material (if I may speak thus for the time being), and let them be so arranged that one of them is attached to the circumference of the other, so that it can move freely around its own center. Now, the one that carries the other on its circumference shall be called the first, and it is fixed to the center of line AB at point C; let the center of the second small circle be marked F, and let a point G be marked at will on its circumference. Now, if point G of the second small circle is applied to A, the endpoint of the assumed line, and F to the mark D of the same, and in an equal time G describes an angle in one direction about the center C that is double the angle described by F about C in the opposite direction; it is clear that in one revolution of the first small circle, the mark G has traversed the line AB twice in its description, and the second small circle has revolved twice. Because in such a description of a straight line through two combined circular motions, point G moves most slowly near the endpoints A and B, but more rapidly in the middle around C, it pleased the Teacher <note>Likely referring to Nicolaus Copernicus or Georg Joachim Rheticus, who developed these models of "libration."</note> to call such a motion of mark G along the line AB an **oscillation** <note>original: "librationem"</note>, since such motion occurs in the likeness of things hanging in the air. This motion is also called **motion along a diameter**: for by imagining a circle whose diameter is AB with center C; from the doctrine of chords, at whatever place on the same diameter AB the point G may be by the combined motion I have described, the straight line HG is established as half the chord subtending double the arc HA, cutting the diameter AB at I, from which the table of **prosthaphaereses** <term>prosthaphaeresis: a geometric correction or "equation" used in early astronomy to account for the difference between a planet's mean and true position</term> is constructed according to AG. The Teacher calls the motion of the first small circle about C the **Anomaly**, for by that motion the prosthaphaeresis is determined. Indeed, let the center F of the second small circle, departing from point D on the circumference of the first toward the left, describe an angle DCF of, for example, 30 degrees; there on the circumference—

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a large circle with a vertical diameter labeled A-B and a horizontal line segment passing through the center C. Inside the large circle, there are two smaller circles. One smaller circle has its center at C and passes through points F and D. The other smaller circle has its center at F and passes through point D. Point D is on the circumference of the larger circle. For as the circle AB, with the line CFH projected from the center C, will contain an arc AH of the same number of degrees as the arc DF of the first small circle: and because the point G of the second small circle <note>The author is describing a system of epicycles, or circles moving upon circles, to explain complex motion</note> has proceeded to the right of ABH by a double ratio, once a straight line is drawn from point H to point G, it is evident that this same line is the half of the double arc AH. Furthermore, GC is the half of the double arc of the remainder of arc AH from the quadrant—that is, 60 degrees—the half of whose double chord is 8660 parts. Therefore, AG is 1340 parts (where the radius is 1000), which is clearly the distance G stands from A on the diameter AB. But if AB is presupposed to be 60, then GA will be 4 of such units, and GB will be 56. From this, by creating a proportional part relative to 24, the position of AG or BG will be obtained, wherever the point G may stop on any part of the assumed fixed straight line in such a case.

# NARRATION

<note>The text continues from the previous page discussing the cycles of planetary or axial motion.</note> is completed, the anomaly of this second libration <note>libration: a slow oscillation or "balancing" motion of a celestial body</note> will be finished in 1,717 Egyptian years. Therefore, the anomaly of the obliquity <note>the tilt of the Earth's axis</note> taken from the tables and doubled, returns the anomaly of the precession; the former is given the name "simple," and the latter "duplicated."

<margin>Simple anomaly and duplicated anomaly.</margin>
But if only this second libration had to be posited, the angle of inclination between the true equinoctial plane and the ecliptic <note>the sun's apparent path</note>—which would certainly be worthy of notice—would not vary, as is evident. Instead, the entire diversity of appearances happening because of this would be detected solely in the inequality of the precession of the equinox. However, with both librations coinciding, the poles of the earth meeting each other with these motions (as has been said) will trace the shapes of twisted little crowns <note>original: "corollarum intortarum," referring to the looped, epicyclical path the pole appears to follow</note> around the poles of the mean equinoctial.

<margin>By these two librations, twisted crowns are traced.</margin>
And when the poles of the earth fall upon the colure <note>a great circle on the celestial sphere</note> marking the mean solstices, the true colure will lie in the same plane as the mean one; and the true vernal point <note>the spring equinox</note> will be joined with the mean one. Yet, unless the poles of both equinoctials are joined, the planes of the equinoctials and the colures—both mean and true, for solstices and equinoxes—will not be entirely joined. Moreover, while the north pole stays in the part from the second libration toward the right limit of A, with the south pole established at the opposite point, the true equinox follows the mean, and the Sun falls into the mean equinoctial before the true one. But when the poles of the earth swap sides of the world—namely, so that the northern pole holds the left side of the colure of the mean solstices and the southern holds the right—the true equinox precedes the mean, and the Sun meets the true equinoctial sooner than the mean. Furthermore, as the poles of the earth advance from A toward B, because the true equinox proceeds as if to meet the Sun, the year measured by the equinoxes decreases for this reason; but from B toward A, as it seems to flee the Sun, the year measured by the equinoxes increases. And as the poles of the earth circle around, a notable increase or decrease of the year is perceived in a shorter span of years.

<margin>By this other libration, the true magnitude of the solar year is at other times made larger.</margin>
<margin>From this, the motion of the fixed stars also appears faster or slower.</margin>
And since the apparent precession of the fixed stars is tied to the annual quantity at the equinoxes, by this very same reasoning, a faster or slower elongation of the points of the solstices and equinoxes from the fixed stars is observed in the preceding direction.

# FIRST SECTION

## A diagram of both oscillations, to demonstrate the change in obliquity and the true intersection of the equinoctial with the ecliptic.

<image-desc>A large circular diagram representing celestial mechanics. A central circle is intersected by several ellipses and straight lines. Key points are labeled with letters: A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, K, L, M, N, O, P, Q, R, S, T, X, Y, Z, and the Greek letter beta (β). The main circle has a vertical diameter labeled B-D and a horizontal diameter. Several lines radiate from C, the center of the main circle, to points on its circumference and to points on the intersecting ellipses. Two smaller, elongated ellipses are shown near point H, intersecting each other. Lines like M-X, M-F, M-R, M-K are drawn from a point M on the circumference. Other lines connect points like Y, C, S, L, T, G, Z, N, D. The diagram illustrates the concepts of libration, obliquity, and the equinoctial and ecliptic sections.</image-desc>

Let **A** be the pole of the <term>Ecliptic</term><note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note>, **BCD** the semicircle of the Ecliptic in the middle of the <term>Solstitial Colure</term><note>The great circle passing through the celestial poles and the solstices</note> **ABD**. Let **E** and **F** be the poles of the mean <term>Equinoctial</term><note>The celestial equator</note> **FCG**, with the mean equinox at **C**, and the mean <term>obliquity</term><note>The angle of the Earth's tilt</note> **BF** or **AE**, which is 23 degrees and 40 minutes.

Of the first <term>libration</term><note>A slow oscillation or "balancing" motion</note>, which moves the position of the true equinoctial away from the mean **E**, let the arc (or straight line) be **HEI**, an arc of the solstitial colure, and its quantity 24 minutes. By this, therefore, the true obliquity is changed. For when the true pole is at **H**, the true equinoctial is **KCL**; but with the same pole at **I**, the equinoctial is **MCN**, whose obliquity there is 23 degrees 32 minutes, and here 23 degrees 28 minutes.

Furthermore, let the position of the second libration (or quasi-straight line) be **OEP**, an arc of the <term>Equinoctial Colure</term><note>The great circle passing through the celestial poles and the equinoxes</note>, and its quantity 36 minutes. By this, the true intersection of the true equinoctial and the ecliptic varies—this is the beginning of the <term>Zodiac</term><note>The starting point of the signs, usually 0° Aries</note> from which the series of signs and the motions of the stars are numbered. For when the true pole is at **P**, the true intersection of the equinoxes is at **S**, but when it is at **O**, it is at **T**; the arc **SY** is 2 degrees 20 minutes according to Copernicus, for that much corresponds to the line **OP** in obliquity.

Furthermore, these two librations combine with each other under this condition:
1. When the true pole is placed at **E**, the midpoint of both librations, the true equinoctial coincides entirely with the mean at **FCG**.
2. From **E**, the true pole tends toward **H** as well as toward **P**, though with unequal pace; for the libration of the equinoxes along **OP** is double the libration of the obliquity along **IH**, because Copernicus asserts the former is completed in 1,717 years and the latter in 3,434 years. Therefore, when the true pole reaches **P**, the other libration will cause it to stand between **E** and **H**. Thus, by the composite motion, the true pole is carried to **Ω** <note>Omega</note>, and it twists the true equinoctial into **RST**, whose obliquity is not yet at its maximum, but its intersection with the ecliptic is furthest from the mean, namely at **S**.
3. When the true pole returns from **Ω**, as if from **P** to **E**, and simultaneously ascends further into **EH**, by the very guidance of both librations and the composite motion, it is carried to **H**. From this, the true equinoctial **KCL** is owed to it, which, having the maximum obliquity according to **BK**, intersects the ecliptic at the mean of the equinoxes, **C**.
4. In like manner, the same true pole returns from **H** to **E**, and descending from **E** to **O**, it is restored at **E** for both. For just as it had arrived at **Ω** from **H**, so now it goes away into **β** <note>Beta</note>. At this place, the true Equinoctial is **XYZ**, responding from the opposite side to the equinoctial **RST**.
5. Each libration, after the same pole is at **E**, restores the true equinoctial to **FCG**. At this time, therefore, the entire variation of the equinoxes...

but half of the change in the obliquity <note>The tilt of the Earth's axis relative to its orbit</note> is completed, because the true pole has moved from E toward both of its limits, P and O, but toward only one limit of the other, H, and has returned to E. <margin>The motion of the Sun's apogee, and the change of its eccentricity.</margin> Regarding the Sun's <term>apogee</term> <note>The point in the Sun's apparent orbit where it is furthest from the Earth</note>, those things which we initially deduced from observations according to the opinion of my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note>, as far as it pertains to its distance from the vernal equinox, have become sufficiently known from what was just said. The diameter of the large orbit, or of the ecliptic, <margin>On these matters see also the theory of the Sun below.</margin> passing through the centers of the Sun and the small circle, is the line of the mean <term>apsides</term> <note>The points of greatest and least distance</note> of the Sun: But the diameter through the centers of the Sun and the large orbit is the line of the true apsides. <margin>In Saturn, this variation is not perceptible because of the great size of its orb.</margin>

And this is the reason why no change in eccentricity is felt in Mercury either, since it similarly encloses the side of the Sun's apogee with its own apogee.

But since, God willing, I have become a spectator and witness to such labors, which my Master the Doctor both sustains with a truly cheerful spirit and has now for the most part overcome, I see that I had never even dreamed of a labor of such great mass. Indeed, this mass of labors is so great that it is not for any common man <note>original: "caluſsis," likely a typo for "caligatis" (common soldiers) or "causis"</note> but for a Hero to be able to bear and finally overcome it. <page-num>133</page-num>
<header>FIRST PART.</header>

<margin>A comparison of the labors of astronomers: which no one is able to overcome without the help of divine aid.</margin>
<margin>In what order Copernicus was accustomed to proceed in investigating astronomical matters.</margin>
...who, having become accustomed through a long life, with great courage and unbroken strength, might carry this burden through to the end just as he had first begun it. To this, the divine Plato—the high priest of wisdom, as Pliny calls him—declares quite clearly in the *Epinomis* <note>A dialogue attributed to Plato concerning the soul and the heavens</note> that **ASTRONOMY WAS DISCOVERED WITH GOD LEADING THE WAY**. Others perhaps interpret this opinion of Plato differently; but I, for my part, see that the Doctor, my Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note>, *always keeps in view* the observations of every age, collected in order like indexes alongside his own. Then, when something must be either established or brought into the art and its rules, he proceeds from those first observations all the way to his own, and weighs by what logic they all agree with one another. Furthermore, those valid conclusions which he has gathered there under the guidance of Urania <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note>, he refers back to the hypotheses of Ptolemy and the Ancients. And after he has weighed these same things with the greatest care, and has perceived that they must be abandoned—driven by *astronomical necessity* <note>original: "astronomica ἀνάγκη" (ananke); the idea that the data forces a change in theory</note>—he assumes new hypotheses, not indeed without Divine inspiration and the will of the Gods. Then, applying mathematics, he geometrically establishes what valid conclusions can be deduced from such things. Finally, he accommodates the observations of the Ancients and his own to the assumed hypotheses; and thus, after all those labors have been exhausted, he at last writes down the laws of Astronomy.

<margin>Another Comparison.</margin>
I think Plato is to be understood in this way: that a mathematician searching into the motions of the stars is most rightly compared to a blind man, for whom a great, infinite, slippery journey, entangled with infinite detours, must be completed with only his staff as a guide. What will happen? Sometimes walking anxiously, seeking the way with his staff, and sometimes leaning upon it in despair, he will call upon heaven, earth, and all the Gods to come to his wretched aid. God will indeed permit him to test his own strength for some years, so that he may finally understand that he cannot be freed from the immediate danger by his staff alone. Furthermore, when he is just about to lose heart, God, taking pity on him, stretches out a hand and leads him by that hand to the desired goal. The Astronomer's staff is Mathematics itself, or Geometry, by which he first dares to test and set foot upon the path. For what are the strengths of human genius for investigating these divine things, so far removed from us? What are our dim-sighted eyes? Therefore, unless God in His kindness has instilled in him Heroic impulses, and led him as if by the hand through a journey otherwise incomprehensible to human reason, I would hardly believe that in any matter of Astro-

->NARRATIVE<-

The astronomer is more excellent and more fortunate than that blind man <note>likely a reference to the blind Orion or a general metaphor for those without scientific sight</note>, except in that, sometimes trusting in his own genius and offering divine honors to that staff of his, he will congratulate himself that Urania <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note> herself has been recalled from the Underworld. However, when he considers the matter rightly within himself, he will feel that he is no more blessed than Orpheus, who indeed perceived in his mind that his Eurydice followed him as he ascended dancing from the Abyss; but afterward, when he reached the mouth of Avernus <note>The entrance to the Underworld</note> and hoped most greatly that he possessed her, she slipped from his sight back to the depths and vanished.

<margin>Anticipating an objection.</margin>Let us therefore weigh, as we began, the hypotheses of the Lord Doctor, my Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note>, regarding the remaining planets, so that we may see whether, with a constant mind and with God leading the way, he has brought Urania through to the world above and restored her to her dignity. Someone might perhaps be able to evade those things which are said concerning the motion of the Earth in relation to the apparent motions of the Sun and Moon—although I do not see how he could have transferred the calculation of the precession to the sphere of the Stars—but certainly, regarding the apparent motions of the remaining planets, if one wishes to look either to the principal goal of Astronomy and the logic and harmony of the system of spheres, or to the ease and pleasantness of the explanations for appearances shining forth from every side, he could demonstrate them more conveniently and correctly by assuming no other hypotheses. Indeed, all these things appear to be most beautifully bound together as if by a golden chain; and each of the planets, in its own position and its own order, and in every diversity of its motion, bears witness that the Earth moves, and that we, because of the changing position of the terrestrial globe to which we cling, believe that they wander about in various ways by their own motions. And indeed, if it is to be seen anywhere else how God has left the world to our disputations <margin>Ecclesiastes 3.</margin>, it is certainly conspicuous in this place above all. Nor truly do I think it can disturb anyone that God allows Ptolemy and other likewise excellent Heroes to disagree in this part, <margin>How and on what occasion the hypotheses of the Ancients arose.</margin> "since this is not of the class of those opinions which Socrates in the *Gorgias* <note>original: "Georgia", a common early modern misspelling of Plato's dialogue *Gorgias*</note> says are ruinous to men"; nor does the art itself, or that divining power flowing from it, suffer any ruin from this. The Ancients attributed every diversity of motion, which they found the three superior planets to have in respect to the Sun, to their own <term>epicycles</term> <note>small circles moved upon the circumference of larger circles in the old Ptolemaic system</note>. Then, when they perceived that the remaining apparent inequality in those same planets could by no means be caused by the logic of the <term>eccentric</term> <note>a circular orbit that is not centered on the Earth</note> alone, and the calculation in the computation of those motions, in imitation of the hypotheses...
