<header>FIRST BOOK.</header>

the theories of Venus, since they agreed with experience and observations. They also thought that a similar explanation for the second apparent inequality <note>The "second inequality" refers to retrograde motion, which the ancients explained using epicycles.</note> should be adopted, such as they concluded Venus to have from demonstrations: namely, that just as in Venus, so the center of any planet's epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</term> would move equidistantly from the center of the eccentric, but would obtain its uniformity of motion with respect to the equant point <note>original: "centri æquantis." The equant is a mathematical point used by Ptolemy to account for the observed speeds of planets.</note>. To this point, the planet itself, by its own motion in the epicycle, departing uniformly from the mean apogee <term>apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from the center</term>, would have a relationship. Furthermore, just as Venus would complete its revolutions by its own peculiar motion in the epicycle, but would proceed according to the mean motion of the Sun in the eccentric: so they, on the contrary, would look to the Sun in the epicycle, but in the eccentric would be carried by their own peculiar motions, as the observations themselves required them to establish, while they strove to keep the Earth in the middle of the universe. But besides those things which they judged competent to save the appearances of Venus, they thought that the theory of Mercury required, in addition, another location for the equant, and that the very center from which the epicycle was equidistant should revolve in a small circle.

<margin>Defects of the ancient hypotheses regarding the motion in longitude.</margin>
These things were certainly discovered acutely, as are almost all the inventions of the Ancients, and were sufficiently consistent with the motions and appearances, if we admit that the celestial spheres have uniformity around their own centers—from which, however, nature shrinks—and if we attribute the first and most notable diversity of the apparent motion of the five planets to them as their own, even though it is established that this appears in them by accident <note>Copernicus argues that what looks like the planet's own motion is actually caused by the Earth's movement.</note>.

<margin>Defects of the same regarding the motion in latitude.</margin>
In the latitudes of the planets, however, the Ancients also seem to neglect that axiom <note>original: "αξίωμα"</note>, namely, that all motions of celestial bodies are either circular or composed of circular ones: unless perhaps someone wishes to understand the reflections and declinations of Venus and Mercury to occur as was said a little before regarding the motion of the Earth's declination, and the declinations of the epicycles in the three superior planets, and the deviations in the inferior ones, to occur through the motions of librations <term>libration: a slow oscillation, real or apparent</term>. Even if this were granted in the reflections and declinations of Venus and Mercury—since the angles of their inclinations, of the planes of the eccentrics, and of the epicycles remain the same everywhere—nevertheless, common calculation refutes the idea that the declinations of the epicycles in the three superior planets, and the deviations of Venus and Mercury, occur through librations. For as concerning the Deviations...

<header>NARRATION</header>

<margin>Book 13, proposition 21, chapter in Ptolemy's Almagest.</margin>I shall only say this: that the proportional minutes, by which we calculate the deviations for the positions of the center of the epicycle <note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one; used in ancient astronomy to explain retrograde motion.</note> outside the nodes and apsides <note>The points in an orbit closest to or furthest from the center of motion.</note>, were sought out and established by the same reasoning as the declinations of the parts of the ecliptic are investigated in the doctrine of primary motion. Thus it happens that at the sixtieth degree from one of the apsides of the eccentric, with the center of the epicycle of Venus being present, we collect a deviation of 5 minutes, but for Mercury 22 and a half minutes. But if the carrying circle <note>The deferent.</note> were set to deviate through oscillations <note>librationes</note>, in such a position of Venus's epicycle, true reasoning would demand a deviation beyond 2 and a half minutes, and for Mercury 11 and a quarter minutes. For in that position of the center of the epicycle, the angle of inclination of the plane of the eccentric to the ecliptic would be found to be no greater than 5 minutes in the former case, and 22 and a half in the latter, by the property of the motion of oscillations. And for this reason, perhaps, Johannes Regiomontanus <note>A famous 15th-century German astronomer and mathematician.</note> thought that students should be warned that the calculation in latitudes is only approximately true.

<margin>What would Rheticus have done if he had noticed those things according to God's Geometry and the regular bodies, about which Mr. Kepler discusses?</margin>Finally, since men—as Aristotle shows elsewhere at length—desire by their nature to know, it is indeed troublesome enough that the cause <gloss>from the phenomena</gloss> <note>original Greek: ἐκ τῶν φαινομένων</note> is everywhere hidden and enveloped, as if in Cimmerian darkness <note>A proverbial expression for intense or perpetual darkness.</note>, which Ptolemy himself also testifies along with us. Meanwhile, I shall not bring forward more concerning the hypotheses of the Ancients regarding the five planets than perhaps the enumeration of the new (so to speak) hypotheses, as well as the comparison to the Ancients, requires. Indeed, I love Ptolemy and those who follow him, and my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note>, from my heart; for I always keep in sight and memory that holy precept of Aristotle: <gloss>loving both, yet it is a sacred duty to prefer the truth</gloss> <note>original Greek: φιλῶν μὲν ἀμφοτέρους, ὡς ὅσιοv προτιμᾶν τὴν ἀλήθειαν; a famous sentiment often associated with Plato and Aristotle.</note>. From these things, although I know not how, I feel my mind more inclined toward the hypotheses of my Master. This happens perhaps partly because I am now finally bringing myself to understand more rightly that most sweet saying which is attributed to Plato for its gravity and truth: <gloss>God always geometrizes</gloss> <note>original Greek: Τὸν Θεὸν ἀεὶ γεωμετρεῖν</note>; and partly because in my Master's restoration of Astronomy, as if the mist had been dispersed and the sky now opened, I gaze with "both eyes" (as the saying goes) upon the power of that wisest Socrates in the *Phaedrus*: <gloss>If I think anyone else is able either to arrive at the many things rightly, or to perceive this as if following in the footsteps of a God.</gloss> <note>original Greek: Εάν τινα ἄλλον ἡγήσομαι δυνατὸν εἶναι ἢ ἐπὶ πολλὰ ἀφικέσθαι ὀρθῶς, ἢ τοῦτο διανοεῖσθαι ὡς μετ᾽ ἴχνους, ὅδε Θεοῖο.</note>

<header>FIRST PART.</header>

<margin>Hypotheses of the motions of the five planets according to longitude <note>Longitude refers to the position of a planet along the ecliptic/zodiac.</note>.</margin>

<margin>NB. These triple hypotheses—namely of two epicycles, of eccentric-epicycles, and of eccentrics—are consistent; therefore, one may use this or that one indifferently at will in demonstrating the motions of all the planets.</margin>

...of the planets, which is seen to happen in them <note>original: "παρὰ τοὺς πέδο τῶν ἥλιον σχηματισμοὺς" — in accordance with their configurations relative to the Sun</note>, occurs because of the annual motion of the earth in its great orbit <note term="Orbis Magnus">The "Great Orbit" is the term used by Copernicus for the Earth's annual path around the Sun.</note>; so that the planets truly proceed with only the other single inequality which is observed according to the parts of the zodiac. For this reason, only those hypotheses by which two diversities of motion can be demonstrated are suitable for them. However, just as in the case of the Moon the Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note> preferred to use an epicycle upon an epicycle, so in the three superior planets <note>Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</note>, he chose eccentric-epicycles to more conveniently demonstrate the order and measurement of their motions; but in Venus and Mercury, he chose eccentric-eccentrics.

<margin>Copernicus relates the centers of the planets and their motions not to the Sun, the center of the World, but to the center of the Great Orbit.</margin>

Since we look up at the motions of the three superior planets as if from the center of the earth, but view the revolutions of the inferior planets as if below us, it was fitting that the centers of the planetary orbits be referred to the center of the great orbit, from which we then most correctly transfer all motions and appearances to the center of the earth itself. Therefore, in the five planets, that eccentric must be understood whose center is outside the center of the great orbit.

<margin>The orbits and motions of the superior stars. See below in the Appendix.</margin>

But so that the reasoning for establishing the new hypotheses may be more rightly understood, and finally that all things may be more and more clear and open, let us first suppose the planes of the eccentrics of the five planets to be in the plane of the ecliptic, and the centers of the carrying circles <note term="Deferent">The large circular path that carries the smaller epicycle.</note> and the equants <note term="Equant">A mathematical point used to account for the observed speed changes of planets.</note> to be around the center of the great orbit, just as among the Ancients <note>Referring to Ptolemaic astronomers.</note> they were around the center of the earth. Then, let the spaces between the center of the great orbit and the points or centers of the equants be divided into four equal parts. Furthermore, let the center of the eccentric for any of the three superior planets be raised to the third division from the center of the great orbit toward the apogee <note term="Apogee">The point in an orbit farthest from the center.</note>, and with the extension of the remaining fourth part, let an epicycle be described on the circumference of the eccentric; thus the structure of the proper motion of each in longitude will appear. If, therefore, according to the opinion of my Master, the planet proceeds in the upper part of this circulating epicycle in the direction of the signs <note>Eastward/Prograde</note>, and in the lower part against the signs <note>Westward/Retrograde</note>, so that when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee of the eccentric, the planet itself is found at the perigee <note term="Perigee">The point in an orbit closest to the center.</note> of its epicycle; and conversely, when the center of the epicycle stays in the perigee of the eccentric, the planet...

# NARRATION

<page-num>138</page-num>

When the planet occupies the apogee <note>the point in an orbit farthest from the center</note> of the epicycle <note>a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</note>: and by this similarity of motions, the planet in the epicycle completes its periods in the same amount of time as the center of the epicycle does in the eccentric; it is clear that, having removed the equants <note>the mathematical points used in older astronomy to account for observed speed changes</note> of the superior planets, the diversity of motion is regular with respect to the center of the Great Orb <note>the Earth's orbit around the sun</note>, and is composed of equal parts. For an epicycle assumed in such a manner succeeds to the function of the equant; and the eccentric around its own center, and the planet in the epicycle toward the center of the epicycle to which it is attached, designate equal angles in equal time.

<margin>The orbs and motions of Venus.</margin> The motion of Venus, however, will be established as follows: having rejected the deferent <note>the main carrying circle</note>, whose place the Great Orb supplies, a small circle is described around the third section by the extension of the remaining fourth. Then the center of the epicycle of Venus—which shall here be called the eccentric of the eccentric, the second eccentric, and the mobile eccentric—is moved on the circumference of said small circle by such a law: that as often as the center of the Earth falls upon the line of the apsides <note>the line connecting the nearest and farthest points of an orbit</note>, the center of the eccentric itself exists at the point of the small circle nearest to the center of the Great Orb. However, when the mean Earth is in its orb between either apsis, the center of the eccentric of Venus itself stays at the point of the small circle most remote from the center of the Great Orb; and it moves toward the same parts in the sequence of the signs <note>the direction of the zodiac</note> just as the Earth moves, performing, however (as follows from these things), two revolutions in one circuit of the Earth.

<margin>The orbs and motions of Mercury.</margin> But the system of Mercury's motions agrees in general with the theory of Venus, with the addition of an epicycle, the diameter of which the planet itself describes through a libration <note>an oscillating, back-and-forth motion along a straight line</note>, on account of the remaining diversity. Furthermore, so that it may accommodate itself to the motion of the Earth, it receives a quantity of 3573 parts from the center of the mobile deferent. The eccentricity of the first deferent is 736 parts; the quantity from the center of the small circle containing the mobile deferent center is 211 parts; and the diameter of the said epicycle is 380 parts—of which parts the distance from the center of the Great Orb to the center of the Earth is 10,000. In its motion, it is assigned such a law that the center of the mobile eccentric (contrary to what happened in Venus) is at its greatest distance from the center of the Great Orb when the Earth is on the line of the planet's apsides; and it approaches to the greatest proximity when the Earth is a quadrant's distance away from the planet's apsides. It will have, as is evident, a fixed epicycle, whose diameter looking toward the center...

of the moving carrier center, the planet itself, crawling by a motion of <term>libration</term> <note>a back-and-forth oscillating motion along a straight line</note>, describes a straight line. This law is observed: that when the center of the moving <term>eccentric</term> <note>a circle whose center is off-set from the center of the universe</note> is at its greatest distance from the center of the <term>Great Orb</term> <note>the orbit of the Earth around the Sun</note>, the planet holds the <term>perigee</term> <note>the point closest to the center</note> of its <term>epicycle</term>, which is the lower limit of the diameter it describes. Conversely, it reaches the other limit, which could be called the <term>apogee</term> <note>the point farthest from the center</note>, when the same center of the moving eccentric is nearest to the center of the Great Orb.

<margin>How the true motions are demonstrated in relation to the center of the Earth.</margin>
<margin>See below in the Appendix.</margin>
This is almost the entire system of hypotheses for saving <note>explaining or accounting for</note> every proper variation of the motion of the planets according to longitude. Therefore, if our eye were located at the center of the Great Orb, the visual rays sent out from it through the planets—like lines of true motions projected onto the sphere of the stars—would be led around the <term>ecliptic</term> <note>the sun's apparent path</note> by the planets in no other way than the ratios of the said circles and motions would require, so as to show their own variations of motion in the <term>zodiac</term>. But because we, the inhabitants of Earth, contemplate the apparent motions of the heavenly bodies from it, we refer all motions and appearances to its center as if to the base and inner sanctum of our dwelling. By drawing lines from there through the planets—as if the eye were transferred from the center of the Great Orb to the center of the Earth—it is clear that the variations of all <gloss>the phenomena</gloss> <note>original Greek: τῶν φαινομένων</note> must be calculated from there, as they are indeed seen by us. However, if one intended to gather the true and proper variations of the planets' motion, that would have to be achieved through lines issuing from the center of the Great Orb, as has been said.

<margin>The motions of the planets are demonstrated first in relation to the center of the Great Orb, and afterwards to the center of the Earth. Just as in the ancient hypotheses, they were first related to the center of the epicycles, and afterwards to the planets themselves.</margin>
Nevertheless, so that we may more expeditiously explain those things which remain to be enumerated <gloss>among the phenomena</gloss> <note>original Greek: ἐν τοῖς φαινομένοις</note> of the planets, and so that the entire treatment may be easier and more pleasant, let there be conceived in the mind not only the lines of the true apparent motions proceeding from the center of the Earth through the planets into the ecliptic, but also those from the center of the Great Orb, which are therefore properly called the lines of the variation of motion. Thus, as the Earth moves in the motion of the Great Orb, when it reaches the point where it is interposed in the same straight line between the Sun and any of the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, or Saturn</note>, the planet will indeed be seen to rise at evening. And because the Earth so situated is nearest to it, the <term>Ancients</term> <note>referring to Ptolemaic astronomers</note> posited that the planet was nearest to the Earth because it was around the perigee of its epicycle. --- Page 152 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the relative positions of the Earth and the planets; this page continues the narrative regarding the apparent motion of the planets (Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn) as viewed from a moving Earth.</meta>

# NARRATION

As the Earth approaches the line of the true and apparent position of the planet—which occurs when the Earth reaches the opposite point of the aforementioned location—the planet begins to disappear in its **evening setting**. At this time, it is at its greatest distance from the Earth, until the line of the planet's true position passes through the center of the Sun. With the Sun intervening between the planet and the Earth, the planet is hidden <note>This is known as solar conjunction</note>. Following this occultation, due to the perpetual motion of the Earth, the line of the Sun’s true position moves away from the line of the planet’s true position. The planet is then seen to rise again in its **morning rising**, once it has attained a sufficient distance from the Sun as required by the arc of vision.

<margin>Description of the mean and true apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the center</note> in the epicycles of the ancients, and in the "Great Orb" of these hypotheses.</margin>
Furthermore, since in the hypotheses of these three planets the **Great Orb** <note>The "Great Orb" or *orbis magnus* refers to the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun in the Copernican system</note> performs the function of the epicycle <note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one; used by ancient astronomers like Ptolemy to explain planetary loops</note> attributed to each planet by the Ancients: the true apogee and perigee <note>The point in an orbit closest to the center</note> of the planet with respect to the Great Orb will be found on the diameter of the Great Orb continued all the way to the planet. However, the mean apogee and perigee are found on the diameter of the Great Orb which moves parallel to the line drawn from the center of the eccentric to the center of the epicycle. When the Earth, in the half of its orbit toward the planet, approaches the planet itself, and in the remaining and opposite half moves away, the extremities of the diameters of the Great Orb will represent the perigees in the former case and the apogees in the latter; for that half succeeds to the place of the lower part of the epicycle, and this to the upper.

<margin>The method of Direction, Station, and Retrogradation of the Planets.</margin>
Suppose that not far from the conjunction of the Sun and the planet, the center of the Earth is at the location of the planet's true apogee (with respect to the Great Orb), and the line of proper diversity <note>The line accounting for the planet's apparent change in position</note> coincides with the line of the planet's apparent position. As the Earth proceeds from this place by its own motion, the line of proper diversity and the line of the planet's true position will begin to intersect within the body of the planet. One line will continue by its regular diverse motion in the **sequence of the signs** <note>Moving forward through the Zodiac (Aries to Taurus, etc.)</note>, while the other, reflecting away from it, will show us the planet moving faster in the **ecliptic** <note>The Sun's apparent path among the stars</note> than it actually proceeds by its own motion. But when the Earth reaches the portion of the Great Orb closer to the planet, this line immediately turns backward into the **preceding signs**, so that the apparent progress of the planet subsequently appears slower to us. Furthermore, because the Earth "ascends" toward the planet, the line of the Sun's true motion will be advanced by the planet; and the planet will be estimated to approach us, as if descending from the upper part. The planet will appear **direct** <note>Moving forward</note> for as long as the Earth's...

<margin>On all these matters, see Chapter 1 of the *Prodromus* <note>Referring to Kepler's *Mysterium Cosmographicum*, often called the *Prodromus*</note> and the tables on page 18; likewise below in the appendix.</margin>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>FIRST.</header>

When the center of the Earth has reached that position of the great orbit <note>The "great orbit" (orbis magnus) refers to the Earth's annual path around the Sun in the Copernican system.</note> relative to the planet, where the daily angle of reflection of the line of the planet's true position in **precedence** <note>Moving backward or westward against the stars.</note> stands equal to the daily angle of its own diversity in **consequence** <note>Moving forward or eastward.</note>: there, because of these two motions canceling each other out, the planet will appear to stand still in its **first station** for several days, according to the ratio of the great orbit to the planet's eccentric, the planet's position in its own orbit, and the proper velocity of its motion. Furthermore, from this same place, as the Earth is made closer to the planet, it happens that we believe the planet to **regress** and move in **precedence**, since the reflection itself notably exceeds the planet's own motion. This continues until the Earth reaches the true **perigee** <note>The point where the planet is closest to the Earth.</note> of the planet with respect to the great orbit, where the planet stands in the middle of its retreat, in **opposition** to the Sun and closest to the Earth. When Mars is found in this position, besides the common reflection or **diversity of aspect** <note>Parallax; the apparent shift in position when viewed from different points.</note> due to the great orbit, it also admits another additional diversity of aspect (on account of the perceptible magnitude of the distance from the center of the Earth to the planet), as diligent observation will testify.

<margin>Note concerning Mars. When it is made closest to the Earth, it admits a **parallax**; for it becomes closer to the Earth than the Sun itself.</margin>

For confirmation of this—which follows from the hypotheses of Copernicus, though it is a sentiment most distantly contrary to the common hypotheses—I wish to add what the most excellent and noble mathematician **Tycho Brahe**, a Dane, observed through his experiments. Since these observations were held by a most skilled craftsman, their credibility certainly cannot be doubted. In a letter given to Master **Peucer** in the year 1588, he writes: *I decided to make a trial of which of those hypotheses found thus far (namely the Ptolemaic or common ones, or those handed down by Copernicus, or those who devised the fiction of homocentrics) approached closer to the truth. And I trusted I would learn this from a most expert observation of Mars itself, specifically when it approached the Earth at its **acronychal** <note>Rising at sunset and visible all night; this occurs when a planet is in opposition to the Sun.</note> rising. For if it approached us more closely than the Sun itself, the Copernican speculation would prevail; if less, I judged one should stand rather with Ptolemy. Finally, with great diligence and no small expense, having acquired various astronomical instruments by which the motions of the stars could be explored centrally—not just to the minute, but even to a half or quarter part of it—I did not fear to undertake this subtle consideration. This was done especially in the year 1582, when Mars was placed in opposition to the Sun at night in the sign of **Cancer**, and moreover was rendered higher in its daily circuit by its northern latitude. Therefore, having then taken many accurate observations, as much around its rising as its setting, and its transit through the meridian, I discovered that Mars caused a greater **parallax** than the Sun itself, and was therefore even closer to the Earth when it was acronychal. At the same time, its daily motion agreed with the Copernican numbers for the whole, because it retreated in precedence a little more swiftly over a certain interval of days than the reasoning of the **Alphonsine tables** <note>Medieval astronomical tables based on the Ptolemaic system.</note> deduced from Ptolemy would allow; and this was on account of the smaller distance from the Earth, which caused the motion to appear a little more intense. When I had explored this sufficiently in Mars, and the same also in Venus...*

I observed Venus several times during the day, even at the very Meridian, and when it was positioned near the sunset ecliptic. Indeed, by another peculiar method around the 24th of February in the year 1587—when within one or two days I saw it both in the evening and the morning (which is otherwise a very rare occurrence)—I proved it by comparing the apparent motion with those things that occur by reason of parallax <note>Parallax is the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from two different lines of sight.</note>. Thus, I found that all these things did not agree with the Ptolemaic hypotheses at all; I was therefore driven, more and more afterward, to grant credit to the Copernican invention.

Lastly, when the Earth is moved forward from this central conjunction with the planet (so to speak), the reflection <note>The "reflection" refers to the apparent backward or retrograde motion caused by Earth's own movement.</note> toward the preceding signs will be diminished by the same proportion as it had previously increased, until, a compensation of motions having been made again, the planet becomes stationary at its second station. Afterward, as the planet's own motion overcomes the reflection, it is directed forward by the proceeding Earth, until finally the planet appears in the middle point of its direction, and the Earth again reaches the true apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the center.</note> of the planet from which we led it; thus it introduces all the aforementioned appearances to us in order for each of the planets.

<margin>The Argument of the planet, which Copernicus calls the Commutation.</margin>And this is the first utility of the Great Orb <note>The "Great Orb" (orbis magnus) is the term used by Copernicans for the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun.</note> in the contemplation of the motions of the planets, by which we are freed from the three large epicycles <note>Small circles moving along the circumference of larger circles; a complex feature of the older Ptolemaic system.</note> in Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars. Furthermore, what the Ancients called the "Argument of the planet," my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note> calls the "Motion of Commutation" of the planet, because through it we calculate the appearances happening by reason of the Earth's motion in the Great Orb. It is established that these are nothing other, with respect to the Great Orb, than the parallaxes of the Moon are in relation to the Earth, on account of the relationship between the Earth's center and its orbits. Moreover, the motion of the center of any planet's epicycle, subtracted from the Earth's equal motion (which is also the mean motion of the Sun), leaves the equal motion of commutation. It is counted from the mean apogee, from which the Earth is also equally distant; hence, the true and apparent motion of any planet in the ecliptic is readily obtained from my Master's tables of planetary prosthaphaereses <note>term: prosthaphaereses <note>Geometrical corrections used to find the true position of a planet from its mean or average position.</note></note>.

<margin>Venus and Mercury, on account of the motion of the Great Orb, appear to move through the Zodiac just like the Sun.</margin>Furthermore, we shall find another part of the utilities of the Great Orb, by no means less significant, in the theory of Venus and Mercury. For since we observe these two planets from the Earth as if from a watchtower: even if they themselves remained fixed just like the Sun, nevertheless, because we are carried around them by the motion of the Great Orb, we would nonetheless think that the planets themselves, like the Sun, traverse the zodiac by their own motions. <margin>Their orbits appear as epicycles.</margin>
<margin>The apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> of their carrying spheres.</margin>
<margin>The mean and true apogee and perigee <note>The point in an orbit closest to the Earth.</note> of common proportion.</margin>
Observations testify that Venus and Mercury also move with their own proper motions in their orbits; besides the mean motion of the Sun, by which they are carried into the succeeding signs, other appearances are also seen in them by accident, on account of the Great Orb <note>The "Great Orb" refers to the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun in the Copernican system.</note>. For in the beginning, we shall consider their orbits as epicycles <note>Small circles whose centers move around the circumference of larger ones.</note>, which, as if on their own carrying spheres equal in pace to the Sun, complete the Zodiac. Thus, with the Earth existing at the perigee of the first carrying spheres, their entire orbits will be thought to be in the apogee of the eccentric, and conversely, at the apogee, the orbits are in the perigee. Furthermore, just as the apogees and perigees for the superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> are determined in the Great Orb itself with respect to the planets: so conversely in the orbits of Venus and Mercury, they are marked with respect to the center of the Earth wherever it may be, and are drawn through all locations of the carrying spheres according to the annual motion of the Earth. The boundaries of the diameter of the moving carrying sphere—which moves parallel to the line of the mean motion of the Sun (namely, that which goes from the center of the Great Orb to the center of the Earth)—are the mean apsides <note>The points of greatest or least distance.</note>. The apsides which are in the part of the moving carrying sphere opposite the Earth shall not be wrongly called the highest; those in the nearer part, the lowest.

<margin>Venus in 224 days. Mercury in nearly 88 days.</margin>
If, however, the annual motion of the Earth were at rest, since Venus completes her revolution in nine months (as said above) and Mercury in nearly three, each in its own space of time would appear to us from the Earth to be joined with the Sun twice; twice stationary, and twice reaching the extreme limits in the curvatures of the carrying spheres: and would appear once as morning star, evening star, retrograde <note>Moving backward against the stars.</note>, direct, at apogee, and at perigee. Furthermore, to an eye in the center of the Great Orb, at least the diverse proper motions of Venus and Mercury would present themselves just as the rest do; namely, traversing the whole zodiac by their motions, they would become opposite to the Sun, and would be seen to behold it in other configurations <note>original: "σχηματισμοῖς" (schematismois) - referring to astrological aspects or geometric alignments.</note>.

<margin>Motion of Venus and Mercury relative to the Earth.</margin>
<margin>Venus in 584 days, that is nearly 19 months and 8 days.</margin>
But in truth, since we neither contemplate the motions of the stars from the center of the Great Orb, nor is the Earth at rest in its annual motion, it will be clear enough why these same appearances appear with such variety to us inhabiting the Earth. Venus and Mercury move past the Earth with a swifter motion according to the size of their orbits, while the Earth itself follows them with its annual motion. # NARRATIVE

<page-num>144</page-num>

<margin>Mercury [completes its cycle] in 116 days, that is, nearly 3 months and 26 days.</margin>
In this span of time, they repeat and show us all the appearances which God willed to be visible from the Earth. The lines of their own diverse motions proceed regularly, completing their revolutions around the center of the <term>Great Orb</term><note>The "Great Orb" (Orbis Magnus) is the term used by Copernicus for the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun.</note> in the time pre-ordained for them by God. However, the lines of their true positions—which are projected from the center of the Earth through Venus and Mercury—are drawn around in a far different manner. This is partly because they are drawn from a point outside the orbits of those planets, and partly because that very point <insert>the Earth</insert> is itself mobile. We think that Venus and Mercury proceed in their orbits with that motion which the Ancients <note>Referring to Ptolemaic astronomers.</note> determined to be their movement in an <term>epicycle</term><note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one; used in old astronomy to explain planetary loops.</note>; yet that motion is actually only the "excess" by which the faster planet exceeds the mean motion of the Earth (or the Sun). My <term>Teacher</term><note>The author refers to Nicolaus Copernicus as "Dominus Praeceptor."</note> calls this excess the "motion of commutation," for the very same reasons he does so for the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>.

<margin>The commutation of Venus and Mercury.</margin>
Thus it happens that all the appearances of Venus and Mercury, which would have appeared even from a fixed Earth, return more slowly because of the Earth's motion. This allows these appearances to occur in all parts of their <term>deferents</term><note>The main circular path carrying the planet or its epicycle.</note> and in all locations of the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The sun's apparent path among the stars.</note>, whereby all the varieties of their motions are detected. For if the Earth were fixed under the sign of Cancer, Ptolemy would by no means have discovered that Mercury has its shortest <term>elongations</term><note>original: "evagationes" — the apparent distance of a planet from the Sun.</note> from the Sun around Libra, and Venus around Taurus. However, wherever the Earth may be in its Great Orb, when Venus or Mercury is found at the sides of its deferent, it will appear to us to be at its maximum distance from the Sun. 

When lines are drawn from the center of the Earth touching both sides of the deferents of Venus and Mercury, they are carried in the sequence of the signs <note>Forward motion.</note> in the upper portion relative to the Earth. In the lower portion, closest to the Earth, they move to the contrary, where they also seem to stand still and move backward <note>Retrograde motion.</note> to our senses. This occurs when the line of the planet's true position creates a daily angle over the center of the Earth moving backward that is equal to or greater than the angle of the mean motion (which is also the Earth's) moving forward. From these facts, it is manifest why Venus and Mercury are seen to be wrapped around the Sun.

<margin>Why Copernicus calls the Earth's orbit "GREAT."</margin>
Furthermore, it is clearer than the Sun itself that the orbit carrying the Earth is truly called **GREAT**. For if Emperors received the surname "Great" because of successfully waged wars or conquered nations, surely this orbit was worthy of being assigned the most august name. For it alone, as it were, makes us participants in the laws of the celestial government; it corrects all the errors of the motions, and [leads them] into...

placed this most beautiful part of philosophy <note>In this context, "philosophy" refers to natural philosophy or astronomy</note> according to its own [breadth]. <margin>How the planets appear to deviate from the ecliptic.</margin>
Furthermore, regarding the latitudes of the planets, one must first see how rightly the name "Great" is attributed to the circle carrying the center of the Earth. This deserves all the more admiration because it is well known that the precepts of the Ancients on this matter are quite perplexed and obscure. While the motions of the planets in longitude provide excellent evidence that the center of the Earth describes the circle we call Great, its usefulness is even more conspicuous in the latitudes of the planets, as if placed in an illustrious light. For although the Earth itself never departs from the plane of the ecliptic <note>The ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun, which defines the plane of Earth's orbit</note>, it is nevertheless the principal cause of all the diversity of appearances in latitude. You indeed, most learned Master Schöner <note>Johannes Schöner (1477–1547), a noted astronomer and mathematician to whom this discourse is partly addressed</note>, see that this orb is to be pursued and embraced with the greatest affection, because it places the entire doctrine of motion in latitude before our eyes so briefly and clearly, with all causes set forth.

<margin>Copernicus deals with these matters in Book Six.</margin>
<margin>Explanation of the latitude of the three superior planets.</margin>
First, let the carrying spheres <note>term: deferentes; the circles or spheres that "carry" the planet in its orbit</note> of the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> be inclined to the ecliptic according to the opinion of Ptolemy, such that their apogees <note>The point in the orbit farthest from the center</note> are found toward the north, and their perigees toward the south. Thus the planets themselves are carried around in their own orbs, just as the Moon is in its tilted orb, from whose plane it does not depart. The lines of proper diversity—the "Dragons of the planets" <note>original: "Dracones planetarum"; a traditional term for the nodes where a planet's path crosses the ecliptic</note> as they are commonly called—will designate the relationships and intersections of the carrying spheres to the plane of the ecliptic for the motions of the planets. However, the lines of true positions, intersecting the aforementioned lines at the centers of the planets, will report the true locations of the planets—closer to or further from the line through the middle of the signs <note>The ecliptic line</note>—according to the position of the center of the Earth in the Great Orb relative to the planet, and the planet's own position in its tilted orb, following the relationship of the angles they form with the plane of the ecliptic, just as mathematical reasoning demands. For this reason, it is clear that while the planet remains in any portion of its carrier and epicycle <note>term: epicycli; small circles centered on the circumference of larger ones, used in ancient astronomy to explain retrograde motion</note> in the tilted circle, and the center of the Earth is in the more remote half of the Great Orb from the planet (which the Ancients called the upper part of the epicycle), the apparent latitudes must become smaller than the angle of inclination of the carrier to the plane of the ecliptic. <page-num>146</page-num>

-># NARRATION<-

The angle of latitude is sharper than the angle of inclination—specifically, the interior angle compared to the exterior and opposite one. Furthermore, when the center of the Earth reaches the half of the great orbital circle <note>The Earth's orbit around the Sun</note> closer to the planet, the apparent latitude is conversely seen to be greater than the angle of inclination for the exact same reasons; for what was previously exterior and opposite is now interior. And this is the reason why the Ancients thought that when the center of the epicycle <note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</note> stood outside the nodes <note>The two points where a planetary orbit crosses the ecliptic</note>, the upper part of the epicycle always existed between the plane of the deferent <note>The main orbital path</note> and the ecliptic, while the remaining half turned toward that side to which the half of the deferent occupied by the center of the epicycle inclined. They believed the diameter passing through the mean longitudes of the epicycle moved parallel to the plane of the ecliptic, and that with the epicycle at the nodes, the planet would have no latitude regardless of its position in its epicycle. This is verified in these hypotheses when the planet stays in one of the nodes and the Earth is found in any part of the great orbit.

If the angle of the surface of the epicycle to its own deferent had been found in the hypotheses of the Ancients to be perpetually equal to the angle of inclination of the plane of the deferent and the ecliptic—that is, if the plane of the epicycle had always been detected to be parallel to the ecliptic—the aforementioned calculation of latitudes would suffice. However, since observations geometrically examined imply something different from this (as can be seen in Ptolemy’s final book of the *Great Treatise* <note>original: "τῆς μεγάλης συντάξεως" (The Almagest)</note>), the Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> posits that through a motion of librations <note>A slow oscillation, like a swinging scale</note>, the angle of inclination of the deferent to the ecliptic increases and decreases by a certain ratio, specifically in respect to the motion of the mean planet in the sloping circle and of the Earth itself in the great orbit. This occurs if, in one period of the commutation motion, the diameter through which the libration happens is traced twice from the extreme limits of the sloping circle. This is observed under such a condition that when the planet is at its evening rising, the angle of inclination is at its maximum, and therefore the apparent latitude is also greater; but at its morning rising it is at its minimum, whence the apparent latitude itself, as was fitting, exists as smaller.

The Master therefore posits that the plane in which Venus moves declines from the plane of the ecliptic (or the great orbit) upon a diameter through its own apsides <note>The points in an orbit closest to or furthest from the center</note>...

# FIRST BOOK.

<header>FIRST.</header>

<margin>On Declinations and Reflections</margin>
...of the first carrying circle <note>original: "deferentis primi"; the deferent is the main circular path in ancient astronomy</note> itself, so that the eastern half is raised toward the north from the flat surface of the ecliptic <note>the apparent path of the Sun</note>, at an angle of inclination which, in Ptolemy’s hypotheses, the plane of the epicycle would maintain with the plane of the deferent; the western half, however, is raised toward the south. By the "eastern half," we should understand that part which extends from the position of the highest apogee <note>original: "summae absidis"; the point furthest from the center</note> in the direction of the signs <note>following the order of the zodiac</note>, etc. With this single and simple hypothesis, it will be easy to perceive all the rules of declinations and reflections, along with their causes, based on the relationship of the Earth's position to the plane of the planet.

<margin>Since the latitudes in Venus depend on a single cause.</margin>
For when, through the annual motion of the Earth, we reach the parts opposite the highest apogee of the first deferent—where we consider the orbit of Venus to exist as an epicycle and to be at the apogee of its own deferent—then the plane in which Venus is carried will appear to us as reflected from the plane of the ecliptic, for in such a position we view it from across. And because we observe that same plane from a lower position, the part which projects toward the north will be to the left of our eyes as they turn toward the south, while the remaining part toward the south will be to the right. 

As the Earth proceeds again toward the planet's highest apogee, the orbit of Venus is believed to descend from the apogee of its eccentric, and we begin to look down upon that very plane carrying Venus—the plane of inclinations—as if from a higher place. Therefore, the reflection is successively changed into a declination, so that at a distance of a quadrant from the previous position, wherever the planet is seen in the elevated parts, it possesses only a declination from the ecliptic. In such a position, since we who are attached to the Earth are opposite the half of the deferent which extends from the highest apogee in the direction of the signs and is raised northward from the plane of the ecliptic, the Ancients said that the epicycle of Venus was in the descending node, and that the apogee of the epicycle declined most greatly toward the north, while the perigee <note>the point closest to the center</note> declined toward the south. 

Furthermore, as the Earth's annual motion carries us upward toward the location of the highest apogee of Venus, its orbit (acting as an epicycle) will seem to seek the lowest apogee of its deferent. The plane of the epicycle (to us, the plane in which the star of Venus resides), which was previously inclined to us relative to the plane of the ecliptic, will appear to reflect itself toward us again. The northern half of the deferent, projecting outside the plane of the ecliptic, will become the right side, because we are looking at the orbit of Venus from above. However, when the center of the Earth reaches the location of the highest apogee of Venus, no declination will be seen, but only reflection; yet the orbit of Venus in the low-

<meta>This page continues the technical astronomical discussion regarding the orbital mechanics of Venus, specifically focusing on the "Narratio" or account of its latitudinal movements as observed from Earth.</meta>

<page-num>148</page-num>
-># ACCOUNT<-

The lowest point of its carrying circle <note>original: "deferentis"</note> is believed, according to the opinion of the Ancients, to be without [latitude]. And here is the order of the appearances <note>original: "τῶν φαινομένων"</note>, while the center of the Earth completes a semi-circulation from the position of the lowest apogee <note>original: "absidis"</note> of Venus in the order of the signs, ascending to the position of the highest apogee of Venus. By the same reasoning, as the Earth descends, the reflection to our sight will gradually be changed into a declination; and because the half of the plane of the carrying circle from the highest apogee in the preceding direction is made opposite to us by such a movement of the Earth, the apogee of the carrying circle of Venus begins to decline toward the south from the plane of the ecliptic <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note>, until the Earth is positioned at the ninetieth degree from the place of the apogee. Then, each half is seen declined toward the plane of the ecliptic, and the orbit, or epicycle of Venus, is thought to be in the ascending node at the highest apogee. As the Earth recedes from this place, the declination is again changed into a reflection, and having reached the place of the lowest apogee of Venus, the Earth begins to produce the same appearances of latitudes in Venus again. From these things it is clear that when the Earth is placed at the line of the apogees of Venus, the plane of the carrying circle appears reflected; but in the quadrants from these, it appears declined, and in the intermediate places, mixed latitudes are observed.

<margin>The latitude of the deviation of Venus.</margin> However, since besides these latitudes, which the Ancients attributed to the epicycle of Venus, another deviation—called by the Ancients and by Ptolemy "of the inclinations or of the bending" <note>original: "τῶν ἐκκλίσεων ἢ ἐγκλίσεως"</note>—mixes itself with these, and they demonstrated this same thing through the center of the carrying circle of the epicycle of Venus (which has now been removed), my Master <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note> judged that another method, more consistent with observations, should be undertaken. Moreover, so that we may more easily follow this method of the Doctor, my Master, for saving the deviation <note>A technical term meaning to mathematically account for observed irregularities</note>, just as we have followed the rest proposed so far: let us establish that the plane we just mentioned is the mean plane, and therefore fixed, from which the true plane wanders now here, now there, by a certain ratio. And because all motions are perceived with less labor and expense in respect to the poles, it must be held at the beginning that one of the poles of the mean plane is raised toward the north from the plane of the ecliptic by the quantity of the angle of inclination, while the other is depressed by just as much toward the south from the opposite side. And what we are about to show concerning the northern pole, or those things which happen around it, must be understood in a similar way—by taking the opposition into account—concerning the southern pole. Accordingly, let us assume there is a mobile circle around the northern pole of the mean plane, of which those things which...

<header>FIRST PART.</header>

from the center, it corresponds to the greatest obliquities of the mean plane from the true plane: however, let the north pole of the true plane itself describe the diameter of the said circle through the motion of **libration** <note>original: "librationis"; a slow swinging or oscillatory motion, like a balance scale</note>. Furthermore, let the mobile circle follow the motion of the planet, so that Venus, advancing in its own motion, leaves behind whichever of the two following intersections <note>nodes</note> it encounters, and this by the law that, when a year is completed, it finally returns to the one it left. Indeed, once a great circle is drawn through the poles of both planes, the said nodes or intersections are determined by counting 90 degrees in both directions from its common intersection with the true plane—specifically where the poles of the true and mean planes differ. 

Meanwhile, while the period of Venus to either of the nodes is completed, the diameter of the said mobile circle is described twice by the pole of the true plane through the motion of libration. Let these things be done in such a way that the planet appears to have entered into such a compact with the center of the Earth, that as often as the Earth is at the **apsides** <note>the highest or lowest points in an orbit</note> of the **deferent** <note>the main orbital circle</note>, Venus—wherever it may be in the true deferent—deviates most greatly toward the north from the mean plane; that is, it stands furthest outside the mean path. 

Furthermore, when the Earth is at a distance of a quadrant <note>90 degrees</note> from the apsides of the deferent, the planet itself, with its entire true plane, lies in the plane of the mean deferent. But as the Earth traverses the remaining intermediate places, the planet also holds its course in intermediate deviations. So that this compact of the Earth and the planet might be perpetual, God ordained that the first small circle of libration (so to speak) should revolve once in the same time that one return of Venus to either of the mobile nodes occurs. 

To make these things clearer by an example: if, at some beginning of the motion of deviation, the north pole of the true plane is at its most southern point from the pole of the adjacent mean plane; and Venus is only at the greatest limit of deviation, which is northern, while the center of the Earth is staying in one of the apsides of Venus; then in the fourth part of a year, the Earth, by its annual motion, will come to the middle place between the apsides, and at the same time the planet will come to its intersection, or mobile node. And because the motion of libration is measured against the motion of the planet toward the nodes or intersections, the first small circle of libration will also complete a quadrant; and through the remaining small circle, which is twice as fast as the other, the pole of the true plane will be positioned under the pole of the mean plane, wherefore both planes will be joined. But as the planet recedes from this node, the Earth will proceed to the other apsis of the first eccentric, and the po—

<page-num>150</page-num>
-># NARRATION<-
<note>original: "NARRATIO"</note>
<sig>Payi</sig>

And the pole of the true plane will be moved by an oscillation <note>term: oscillation (libration); a back-and-forth motion used to explain periodic changes in inclination</note> from the pole of the mean plane toward the north. Thus it happens that even if Venus is in the south, as in our example, the southern latitude is nevertheless diminished; if it is in the north, the latitude increases. However, at the point where it arrives, the pole of the true plane will reach its maximum northern limit through the motion of oscillation, and the planet, by its annual motion toward the nodes <note>term: nodes; the two points where a planet's orbit intersects the plane of the Earth's orbit</note>, will again have its greatest deviation toward the north midway between each intersection. It appears, therefore, that the use of this assumed circular motion is so that the revolution of Venus toward the nodes occurs within a year, and with the Earth always positioned on the line of the apsides <note>term: line of apsides; the major axis of an elliptical orbit passing through the points closest and furthest from the center</note>, the planet—wherever it may be in its true plane—has the greatest deviation from the mean plane, and when the Earth is positioned midway between each apsis, it is at the nodes. Furthermore, by the motion of oscillation, it happens that when Venus is in one of the nodes, both planes are joined; and that part of the true plane which it enters always departs from the mean toward the north: whereby, as is fitting, this latitude remains perpetually northern.

<margin>The reasons and method for the latitude of Mercury.</margin>
Just as the plane of Venus, which we have chosen to call the "mean," is intersected by the ecliptic <note>term: ecliptic; the apparent path of the Sun, representing the Earth's orbital plane</note> on the line of the apsides of the first eccentric; and the half of that plane extending from the highest apsis <note>term: highest apsis (apogee); the point in an orbit farthest from the center of motion</note> in the order of the signs <note>original: "in consequentia"; following the zodiacal sequence</note> projects toward the north, with the remainder turning toward the south by the law of opposition:
<margin>The latitudes of Declinations and Reflections in Mercury.</margin>
<margin>The latitude of its Deviation.</note>
So in Mercury, by a similar reasoning, there is a mean plane which is inclined on both sides from the plane of the ecliptic over the line of its own apsides, as was appropriate, so that conversely the half of the mean plane from the highest apsis in the preceding direction <note>original: "in antecedentia"; against the order of the zodiacal signs</note> is northern. Therefore, in the annual revolution of the center of the Earth, the declinations and reflections in Mercury will be found to be swapped relative to those of Venus. Truly, so that this variety might be more conspicuous, God also arranged the deviation of the true plane of Mercury from the mean, so that it...

<margin>Epilogue.</margin>Most illustrious and learned Master Schöner, always to be honored by me as a Father: it now remains for you to look kindly upon this work of mine, such as it is. For although I am not unaware of what my shoulders can bear, or what they might refuse to carry, nevertheless your singular and (so to speak) paternal love for me has ensured that I did not fear at all to go under this heaven and, as far as was possible, to report everything to you. I pray that God, the Best and Greatest, may deign to turn this to good, and breathe upon me, so that I may lead the labor I have begun along the right path to the proposed end. If, however, anything has been said with a certain youthful heat (for as the saying goes, we are always endowed with a spirit more great than useful) or has slipped out through imprudence, which might seem to be said too freely against venerable and holy Antiquity than perhaps the magnitude and gravity of the matters required, you certainly—and of this I have no doubt—will take it in the better part, and look rather at my spirit toward you than at what I have actually achieved.

<margin>Copernicus is excused, because he departed from the opinion of the ancients not at all out of a desire for novelty, but because the facts themselves demanded it.</margin>Furthermore, I would wish you to be certain of this concerning that most learned man, the Doctor, my Teacher, and to hold it as your firmest conviction: that for him nothing is more important or more ancient than to follow in the footsteps of Ptolemy, following the Ancients and those much older than himself, just as Ptolemy did. However, while he understood that the phenomena <note>original: "τὰ φαινομένα"</note> which rule the Astronomer, and mathematics itself, compelled him to assume certain things even against his will, he thought it sufficient meanwhile if he directed his arrows toward the same target as Ptolemy with the same art, even if he assumed a bow and arrows made of a far different kind of material than that man used. And at this point, one must seize upon that saying: "He who intends to be free in his mind..." <note>original: "διὰ τ᾽ ἐλευθερίειν τὴ γνώμην τὸν μέλλοντα" - Rheticus begins a Greek quote emphasizing intellectual freedom in scientific inquiry.</note>

# NARRATION

...philosophy <note>original: "φιλοσοφίαν"</note>. Furthermore, my Distinguished Teacher is as far removed as anyone can be from that which is foreign to the character of any good man—and especially to a philosophical nature. So far is he from thinking that one should rashly depart from the opinions of the Ancient philosophers out of a mere desire for novelty, unless for great reasons and because the facts themselves demand it. His age, the gravity of his character, the excellence of his learning, and finally the loftiness of his intellect and greatness of his soul are of a different sort than to allow such a thing to befall him. Such behavior belongs either to youth, or to <term>the superficial observation of those who think highly of themselves</term> <note>original: "τῶν μέγα φρονόντων ἐπιθεωρία μικρᾶ"; a reference to Aristotle's critique of arrogant but shallow thinkers</note>, as I may use Aristotle's words; or it belongs to those of burning temperaments who are moved and ruled by every wind and by their own passions, so that, as if their <term>helmsman</term> <note>original: "κυβερνήτῃ"</note> had been cast overboard, they seize upon whatever comes their way and defend it most violently.

<margin>”</margin> But let truth prevail, let virtue prevail, and let the arts always be held in their
<margin>”</margin> proper honor. Let every good Master of his craft bring to light what is
<margin>”</margin> beneficial, and let him defend it in such a manner that he appears to have
<margin>”</margin> sought the truth. Indeed, my Distinguished Teacher will never shrink from
<margin>”</margin> the judgments of good and learned men, which he intends to
<margin>”</margin> undergo of his own accord.

### A PANEGYRIC ON
#### PRUSSIA.

<note>The title "Borussiae Encomium" refers to Prussia, a historical region on the south-eastern coast of the Baltic Sea.</note>

<image-desc>Large ornate initial P</image-desc>PINDAR, in that Ode which is said to have been consecrated in golden letters within the temple of Minerva <note>The Greek goddess of wisdom, Athena</note>, while celebrating Diagoras of Rhodes, the boxer and Olympic victor, says that his homeland is the daughter of Venus <note>Aphrodite</note> and the dearly beloved wife of the Sun. He further claims that Jupiter <note>Zeus</note> rained much gold there, specifically because the people worshipped his daughter Minerva. For this reason, the land was made famous by wisdom itself and by the art of *composing panegyrics* <note>original Greek: ἐγκωμιοποιΐας; the craft of writing speeches of praise</note>, which they cultivated with great devotion. I certainly do not see anyone who could more aptly apply this noble *encomium* <note>original Greek: ἐγκώμιον; a formal expression of high praise</note> of the Rhodians to any other region in our age than to Prussia (about which I intend to say a few words, as you also perhaps wished to hear). Nor do I doubt that the same governing deities would be found presiding over this region if some skilled Astrologer, with diligent care, were to investigate the stars ruling this most beautiful, fertile, and happy land. For just as Pindar says:

> The ancient sayings of men tell,
> that when Zeus and the immortals
> were dividing the earth,
> Rhodes was not yet visible in the open sea,
> but the island lay hidden
> in the salty depths.
> And since the Sun was absent,
> no one designated a share for him;
> and so they left him without a portion of land,
> though he was a holy god.
> But when Zeus was reminded, he intended
> to cast the lot again, but the Sun
> would not allow it; for he said
> he himself saw, within the gray sea,
> a land growing from the bottom,
> rich in food for men
> and fruitful for flocks.
> <note>original: Pindar, Olympian Ode 7. This passage describes the mythological origin of Rhodes rising from the sea.</note>

<note>original: "Borussia"</note>...since Amber is found so very far from the shore? For this reason, by the same law and as a gift of the Gods, it fell to Apollo as if born from the sea—Apollo, who once loved Rhodes as his wife, but now loves this land. Can the Sun not reach Prussia with direct rays just as it does Rhodes? I admit it, but he compensates for this in many other ways; and what he provides in Rhodes through the directness of his rays, he achieves in Prussia by his long delay above the horizon <note>The author refers to the longer daylight hours in northern summers</note>. 

<margin>Amber in Prussia.</margin> Furthermore, I think no one will deny that Amber is a peculiar gift of God, with which He wished to adorn this region above all others. Indeed, if anyone considers the nobility of Amber and the use it has in medicines, they will judge it—not without justice—to be sacred to Apollo, and even his own excellent gift, with which he might endow Prussia, his wife, in great abundance as if with a most precious ornament. 

And since Apollo, besides the medical art and **prophecy** <note>original: "μαυτικὴν" (mantikēn)</note>, which he first discovered and cultivated, is also possessed by a passion for hunting, he seems to have chosen this region above all others. And since he foresaw long ago that the monstrous Turks would devastate his Rhodes, it does not seem unlike the truth that he transferred his seat to these parts and migrated here with his sister Diana. For into whichever parts you turn your eyes—if you consider the forests, the game parks (which to the Greeks are **paradises** <note>original: "παράδεισοι" (paradeisoi)</note>), and the apiaries—you would say they were planted by Apollo. If you look at the groves and fields, and their rabbit warrens and aviaries; if the lakes, ponds, and springs, you would call them sacred to Diana and the fish-ponds of the Gods. And so it appears he chose Prussia above other regions, I say, as his own paradise. Besides stags, fallow deer, bears, wild boars, and other such commonly known wild beasts, he brought in even the aurochs, elks, bisons, etc., which can scarcely be found in other places. Meanwhile, I shall pass over in silence the many and very rare kinds of birds, as well as types of fish. 

The offspring, however, which Apollo has received from his wife Prussia are: **Königsberg** <note>original: "Regius mons"</note>, the seat of the most illustrious Prince, Lord Albert, Duke of Prussia, Margrave of Brandenburg, etc., the patron of all learned and famous men in our age. **Toruń** <note>original: "Torunna"</note>, once famous as a trading center, but now famous enough for its foster son, my Lord Teacher. 

<margin>To Nicolaus Copernicus.</margin> **Gdańsk** <note>original: "Gedanum"</note>, the metropolis of Prussia, conspicuous for its wisdom, the majesty of its Senate, its wealth, and the glory of its reborn literature. **Warmia**, a college of many learned and pious men, famous for the most reverend Lord Johannes Dantiscus, a most eloquent and wise Prelate. **Malbork** <note>original: "Marienburgum"</note>, the treasury...

### P R A I S E.

<margin>A</margin> <note>The capital 'A' likely marks the start of a new section or serves as a reference point.</note> The territory of the most serene King of Poland. Elbing <note>Modern Elbląg, Poland</note> is an ancient seat of Prussia, which also undertakes the sacred care of letters <note>scholarship and literature</note>. Culm <note>Modern Chełmno</note> is famous for its learning, and from it the Culm Law <note>term: Culm Law; a set of legal ordinances used in many Central European cities during the Middle Ages</note> took its origin. Indeed, you would call the buildings and fortifications the palaces and temples of Apollo. The gardens, the fields, and the entire region are the delights of Venus, so that it might not undeservedly be called *Pious*. Furthermore, it is no secret that Prussia is the daughter of Venus, whether one considers the fertility of the earth or the beauty and charm of the whole region. Venus is said to have been born from the sea; so too is Prussia the daughter of the sea and of Venus. Therefore, she not only provides such fertility that Holland and Zeeland are nourished by her grain, but she is also, as it were, a Granary for neighboring Kingdoms, as well as for England and Portugal. Besides these, she supplies others abundantly with the very best kinds of fish and other precious things in which she herself overflows. Moreover, Venus, being solicitous for those things which pertain to refinement, splendor, and living well and humanely—and since the nature of the soil did not deny it—ensured that they could be born and held in these parts; finally, with the help of the sea, she brought it about that they could be conveniently imported into Prussia from elsewhere. But since these things are better known to you, most learned Master Schöner, than that they should be related by me at greater length, and are treated by others in entire books published on that subject, I shall dispense with a more copious Praise.

I will only add this: just as the Prussian people are populous by the blessing of the presiding Deity, so too are they endowed with singular humanity. Furthermore, since they worship Minerva <note>The goddess of wisdom and the arts</note> with every kind of art, they feel the kindness of Jupiter because of this. For, not to speak of the lower arts attributed to Minerva, such as Architecture and those related to it, first the most illustrious Prince, then all the Prelates and Nobles of Prussia, in whose hands the highest power rests, and the Governors of the Republics, as befits Heroes, embrace with the greatest zeal the letters now being reborn throughout the world: and so, both individually and by common counsel, they strive to nourish and propagate them.

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>OF PRUSSIA</header>

-> "...public peace-lovers, cities established by good laws, wise men, the honorable and holy education of children, and finally the pious and pure propagation of religion, etc." <-

The shipwreck of **Aristippus** <note>A Greek philosopher and pupil of Socrates</note> is often cited, which they say he suffered near the island of Rhodes; when he was cast ashore and beheld certain geometric figures drawn upon the sand, he commanded his companions to be of good cheer, crying out that he saw the traces of men. Nor did his opinion deceive him, for through the learning in which he excelled, he easily obtained the necessities for sustaining life from learned and virtue-loving men, both for himself and his companions. So, as the Gods may love me, most learned Master Schöner, since the Prussians are most hospitable, it has not yet happened to me to enter the house of any great man in these parts without either seeing geometric figures immediately upon the very threshold, or discovering Geometry seated within their minds. Therefore, almost all of them, being good men, pursue students of these arts with whatever zeal and service they can; for indeed, true wisdom and learning are never separated from goodness and beneficence.

<margin>Tiedemann Giese was the author of the suggestion that Copernicus should complete new Astronomical Tables, and the one who urged him to publish them with their proofs.</margin>

But I am accustomed to marvel especially at the zeal of two great men toward me, since I easily recognize how meager is my own furniture of learning, and I measure myself by my own foot <note>A Latin idiom meaning to judge oneself by one's own modest standards</note>. One is the most ample Prelate, whom I mentioned near the beginning, the most reverend Lord, Master Tiedemann Giese, Bishop of Kulm. When his Reverence had most holily fulfilled the choir of virtues and doctrine, just as Saint Paul requires in a Bishop, and had understood that it brings no small momentum to the glory of Christ that a correct sequence of times in the Church, and a certain system and doctrine of motions should exist, he did not cease to exhort the Master Doctor, my Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note>—whose zeal and learning he had known well for many years—to undertake this province, until he finally impelled him to do so.

<margin>The Alfonsine tables were published bare, without any reasons or demonstrations. Copernicus wished to publish his Tables in the same way. See his own preface to Paul III.</margin>

Moreover, since my Teacher was by nature <term>socially minded</term> <note>original: *κοινονικὸς* (koinonikos) — meaning one who shares or acts for the common good</note>, and saw that the literary Republic also had need of a correction of the motions, he easily yielded to the prayers of the most reverend Prelate and friend. He undertook to compose Astronomical Tables with new rules, and promised that if he could be of any use, he would not defraud the Republic of his labors, as others—such as Johannes Angelus—have done. But since he had perceived long ago that observations, by a certain right of their own, require such hypotheses as would not so much overturn...

<summary>Rheticus praises the intellectual hospitality of Prussia and describes how Bishop Tiedemann Giese successfully persuaded a reluctant Copernicus to publish his revolutionary astronomical findings and tables for the benefit of the "literary Republic."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Encomium" (Praise) of Copernicus’s work, specifically discussing the debate over whether he should publish his revolutionary theories or merely provide updated calculation tables.</meta>

# ENCOMIUM
<note>A formal speech or piece of writing that praises someone or something highly.</note>

<margin>It is uncertain what Alfonso <note>King Alfonso X of Castile, patron of the Alfonsine Tables</note> thought regarding the motion of the spheres, or what Ptolemy <note>The 2nd-century astronomer whose Earth-centered model Copernicus challenged</note> believed.</margin>
<margin>For the calculation itself contradicts the opinion of Peuerbach <note>Georg von Peuerbach, a 15th-century astronomer</note>, which most people accept.</margin>
<margin>Tidemann <note>Tidemann Giese, Bishop of Culm and close friend of Copernicus</note> persuaded Copernicus with excellent arguments to imitate the honesty of Ptolemy rather than the fear or envy of Alfonso.</margin>
...hitherto regarding the order of motions and spheres, as it was commonly received and believed, having been discussed and examined, and how much it would clash with our senses: he judged that the Alfonsines should be imitated rather than Ptolemy, and that the Tables should be proposed with diligent rules <term>canons: sets of rules for astronomical calculations</term> but without the demonstrations <note>The mathematical proofs and physical theories behind the numbers</note>. In this way, it would cause no disturbance among the Philosophers; the common Mathematicians would have a corrected calculation of motions; but the true Craftsmen, whom Jupiter has looked upon with more favorable eyes, would easily arrive at the principles and sources from which everything was derived from the numbers provided. (Just as up until now, learned men have had to labor over the true hypothesis of the motion of the stars from the doctrine of the Alfonsines). Thus, everything would be clearly established for the learned; yet the common crowd of Astronomers would not be deprived of the practical use, which is all they care for and seek without true science; and that rule of the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras who kept their most advanced knowledge secret from the uninitiated</note> would be observed: that philosophy should be practiced in such a way that the inner secrets of philosophy are revealed only to the learned and those initiated into mathematics, etc.

<margin>1. The work itself would be imperfect. This is evident from the example of the inconveniences arising from the Alfonsine Tables.</margin>
The Most Reverend <note>Referring to Tidemann Giese</note> showed that this gift to the Republic <note>The "Republic of Letters" or the scholarly community</note> would be imperfect unless he also proposed the causes of his Tables, and, in imitation of Ptolemy, added the plan, the reasoning, and the foundations and demonstrations upon which he relied to investigate the mean motions and <term>prosthaphæreses: corrections applied to mean motion to find true position</term>, and continued the "roots" <note>epoch dates used as starting points for calculations</note> to the beginnings of time. To this he added how much inconvenience and how many errors had been introduced into the Alfonsine Tables when we were forced to assume and approve their decrees, no differently than they were accustomed to do, *instead of the "therefore"* <note>original: "ἀντὶ τῆς ἄρα" (anti tēs ara). A Greek phrase meaning they accepted conclusions as premises without proof</note>, which has absolutely no place in Mathematics.

<margin>2. Hardly any Craftsmen will dare to bring forth the true foundations of the tables once discovered.</margin>
Furthermore, since these principles and hypotheses are diametrically opposed to the hypotheses of the Ancients, there would hardly be anyone among the Craftsmen who would eventually see through to the principles of the Tables; and who, after the Tables had gained authority by agreeing with the truth, would bring them into public view. He argued that the situation here is not like what often happens in Empires, councils, and public affairs, where plans are hidden for a time until the subjects, having perceived the benefit, make it certain that they will approve of the plans themselves.

<margin>3. The Learned and Philosophers will, on this occasion, inquire all the more diligently into physical principles.</margin>
But as far as the more prudent and learned Philosophers are concerned, they will examine the series of Aristotelian disputation more diligently and weigh how Aristotle, after he had argued with many...

<summary>This page details the persuasion of Copernicus by his friend Tidemann Giese to publish his full astronomical theories and proofs rather than just the calculation tables. Giese argues that hiding the underlying principles (the heliocentric model) would be scientifically dishonest and would repeat the errors of previous astronomical systems like the Alfonsine Tables.</summary>
<keywords>Copernicus, Tidemann Giese, Ptolemy, Alfonsine Tables, mathematical demonstrations, Pythagorean secrecy, Aristotle, astronomical hypotheses</keywords>
