# PRUSSIA

<margin>Book 2, last chapter, On the Heavens.</margin>
<margin>Whether the motion of the Earth in the center of the World has been most clearly demonstrated.</margin>
<margin>Whether the parts of the Earth seek the center or the circuit, and whether the motions attributed to the earth should be judged as violent?</margin>
<margin>Can straight and circular motion be separated in actuality?</margin>
->After [Aristotle] believed he had demonstrated the immobility of the earth with his arguments, let him finally flee to that argument: *Mathematical matters and the things said by Mathematicians concerning the descriptions of the heavens; for the phenomena happen to agree with the layouts of the figures by which the order of the heavens is defined, as if the earth were situated in the middle.* <note>Original Greek: Μαθηματικὰ τοῦτοις καὶ τὰ παρὰ τῶν Μαθηματικῶν λεγόμενα... This refers to the Aristotelian view that mathematical models of the stars only work if the Earth is the stationary center.</note> Furthermore, they shall determine from this that if this conclusion cannot be subjected to the preceding disputes, lest we waste our oil and labor <note>A Latin idiom meaning to waste time and effort.</note>, the true account of astronomy must rather be adopted. Then, suitable solutions for the remaining disputes must be tracked down, and by returning to first principles, it must be examined with greater diligence and equal zeal whether it has been demonstrated that the center of the earth is also the center of the universe; and if the earth were raised to the orbit of the Moon, whether the torn-away parts of the earth would seek the center of their own globe, or that of the universe; especially since they all fall at right angles to the surface of the terrestrial globe. Moreover, since we see that a natural magnet has a motion toward the north of the diurnal revolution, [one must ask] whether the circular motions attributed to the earth are necessarily "violent" <note>In Aristotelian physics, "violent" motion is forced and contrary to an object's nature, whereas "natural" motion occurs spontaneously.</note>. Further, whether three motions—from the center, toward the center, and around the center—can be separated in actuality: and other things which Aristotle used as foundations to refute the teachings of Timaeus and the Pythagoreans. And these and similar things they will weigh within themselves, if they wish to look toward the principal goal of Astronomy, and toward the power and industry of God and Nature.

<margin>4. The learned, who fight more stubbornly for their conceived ancient opinions, are not to be feared: But a common fortune must be undergone with other soldiers.</margin>
4. But if the learned everywhere intend and resolve to insist more sharply and stubbornly on their own principles: he warned the Master Teacher <note>Likely referring to Nicolaus Copernicus.</note> that he ought not to expect a better Fortune than that which belonged to Ptolemy, the Monarch of this discipline. Regarding him, Averroes—otherwise a supreme Philosopher—after he had concluded that epicycles and eccentrics <term>epicycles and eccentrics: mathematical circles used in the Ptolemaic system to explain the complex paths of planets</term> could not exist at all in the nature of things, and that Ptolemy was ignorant of why the Ancients had posited gyrating motions, finally pronounced: *The astronomy of Ptolemy is nothing in reality, but it is convenient for computation; it does not exist.*

<margin>Book 11, on the 12th [book] of the Metaphysics, Comment 45.</margin>
Furthermore, the outcries of the unlearned—whom the Greeks call *unobservant, uncultured, unphilosophical,* and *ignorant of geometry* <note>Original Greek: ἀθεωρήτους, ἀμούσους, ἀφιλοσόφους, καὶ ἀγεωμετρήτους</note>—are to be held as nothing, since good men undertake no labors for the sake of such people.

<margin>5. The outcries of the unlearned are to be made nothing of.</margin>
By these and many other arguments, as I have learned from friends who are aware of all these matters, the most learned Prelate finally prevailed upon the Master Teacher, so that

<summary>This page defends the Copernican heliocentric theory by challenging Aristotelian physics and citing Averroes' critique of Ptolemy's mathematical models as mere computational tools rather than physical realities. It encourages the author to ignore the criticisms of the unlearned and the stubborn adherence of traditional scholars to ancient errors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's account of his travels in Prussia (Borussia), specifically detailing his interactions with high-ranking patrons and scholars who supported the work of Nicolaus Copernicus.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
->PRUSSIA<-
<sig>X</sig>

...he acted so as to promise that he would permit the learned and posterity to judge his labors. For this reason, good men and students of mathematics will rightly join me in giving great thanks to the most reverend Lord of Culm <note>Tiedemann Giese (1480–1550), Bishop of Culm and close friend of Copernicus</note> for performing this service for the Commonwealth.

Since this most generous Prelate loves these studies exceedingly and cultivates them diligently, he also possesses a bronze armillary <note>A model of objects in the sky, consisting of a spherical framework of rings</note> for observing the equinoxes, similar to the two—though somewhat larger—which Ptolemy records as having been in Alexandria, and which the learned flocked from all over Greece to see. He also arranged for a gnomon <note>The part of a sundial that casts a shadow</note> truly worthy of a Prince to be brought to him from England, which I saw with the greatest pleasure of mind, since it was fashioned by an excellent craftsman who was himself not unskilled in mathematics.

The other of my patrons is the distinguished and vigorous Lord Johannes von Werden, Burgrave of Neuenburg, etc., and Consul of the renowned city of Danzig <note>original: "Gedanensis"</note>. When he heard from certain friends about my studies, he did not disdain to greet me, such as I am, in his own words, and to request that I meet with him before I departed from Prussia. When I mentioned this to my Teacher <note>Referring to Nicolaus Copernicus</note>, it pleased him on my behalf, and he described the man to me in such a way that I understood myself to be summoned as if by that Achilles of Homer. For besides the fact that he excels in the arts of war and peace, he also cultivates Music with the favor of the Muses, so that with the sweetest harmony he may refresh and rouse his spirits to undergo and endure the burdens of the Commonwealth; he is worthy of being made by God the Best and Greatest a <gloss>Shepherd of the people</gloss> <note>original Greek: "Ποιμένα λαῶν"</note>; and blessed is the Commonwealth over which God has placed such Administrators.

Socrates, in the *Phaedo*, condemns the opinion of those who said the soul is a harmony, and rightly so, if they understood nothing beyond the temperament <note>original: "crasim," referring to the mixing of bodily humors</note> of the elements in the body. But if they defined the soul as harmony because the human mind alone, along with the Gods, understands harmony—just as it alone counts, for which reason some have not feared to call the soul a Number—and further because they saw that the most serious diseases of the soul are sometimes healed by musical concerts: then this opinion, that the soul of a man (especially a heroic one) is called a harmony, will seem to hold no disadvantage. Therefore, one might most rightly call those Commonwealths blessed whose governors possess harmonic souls, that is, philosophical natures. Certainly, that Scythian <note>Likely a reference to King Ateas, who preferred the neighing of a horse to the music of a famous flautist</note> by no means...

# IN PRAISE OF PRUSSIA

...than he who preferred to hear the neighing of a horse rather than the most excellent musician, whom others were listening to with utter amazement. I only wish that all Kings, Princes, Prelates, and other leaders of realms might receive their souls from the mixing bowl of harmonic souls; then I would not doubt that these finest disciplines—which are to be sought after primarily for their own sake—would maintain their proper dignity.

These are the matters, most illustrious Sir, which I thought should be written to you at present concerning the <term>Hypotheses</term><note>Refers to the Copernican heliocentric model</note> of my Lord Doctor <note>Nicolaus Copernicus</note>, about Prussia, and about my patrons. Farewell, most learned Sir, and do not disdain to guide my studies with your counsel; for you know that we young men have the greatest need for the advice of our elders and those more prudent. Nor does that charming Greek sentiment escape you: <gloss>The wisdom of the elderly is better than that which is written</gloss> <note>original: "Γνῶμαι δ᾽ ἀμείνως εἰσὶ τῶν γεγραπτέων"</note>.

From our study in <term>Warmia</term><note>A region in northern Poland where Copernicus lived and worked</note>. The ninth day before the <term>Kalends of October</term><note>September 23rd</note>, in the year of our Lord 1539.

# ON THE DIMEN-
## SIONS OF THE ORBS AND CELE-
### STIAL SPHERES ACCORDING TO
The Prussian Tables, following the opinion of Nicolaus
Copernicus.

### APPENDIX BY MASTER MICHAEL
MAESTLIN, PROFESSOR OF MATHEMATICS
*at the University of Tübingen.*

<note>The large decorated initial 'N' begins the word 'Lest' (Nequid)</note>LEST ANYTHING SHOULD BE LACKING from the most learned and ingenious *Forerunner* <note>referring to Kepler's "Prodromus" or "Mysterium Cosmographicum"</note> of the highly skilled mathematician Master Johannes Kepler, and from Rheticus’s eloquent *Narration* <note>The "Narratio Prima," the first published account of Copernican theory</note>: I have deemed it worthwhile to add to them, by way of an Appendix, the dimensions of the magnitudes—or rather the altitudes—of the Spheres of the World in which the wandering stars <note>The planets</note> are moved, according to the hypotheses of Copernicus. Furthermore, although these could have been excerpted and recorded without much labor from Copernicus himself, and partly also from Ptolemy: I preferred instead to receive them from Erasmus Reinhold. For he himself—as he testifies in the preface to the *Prussian Tables* <note>Astronomical tables published in 1551 which updated Copernican calculations</note>, and as the numbers of those tables attest—examined the observations and demonstrations proposed by Copernicus much more exactly and accurately than Copernicus himself did. Therefore, I consider the numbers with which Reinhold measured the Spheres of the World, and from which he constructed those tables of his called "Prussian," to be somewhat more perfect. It was to be hoped, however, that Reinhold’s *Commentary* on the books of Copernicus—or rather on his own Prussian Tables, which he promised several times—were available to us. For from that work, we would not only be able to describe these dimensions, but Astronomy itself would undoubtedly be greatly aided by many supporting tools for the easier restoration of the defects from which it still suffers. But because both this and other most useful works were interrupted by the man's unexpected and untimely death: the other remaining path must be attempted, by which one returns from the end to the beginning by a retrograde method. Therefore, the numbers written in his own Tables must be resolved, and from them, those values which Reinhold [found] through a diligent examination of observations and demonstrations for the purpose of founding those very Tables...

## THEORY OF THE SUN, OR RATHER
->of the Great Orb of the Earth, and its dimensions.<-

<image-desc>A complex astronomical diagram showing a central point A representing the Sun in the Copernican system. Around A are several concentric and eccentric circles. The outermost circle, labeled B C D, represents the zodiac in the sphere of fixed stars. An eccentric circle E F G represents the Earth's annual orbit, whose eccentricity is mobile. Its center H revolves around point I in a small circle H K L. Lines connect various points: A to G, A to K, F to N, E to M, B to R, etc. Other points labeled include P, Q, O, S, D. The diagram illustrates the varying eccentricity of the Earth's orbit.</image-desc>

In this Diagram, *A* is the center of the World, where, according to Copernicus, the Sun resides. *B C D* represents the zodiac in the sphere of the fixed stars. *E F G* is the eccentric, or the annual orbit of the earth, the eccentricity of which is established as mobile; for its center *H* is turned around *I* in a small circle *H K L*. When this center is at the highest point *R* from *A*: the eccentric is, as was said, *E F G*, and the eccentricity *A H* is as great as it can possibly be. But with the same center brought down from the summit into *I*: the eccentric, its position having changed, is transposed from *E F G* into *M N O*, and the eccentricity *A L* becomes the smallest of all, according to the 8th proposition of the third book of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Euclid's Elements of Geometry</note>. Here one must understand the <term>eccentric of the eccentric</term> <note>original: "eccentrus eccentri"; a secondary circle used to explain why the primary orbital circle's center appears to move</note>, or the <term>eccentreccentric</term> orb, in whose embrace this eccentric *E F G* and *M N O* is contained, and which leads its center in *H K L* around its own center *I*, just as is conceived in the ancient hypotheses of *Ptolemy*, or the common hypotheses in the Theory of *Mercury*.

Astronomers have understood this kind of approach of the true eccentric center toward *A*, and its withdrawal from the same, by the variation of the <term>prosthaphaereses</term> <note>original: "prosthaphærefeon"; from Greek, meaning the 'add-subtractions' or equations used to correct the difference between a planet's mean and true position</note>. For in the age of *Hipparchus* and *Ptolemy* these were much larger, and were subsequently found to be smaller. This approach of the centers is in total agreement with these observations. For, having drawn the straight lines *H F* and *L N* outside the line of the <term>apogee</term> <note>original: "apogæi"; the point in an orbit farthest from the center of attraction</note>.

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof for the varying eccentricity of the Earth's orbit, using a Copernican model where the Sun is central but the Earth's path shifts over long periods of time. It reconciles ancient observations from Ptolemy and Hipparchus with modern calculations by employing a secondary "eccentric" circle to move the center of the Earth's main orbit.</summary>

<page-num>169</page-num>
-> APPENDIX <-

with straight parallel lines, likewise AF and AN, of which the former designates the mean motion and the latter the true motion of the Sun or the Earth in the same arc of the eccentric (because EF and MN are equal by the 26th proposition of the third book <note>referring to Euclid's Elements</note>). But it is manifest that the angle HFA is greater than LNA, from the fact that while FHA and NLA are equal by the 29th proposition of the first book, FAH is seen to be smaller than NAL, as if it were a part of it.

Copernicus reasons that the center of the eccentric has descended along a circular line from H toward L based on the unequal motion of the Sun's apogee <note>The apogee is the point in an orbit farthest from the center</note>. For that which Ptolemy believed to be entirely immobile from the time of Hipparchus to his own, due to its excessive slowness, Copernicus discovered to proceed more swiftly. With these findings agrees the motion of the center I, in the small circle IPQ, which is equal around A, by which the line of the mean apogee AIB moves uniformly through the zodiac according to the order of the signs; but it moves with the center of the true eccentric at H upward in the opposite direction, and around L according to that order. There, therefore, around H, the line of the true apogee—drawn from the center A through the center of the true eccentric—meets the line of the mean apogee at the maximum eccentricity with almost equal steps: but here around L, both tend in the same direction. Moreover, the straight line from A, tangent to the small circle, marks the maximum distance between the true and mean apogee, such as AKR is here, indicating the apogee in S of the eccentric described from K (only the arc is depicted here).

The motion of the eccentric is annual. The motion of the anomaly <note>The angular distance from the apogee</note> from the mean apogee is also nearly annual. However, the revolution of the center of the true eccentric in the small circle HKL is, according to Copernicus (Book 3, Chapter 20), 3,434 years—equal, of course, to the anomaly of the obliquity of the ecliptic. To the circle IPQ, Copernicus attributes an annual motion (Book 3, Chapter 22) of 24 seconds, 20 thirds, and 14 fourths <note>original: "24.'' 20.''' 14.''''"</note> under the sphere of the fixed stars. Therefore, its period amounts to 54,000 years. Reinhold, following his own calculations, makes its annual motion 25 seconds, 33 thirds, and 12 fourths; for such is the difference between the simple motion of the Sun and its annual anomaly. Therefore, the revolution is completed in a little more than 50,000 years. But under the zodiac, it completes a revolution in 17,000 years.

Reinhold himself provided the dimensions of these Orbs in the table of the Sun's eccentricity, on leaf 98. For of such parts as the semi-diameter of the eccentric is 1,000,000, he assumed the maximum eccentricity AH to be 41,700. (Copernicus, Book 3, Chapter 21, placed it at 417).

### THEORY OF THE MOON, AND ITS
->dimension.<-

Copernicus, having rejected the eccentric-epicycle <note>A model using a circle whose center is off-set from Earth, carrying a smaller circle</note> of the common hypotheses from Lunar theory—as it satisfies neither the apparent size of the Lunar body (as held above on page 109) nor the lunar parallaxes <note>The displacement in the apparent position of the Moon when viewed from different lines of sight</note>—explains the motion of the lunar course through a concentric circle with two epicycles.

<note>The following diagram illustrates the Copernican lunar model, which uses nested circles to account for the Moon's complex speed and distance changes without moving the Earth from the center of the main orbit.</note>

In this diagram, A is the center of the earth; B C D is a portion of the concentric lunar circle or orbit; E F G H is the first, larger epicycle, whose apogee <note>the point furthest from Earth</note> is E, and perigee <note>the point closest to Earth</note> is G; at F and H the smaller or second epicycle I K L M is placed. The orbit A B C carries that larger one in a monthly motion through the zodiac according to the sequence of the signs; the larger epicycle, however, carries the smaller one at its top against the direction of that same orbit, also in a nearly monthly motion, according to E F G H: but the smaller one moves the moon attached to it in a half-month period according to I K L M. To the concentric circle, Copernicus assigned the duty of the eccentric used in the common Ptolemaic hypotheses; to the smaller epicycle, however, he assigned the role of the eccentricity of those same hypotheses, by this law of motion: that the Moon is found in Conjunction <note>New Moon</note> and Opposition <note>Full Moon</note> at point I, the point nearest to the center of the larger epicycle, but in every Quadrature <note>Half Moon</note> at point L, the point furthest from that same center. From this it happens, therefore, that the epicycle of the apparent motion of the New and Full Moon is N I O, but for the half-moon it is P L Q. The smallest of these has the
->pro-<-
->sthaphaeresis<- <note>The correction or "equation" added or subtracted to find the true position from the mean position</note>

<page-num>165</page-num>
->APPENDIX<-

## APPENDIX

The prosthaphaeresis <note>original: "fthaphæres" - a technical term for the geometric correction added or subtracted to find a planet's true position</note> is smallest according to the line CAI, and here it is largest according to the line CAL; not because the first epicycle <note>a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</note> itself becomes closer to or further from the Earth due to some eccentric, but because the apparent epicycle, being composed as it were of two, increases and decreases in size. Furthermore, this second epicycle accounts for the irregularity of the first. For the arc which Ptolemy (in Book 5, Chapter 5) believed to intervene between the mean and true apogee <note>the point in orbit farthest from Earth</note> of the epicycle, the smaller epicycle restores by the arc IK or IM, by which the arc EF or angle ECF is increased or diminished by the angle FCH or FCM.

It is indeed a marvelous thing that Ptolemy—here truly blind (if I may use the same simile as Rheticus <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus, the mathematician who first published Copernicus's ideas</note>), guided only by his staff—aimed so excellently at the mark in demonstrating the motion of longitude. 1. He posits an eccentric which moves uniformly not about its own center, but about the center of the Earth; in its place, Copernicus substituted a concentric sphere. Therefore, the regularity of its motion about the center of the Earth is owed to it not by mere assumption, as in the former case, but by right. 2. In both systems, the epicycle appears to increase and decrease: in Ptolemy’s, because of its approach to and recession from the Earth, but in Copernicus’s, because of the composite motion of two epicycles. 3. The motion of the Moon in the epicycle is unequal in both; in the former, because of a certain contrived "mean apogee," and here, because of the magnitude of the second epicycle—except that this equation increases there at the beginning of the anomaly <note>the angular distance from the apogee</note>, and here at the end. 4. And although in specific numbers these equations differ greatly between Ptolemy (or the Alfonsine Tables <note>13th-century astronomical tables</note>) and Copernicus (or the Prutenic Tables <note>16th-century tables based on Copernicus</note>)—such as the fact that the maximum equation of this anomaly is quite different in both location and quantity: for in Ptolemy it grows to 13 degrees, 8 minutes, and 7 seconds at an anomaly of 114 degrees, 3 minutes, and 53 seconds, as Pedro Nunes <note>original: "Petrus Nonnius"</note> most learnedly demonstrates in his 4th, 5th, and 6th annotations on the *Theories* of Peuerbach <note>original: "Purbachij"</note>. Yet according to the Prutenic Tables, that prosthaphaeresis rises to 12 degrees, 26 minutes, and 58 seconds when the anomaly is 77 degrees, 33 minutes, and 2 seconds. Nevertheless, at that point—namely, a quadrant from the start of this anomaly—where Ptolemy took the occasion to separate the true apogee from the mean in Book 5, Chapter 5, they agree wonderfully.

Reinhold <note>Erasmus Reinhold, author of the Prutenic Tables</note> did not express the numbers for the dimensions of the particular spheres here and in the following sections as clearly as he did for the Sun; they can, however, be derived...

It highlights specific discrepancies in the calculation of the Moon's maximum equation between the Alfonsine and Prutenic astronomical tables.</summary>

### APPENDIX

From the tables of <term>prosthaphaereses</term> <note>original: "prosthaphaereseon"; these are the "additions or subtractions" used to correct mean planetary motion to true motion</note> they can be derived as follows. The <term>prosthaphaeresis</term> of the first <term>epicycle</term> <note>a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</note> at 1 <term>Sexagesimal</term> <note>a unit of 60 degrees in astronomical calculation</note> 38 degrees is 4 parts, 55 minutes, 54 seconds, with an excess of 2 parts, 44 minutes, 6 seconds, which results in 7 parts, 40 minutes. This is the <term>prosthaphaeresis</term> of the <term>half-moon</term> <note>original: "lunae diuiduae"; the moon at quadrature</note>, which is clearly the greatest of all, namely the <term>angle</term> C A L, where A L is tangent to the epicycle P L C. <term>Ptolemy</term> demonstrates this in Book 5, Chapter 3, from observations; <term>Copernicus</term> in Book 4, Chapter 8, resumes it, although it is confirmed by no new observation. There is no doubt, therefore, that Reinhold also intended this to be the foundation of his calculation. Therefore, in the <term>triangle</term> C A L, because of the right angle of contact, the angle at A is 7 parts 40 minutes; wherefore, if A C is 1,000,000, then from the table of <term>Sines</term>, C L or C P, or C Q is given as 133,410.

Next, according to the degree of <term>anomaly</term> <note>the angular distance of a planet from its apogee</note> 1 <term>Sexagesimal</term> 30 degrees, the <term>prosthaphaeresis</term> of the second <term>epicycle</term> is noted as 12 parts, 9 minutes, 56 seconds. This is the angle F C K here, corresponding to the <term>quadrant</term> I K, because F K is perpendicular to C F. Therefore, if C F is 1,000,000, from the second table or the table of tangents, F K is given, or its equal F L, as 215,579. Hence the total C L is 1,215,579. But where C L was just now 133,410, C F (or C E or C G) becomes 109,750, and the remainder F L or F I is 23,660; likewise C I (or C N or C O) is 86,090. In parts where A C is 1,000,000. From these are produced the maximum <term>distance</term> of the new and full <term>Moon</term>, A H, 1,086,090, and the minimum, A O, 913,910. For the half-moon, however, the maximum distance A P is 1,133,410, and the minimum is 866,590.

The apparent size of the lunar body according to this <term>hypothesis</term> of two <term>epicycles</term> (about which see above, page 109) agrees much more correctly than with the <term>eccentric-epicycle of Ptolemy</term>. For if the apparent <term>diameter</term> of the new and full Moon at its highest point in N is 30 minutes <note>original: "scr. primorum"; scruples or minutes of a degree</note> (as Copernicus in Book 4, Chapters 19 and 22, and the Prutenic Tables, folio 122b, assume), it will become 35 minutes 39 seconds at O, and 28 minutes 45 seconds at P, the <term>apogee</term> <note>the point furthest from Earth</note> of the <term>quadrature</term>, and 37 minutes 36 seconds at Q, its <term>perigee</term> <note>the point closest to Earth</note>. The difference between the maximum and minimum is 8 minutes 51 seconds, which is consistent with observations. The <term>eccentric-epicycle</term> requires a much greater difference; for since Ptolemy in Book 5, Chapter 4, calculates the line of the perigee as 39 parts 22 minutes where the line of the apogee is 60, and the radius of the epicycle is 5 parts 15 minutes, the highest moon must be to the lowest as 65 parts 15 minutes to 34 parts 7 minutes. If the moon's diameter there appeared to be 31 minutes 20 seconds, as Ptolemy proves in Book 5, Chapter 14, it would here become 59 minutes 56 seconds—nearly double—which all universal experience rejects.

## MEASUREMENT OF THE LUNAR SPHERE, AND
### Of the Great Orb, or the distance of the Moon & Sun from the Earth, by Earthly radii: Likewise a comparison of the true magnitudes of the Sun, Earth, & Moon.

Erasmus Reinhold <note>Erasmus Reinhold (1511–1553) was a leading astronomer and mathematician who calculated the Prutenic Tables based on Copernicus's work.</note> in his general Table of parallaxes <note>term: parallax (the apparent displacement of a celestial object when viewed from different points on Earth)</note> of the Sun ☉ & Moon ☽ in the vertical circle, on page 137.a., counts the parallaxes of the Moon at the 4 limits of its orbit, see at r, N, O, & Q of the preceding diagram. Of these, those which occur at the horizon are the greatest: 1. at r is 50' 43'' (according to the difference of the first & second limit of 2' 13'', subtracted); 2. at N is 52' 56''; 3. at O is 62' 54''; 4. at Q is 66' 21'' (according to the difference of the third & fourth limit of 3' 27'', added).

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a circle with center A and a point B on its circumference. A line extends from A through B. Another line extends tangentially from B to a point G, forming a right angle at B. This illustrates a right-angled triangle ABG.</image-desc>

In this diagram, AB is the radius <note>original: "semidiameter"</note> of the earth, BC is the line of sight to the Moon lying on the horizon, and AC is its distance from the center of the earth. Therefore, in the right-angled triangle ABC, because of the angles given through these parallaxes, the line AB becomes known from the table of sines <note>original: "canone sinuum"</note>, in parts of which AC is the total sine of 1,000,000. See: in the first limit 14,752; in the second 15,397; in the third 18,396; in the fourth 19,299. But for those in which AC was found a little before in the four limits: 1. according to AP 1,133,410; 2. according to AN 1,086,090; 3. according to AO 913,910; 4. according to AQ 866,590; in such parts where AC, the radius of the Moon's sphere, was 1,000,000, the radius of the earth AB is produced here as 1. 16,720; 2. 16,723; 3. 16,721; & 4. 16,724. Moreover, having made a comparison of these and others in that Table of parallaxes, it is found that the true length of AB was produced by Reinhold, through a more exquisite discussion of the observations of Copernicus <note>Nicolaus Copernicus, the astronomer who proposed the heliocentric system</note> Book 4, Chapter 17, as 16,723, in parts of which the mean altitude of the lunar Orbit AC is 1,000,000. Therefore, in parts where the radius of the Earth is one part, the radius of the lunar sphere AC comes out to 59 parts 48'; AP 67 parts 47'; AN 64 parts 57'; AO 54 parts 39'; AQ 51 parts 49'; likewise CE or CF 6 parts 34'; FI or FL 1 part 25'.

<note>original: "h. e. semidiameter eccentriçi orbis Magni"</note> that is, the radius of the Great eccentric orbit (to which those parallaxes <note>The apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different positions</note> were calculated) is 1,000,000; because of the angle at C of 2' 58", A B becomes 863. But since these numbers deal with the smallest values, in which a few "third" minutes <note>A division of time or angle smaller than a second; 1/60th of a second</note> (which are not noted here) are neglected, they produce a difference that is not to be ignored: therefore, the remaining parallaxes must be examined in a similar way. However, once this is done (I omit that examination because it is rather lengthy), it is finally discovered that they all agree on 862. There is no doubt in my mind that Erasmus Reinhold <note>A German astronomer and mathematician (1511–1553) who calculated the Prutenic Tables</note> used that value in constructing his table. Hence, for those units where the radius of the Earth is one part, the radius of the eccentric orbit of the Sun or the Great Orbit is approximately 1160 parts and 6'. Its maximum eccentricity, the line A H (in the figure on page 162), is 48 parts and 23', and the minimum A L is 37 parts and 21'; the difference between these, H L, is 11 parts and 2'. From these, the distance of the Sun at apogee <note>The point in orbit farthest from the Earth</note> from the Earth at maximum eccentricity A B is calculated as 1208 parts and 29', while at perigee <note>The point in orbit closest to the Earth</note> A G is 1111 parts and 43'. But at minimum eccentricity, the apogee distance A M is 1197 parts and 27', and the perigee A O is 1122 parts and 45', in units where the radius of the Earth is one part. Furthermore, one radius of the Earth contains almost 860 German miles, of which 15 are counted in one degree of its great circle.

For investigating the magnitudes of these three bodies—the Sun, the Moon, and the Earth—let the closely preceding diagram be used (for its drawing can also serve this purpose), in which B is the Earth, and A B is the radius of the Sun or the Moon, encompassed by the angle of vision A C B. For the apparent radius of the Sun at the apogee of maximum eccentricity, Reinhold assumes that which Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer</note> put forward in Book 5, chapters 14 and 15, which Al-Battani <note>Latinized as Albategnius; a 9th-century Arab astronomer</note> also confirmed in chapter 30, and Copernicus <note>Nicolaus Copernicus, who proposed the heliocentric system</note> in Book 4, chapter 19 (though without his own specific observation) re-adopted: namely 15' 40". Hence, where A C is 1,000,000, from the angle A C B of 15' 40", the line A B becomes 4557. But where A C was found above to be 1,041,700, A B is 4747, in units where the Earth's radius is 862. From this it is known that where the diameter of the Earth is one part (since the ratio of whole diameters is the same as that of half-diameters), the diameter of the Sun is 5 parts, 30' 30". From the cubic multiplication of the diameters, the proportion of the bodies emerges, for it is demonstrated in the final proposition of Book 12 of Euclid <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Geometry"</note> that this ratio is tripled. Therefore, the Sun is one hundred sixty-seven (167) times larger than the terrestrial globe.

### APPENDIX

Thus, since the diameter of the new and full Moon is set at 30 minutes <note>30' of arc</note> by Copernicus and Reinhold <note>Erasmus Reinhold (1511–1553), a leading astronomer who calculated the Prutenic Tables based on Copernicus</note> (although Ptolemy makes it 31' 20", and Al-Battani <note>original: "Albategnius," a 9th-century Arab astronomer</note> 29' 30"), the line AB will be 4,363 units, where AC is 1,000,000. But where AC was previously 1,086,090 and the Earth's semi-diameter <note>radius</note> was 16,723, then AB is 1,739. Therefore, in a ratio where the Earth's diameter is one part, the Moon's diameter will be 17' 0" <note>0.283 of Earth's diameter</note>. By cubic multiplication <note>calculating volume</note>, it becomes known that the Moon equals one forty-fourth part of the Earth. The Sun, however, in its magnitude, exceeds more than 73 hundred <note>7,300</note> Moons.

For the sake of delight, I am pleased to add this as well. Since Astronomy, with the support of the mathematical wings—namely Geometry and Arithmetic—has flown all the way to the Sun and Moon and measured both their heights and magnitudes: Come now, let Urania <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note> herself explain to us those circles which we see as flat from the Earth (for the Sun and Moon appear as flat circles), and let her reveal the circumferences, diameters, and areas of said circles in German miles, as a more commonly known measure.

Having found the proportion of the diameters of the Sun and Moon compared to the Earth (which is: Sun 5 parts 30' 30"; Earth 1 part; Moon 0 parts 17' 0"), and likewise knowing the circumference of the Earth's great circle to be 5,400 German miles <note>A German mile was roughly 7.5 kilometers</note>: those other things which are sought will not remain hidden.

1. Since the proportion of the circumferences of a circle is the same as that of the diameters: it will be known from this that the circumference of the Sun's circle contains a little less than thirty thousand (30,000) miles, while the Moon's contains more than one and a half thousand (1,500) miles.

2. Archimedes demonstrates that the proportion of the circumference to the diameter is roughly triple and one-seventh (just as 22 is to 7) <note>The classic approximation of Pi (π)</note>. From this is elicited a diameter for the Sun a little less than ninety-five hundred (nearly 9,500) and a diameter for the Moon of nearly five hundred (nearly 490) German miles. These diameters appear to us as two feet, or rather one foot, divisible into 12 digits <note>A "digit" or "finger" was a standard unit for measuring eclipses</note>.

3. Half the diameter multiplied by half the circumference measures the area of a circle, which, as Archimedes demonstrates regarding the circle, is equal to a right-angled triangle comprised of the semi-diameter and the circumference of the circle. Therefore, the area or plane of the Solar circle exceeds the number of seventy thousand thousands (70,000,000) German miles. The plane of the Lunar circle, however, is one hundred eighty-six thousand...

### THEORIES OF JUPITER (♃), SATURN (♄), AND MARS (♂).

<image-desc>A complex astronomical diagram showing concentric and eccentric circles, epicycles, and various lines connecting points. The outermost circle is labeled with points G, H, D, Q, E, A, B, C, L, K, S, P, R, V, T, X. Inside, there are smaller circles and intersecting lines. Key points include M (center of a small inner circle), E (center of a larger circle), A (another center), and N (center of an epicycle). Lines connect these points and intersect the circles, forming triangles and other geometric shapes. The diagram illustrates planetary motion with epicycles and eccentrics.</image-desc>

->In this diagram, **A** is the center of the eccentric circle **B C D** of one of the superior planets, Jupiter, Saturn, or Mars. **E** is the center of the Great Orb <note>The "Great Orb" or "Orbis Magnus" is the term used by Copernicus for the Earth's annual orbit around the Sun</note>. Copernicus accepts this as the center of the World, just as was held above on pages 51 and 137; for this corresponds (by making a suitable comparison) to the center of the World in the hypotheses of Ptolemy. Now, the radius of the epicycle <note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</note> placed at **B**, **C**, and **D** is equal to a third part of the eccentricity **A E**. Copernicus posits that the revolutions of the eccentric and the epicycle are equal: the eccentric moves simply in the order of the signs <note>i.e., eastward</note>, while the epicycle moves likewise in its upper part, but conversely in its lower part. This is governed by the law that when the epicycle is at **B**, the planet is located at its perigee <note>the point closest to the center</note> **G**; and conversely, when the epicycle is at **D**, the planet is at **H**. By this equality of motions, it happens that when the center of the epicycle is outside the apogee <note>farthest point</note> **B** and perigee **D**, such as at **C**, the angles **B A C** and **K C L** (**K** here being the same as **G** or **I** there, and **L** being the planet) are equal. From this, it follows that the line **N L** (assuming **A N** is equal to the radius of the epicycle) is necessarily parallel to **A C**, and the angle **B N L** is equal to the angle **B A C**. Therefore, just as the center of the epicycle moves around **A**, so the planet has an equal motion around **N**. The path of the planet, however, is the line distorted from **G** through **L** into **H**, which circles almost circularly around point **M**, which is distant from **A** by a third of the eccentricity **B A**. However, Copernicus demonstrates in Book 5, Chapter 4, that this path is not perfectly circular. From this, the following points arise: 1. That Ptolemy thought this path of the planet **G L H** was truly circular, and therefore placed the eccentric of the planet around **M**. 2. That he did not place this eccentric **G L H** around **M** for...<-

It highlights the differences between the Copernican model and the earlier Ptolemaic system regarding the circularity of planetary paths.</summary>

<sig>Y 3</sig>

## APPENDIX

It is established that the planet revolves uniformly not around its own center M, but around a foreign center, which for that reason is called the <term>Center of the Equant</term> <note>The equant is a mathematical concept used to explain the observed speed changes of planets; it is the point around which the motion appears uniform.</note>, and it possesses double the eccentricity of M itself. Behold, though blind in both eyes <note>A metaphorical reference to the difficulty of perceiving these celestial mechanics through simple observation.</note>, how he has not strayed from the path at all, guided only by the astronomer’s staff or rod!

But if we prefer to demonstrate the motions of these planets through an <term>eccentric</term> <note>A circular orbit where the Earth is not at the exact center.</note> of an eccentric, everything will return to the same result. The circle BCD, with center A, represents this; it moves the <term>center of the true eccentric</term> on the circumference NM—toward N according to the order of the signs, and toward M against the order of the signs. It follows this law: whenever the <term>planet</term> <note>original: "planeta"</note> is either in the <term>apogee</term> <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> or in the <term>perigee</term> <note>The point in an orbit closest to the Earth.</note> of its eccentricity, the center of the true eccentric is at M, and its eccentricity is according to GCH. However, when the same planet is a quadrant's distance from there, here at C or rather L, the center of the true eccentric ascends to N, and shows the eccentricity according to FCI, where FL is a quadrant. Therefore, the path of the planet is again the curved line GLH, not very different from a circle. And let N be the point of equality, around which the planet itself moves regularly; for when it is placed at G or H, a straight line is drawn from the center of the true eccentric to the planet through N, because they are on the same line. When it has moved to L, that center is entirely united with N. Elsewhere, however, wherever the center of the true eccentric stays outside of M and N, a straight line from it to the planet is always passed through N. Necessarily, therefore, just as the motion of the planet is uniform around the center of the true eccentric (which is nonetheless mobile), so it is likewise made equal around N. This is the first <term>Anomaly</term> <note>An inequality or irregularity in planetary motion.</note> of the planet in relation to E, the <term>Center of the World</term> <note>The center of the universe, traditionally the Earth in this context.</note>.

After these things, the circle OPQR around E represents the Great Orb of the Earth. By this orb, the second <term>Anomaly of the Planet</term> which it has in relation to the Earth is demonstrated, because the Earth, looking toward the planet from different parts of its orb, produces various angles of apparent motion. For example: if the planet is at G, its motion relative to the center of the world E is only the same as its apparent motion if the Earth is at Q or O. For from the half-circle QRO, the planet appears further along in the sequence of signs, but from OPQ it appears further back from the line EG. Likewise, the motion of the Earth from P (or slightly before) through Q to R (or slightly after) causes the planet to appear to move faster in the sequence of signs than the line EG requires; but when the Earth passes through O, whatever was gained in that velocity is entirely taken back here. Whence the planet not only becomes slow, but appears to move backward in the signs. <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose geocentric model dominated for over a millennium.</note> explained these things through a specific...

APPENDIX

<note>The author argues that while previous astronomers required a separate small circle (epicycle) for every planet, the Copernican system simplifies this.</note> He believed that a specific epicycle was created for each individual planet; here, however, this one "Great Orb" <note>The Earth's annual orbit around the Sun</note> is sufficient.

## DIMENSION OF THE SPHERES.

In the *Prutenic Tables* <note>The astronomical tables published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551, based on Copernicus's calculations</note> at the Anomaly of 1 Sexagesimal and 30 degrees <note>90 degrees</note>, the equation of the eccentric <term>prosthaphaeresis: the difference between the mean and true position of a planet</term> is recorded as 6 degrees, 29 minutes, and 53 seconds. That anomaly in the Diagram is B C, or the quadrant F L from the apogee <note>the point furthest from the center</note> of the eccentric, and the equation is the angle N L E, due to the eccentricity of the orbit F L I from E; or it is the angle R E T from the mean apogee of commutation (with line R E P being parallel to line N L, or A C) to T, its true apogee. Now, in units where the semi-diameter of the sphere E A C, or N L, is 100,000 (this number is sufficient as the total sine <note>the radius of the mathematical circle used for calculation</note>, and it is likely that Reinhold used no larger a value), from the second table or the table of tangents, N E is 11,390, and its half, M E, is 5,695. From these, Saturn’s apogee distance E G from the center of the World is known to be 105,695, and the perigee <note>the point closest to the center</note> distance E H is 94,305, of which the semi-diameter of the sphere itself is 100,000. Copernicus, in Book 5, Chapter 9, has 10,569 and 9,431, in units where A B is 10,000; for in Chapters 5 and 6, he calculated E N as 1,139 and E A as 854, and B G or A N as 285.

Next, at the same Anomaly of 1 Sexagesimal and 30 degrees, the equation of the parallax of the orb is noted as 1 degree, 53 minutes, and 40 seconds, with an Excess of 0 degrees, 42 minutes, and 19 seconds to be added, so that the other equation becomes 6 degrees, 35 minutes, and 59 seconds. The first of these is the angle E G F, and the other is E H F, corresponding to the quadrant of the Great Orb O P or Q P—the former while Saturn is at its apogee G, and the latter while it is at its perigee H. Therefore, in the right-angled triangles G E P and N E P, through the angles given at G and H, the remainders to the right angles, G P E and H P E, also become known. Therefore, in units where E P, the semi-diameter of the Great Orb, is 100,000 (it is pleasing to make the semi-diameter of the Great Orb the total sine here as well as in the following sections, so that it becomes a common measure for all spheres), from the second Table, E G is produced as 968,600 and E H as 864,312 or 864,300. (One may assume this number instead of that one without any perceptible difference; for it is neither necessary nor profitable to wish to shake out every tiny detail with excessive subtlety—a practice which could be proven by many examples to have been followed by Reinhold as well). From this, the total G E H is 1,832,900, and its half is 916,450. But in units where the semi-diameter of the Great Orb is one part, the maximum altitude of Saturn from the center of the world, E G, comes out to 9 parts.

### A P P E N D I X

9 parts, 41 minutes, 10 seconds; and the minimum distance of Saturn <note>represented by the symbol ♄</note> is 8 parts, 38 minutes, 35 seconds. The mean distance, however, which is the <term>semidiameter</term> <note>half the diameter, or the radius</note> of the sphere of Saturn, is 9 parts, 9 minutes, 52 seconds. Furthermore, the straight line of Saturn's semidiameter contains, as we calculated above, 1160 semidiameters of the earth.

Where, however, and how great this maximum <term>prosthaphæresis of parallax</term> <note>original: "parallaxcos prosthaphæresis" — a correction or adjustment used to account for the difference between the planet's observed position and its true position due to the Earth's motion</note> may be when Saturn is turning in its apsides, is readily apparent; for the straight lines G V and H X, which are tangent to the Great Orbit <note>the Earth's orbit around the sun</note>, contain these values along with the already known lines E G and E H. Therefore, E G and E H, from the Table of hypotenuses or secants, provide the angle E G V at the <term>apogee</term> <note>the point in the orbit farthest from the center</note> as 5 degrees, 55 minutes, 33 seconds; and E H X at the <term>perigee</term> <note>the point in the orbit closest to the center</note> as 6 degrees, 38 minutes, 39 seconds. The complements of these angles to a right angle are the angles G E V and H E X, or the arcs O V and O X, indicating the distance of the earth from the apogee of commutation, which is 1 Sexagesimal, 35 degrees, 55 minutes, 33 seconds in the former case, and 1 sexagesimal, 36 degrees, 38 minutes, 39 seconds in the latter.

### D I M E N S I O N S  O F  T H E  S P H E R E  O F  J U P I T E R <note>symbol ♄ used in header likely refers to the section, but the text transitions to Jupiter</note>

In the same manner, one must proceed with the tables of Jupiter. For an <term>Anomaly</term> <note>the angular distance of a planet from its apogee</note> of 1 Sexagesimal, 30 degrees, <term>Reinhold</term> <note>Erasmus Reinhold, a leading astronomer who calculated the Prutenic Tables based on Copernicus</note> calculated the eccentric prosthaphæresis as 0 degrees, 13 minutes, 40 seconds, which is the angle N L E. Therefore, in a system where N L (the semidiameter of Jupiter's orbit) is 100,000, the line E N is found from the "fruitful" table <note>likely referring to the table of sines</note> to be 9,150, and its half, E M, is 4,575. Wherefore, Jupiter's apogee distance from the center of the World, E G, is 104,575, and the perigee distance E H is 95,425. <term>Copernicus</term> in Book 5, chapters 10 and 11, reckons E N as 917 and E A as 687, and B G or A N as 229, in a system where A B is 10,000.

Then, in the same place, the prosthaphæresis of the parallax of the Great Orbit, with Jupiter turning at its apogee, E G P is 10 degrees, 19 minutes, 50 seconds; and with the excess of 0 degrees, 57 minutes, 58 seconds added, the parallax of the same orbit, with Jupiter staying at its perigee, E H P is 11 degrees, 17 minutes, 48 seconds. Therefore, just as in the case of Saturn, where the semidiameter of the Great Orbit E P is 100,000, here from the "fruitful" table E G will be 548,600 and E H will be 500,600; and the total G H is 1,049,200, and its half G M is 524,600. But in a system where the semidiameter of the Great Orbit is one part, the maximum altitude of Jupiter from the center of the world, E G, will be 5 parts, 29 minutes, 10 seconds; the minimum 5 parts, 0 minutes, 22 seconds; and the semidiameter of the Jovian Sphere 5 parts, 14 minutes, 46 seconds.

The maximum prosthaphæreses of the parallaxes of the Great Orbit in the sphere of Jupiter, from the same numbers 548,600 and 500,600 from the table of hypotenuses...

## THE SPHERE & ITS DIMENSION.

The calculation of the dimension of the Martian sphere is a little more intricate, because Erasmus Reinhold <note>A leading 16th-century astronomer who calculated the Prutenic Tables based on Copernicus.</note> computed all the equations <term>prosthaphaereses</term> <note>From Greek: the corrections added or subtracted to find a planet's true position from its mean motion.</note> of Mars according to its diminished eccentricity. For after the center of the Great Orb <note>The Earth's orbit in the Copernican system.</note>—which we have hitherto accepted as the center of the world for the reasons stated above—approached the true Sun, the center of the World, and thus also significantly approached the center of the orbit of Mars, it also changed its eccentricity EA or EM so that it became perceptible in the equations; see Copernicus, Book 5, Chapter 4, folio 143.a. and Chapter 16, folio 158.a., as well as above on page 131.

At the Anomaly, in the tables for Mars <note>original: "♂"</note>, 1 Sexagesimal <note>A unit of 60 degrees.</note> 30 degrees, the equation of the eccentric is found to be 11 degrees, 3 minutes, 3 seconds, which is the angle LNE; because of this, the second table reckons EN as 19530 parts, where the radius of the sphere itself, NL, is 100000.

Then, at the same anomaly of 1 Sexagesimal 30 degrees, the equation of the parallax of the Orb at apogee, angle EGP, is set at 30 degrees, 59 minutes, 4 seconds; and with the excess of 5 degrees, 1 minute, 58 seconds added, the parallax at perigee, angle EHP, is 36 degrees, 1 minute, 2 seconds. Therefore, in units where EP is 100000, EG must be 166530 and EH 137550; and the total diameter GH is 304080. The half, then, HM or AB, is 152040, and EM is 14490. But in units where the radius of the sphere of Mars, AB, was previously 100000, EM comes out to 9530, where EM was also 19530. Wherefore the remainder MN is 10000; however, EM and MN ought to have been equal unless the eccentricity had been diminished. Reinhold assumes it to be smaller by 470 parts. Copernicus, in Book 5, Chapter 15, teaches that EN was demonstrated by Ptolemy to be 2000 parts and EA 1500; but he says in Chapter 16 that he himself found EA to be 1460, in units where AB is 10000.

Furthermore, in units where EP is one part, in this minimum eccentricity, EG is 1 part, 39 minutes, 55 seconds; and EH is 1 part, 22 minutes, 32 seconds; and the radius of the sphere of Mars, AB, is 1 part, 31 minutes, 13 seconds.

Also, the greatest equation of the parallaxes from the table of the hypothe-

The hypotenuses <note>original: "pothenusarum" - likely a shorthand or typo for hypotenusarum</note> offer those numbers, 166, 30. and 137550. See the angle Æ G V as 36 degrees, 50 minutes, 18 seconds; and Æ H X as 46 degrees, 38 minutes, 8 seconds.
But in the maximum eccentricity Æ M, of 10,000 parts (of which A B is 100,000), this Æ M is found to be 15,204, where Æ P is 100,000. Therefore Æ G becomes 167,344 and Æ H becomes 136,836. But where Æ P is one part, Æ G becomes 1 part, 40 minutes, 21 seconds; and Æ H becomes 1 part, 22 minutes, 6 seconds. Thus also N L E, the angle of the prosthapheresis <term>prosthapheresis: a geometrical correction used to find the true position of a planet from its mean position</term> of the eccentric, is found to be 11 degrees, 18 minutes, 36 seconds.

->T H E O R Y O F T H E S P H E R E O F V E N U S,
and its dimensions.<-

The logic of the motion of Venus is very similar to that in the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>; for its theory can be explained equally through an eccentric of an eccentric, or through an eccentre-epicycle, or if one prefers, through a concentric with two epicycles, just as was done in the preceding sections. The exception is that Venus is closer to the center of the world, inside the Great Orb <note>the Earth's orbit in the Copernican system</note>; and that while the epicycles or eccentre-eccentrics of the three Superior planets are commensurable with their own eccentric, those in Venus do not follow their own eccentric, but rather the Great Orb. From this it happens that those Superior planets strictly maintain a single path for their motion, namely the circle (or quasi-circle) G L H; Venus, however, included within these narrow spaces, does not remain so, but, as is clear in the next figure, wanders into all the angles of the region enclosed between the circles marked with dots.

The outer orb Q L K, around Æ, is the Great Orb of the Earth; the sphere of Venus lies within it. The motions of its orbs are bound by these laws: Whenever the Earth is in Q (the apogee <term>apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from the center</term>) or R (the perigee <term>perigee: the point in an orbit closest to the center</term>) of Venus, the center of the eccentric of Venus at M approaches as closely as possible to Æ, the center of the World, and Venus itself is found in the circle G C M. However, when the Earth is placed in quadrature <term>quadrature: a position 90 degrees from the sun</term>, as around L, that center recedes to point N, the furthest distance from the center of the World, and Venus is seen in the eccentric F C I, wherever it may be within it, etc.

By this theory, the decrease in the eccentricity of the Great Orb—as its center Æ approaches the center of the orb of Venus A—is perceptible in the same way as in Mars; for because of this, the prosthaphereses are found to be diminished. Reinhold <note>Erasmus Reinhold (1511–1553), an astronomer who calculated the Prutenic Tables based on Copernicus</note> also calculated them here according to the diminished eccentricity.

Concerning this approach of the center of the great circle <note>The Earth's orbit</note> to the centers of the orbits of Mars ♂ and Venus ♀, as well as to the Sun ☉, one may see in Table 5 above on page 51, in the first part of which their greater distance appears, and in the second, their lesser distance. Concerning Mercury ♿, it was discussed on page 66.

However, the reason that nothing is perceived in Mercury, even though the center of the great circle has moved away from its center, is due to the amplitude of its orbit, in relation to which the entire diameter of the small circle of the eccentricity of the great circle has no perceptible regard, just as Rheticus <note>Georg Joachim Rheticus, the mathematician who was Copernicus's sole pupil and instrumental in publishing his work</note> warns on page 131. Calculation teaches the same. For the line of the <term>perigee</term> <note>The point in an orbit closest to the Earth</note> I E H, found a little before, is (in the diagram on page 170) 864,300 parts, of which the <term>semidiameter</term> <note>Radius</note> of the great circle E x is 1,000,000 (it is pleasing to demonstrate the proposition at the perigee distance, as being that in which, if anything can be perceived, it would become most sensible). If to this the difference of the eccentricity of the great circle, 951 of those same 1,000,000 parts, is added and subtracted, they will be 863,251 and 863,349. Through these, however, from the table of hypotenuses, the angle of the parallax of the orbit is elicited as 6 degrees, 38 minutes, 12 seconds and 6 degrees, 39 minutes, 5 seconds; the difference of which from the 6 degrees, 38 minutes, 39 seconds found above for the prosthaphæresis is 26 or 27 seconds—in no way sensible in observations. A greater difference indeed emerges in the prosthaphæresis of the eccentric, yet even that is to be disregarded. For of such parts as N L is 100,000 and N E is 11,390 in the same diagram, the diameter of the small circle of the eccentricity of the great circle is found to be 104. When this is likewise added to N E and subtracted from it, 11,494 and 11,286 are produced. To the former of these, from the second table, a prosthaphæresis of 6 degrees, 26 minutes, 20 seconds is due; to the latter, 6 degrees, 33 minutes, 24 seconds; which differs from the 6 degrees, 29 minutes, 53 seconds of the tables by 7 minutes and 32 seconds—which the Experts <note>original: "Artifices"; referring to skilled astronomers or calculators</note> repeatedly say is by no means to be cared for in such matters.

# APPENDIX

[Image Description: A complex circular astronomical diagram. A large outer circle, labeled 'S' on the left and 'R' on the right, encloses several smaller, intersecting circles and ellipses. A central vertical line runs from 'D' at the top to 'B' at the bottom. Along this line, from top to bottom, are points 'H', 'K', 'P', 'N', 'E', 'G', 'I', 'A', 'L', 'O', 'Q'. A horizontal line connects 'F' and 'M' through 'E'. Other lines and curves connect various points, forming a system of epicycles and eccentrics.]

with motions combined much differently than in the preceding sections. Its <term>Theory</term> <note>The mathematical model used to predict a planet's position</note> according to Copernicus is as follows. With **A** as the Center of the World (which here, as in the previous sections, is the center of the <term>Great Orb</term> <note>The Earth's annual orbit around the Sun</note>), the Great Orb is **B C D**. **E** is the center around which the center of the <term>eccentrepicycle</term> <note>A secondary circle whose center moves along another eccentric circle</note> revolves within the small circle **F G**, though governed by different laws. 

Whenever the Earth is in the line of Mercury's <term>apogee</term> <note>The point furthest from the center</note> **B** or <term>perigee</term> <note>The point closest to the center</note> **D**: the center of the eccentrepicycle ascends to the peak of the small circle furthest from **A**; therefore, the position of the eccentric itself is **H I**. Mercury itself, however, is found at the lowest peak of the <term>epicycle</term>, **K** or **L**, closest to the center of the eccentric. Thus, in that position, the circle of Mercury’s path is **K M L**, the narrowest it can possibly be. (To this, in the theory of the Moon on page 164, the epicycle **N I O** corresponds). 

But if the Earth is in a position midway between the <term>apsides</term> <note>The points of greatest and least distance</note>, as at **C**: the center of the eccentric descends to **G**, the point closest to the center of the world **A** (these things happen in a manner entirely contrary to the superior planets) and resets the eccentric at **N O**. Mercury itself then climbs to the highest peak of the epicycle, **P** or **Q**, and describes the widest orbit of its path, according to **P R Q** (to this, the lunar epicycle **P L Q** corresponds). 

Copernicus established, however, that Mercury does not move through the circumference, but ascends and descends through the *diameter* of this epicycle; such <term>librations</term> <note>Oscillatory or back-and-forth motions along a straight line</note> can occur according to the method explained on page 125. 

By these motions, therefore, it happens that when the Earth is at **B**, the <term>prothaphaereses</term> <note>Geometric corrections used to find the true position of a planet from its mean motion</note> of Mercury's <term>parallax</term> are the smallest of all; for Mercury's orbit is both at its smallest, according to **K M L**, and appears smallest because the Earth is at its furthest distance from it. But at the perigee **D**, the orbit is again indeed at its smallest, but it appears larger because it is at its closest. In the <term>quadrature</term> <note>A position 90 degrees from the sun</note>, however...

...at point C, the orbit is indeed at its largest, but because it is further from the Earth, it appears no larger. However, around point S—at a third of a circle from B, or a sixth from D—Ptolemy and Copernicus establish that it appears largest, and therefore requires the greatest <term>prosthaphaereses</term> <note>The "prosthaphaeresis" is a geometric correction used to find the true position of a planet by accounting for the difference between its mean and true motion.</note>, by which it deviates furthest from its mean position or from the Sun; this occurs because, at that location, the mutual compensation of distance and true size produces that effect.

It is easily evident from what has already been said that the dimension of the orbits of <term>Mercury</term> must be investigated differently from the tables of <term>prosthaphaereses</term>. For even if the parallaxes are smallest at the <term>apogee</term> <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note>, the excess placed nearby does not belong to the <term>perigee</term> <note>The point in an orbit closest to the Earth.</note>, but to another location a sixth of a circle distant from it, where the orbit has a different size and the center has a different position. Nor should the proportional minutes be sufficiently trusted here. Therefore, one must proceed as follows:

1. For the first <term>Anomaly</term> <note>The angular distance of a planet from its apogee.</note> of 1 Sign and 30 degrees, the <term>prosthaphaeresis</term> of the <term>eccentric</term> is 2 degrees, 59 minutes, and 40 seconds, which is angle ACF. Therefore, if AC is 100,000, AF becomes 5,231 from the second calculation. And here is the closest approach of the center of the <term>eccentrepicycle</term> <note>A complex model combining an eccentric circle with an epicycle to account for Mercury's unique motion.</note> to A.

2. For the second <term>Anomaly</term> of 2 Signs and 0 degrees—that is, a third of a circle from the <term>apogee</term> (where the parallaxes are greatest)—the <term>prosthaphaeresis</term> of the eccentric is 2 degrees, 43 minutes, and 21 seconds. In the present diagram, the Anomaly BS is a third, or 120 degrees, and DS is 60 degrees. The center of the eccentric, by the required analogy of motion (for the motion of the <term>eccentric-center</term> is double that of the <term>great orbit</term> <note>The "great orbit" refers to the Earth's annual path around the Sun in the Copernican system.</note>), reaches point T; therefore FGT is two-thirds, and F will be a sixth. Thus, the chord GT is equal to the semidiameters EG and ET, according to the 15th proposition of the fourth book <note>A reference to Euclid's Elements regarding the construction of hexagons and equilateral triangles within circles.</note>.
